# Roadmap 2: the programme after 6.3.2

**Written 14 September 2026.** [Roadmap.md](Roadmap.md) is the record: 864 rows done, a handful open, three dozen deferred with their reasons. It has grown into the history of the project, and it is kept because every row in it is a claim that was checked. This file is the plan: what still stands between this server and the best mail server there is, held against what the people who run Gmail, Outlook.com, Exchange, Dovecot and Stalwart offer, and worked through in the order below. A row moves out of here into Roadmap.md when it is done, with the measure that says so.

The legend is Roadmap.md's: ✅ done and verified, 🔄 underway, ⬜ not started, ⏸️ deferred with the reason written down.

**Where it stands:** 176 done, 2 in motion, 8 not started, 4 paused.

That line is counted from the rows below by `build/check-roadmap2.py`, which CI runs; a branch that flips a row runs it with `--write` and commits the line with the row.

## 2. The Linux control panel: the browser Control Deck to parity

The desktop Control Panel is WPF and runs on Windows only; that will not change, and it should not - it is the best-in-class Windows administration program this server has. On Linux the administration surface is the browser Control Deck served at `/WebAdmin`, the REST API, and `hmailserver --set-admin-password`, `--create-database`, `--upgrade-database`. When this section was written on 14 September the API had 81 routes and the Deck ten views. On 15 September, by the census `build/check-deck-parity.py` writes into `hmailserver/docs/DeckParity.md`, the API has 242 routes (110 of them writes) and the Deck 19 views; of the 365 properties the desktop program writes, all 365 are writable over REST and 359 are reached from a Deck view. Parity is the item, and the census is how it is measured.

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **A census: the desktop Control Panel's every page and field against the REST API and the Deck** | **Done 15 September 2026.** `build/check-deck-parity.py` (landed 14 September, #260) writes `hmailserver/docs/DeckParity.md`, and `style.yml` now regenerates it on every pull request and fails when the committed copy is behind, as `ci.yml` does for the settings index; the script gives the same bytes on Linux and Windows. On the tree of 15 September after the remote-domain fold: 365 properties the desktop writes, 365 writable over REST, 359 reachable from a Deck view, 0 missing over REST and 6 over REST that no Deck view reaches (a folder permission's four fields, and an application password's name and active flag); 242 routes, 110 of them writes; 19 views. When it landed the figures were 335, 306 and 248. The number still to reach zero is the six unreached properties, which is section 2's measure and belongs to the Deck row below; `--strict` fails on it and is what the section's last row will turn on. |
| ✅ | **The REST write routes the census finds missing** | **Landed 14 September in two batches (#260, #262), and the last gap on 15 September:** the anti-virus settings group, sixteen more account fields, nine more domain fields, the distribution list's settings and a `PUT` for it, DNS blacklists, SURBL servers, white-list addresses, blocked senders, incoming relays, blocked attachments and the greylisting white list as resources, the cache group's eight ceilings and lives, an IP range's expiry, `abort_spam_flagged` on a rule's forward and reply, `user_interface_language`, and application passwords and folder permissions administered under the account's address. That took 95 missing down to 2, groups and their members. On 15 September the groups resource landed (`/api/v1/groups`, `/{id}` and `/{id}/members`, five tests in `RestApiGroups.cs`). The census on the tree of 15 September reads **349 of 349**: every property the desktop program writes is writable over REST. It had been corrected first for what it misread (a diagnostic's inputs, a parent's id, a name that is a key). What is left is the Deck's own reach, 343 of 349 (a folder permission's four fields, and an application password's name and active flag), which belongs to the next row. The row as written: Each validated by the code that owns the value, as the settings, rules, routes, aliases, accounts, certificates and listeners already are. |
| ✅ | **The Deck rebuilt as one page the way the webmail was** | **Done, 17 September 2026.** The page has 34 views and the census reads **402 of 402**: every property the desktop Control Panel writes is writable over REST and reachable in the Deck, with five assignments left out of the count and named in [DeckParity.md](hmailserver/docs/DeckParity.md) - three parent links a REST body carries in its path, and the two domain names the diagnostic run takes as arguments. **The reach half** (14 September, #260, #262): a harness of its own (`build/check-deck-script.py` and `build/deck-script-test.js`, in CI), full domain editing, IP ranges with expiry, fetch accounts, backup, the account editor in full, distribution lists and aliases under their domain, the seven small collections from one description, server messages, the scripting, cache and indexing groups with their verbs. **The shape half** (15 September): the webmail’s palette and dark theme from one `:root` block, the console’s spacing, radii and type ramp, a command bar and a navigation column that becomes a rail under 900 pixels, and no pointer needed - a skip link, `/`, `?`, Escape, arrows and Enter, and focus rings. **The last eight pages** (17 September): API keys; the quarantine; the archive and its legal holds; the metric history as a chart, a counter drawn as what changed between samples and a gauge as it stood; the update page with its three verbs; the DNS records each domain publishes, in one block to paste at a registrar; a folder’s access-control list, granted, changed and revoked; and the diagnostic run, over `POST /api/v1/diagnostics/run`, which is new. Two settings of the quarantine digest that only the desktop program could write are on the settings route as well, which is what took the census to 402. The catalogues hold 1,165 keys in all twenty languages and the harness is at **747 checks**. |
| ✅ | **The Deck in the twenty languages** | **Done 15 September 2026.** Twenty, not seventeen: the twenty languages the webmail reads left to right (it has since added Arabic, Hebrew and Persian, 23 in all), from catalogues of the Deck's own. There are 621 texts in `hmailserver/installation/WebAdmin/languages/<code>.json` on the tree of 15 September, in the form the webmail's catalogues keep and written against the webmail's register and words. Every text a reader sees uses one mechanism: `t()` and `tf()` for the sentences the script builds, `K()` for a table's hints, titles and captions, and the static markup walked once a catalogue is known, so English needs no catalogue and no request. The language is the browser's, or the one chosen in the switch, which this browser remembers, with English as the fallback. The Windows installer, CMake, the AppImage, `post-build.bat` and `build/build.ps1` all ship the directory. The server serves one at `GET /deck-lang/<code>.json`, unauthenticated as the page is, reading the bytes from disk. `build/check-deck-languages.py` holds every catalogue to the page in the same CI step as `check-portal-languages.py`, and the page's harness is at 448 checks. What stays English on purpose: a settings key's caption and description, which come from the server's OpenAPI document, and the sentence a refused route answered, inside a wrapper that is translated. |
| ✅ | **The Linux package's administration story written down** | **Done 17 September 2026: the README half on 15 September (6a4c48bd7), the wiki half on the 17th.** The wiki's *Installing on Linux* page now walks the first domain in the Control Deck (with `curl` kept as the alternative), says the Deck reads and writes with the census as it stands - 25 views reaching 382 of the 388 properties the desktop program writes - and gives the suite measure for the tree of 16 September (1,606 tests put against a Linux server, 1,326 pass, 280 skip, 0 fail) and the split into two CI halves; README's parity sentence reads the same census. What it said before: README's Linux section now says what the server reaches over REST: a domain whole over `PUT /api/v1/domains/{domain}` (DKIM signing included), accounts in full, distribution lists, routes, rules, IP ranges, fetch accounts, the settings groups, certificates and listeners, application passwords, folder permissions, the message store and the groups. It names the Control Deck as the front end and says what is still outside: `Account.ExportMessages`, a GSSAPI bind and a re-keying command. **What is left:** the wiki's *Installing on Linux* page, unchanged since 11 September. It still says under *Administering it* that the Deck's settings, logs, certificates and rules pages only read. It still gives the 6.3.0 suite measure (756 passed, 649 skipped) under *What is not there yet*. Its first start walks the first domain with `curl`, not the Deck, although README says the page walks it using the Deck. README's own parity sentence is also out of date: it reads 329 of 337 with the groups as having no page, where the census reads 343 of 349 and the Deck has a groups view. The row as written: An administrator who installs the .deb, .rpm or AppImage should reach a configured server without reading source: the README's Linux section walks it, and the Deck is the tool it names. |

## 3. The webmail against Gmail and Outlook.com

What it has, measured against the two on 14 September: a reading pane (right, below, off), conversations, a list with sender, subject, snippet, time and hover actions, search with operators and an options panel, labels with colours, stars, pins, mute, block, sweep, archive, junk, snooze, scheduled send, undo send, templates, signatures and identities, vacation, forwarding, rules, drag-and-drop to folders, right-click menus, keyboard shortcuts and a palette, tabs (Primary, Social, Promotions, Updates, Forums) or Focused and Other, select-all across a folder, attachments with previews and large ones as links, S/MIME, read receipts, one-click unsubscribe, contacts with CardDAV, notifications, offline reading and an outbox, a phone layout that installs as an app, dark theme, twenty languages. What it lacks:

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **The README's screenshots against the real bench** | **All three retaken against the bench on 17 September 2026, from the tree of batch 11: the inbox in its tabs with a conversation open in the reading pane, the message on its own with the reading pane off (an external-sender chip, an attachment card, the reply bar), and the Control Panel's Welcome page on its design system at 1920x1200. The scripts live in the repository now, under `build/readme-shots/` - `bench.ps1 -Up|-Down` brings the REST listener up on 9530 with a throwaway domain through the settings store (the file is a copy since schema 6042), `seed.ps1` fills the mailbox, `shots.py` takes the two webmail shots with Playwright - and the Welcome shot is `build/capture-cp.ps1 -Launch -Nav Welcome`. What the row said before:** The webmail's inbox (in its tabs, a conversation open in the reading pane) was retaken against the bench on 14 September (d3c5984f0). **What is left:** the message-open shot, `webmail-message.png`, last taken 13 September before the tabs, the docked compose and the pop-out existed. The Control Panel's Welcome shot, `control-panel-welcome.png`, was taken 12 September with `build/capture-cp.ps1`, before the Control Panel moved onto its new design system on 15 September. The seeding and screenshot scripts (`readme-seed.ps1`, `readme-shots.py`) live in a session scratchpad, not the repository, and cover only the two webmail shots. |
| ⬜ | **A UX audit on the real bench, desktop and phone, light and dark** | Every view walked with a seeded mailbox and screenshots read, the way the tab bar's overflow was found; what it finds fixed on the same branch. Then the same by a screen reader (NVDA) and by keyboard alone, since accessibility is where Gmail is strongest and most webmails weakest. |
| ✅ | **Calendar and tasks** | **Done 17 September 2026.** The server half - CalDAV, the iCalendar module, recurrence - since 15 September; the page since 17 September: the webmail's Calendar page (month, week, day, agenda) and Tasks page over `/api/v1/me/calendars`, `/api/v1/me/calendar/events` and `/api/v1/me/tasks`, several calendars with colours and shares (schema 6064), and the invitation card with iMIP. Section 4 has the rows; `docs/Calendar.md`. |
| ✅ | **Contact groups** | **Done 17 September 2026, with no schema change.** A group is a row of `hm_contacts` like any contact, and what makes it a group is its card: `KIND:group` (vCard 4.0) or `X-ADDRESSBOOKSERVER-KIND:group` (3.0), each member a `MEMBER` / `X-ADDRESSBOOKSERVER-MEMBER` line holding `urn:uuid:<the member's UID>` or `mailto:<address>`. A group has no address of its own, so its row holds the marker `group:<uid>` in the address column, which is never written into a card or answered over the API. The webmail's Contacts page gains a Groups card - make one, rename it in place, add and remove members by name from the same completion the To field uses, delete with an Undo - and the To, Cc and Bcc completion offers a group by name with how many it reaches and writes out the people, a group inside a group expanded once. `POST /api/v1/me/messages` does the same for any client: an entry with no address in it that is a group's name becomes its recipients. `/api/v1/me/contacts/groups` is the list, the create, the read, the rename, the member add and remove; `/api/v1/me/contacts` lists a group too, with `kind: group`, so one request serves the completion. CardDAV serves and accepts group cards as they came, so a group made on a phone is the one the page shows. A contact deleted is taken out of every group that named it, and an erased address out of every group of every account. `docs/Contacts.md`; `API/RestApiContactGroups.cs` and 17 harness checks. A group holds at most 500 members and a book at most 200 groups, on both surfaces, and a listing resolves every group against one index of the rows, so reading the book costs its size and not its square. **What is thinner than the row:** a name made here cannot hold `, ; < > " @`, since the recipient list is split on those, and a phone's group whose FN does hold one is listed and writable but not addressable by name; two cards from a client can leave two groups with one name, which the routes refuse - such a name is then left unexpanded and the send refused, rather than reaching whichever came first; a group card brought in from a file resolves only the members it names by address; and a group is one account's address book entry that expands at send time, not a distribution list - mail from outside to a group's name is not a group and never was. |
| ✅ | **Important, and sections of the inbox** | **Done 17 September 2026, in the page alone: no schema change, no new route and no new setting.** Gmail's importance marker as a heuristic that runs in the browser and writes one IMAP keyword. Eight reasons with weights, two points to be important: the reader's own mark on that sender (±4), whom they write to (the address book, or written to from this page since signing in, +3), whom they answered (+2), whose mail they open (+1) or star (+1), being named in a To of five or fewer (+1 - the folder listing carries To and not Cc, so a Cc-only recipient does not earn it), and the tab bar's Social/Promotions/Updates/Forums (-2). A message is judged once per session, at most twenty-five in a pass, and never at all once it carries `$Important` or `$NotImportant` - which is how the reader's own word wins for good. The mark goes on through `PUT /api/v1/me/messages/{id}/flags` as `$Important`/`$NotImportant`, so every other client sees it and the sidebar's **Important** view is the search `label:$Important`; the tally that decides it is `localStorage` under one key per account, sent nowhere and swept out - every tally in the browser, not only the one that can be named - however the session ends. An address is asked for as an own property of a map with no prototype, so a sender writing `From: <constructor>` is a stranger like any other. Corrected from the row's arrow, the open message's menu, the row menu, the palette, or Gmail's `+`/`=` and `-`, with an Undo that restores exactly the keywords the message had, offered over what did change when the server takes some of a conversation and refuses the rest; a mark counts once against its sender however many messages it covers. A listing drawn from the offline store is shown and never judged, and a mark made with a connection reaches both copies this browser keeps. Two account preferences through the preferences route: **Importance marker** (decided here / only what I mark by hand / no marker) and **Inbox sections** (Important, Starred, Everything else, with counts and folding heads). The tabs sort first and the sections divide the open tab, and a section is presentational with its rows a group its heading names, so the list is still a list to a screen reader. `docs/ImportantMail.md`; forty-three checks in `build/portal-script-test.js`, and two more with the offline checks. **What is thinner than the row asks:** it is eight explainable reasons and a threshold, not Gmail's model - there is no reputation, no reading time and nothing learned from anyone else - and because nothing is learned on the server, what one browser learns does not follow the person to another (the keywords do). Nothing is sorted, hidden or delayed for being unimportant. |
| ✅ | **Delegation in the page** | **Built 11 September 2026, before this row was written.** `GET /api/v1/me/folders` answers a `shared` list beside the account's own tree: the public folders, and each owner's folders this account holds a right on. The sidebar shows them under "Shared by <owner>" or "Public folders". A shared folder opens and reads under the rights granted, and a read-only share refuses a flag or a delete with 403 (`RestApiSelfService.ASharedFolderAppearsUnderItsOwnerWithTheRightsGranted`). Sending as the mailbox: the post right on the owner's INBOX makes the owner a `granted` identity in the From picker, a reply to mail addressed to the owner goes out from the owner by default, and the message leaves in the owner's name (the identities test in `RestApiSelfService`). Both follow the IMAP ACL setting, which is on by default. What it does not do: move a message between the account's own mailbox and a shared one (refused with 400), or let an owner grant access from the page (an administrator sets folder permissions, or an IMAP client with ACL support does). The page harness does not yet draw a shared tree. |
| ✅ | **Emoji, images inline in compose, tables** | **Done 17 September 2026, in the page alone: no route, no setting and no schema change.** *Emoji* beside *Formatting* opens a picker built from a table in the page - about a thousand characters in eight groups, since the page's content policy runs no script but its own and a message being written should ask nothing of the network - searched by name (in English; the group headings are translated), `Enter` taking the first match, the last thirty used kept in this browser and forgotten at sign-out, and one picked going where the cursor is in either editor. A picture goes in by paste, by drop or with *Image*: typed by its first bytes and not its name, drawn down to 1,200 pixels on its longer side on a canvas, held as a `data:` URL and moved into a `cid:` part of `multipart/related` by the send and draft routes, which is what Gmail and Outlook display - the path the formatted signature already used (`InlineImageParts`). A picture pasted into the plain box turns formatting on. *Table* inserts rows by columns with a header row or not, and with the cursor in a cell offers a row above or below, a column left or right and the deletion of the row, the column or the table; `Tab` moves on and adds a row in the last cell. Every table is written with `border`, `cellpadding`, `cellspacing` and `border-collapse` as attributes, because Outlook renders with Word's engine and ignores a stylesheet. The rebuild admits `img` with a `data:` source and `table`, `tr`, `td`, `th` and nothing else new, so a table pasted from a spreadsheet arrives as its cells and their words with its styles, widths, spans and handlers gone; the plain part writes a table as tab-separated lines and a picture as its name in brackets, and switching back to *Plain text* asks first. A draft with formatting now reopens formatted, its own pictures back in the editor as the `data:` URLs they were - a draft and nothing else: `#compose?draft=<id>` names a message by its number, so a message marked `\Draft` is the only one whose HTML is put into the page's own document, and the rebuild admits a `data:` source whole or not at all (the whole value the base64 alphabet, escaped again where it is written) so that no `src` can carry markup with it. Two defects found on the way are fixed with it: a plain key typed in the rich editor ran the page's shortcut of that letter, because the shortcut map knows a text field by its tag and the editor is a `div`; and a new message started with the last formatted one's words, because blanking the form switched formatting off after it had emptied the box rather than before. **What it does not do:** merged cells (`colspan` and `rowspan` are dropped, so a pasted merge arrives as a short row), column widths, cell colours, resizing a picture once it is in or editing its `alt`, a picture from a web address (dropped rather than fetched - that is a tracking pixel), an animated GIF that had to be made smaller (redrawn as a still PNG), emoji names in any language but English, skin tones and flags. A message holds at most 64 pictures, which is how many the send route can put back in place, and the sixty-fifth is refused by the page rather than sent as a `data:` URL no mail client shows; what a reply, a forward or a reopened draft carried over counts against the same twelve megabytes as the pictures do, so a forward with pictures added is refused in a sentence rather than by a connection that dies; a break inside a cell is a space in the plain part rather than a new row; and a file refused in a paste or a drop of several is still named when the files after it go in. 54 harness checks in `build/portal-script-test.js`, 36 keys in the 23 catalogues, `docs/FormattedMessages.md`. |
| ⬜ | **Performance at a hundred thousand messages** | Measured, not assumed: the list is chunked, the search bounded; a seeded folder of that size on the bench, timed for open, scroll, search and select-all. |
| ✅ | **A second account in the same page** | **Done 18 September 2026, with no schema change and no new setting.** Two mailboxes side by side, as Outlook has them: the account the page signed in as and one more, added under Settings, Other accounts with its address and password. The server's part is one query parameter on the existing route - `POST /api/v1/session?bearer=1` answers the account's session token in the body and sets no cookie, since a cookie is one per origin and the first account's is already there - and the token is then accepted as `Authorization: Bearer` exactly as the cookie would be: the same table, the same idle and lifetime ceilings from the same policy, the same `X-Requested-With` on a write, ended by the same password change and device revocation, listed by `GET /api/v1/me/sessions` with `bearer: true`. A token made for one form is accepted in that form only (a cookie's value as a bearer, or a bearer's value in a Cookie header, is refused), it is looked up in constant time like the cookie, never logged, and never minted for the administrator (403); and it is the mailbox and nothing more - an account that holds an administrative role keeps it on its password and its cookie and loses it on a bearer, which the administration API refuses (403) as it refuses an ordinary account, for the reason the administrator is refused the form. The account the request's own cookie already stands for is refused the form (409), under its own spelling or an alias domain's, so that the one-per-device rule cannot end the cookie's session for it. `DELETE /api/v1/session` with the bearer ends that session and touches no cookie. The page holds the token in memory and nowhere else - not `localStorage`, not `sessionStorage`, not a cookie, not a URL - so a reload forgets it, which the Settings card says; both accounts stand at the top of the folder column with their unread counts (the one not shown kept fresh every half minute with the change route, as that account), and a press is a sign-out of the view and a sign-in of the other: folders, list, reading pane, search, compose (From is the active account's identities), contacts, calendar, tasks, filters, Security and every setting are all read under the active credential because nothing else is left, a message being written is saved as a draft of the account it was written in before the credential changes, and a message opened in one account is answered from that account only. What this browser keeps stays the first account's: the offline store, the outbox, the push subscription, the service worker and the app badge, and the smaller things too - the recent searches, the tracker habits, the Important tally, the follow-up and reminder marks - none of which is read or written while the second account is shown (what it must remember, which follow-ups and reminders it was told about, is held in the page with the token); the second account's mail never enters the store, its push routes are never asked, and the Offline and Notifications cards say whose they are while it is shown. The second account's own Security page lists the session with a badge and can end it; when the server ends it, the page comes back to the first account and says so. `docs/SecondAccount.md`; `API/RestApiSecondAccount.cs` (seven tests, port 9666) and sixty-seven harness checks in `build/portal-script-test.js`, whose fake server answers a second mailbox by the bearer it sees. **What is thinner than the row asks:** one second account at a time, not several; a reload forgets it, on purpose, because a credential a script can read should not outlive the page; remote images are not loaded in its messages - a frame's own request carries the cookie, so its HTML is fetched with the token and shown from a blob, and a document shown that way is under this page's own policy, which allows none (the bar says so; `cid:` images show as always); downloads for it are fetched by the page and saved from a blob; its S/MIME keys are unlocked by hand on the Security page, since the password typed to add it is sent once and not kept; the pop-out buttons and the Gmail/Outlook.com import are withheld while it is shown, since a new window and the provider's round trip both load the page as the first account; a confidential link in its mail asks for proof, since that page reads the cookie alone; and with the one-session-per-device policy on, adding an account here ends that account's own session in this browser, as any second sign-in from one device does. The message HTML route now also writes its policy into the document as a `<meta>` tag, so it is under the same policy in a blob frame as behind its own header. What it costs is said plainly in the doc: a token a page holds is a credential a cross-site script could read where an `HttpOnly` cookie is not, and the webmail's policy - no inline script, no eval, nothing from anywhere else - is what makes that acceptable. |
| ⏸️ | **Confidential mode, tracking, translation, AI writing, chat and video** | Declined with reasons: tracking readers is the thing this project refuses to do to them (the webmail counts and blocks tracking pixels instead); translation and AI writing need a third-party service or a local model, and neither belongs in the server until one can run on the server's own machine; chat and video are not mail. Confidential mode is no longer declined here. Section 10's *A message that expires* (15 September 2026) plans it as a message kept on this server behind a link, and says plainly that it prevents accidental forwarding but not a determined recipient, which answers the objection this row made. |

## 4. Calendar and tasks (Phase 6 of the client programme)

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **CalDAV on the web-services listener** | **In the tree since 15 September 2026 (the batch after 6.3.3), unreleased.** RFC 4791 beside CardDAV, on the WebDAV layer the two now share (`DavSupport`: the bounded XML reader, HTTP Basic through the IMAP logon's lockout, the If-* preconditions, the response builders): `/.well-known/caldav` to this server's own `/dav/`, the principal answering `calendar-home-set`, the account's calendars under `/dav/calendars/<address>/<name>/` (one, `calendar`, until MKCALENDAR or the page makes more; since 17 September with `calendar-color`, PROPPATCH of the name and colour, and shares as `shared-{id}/` in the other account's home), PUT/GET/DELETE with ETags (409 `no-uid-conflict` naming the other object, 403 with the RFC's own precondition names, 415), PROPFIND 0 and 1, REPORT calendar-query (comp-filter, time-range narrowed in SQL and expanded through the module, prop-filter to is-not-defined, text-match and time-range), calendar-multiget (deduplicated, 64 MiB and 507), sync-collection with tombstones; the DAV header `1, 3, addressbook, calendar-access`. Schema 6041: `hm_calendars` and `hm_calendarobjects` (the ics as sent, its span and its first and last instance in UTC, an ETag, a sync token, a tombstone). `POST /api/v1/calendar/expand` expands an iCalendar text over a window for tests and tools. Eleven tests in `RegressionTests.API.CalDav`. Not there: `calendar-timezone` storage, free-busy, partial `calendar-data`, VALARM time-range, MKCALENDAR (one calendar per account). |
| ✅ | **iCalendar** | **In the tree since 15 September 2026, unreleased.** `ICalendar.cpp`: RFC 5545 parsed and written - VEVENT, VTODO, VALARM, VTIMEZONE, folding at 75 octets UTF-8-safe, parameter quoting, the four escapes - bounded (depth 8, 2,000 components, 50,000 properties); DATE, DATE-TIME floating and UTC, TZID through the object's own VTIMEZONE or a built-in table of 592 IANA and Windows names with 2026 rules, gap and overlap hours decided; RRULE DAILY to YEARLY with INTERVAL, COUNT, UNTIL, BYMONTH, BYWEEKNO, BYYEARDAY, BYMONTHDAY, BYDAY with ordinals, BYSETPOS, WKST, expanded in the DTSTART's wall clock across DST; EXDATE, RDATE (DATE, DATE-TIME, PERIOD), RECURRENCE-ID overrides; bounded at 10,000 instances so a hostile rule costs milliseconds. Refused rather than approximated: SECONDLY/MINUTELY/HOURLY, BYHOUR/BYMINUTE/BYSECOND, RANGE=THISANDFUTURE, unknown zones. Rules are today's, not history. The object a client sends is the object it reads back byte for byte except folding. |
| ✅ | **iMIP: invitations by mail** | **Done 17 September 2026.** An event with guests mails each a METHOD:REQUEST as text/calendar beside its text (RFC 6047), an update with the sequence stepped, a CANCEL on deletion. A message carrying a calendar part is marked in its JSON; the page's card reads it (`GET /api/v1/me/messages/{id}/invitation`) and offers Accept, Maybe, Decline and a new time: the answer keeps the event in the reader's calendar with their PARTSTAT and mails the organiser a REPLY (`POST .../invitation/reply`), a proposal mails a COUNTER, a cancellation is removed. On delivery, a REPLY is filed into the organiser's stored event and a CANCEL marks the guest's copy cancelled (`Common/Util/Imip.cpp`, hooked in LocalDelivery). `docs/Calendar.md`; tested end to end in `API/RestApiCalendar.cs` between two accounts of one server. |
| ✅ | **The calendar page** | **Done 17 September 2026.** Month, week, day and agenda views in the reader's zone; create by a click on a day or an hour; move by drag between days or onto an hour, resize by dragging the bottom edge in quarter hours, a recurring occurrence changed alone or as the series; a recurrence editor (daily to yearly, an interval, weekdays, an end by count or date), a reminder, time zones (the event keeps its own TZID; the page shows its own zone), several calendars with colours, shown or hidden each; shared calendars through CalDAV sharing (`shared-{id}/` in the sharee's home with the privileges the share grants, `owner` and `calendar-color` on it). Reminders while the page is open, as toasts and browser notifications. `docs/Calendar.md`. |
| ✅ | **Tasks** | **Done 17 September 2026.** The Tasks page lists every VTODO of every calendar the account can see, soonest due first, with the due day or time, the priority, the list and whether it is done; adds one with a due day; ticks it off, writing STATUS:COMPLETED, COMPLETED and PERCENT-COMPLETE:100 as a client does; edits the notes, priority, due time and list. What Apple Reminders and Tasks.org put in over CalDAV is in the list and what the page adds is on the phone; task lists are kept apart as calendars of their own, made in the page or by MKCALENDAR. `API/RestApiCalendar.cs` (both benches) and the page's harness. |

## 5. The Linux port beyond compiling

(The .deb's Boost pin, once a row here, was closed on 11 September: Boost is linked statically and the packages install on Ubuntu 26.04, proven in CI.)

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **The service host: systemd, and a live update on Linux** | **Done 18 September 2026.** The apply step on Linux is `platform/packaging/hmailserver-update`, a POSIX sh script the packages install as `/usr/lib/hmailserver/hmailserver-update` and the AppImage carries, run as root OUTSIDE the server's unit: the unprivileged service (`hmailserver`, `ProtectSystem=strict`, no capability but `CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE`) writes a one-file request to `/run/hmailserver` - the unit's new `RuntimeDirectory=hmailserver` - and `hmailserver-update.path`, a root path unit `postinst`/`%post` enable and start, triggers `hmailserver-update.service`, which reads and removes the request, checks every line (paths inside the Updates directory root's own configuration names, no symlinks, the server's unit only; a refusal goes to the journal alone, because root writes nothing into the service user's directory while a process of theirs may be alive to swap a file for a symlink - the server reports a request taken and never answered by itself), stops the unit (from which point every file root makes there is made under a random name and renamed into place), verifies the package AND the rollback image again as root with the new `hmailserver --verify-update` (the same Sigstore check the server made, by a process the service could not influence) and then applies the version rule - the package's own version, read from the package and never from the request, must be newer than what the package database says is installed, and a rollback image must be exactly the installed version, because every past release is validly signed too; the two together are what make a request from an unprivileged process safe to take - installs with `apt-get`/`dpkg` or `dnf`/`rpm`, starts the unit, waits for it to stay active (and `/readyz` where the metrics listener is on), reinstalls the previous version's package when it does not, and writes the SAME `Updates/last-apply.txt` line the Windows helper writes (`ok`, `failed`, `rolled-back`, `rollback-failed`, `no-rollback`), which `ReportLastOutcome`, the REST route and the Deck read unchanged. The checker now decides the artefact by what owns the binary (dpkg's record, `rpm -q`, `APPIMAGE` in the environment) rather than by which tool is on the machine, and an AppImage install is a swap: the new image copied beside the old and renamed into place, the old kept as `.previous` for the rollback, the image required to be owned by the applying user and writable by nobody else. A server whose request nobody takes tries `systemd-run` (right where the server is root under a unit, refused for the packaged service by design), and one under no unit is told so and left with the verified file. No token on Linux: the package's post-install runs the schema upgrade. The unit already had `Restart=on-failure`, the `After=` set and the hardening block; what it gained is the runtime directory. Proven: `build/test-hmailserver-update.sh` runs the real script under dash against fakes through thirty-one cases (every outcome, both package managers and the fallbacks, the AppImage swap and rollback, the refusals with nothing written, planted symlinks followed by nothing, the tampered file, the version rule - a signed downgrade refused, the request's version overruled by the package's, a rollback of the wrong version dropped - every write bit an image or its directory could carry, `--dry-run`) - 138 checks, green on Ubuntu 26.04 - and runs in `packaging.yml` with `systemd-analyze verify` of the three units; the `.deb`/`.rpm` file assertions in `linux-build.yml` cover the three new files and the path unit's enablement; the C++ compiles with clang and MSVC. Run for real, in a privileged Ubuntu 26.04 container with systemd 259 as PID 1 on the lab VM: the `.deb` from this tree installed, the server up under the packaged unit against PostgreSQL, a 6.3.4 package from the same tree, both signed under the regression suite's own Sigstore with its trust named in the root-owned configuration, and then over REST check, download, install - the path unit took the request, root verified both packages, apt-get upgraded 6.3.3 to 6.3.4, the unit came back ready, `ok 6.3.4 ...` was written and `GET /api/v1/update` carried it after the next start; and the rollback - PostgreSQL stopped, the upgraded server active but `/readyz` 503 for the sixty seconds, a real apt downgrade to 6.3.3, `rolled-back 6.3.4 ...`, the server on 6.3.3 and ready. `--verify-update` was also run against the real v6.3.3 release `.deb` and bundle under the public trust: verifies, and refuses a one-byte change. That run found that no real bundle had ever verified on Linux (the bundle was read through the wide String and Rekor's checkpoint em dash emptied it in the C locale), fixed by `SigstoreVerifier::ReadBundle`, a byte-exact read used everywhere a bundle is read. Not run for real: a release-signed package under the public trust end to end (the first release after this is the occasion), the `.rpm` route on a dnf host and the AppImage swap under a unit, both fakes only. Left out on purpose: an AppImage from a terminal or as a non-root user under a unit (no supervisor to restart it, or no root to reach; it is told and the file waits), and Arch, where units are not enabled by packages and `systemctl enable --now hmailserver-update.path` is the administrator's line. The row as written: The update helper is a Windows program; on Linux the apply step is a package upgrade or an AppImage swap under a systemd unit that restarts the server, with the same outcome file and the same rollback. |
| ✅ | **The Linux suite's remaining skips** | **Done 17 September 2026.** Measured again, on the batch-11 tree, from the two CI halves that now run it: 1,725 results, 1,439 passed, none failed, 286 skipped - the largest reasons the public folders no route reaches (42), a stored message's MIME as a COM object (36), Application.Database (36), the event-script engine this build lacks (24), the anti-spam address lists (22) and the COM logon (17). Four of the reasons the row named are gone: an account's own rules are `GET` and `POST /api/v1/accounts/{address}/rules` and `PUT` and `DELETE /api/v1/accounts/{address}/rules/{id}`, the same documents and handlers as the global rules on the collection `Account.Rules` holds, scoped to the address's domain and in force for the next message; `Account.ExportMessages` is `POST /api/v1/accounts/{address}/export`, with the export walk moved out of the COM unit into `Common/Util/MessageExport` so both call one; `Utilities.EraseAddressTraces` is `POST /api/v1/addresses/erase`; the administrator's second factor is `GET`, `POST` and `DELETE /api/v1/administrator/second-factor`, the enrolment answering the otpauth URI once and refusing a second enrolment while one stands; and the directory-linked account, which the route took all along, is now read and written by the shim. The export, the erasure and the enrolment are the administrator password's alone, as the key store is. The shims reach every one of them - the account's rules as the same collection type with only its path different - the privacy fixture joins the Linux project, and two Windows fixtures pin the routes: `API/RestApiAccountRules` (the rule is the account's alone, acts on its next message, is replaced and deleted, and is 404 through any other address) and `API/RestApiOperator` (the export on disk, the erasure of an alias, and the second factor enrolled, enforced on the next request with a code computed by an independent RFC 6238 implementation, refused twice, and disabled). Still skipped for reasons that stand: the event-script engine, GSSAPI, the live log, and everything that is COM by nature. The row as written: The anti-virus settings and distribution lists over REST landed 14 September (#260; the hosted run on that tree: 1,493 tests, 1,246 passed, 2 failed, 245 skipped). The message object, application passwords and folder permissions landed the same day (#262; hosted run 1,520 tests, 1,289 passed, 0 failed, 231 skipped, on the wiki's Changes page, section 4d), each with the shim taught to use it. On 15 September Linux suite wave J (af74d51e8) added the groups over `/api/v1/groups` (the shims' `Settings.Groups`, `Group` and `Group.Members`), `Message.Copy` and `Utilities.CriteriaMatch`. It also linked the three fixture files that had not compiled against the shims: `IMAP/ACL.cs`, `API/Basics.cs` and `IMAP/MessageUids.cs`. Left: `Account.ExportMessages`, which writes files on the server's own disk and has no route. An account's own rules, which no administrative route reaches, so the shim stops the test. Account and administrator two-factor enrolment, directory sync, the event-script engine, a GSSAPI bind and the live log. And the skip count measured again on a Linux server for the tree after wave J, which was built but not run. **24 September 2026:** the missing engine is no longer the reason - the Linux builds run event scripts in JavaScript (the row **Event scripts on Linux**), and the shim's `Scripting.Enabled` gate asks `build.script_languages` rather than `script_engine`. The 24 tests it stopped all still skip, now with the language as the reason, because their handlers are VBScript (one is JScript); 15 of them have a JavaScript counterpart in `API/EventsJavaScript.cs`, which runs beside them, and nine have none. The counts wait for the lab VM's run. |

## 6. Measured quality

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **Native coverage, measured** | **Measured 15 September 2026: 73.48% of the native server's lines** (67,790 of 92,260 under `hmailserver/source/Server`). OpenCppCoverage 0.9.9 ran over the whole Windows regression suite against the 6.3.3 shipping binary run as a console server (`hMailServer.exe /Debug`, `HM_CONSOLE_SERVER=1`). The figure is published in Roadmap.md's three coverage rows and on the wiki's Changes page, section 4e. Two fixes had to come first, both in the tree: the server reads its mode from the last non-empty token of its command line, and the suite's DNS set-up no longer ignores a console-server run. The figure is line coverage only (the tool has no branch mode) and below the silver bar of 80%, so Roadmap.md's `test_statement_coverage80` and `achieve_silver` stay open. The script that ran it is not in the repository yet, so the measure cannot be repeated from the tree. |
| ✅ | **The bench, doubled** | **Done 16-17 September 2026.** A second Windows bench, a Windows Server 2025 VM at 192.168.11.150 provisioned entirely over SSH in an evening - the build tools, the libraries, SQL Server Compact, ClamAV, SpamAssassin, the repository, the service registered from its own Release build and the bench database created over COM - takes half of every gate: the Windows suite is split by namespace into two halves of about 1,900 seconds of test time each (API, IMAP, AntiSpam, Sieve, POP3, Rules, Domains, MIME, Stress, Installation and Shared here; Security, Infrastructure, SMTP, AntiVirus, SSL and ExternalAccounts there), run at once by one script, and a gate that took fifty minutes takes thirty-five. The first split gate, on 16 September, found a fixture that waited for a restore by watching a one-second stamp and, on the faster bench, missed a restore that finished in 100 ms. The Linux suite in CI is split the same way from 17 September: two matrix halves of about 2,600 seconds each (API, Sieve, AntiSpam, POP3, SSL, ExternalAccounts, Domains, MIME, Stress, Rules, Installation, Shared; IMAP, Security, Infrastructure, SMTP, AntiVirus), after the whole suite ran seventy-five to ninety minutes and was cut off at the ninety-minute job limit. `hmailserver/docs/RegressionEnvironment.md` says how the second bench was made. The row as written: One Windows bench runs one gate at a time, about ninety minutes each, and the queue of 14 September was six deep. The Ubuntu VM as a second bench for the Linux suite, in parallel. |
| ✅ | **A fuzzing job that never stops** | **Written 15 September 2026; the first scheduled run came back green on 16 September at 02:31 UTC.** `.github/workflows/fuzz-nightly.yml`: every night at 02:10 UTC on a hosted Windows runner, the three MIME harnesses built with the runner's clang-cl, every kept input and committed reproducer replayed first, then each harness for forty minutes with two workers; the corpus kept in the Actions cache under a per-run key so it grows night by night; a finding fails the job and lands as an artifact. The scheduled run runs; iCalendar and vCard harnesses (the parsers this server now has) are the next targets, as rows of their own when they are started. |
| ✅ | **A heap-corruption abort under concurrent IMAP LIST and folder churn** | **Found and fixed 23 September 2026.** The abort came back a third time, in the full gate of the environment row at 18:33, in the same fixture (`Stress.IMAP.ConcurrentConnectionsStressTests.ConcurrentListAndFolderChurnListsEveryExistingFolder`), and this time the access violations that followed it left two minidumps. With no debugger on the bench, a small reader (the pure-Python `minidump` package and dbghelp through ctypes, symbolising every stack word that falls inside a loaded module against the build's own PDB) put the thread that hit STATUS_HEAP_CORRUPTION inside `IMAPCommandLIST::ExecuteCommand` -> `FolderListCreator::GetIMAPFolderList` -> `IMAPSpecialUse::Resolve` -> `CollectExplicit_`, recursing through the subfolder collections, with a folder collection's destructor on the same stack. `CollectExplicit_` iterated `folders->GetVector()`, the live unlocked vector of the account's shared, cached folder tree, while other sessions of the same account created folders (a `push_back` that reallocates) and deleted them (an erase that destroys a subfolder collection): a range-for over freed memory, copying `shared_ptr`s out of it. The LIST walk itself had been moved to the locked copy `GetSnapshot()` for exactly this reason (upstream #566); the special-use pass it calls for every line had not. It iterates `GetSnapshot()` now (`IMAP/IMAPSpecialUse.cpp`), and the stress fixture that crashed ran three times green against the fixed server before the full gate that landed it. The same pass also serves the SpamAssassin learner, the retention task, the Sent-copy placement and the webmail's folder routes, all of which read a tree other sessions change. The crash-oracle and WER record had the same code at the same ntdll offset on 15 and 22 September: one bug, three sightings. Other `GetVector()` loops over collections that another thread can change (the cached rules in `RuleApplier`, `ACLManager`'s copy, the external-fetch manager) are the same shape and are swept in the row below it. |
| ✅ | **Every loop over a shared collection iterates a locked copy** | **Done 24 September 2026, and its review applied the same day.** Found 23 September with the heap-corruption fix above: `Collection::GetVector()` hands out the live, unlocked vector, and a range-for over it while another thread refreshes, adds or removes is a read of freed memory. Every one of the 87 calls of `GetVector()` and `GetConstVector()` on master was read, and so was every member of a `Collection` subclass that touches `vecObjects` itself. **Five collections another thread changes, fixed, and one moved defensively.** `RuleApplier::HasRejectAction` and `WouldReject` iterate `GetSnapshot()`: they are handed ObjectCache's global and domain rules for every message accepted over SMTP and every send from the webmail, and `GetDomainRules` refreshes a domain's IN PLACE on whichever thread asks first after a save, the global ones by a restore. `RuleApplier::ApplyRules` - the loop over an account's cached rules, and over a domain's and the global ones at delivery - and the REST rules listing walked the same collections by index, memory-safe but able to skip a rule or run one twice across a refresh, and both now take one `GetSnapshot()`, which is what makes the account rules this row named done. `IncomingRelays::IsIncomingRelay`, `Routes::GetItemByNameWithWildcardMatch` and `RouteAddresses::DeleteByAddress` walked or erased the vector of SMTPConfiguration's shared collections without the mutex and now take it. ObjectCache's domain aliases, built before they are published, replaced on reload and never changed, moved to a snapshot in `ImpersonationCheck::HostedDomains_` defensively; `DomainAliases::ApplyAliasesOnAddress` walks the same object unlocked, deliberately, on every `RCPT TO` and logon. **Owned: 84**, each carrying `// owned by this call` and its reason - 58 a message's own recipients or attachments, 15 a local collection loaded above, 9 a collection a getter loads new for every caller, and two more. Two of the three suspects the row named are not shared - `ACLManager`'s copy is of a permission list loaded new for every question, and the fetch manager's list is its thread's alone - and neither are the TCP/IP ports (`Configuration`'s `tcpip_ports_` is declared and never assigned), nor any `Domains` collection. **The elements as well as the vector:** a locked copy protects the vector, not what is in it, and REST wrote the fields of the live shared route, DNS black list, SURBL server and incoming relay while sessions read them. `PUT /api/v1/routes/{id}` and the black-list, SURBL and relay updates now write a new object, save it and refresh the collection, with the same answers and the same audit records; COM's setters, which still write the live object, are the row below. `PersistentRoute::ReadObject` loads a route's addresses, as a rule's parts are loaded, so they are never created lazily on a route other threads hold, and `Route::GetAddresses`' lazy path, left for a route built in memory, is locked. `build/check-collection-iteration.py` fails on any call of either name outside `Collection.h` without its marker - in the comment of its own line, or the whole of the line before - on one marker over two calls, and on a call inside a `#define`; its `--self-test` proves each, and that a marker in a string, a comment, a declaration and `Collection.h` are not taken for more than they are; `style.yml` runs both. `RegressionTests.Stress.SharedCollections` (REST port 10120, REST and COM only, in the Windows suite): a domain's and an account's rules created and deleted for thirty seconds against sixty standing domain rules - fifty-nine that do not reject, then one that does, so both walks cross all sixty - while three accounts submit through the send route, every message delivered; and routes appended, refreshed and erased for thirty seconds while the send route resolves an unhosted address past forty standing routes, each refused as not in the route list and nothing queued. Both are survival smoke tests, not seen to catch the old code: against a build with `HasRejectAction` and `WouldReject` put back to `GetVector()` and `ApplyRules` to its index walk, the rules race passed three times in three (40 edit rounds each; 360, 362 and 360 messages, every one delivered), and the routes race has not been run against it. Untested, and proven by reading only: the relay lock, the route-address lock and the alias snapshot. The table of every site is in `ARCHITECTURE.md`. |
| ✅ | **A COM change to a shared configuration object is written onto a copy** | **Built 24 September 2026, and its adversarial review applied on 25 September (two must-fix, nine should-fix and nine notes: every must-fix and should-fix applied, and the notes but one). Not yet compiled or run against a bench: written in a worktree beside four other rows, it is compiled, built and gated when the branch is folded; it has no schema step, and every checker is green, the schema chain's included.** Found 24 September 2026 by the review of the row above: the Control Panel's COM objects for routes, incoming relays, DNS black lists and SURBL servers held the very objects in SMTPConfiguration's and the anti-spam configuration's shared collections, and their `put_*` methods wrote the fields of the live object while every session read them - a string assigned under a reader frees the buffer the reader is copying, the use-after-free class of the heap-corruption abort one level below the vector, and a range written in two halves hands a session half of each. **The shape, for every COM item of a shared collection:** `COMCollectionItem` (`COM/COMCollection.h`) keeps it once. A collection the running server reads attaches its items with `AttachSharedParent`; the first `put_*` on such an item copies it - from the collection's item with its id as it is now, so a refresh since the item was handed out is not undone - and the COM object holds and writes the copy from then on, its `get_*` reading the copy; `Save()` saves the copy and reads the collection again, which swaps in new items under its lock while a session keeps the one it holds. A `Save()` with nothing put before it copies first as well, so a persister is never handed the running item (the policy's key is trimmed on the object saved) and an object handed out before a refresh saves the running item's values rather than writing back the ones it was handed. A read that fails after a successful save is reported (error 7390) and the `Save()` still succeeds, since the save did; until the list is next read, a later change to the same item starts from the old values and its save puts them back, which the error says. A new item, from `Add()`, is nobody else's until its `Save()` appends it and is written directly until then. A route has no copy constructor (it holds the lock over its lazily loaded addresses), so `Route::CopyFields` makes the copy, for REST's route update as well, which had a field list of its own; `InterfaceRoute::get_Addresses` hands out the running route's list whether or not the route object holds a copy, so an address added or deleted is in effect at once, as it always was; and a route's `Addresses` held while a refresh of the routes replaced the route (its own COM save, `Routes.Refresh()`, a REST update) is the retired route's list, through which an address saved or deleted is saved or deleted and the running route's list read again (`InterfaceRouteAddresses::ReachRunningRoute`), so it reaches the running route too. **The four the row named, and six more the sweep found of the same shape:** `InterfaceRouteAddress` (a running route's addresses, asked by every session), `InterfaceRemoteDomainPolicy` (read by every delivery; its refused save had put the running record back by reading it in place), `InterfaceServerMessage` (copied by every bounce and virus notice, and attached with no parent at all), `InterfaceBlockedAttachment` (compared with every attachment's name), `InterfaceSSLCertificate` (read by the listeners, the alert check, the metrics scrape and REST) and `InterfaceGroup` (looked up by name by IMAP's ACL commands, and handed out by `InterfaceIMAPFolderPermission::get_Group` too, with no parent before); the seven collection `Refresh()`s this needed now answer whether the read succeeded. The REST server message, blocked attachment and group updates had the same defect on the REST side - the first wrote the running item before saving it, the other two wrote the saved values back onto it - and now save a copy and read their collection again too. `build/check-com-shared-copies.py` (with `--self-test`, run by `style.yml`) holds the ten classes to the shape: no `put_*` writes the item, `Save()` copies before it saves and publishes after, and every hand-out attaches the shared collection; against the COM sources before the row it reports all 59 puts and all 38 hand-outs. **Safe, and why:** TCP/IP ports, security ranges, the white list, the greylisting white list and bypass entries, blocked senders and sender list entries are loaded new for the COM object, and sessions read caches that copy under a lock of their own; domains, accounts, aliases, lists and their members, domain aliases, rules, fetch accounts, app passwords, group members and folder permissions are loaded by COM for itself. **Left:** `InterfaceIMAPFolder` and `InterfaceMessage` - the cached folder tree and messages are written in place by the IMAP server itself (RENAME, STORE) and by REST's mailbox rename, so a copy on the COM side would not make them safe; the folder's own fields want a lock of their own, a row of its own. **What a COM caller sees now:** a `put_*` is not in effect until `Save()` - the running server, and any COM object read from the collection before the save, keep the values they had - and a `put_*` never followed by a `Save()` changes nothing at all; after the save an object read from the collection again has the saved values and one read before it keeps what it read; and a `Save()` the database refuses leaves the running item as it was, the object keeping the refused values to be corrected and saved again - except a remote domain policy, which is read back as the database holds it, as before. A `get_*` on the object a `put_*` was made on reads what was put. Two places in the suite relied on the old behaviour and now save: the basic setup's switching off of `multi.surbl.org` (a `put_*` with no `Save()`, which had switched it off in memory while the database kept it on) and `SMTPClientTests.TestAuthFailurePasswordInBounce`'s relay credentials. Fixtures `API/ComWritesOntoCopies.cs` (fourteen tests: one per class - the put read back from its own object, the running item unchanged before `Save()`, the saved values after it, and an object read before keeping what it read, each failing against the code before the row by the trace in the fixture's summary, the group's reads through one `Groups` object because `Settings.Groups` reads the groups again every time - and a refused route save, an address list held across the route's save, a `Save()` with nothing put, and a save whose list cannot be read again, which the database fault injector provokes and which reports HM7390), the REST blocked attachment and server message tests each holding a COM object across the PUT (the group update is proven by reading only: the REST groups route reads the groups again before every request), and `Stress/SharedCollectionsOverCom.cs` (REST port 10170, Windows suite: three races of thirty seconds flipping a route, a DNS black list, a SURBL server, a relay, a policy, a group, a blocked attachment and a server message between two whole states over COM, pausing after each put, while the send route resolves past the routes and the REST listings read every field - the listings are the only readers in the two races without routes; every listing must show each item whole, which the pauses make the old code fail for most of every write of the six multi-field items, and the process, the crash oracle and the error log must come through clean - the group and the message, with one field each, are survival tests only; not run against the old code). `docs/Upgrading.md` tells an upgrader what a COM script now sees. The COM API page of the wiki, *The COM API and Scripting*, is outside the tree and is to gain the caller's paragraph when the branch lands. Left from the review: an account-level COM session can rename a server-wide group it reaches through a folder permission (`InterfaceGroup::Save` asks no administrator), which is older than this row and an authorisation change of its own. [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md), "A COM change to an item of a shared collection is written onto a copy". |

## 7. What the deep dive of 14 September found missing

Each of these was checked against Roadmap.md before it was written here: none has a row that ships it, and several have a row that says, in so many words, that it does not exist. Grouped by who feels the gap first.

### The administrator

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **Alerts** | **Done 15 September 2026.** All six notice-and-tell-nobody conditions now raise an event: a backup that failed, a certificate within the rule's threshold of expiry, the disk at its warning band or below its floor, a work queue whose every thread is stuck, auto-bans past a rate in the last hour, and a minidump newer than the last one recorded. A condition is a row in `hm_alertrules` - name, threshold, on or off, mail and/or webhook, cool-down, digest - so a seventh is a row and never a schema change; the six ship with `backup.failed` and `certificate.expiring` on and the rest off, and nothing is sent until `AlertRecipient` and `AlertSenderAddress` are set. Whatever notices writes a row and returns, and `AlertTask` is the only thing that sends, so a notification about the delivery queue is never submitted into the delivery queue by the thread stuck on it. Four things stop it flapping: a state condition that is already raised is not raised again, an occurrence is bounded by its cool-down, there is a ceiling per hour across the lot, and the daily digest is the quiet default. The webhook body is signed HMAC-SHA256 over `timestamp.body` with the timestamp and signature in headers, retried on the next pass and then at 1, 5, 15 and 60 minutes, and dead-lettered after the last attempt with the event kept. Nine settings in `hm_settings`; five REST routes; `docs/AlertsAndAuditTrail.md` with the verification recipe in Python and JavaScript. **Not done:** a Control Panel page for the rules, and `hmctl` verbs. |
| ✅ | **Outbound webhooks** | **Built 16 September 2026; gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** Five things that happen to mail now reach a subscribed endpoint as a signed POST: `message.received` (the SMTP acceptance, beside the per-domain counters, once per local recipient), `message.delivered` (the journal both deliverers already call), `message.bounced` (beside `OnMessageBounced`, which is what a bounce actually is - a report written and queued for the sender, and so not a retry and not a `RCPT TO` refusal), `message.quarantined` (after the row exists) and `account.locked` (in the branch that sets the lockout, outside the mutex every authentication takes). Each is raised by the path that owns it, never by anything that polls, and raising writes one queue row per matching subscription and returns - so a webhook about a delivery is never POSTed by the thread doing the delivering. `WebhookTask`, once a minute, is the only thing that sends. **What is built:** `hm_webhooks` and `hm_webhookqueue` on schema 6050, with two `hm_domains` columns (`domainwebhooksenabled`, on; `domainwebhooksubjects`, off); a subscription of an endpoint, a shared secret, the events, domains and addresses it is confined to (empty means every one), whether it is on, whether its bodies may carry a subject, and a rate limit; the body kept in the queue as it will be sent, so it survives a restart and a receiver that was down for ten minutes gets the ten minutes it missed; `AlertManager`'s signing and backoff ladder CALLED rather than copied (HMAC-SHA256 over `timestamp.body`, then none, 1, 5, 15, 60 minutes, then a dead letter that keeps the body and the reason and reports HM6971 once); a ceiling per subscription per minute that DROPS and counts rather than queues, because a queue that grows without bound is how a busy hour becomes a flood an hour later; eight REST routes refused to every API key with a 401, for the reason the alert rules are, and creating, changing or testing a subscription needing `manage_server` because an endpoint is an address this server will connect to (the documents say where an endpoint may be - anywhere this server can reach, private ranges included, on purpose - and what follows from that); the shared secret protected at rest with `Crypt::ProtectSecret` as a route's password is; one delivery pass at a time in the process, bounded to 200 rows and 60 seconds, and an endpoint that gives no answer tried once a pass rather than once a row; `account.locked` raised only for a name this server hosts, so an outsider's invented names cannot spend a subscription's ceiling; four settings in `hm_settings`, their ranges refused over REST with the COM sentence rather than clamped; COM (four `Settings` properties, two `Domain` properties, and the subscriptions as JSON on `Utilities` rather than as a new interface); a Control Deck page in all twenty languages with eighteen new harness checks; `hmctl webhook` and `hmctl webhooks`, seven PowerShell functions and sixteen `check-cli.py` cases; `docs/Webhooks.md`; and fourteen tests in `RestApiWebhooks.cs`, linked into the Linux project too, which recompute the signature the way a receiver would, require a DIFFERENT secret to produce a different one, and assert on what the endpoint received and the queue's states rather than on the count one pass answers. **What a body does NOT carry, and the documents say so first:** the message - no body, no attachments, no headers - and no subject unless the domain's `webhook_subjects` and the subscription's `include_subject` BOTH say so, and then only on `message.received` and `message.quarantined`, whose owning paths already hold it. **What is not built:** no inbound webhook; no per-subscription retry policy; no overlap window while a secret is rotated; a subscription is not in the configuration backup, as an alert rule is not; the ceiling's count forgets a restart, being a rate rather than configuration; and one-pass-at-a-time is per process, so two nodes on one database would need a claim step that is not built. The server and both test projects compile, every checker passes, and the fixture ran green in that gate. |
| ✅ | **Declarative configuration** | **Done: the export, the diff and the apply landed 15 September 2026, in `hmctl config`, and what the document does not carry is settled on 17 September as the boundary rather than the backlog.** (`hmailserver/docs/CommandLine.md`): one JSON document holding the settings groups, the ini keys the server exposes, the domains and their accounts, aliases and distribution lists, the groups, rules, routes, listeners and IP ranges, over the REST API rather than COM - so it works against a Linux server and from a machine that is not the server, which `build/hmconfig.ps1` cannot. The document is sorted throughout and carries nothing the server allocated - no identifier, no count, no time - so two exports of an unchanged server are the same bytes; that is the property a repository needs, and it is checked rather than claimed. `diff` exits 1 when anything would change, so a pipeline gates on it like a failing test; `apply` prints the plan and changes nothing unless `--force`, and a deletion needs `--allow-delete` on top of that, because a document written from one server and applied to another must not quietly remove what the second one has. Twenty checks in `build/check-cli.py` hold all of it against a recorded server, including that an apply without `--force` makes no request that is not a GET. **Outside the document, on purpose:** certificates and the directories (files and paths of one machine, which would describe a server that does not exist if copied), and what lives under an account (application passwords, fetch accounts, folder permissions - the account holder's rather than the configuration's). The GitOps loop is two commands in a workflow - `diff` as the check, `apply --force` as the deployment - and is documented as such in `hmailserver/docs/CommandLine.md` rather than built as a daemon. |
| ✅ | **An audit trail** | **Built 15 September 2026; the holes the reconciliation found are closed in the code, and it went through the batch-10 gate green on 16 September 2026.** A change is one row in `hm_audit`: who (the administrator, the account or `key:<id>`), what (the object and the action; for a setting, the value it had and the value it was given), when, from where (the peer address, empty for COM) and over which interface: COM, REST or the Deck. Recording happens at three places below the interfaces. `DatabaseConnectionManager::Execute` covers the 30 tables `AuditTrail.cpp` lists; `PropertySet::Set*` covers `hm_settings`; and `IniSettingStore`'s write and remove cover the `[Settings]` keys in `hm_inisettings` (COM `SetIniSetting` and `DeleteIniSetting`, `/api/v1/settings/ini`, and the `/api/v1/settings` fields kept there), with `(default)` for a side that has no stored value. Who made the change comes from a thread-local actor installed by the REST dispatcher, by COM's two authorisation questions, and by an `AuditScope` in the COM methods that ask neither. A column or setting named like a password, secret, token, hash, salt, API key or private key is recorded as `(changed)`, except fifteen `[Settings]` keys that only look like one (the seven `PasswordPolicy*` keys among them), named exactly. Each row carries the SHA-256 of the row before it and its own. `GET /api/v1/audit/verify` names the first edited row, or the row that points at one removed, and does not mistake retention for tampering. `GET /api/v1/audit` pages and filters; it is administrator-only and refused to any key with a 401. The Deck's **Audit trail** page is in all twenty languages, and recording is on by default. **Closed on 15 September (branch audit-holes):** five of the listed table names were not tables (`hm_domainaliases`, `hm_incomingrelays`, `hm_whiteaddresses`, `hm_greyliststats` and `hm_blockedsenders`), so domain aliases, incoming relays, both white lists and blocked senders were never recorded; the list now names `hm_domain_aliases`, `hm_incoming_relays`, `hm_whitelist`, `hm_greylisting_whiteaddresses` and `hm_blocked_senders`, and adds folder permissions (`hm_acl`), remote domain policies (`hm_remotedomainpolicies`) and the archive index (`hm_archiveindex`), so placing or releasing a legal hold is recorded while the archive's own writes, which run with no actor, are not. No `[Settings]` key was recorded; every one now is. The twelve syslog setters on COM's Logging object, `RemoteDomainPolicies.DeleteByDBID` and `DeleteIniSetting` installed no actor; they now do. `build/check-audit-choke-point.py` passes, runs in `style.yml`, and also fails on a listed name no create script defines and on a created table that is neither listed nor named in the checker with the reason it is not. **Left:** a gate. `RestApiAuditTrail.cs` (eighteen tests, nine of them for these holes) passed in the batch-10 gate, after the batch-seven gate was void on the Compact `max(id)` read fixed in 9255b569d and the batch-eight gate stopped at the test build. Not recorded, and each named under *What this does not do* in `docs/AlertsAndAuditTrail.md`: an account sharing its own folder over IMAP `SETACL`, because no administrator made that change; a restore, neither the fact of it nor anything it replaces, because it runs on the maintenance work queue where no actor is installed (whether it should be one row or a row per value is undecided, and the checker refuses a record call in `IniSettingStore::XMLLoad` until it is); creating or revoking a REST API key, which lives in its own store file; an administrator releasing or deleting a quarantined message, which ends in the same row delete either way; creating, renaming or deleting a public folder, whose table also holds every account's own folders (its permissions are recorded); and the parts of `hMailServer.ini` that are not settings: the administrator password and its TOTP secret in `[Security]`, the folders in `[Directories]` and the database connection in `[Database]`, which the setup wizard writes before there is a database to record into. |
| ✅ | **The reports our own domains receive, read** | **Built 17 September 2026 on branch row92, schema 6070.** A domain names a report mailbox (`Domain.ReportMailbox` over COM, `report_mailbox` on `PUT /api/v1/domains/{domain}`, a Reports received tab in the Control Panel and a field in the Deck's domain editor; one address in a domain this server hosts, or empty, which is the default and reads nothing), publishes it as `rua=mailto:` in its `_dmarc` and `_smtp._tls` records, and a message delivered to that address and to nothing else is opened in the deliverer beside the complaint intake: the attachment - gzip, zip (stored or deflate) or plain, told from the bytes rather than the name - is unwrapped, a DMARC aggregate report (RFC 7489 Appendix C, and the RFC 9990 namespaced form) becomes one row in `hm_dmarcreports` and one in `hm_dmarcreportrecords` per record, a TLS-RPT report (RFC 8460) one row in `hm_tlsreports` per policy, each keyed on the reporter and the report id so a retry counts once, and the message is delivered unchanged either way. The XML reader is the server's own, with no entities beyond the five predefined, a DOCTYPE that declares any refused, 32 levels, 400,000 nodes, 16 MB inflated, ten parts a message, 2,000 records a report and a message, 20,000 records and 1,000 TLS rows a domain a day (past which a report is refused whole with a log line, since the address is published and anybody may post to it), a policy domain that is not a host name refused before any lookup, and every reader is handed hostile input in the class tester. A report is recorded only against a hosted domain that itself names a mailbox, so one mailbox serves an organisation's domains and a stranger cannot start a chart for a domain whose administrator asked for none. `GET /api/v1/domains/{domain}/dmarc-reports/summary?days=` is the chart's data - messages, passed (aligned DKIM or aligned SPF), failed, quarantined, rejected, the reporters, a bar per day and the sources most messages first - with `/dmarc-reports`, `/dmarc-reports/{id}` and `/tls-reports` beside it, scoped to the domain for a confined key, and `POST /api/v1/domain-reports/expire` running the retention sweep the six-hourly task runs on `DomainReportRetentionDays` (365). The Deck's DMARC page, reached from the domain row beside Complaints, draws the chart passed against failed, the totals, who sends as the domain, who reports, the reports a page at a time with a way into one report's records, and the TLS reports, and says when no mailbox is named rather than drawing an empty chart; `hmctl dmarc` and four PowerShell cmdlets print the same documents. What carried a report is kept with it - the `dmarc=` verdict and `header.from=` of the Authentication-Results field this server wrote on the message, when `AuthenticationResultsEnabled` is on - so the reporters' table says how many of each one's reports arrived in a message that passed DMARC, since `org_name` is text a stranger can write. The mailbox follows a rename of the domain that hosts it and is cleared, with a log line, when that domain is deleted; it is judged only when written, so a save of another field is never refused for it; and a credential confined to a list of domains may name a mailbox only in a domain on its list. The sweep runs in passes of 5,000 until none remain and says when a hundred passes did not finish. **Thinner than dmarcian:** no DNS lookups of the sources (no PTR, no "this is Google"), no forensic (`ruf=`) reports, no alert when the pass rate falls, and a report with more than 2,000 records is cut short with a log line saying so - the row asks for the chart, and that is what was built. `hmailserver/docs/DmarcReports.md`; `RestApiDomainReports.cs` on port 9626. |
| ✅ | **Own-reputation watch** | **Built 16 September 2026 on branch `reputation-watch`, schema 6049; gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** Once a day the server takes the addresses it sends from - `SMTPDeliveryBindToIP`, the bind-to-address action of any active global rule, and `ReputationWatchAddresses`, which is where the public address of a server behind NAT is written because nothing on the machine can discover it - and asks each list in `ReputationWatchLists` about them (`ipv4:zen.spamhaus.org`, `ipv4:bl.spamcop.net`, `ipv4:psbl.surriel.com`, `ipv4:bl.mailspike.net` by default - every shipped zone is marked IPv4-only, including ZEN, whose free public zone answers no IPv6 query: an unmarked list answers NXDOMAIN for an IPv6 address, which reads exactly like not listed, so an IPv6 address is reported as not covered until a list whose IPv6 coverage has been read is named). Before any address, each list is asked about the two entries RFC 5782 requires of it, so a list that refuses this resolver (Spamhaus answers `127.255.255.254` through a public one), that is not answering, or that answers for every address is told apart from one that says nothing is listed; a lookup that fails is never reported as clean and never clears a listing an earlier check found. A private, loopback, link-local, shared or mapped address is not looked up at all, and the report says so. `reputation.listed` is seeded on and names the address and the list; it clears at the first check that finds nothing listed, and a listing no check has confirmed for a week stops counting as one rather than holding the alert raised for ever behind a retired zone. The clear says why it cleared: the sentence claiming that no address is on any list is written only when every configured pair gave a definite answer, and a list that refused, one that never answered, a pair nothing has checked and a listing that merely aged out each have their own words. Switching the watch off clears a standing condition rather than leaving it raised for ever, and says that is what happened. A check is bounded at 120 seconds, because the scheduler runs its due tasks on one thread; the pairs it does not reach are failed lookups rather than clean, a second check over REST is refused with 409 rather than queued, and over COM it fails at once for the same reason, and the daily check decides whether it is due under the lock it runs under. The watch is seeded **on** in a new installation and **off** by the upgrade step, since an existing server may already have a delivery bind address and an upgrade must not start sending it to third parties daily.
