The report is `GET /api/v1/reputation`, the Deck's **Outbound reputation** page in all twenty languages, `hmctl reputation` (exit 1 when listed or unanswered, and when the watch is off it says so and exits 0 rather than alarming for ever over pairs nothing is checking), `Get-HmReputation`, and COM's `Utilities.GetReputationReport`; a check on demand is `POST /api/v1/reputation/check`, `Check now`, `hmctl reputation check` and `Utilities.RunReputationCheck`. Thirteen regression tests in `RestApiReputationWatch` (linked into the Linux suite, and everything they do they do over REST - emptying the stored rows is a check against no lists, because `Application.Database` is a shim there whose `ExecuteSQL` ignores the test that calls it) and three in `ReputationWatchCom`; see `hmailserver/docs/ReputationWatch.md`. **Not done:** domain-based lists (URIBL, SURBL) and the domain's own reputation, and anything that asks a list to remove an address. |
| ✅ | **Outbound content policy (DLP)** | **Built 17 September 2026 on branch row95, with no schema change: four conditions and one action in the mail flow rules, not a second engine.** `sensitive_content` is the detectors that found something in the readable text - the plain part, the HTML part with its tags removed, and every attachment whose bytes are text whatever its declared type: `card_number` (13 to 19 digits with spaces or hyphens between groups, Luhn-valid, not one digit repeated), `iban` (a country in the IBAN registry, its exact length, mod 97) and `account_number` (the new `dlp_account_number_pattern` setting's regular expression, refused over REST unless it compiles, `HM6821` once if one arrives another way). `attachment_type` is every attachment's type by its first bytes rather than its name (executable, elf, pdf, zip, office_xml, jar, office, rar, 7z, gzip, png, jpeg, gif, rtf, script, text, empty, binary), `external_recipient` every recipient this server does not host (not a local name at RCPT, not a local account, not a hosted domain - so a retry is judged the same way), and `content` the readable text itself for a pattern of the rule's own. Each is a `;` list as the attachment name is, so `contains` reads as "any of them is"; a sealed message declines the two text conditions as the body condition does. "Marked internal" is the existing header condition on `Sensitivity: Company-Confidential` with `external_recipient contains @`, not a new one. Block is the existing `reject`, whose 550 now names the rule when the action has no text; quarantine for review is the existing `quarantine`; **warn the sender** is the new `warn_sender` action: the message goes where the other actions send it and a sender this server hosts is mailed `Policy notice: <rule>` from their domain's postmaster, auto-generated, with the action's text - never to an outside sender, which would be backscatter. Surfaces: the REST words and the OpenAPI text, the Deck's rule editor (which also stopped turning the bracketed prose after `reject` and `add_disclaimer` into inputs, with the harness recording brought up to the live document), the Control Panel's criteria and action dialogs and rules list in the seventeen languages, COM's `eFTSensitiveContent`..`eFTContent` and `eRAWarnSender` with the Linux shims. `Rules/OutboundContentPolicy.cs` (ports 9636-9640, seven tests: the 550 in the rule's words with the same text delivered between colleagues and a Luhn-failing number leaving; an IBAN in a text attachment quarantined; a program called report.txt deleted by its bytes; the notice with the message still delivered; no notice to an outside sender; Sensitivity plus an outside address refused with the rule named; the pattern setting refused, matching, and `content` reading a .csv sent as octet-stream), the in-process `ContentPolicyTester` (published Luhn and IBAN vectors, a phone number and a run of zeros that must not count, the file signatures), and a REST round trip. `hmailserver/docs/OutboundContentPolicy.md`. **Review of the same day, applied:** a message a rule quarantined and a person released is not held again by the rule (the release mark the sender lists honour is honoured by the deliverer's quarantine hold too) and its sender is not warned twice; `warn_sender` goes once per message, on the first delivery attempt, not once per retry of a greylisted recipient; a reject speaks to the webmail - `POST /api/v1/me/messages` runs the same submission rules as DATA and answers 403 `policy_rejected` with the rule's text - and a reject met after acceptance tells a hosted sender the message was not sent; the account-number search runs through RuleGuard's breaker (`HM6821` does not compile, `HM6822` abandoned or over budget, rested five minutes) instead of failing open in silence; a card followed by its security code is found by its prefix at a separator; a UTF-16 attachment is read through its byte-order mark; a control character in a rule's name or a reject's text is refused over REST and written as a space where the value goes into a 550 or a header; the notice's failures have a code per cause (`HM6820`, `HM6823`, `HM6824`); the doc no longer suggests an account's rules can be an outbound policy. Four tests added to the fixture (a release, a 451 first, the webmail refusal, the reject after acceptance) and the round trip refuses the line breaks. **Not done, and said there:** the text of a PDF, a Word file or a spreadsheet is not read (they are held or refused by type, not by content); no fingerprints, classifiers or term dictionaries; no policy tip in the mail client before Send; no per-attachment size (message_size is the size condition). The row as written: Rules on what leaves: card and account numbers by pattern, attachment types, size, an external recipient on a message marked internal - block, quarantine for review or warn the sender. Exchange has it; no open server does it well. |
| ✅ | **Compromised-account detection** | **Built 17 September 2026 on branch `row94`, schema 6071.** Per-account sending limits exist. What is missing is the shape of an account takeover: a sudden change in volume, recipients, hour or client address, which locks the account, keeps the mail in the queue for review and alerts. Done as `CompromiseDetector` ([CompromisedAccounts.md](hmailserver/docs/CompromisedAccounts.md)): every authenticated submission - SMTP `AUTH` + `DATA`, the webmail's send - is judged against the account's own baseline of the last `compromise_baseline_days` (`hm_accountactivity`, one row per submission; `hm_accountrecipients`, whom it has written to) on four signals - volume past `compromise_volume_factor` times its busiest hour (2 points), a recipient set that is mostly strangers (2), an hour of the day never seen (1), a client network never seen (1) - and `compromise_score_to_lock` (3) is the takeover; an account below `compromise_minimum_history` is judged by the two floors alone. `compromise_detection_mode` is `watch` by default (baseline built, `account.compromised` raised saying what enforcing would have done, nothing held or locked) and `enforce` does what the row says: the message is saved as `hm_messages.messagetype` 4, which the deliverer never selects, with a row in `hm_heldmessages`; the account's other messages queued within the hour are held with it; `AccountLockout` holds the name at every protocol's logon until unlocked, with no clock and no clearing by a password; browser sessions are ended; the alert and the `account.locked` webhook are raised. Per account (`hm_accountreview`): exempt, sensitivity (relaxed, strict), the lock and the last shape seen. `GET /api/v1/review` lists what is held; release or discard; `GET/PUT /api/v1/accounts/{address}/review` and `.../review/unlock`; the general unlock (REST and COM) clears this lock too; the Deck's **Held for review** page; the retention sweep discards held mail unreviewed for `compromise_held_days` and prunes the baseline. The review of the same day closed what the first cut left open: the lock is enforced for a bearer token, a Kerberos ticket and a client certificate as well as a password (`AccountLogon::RefusedByHold`, on all three protocols); a scheduled draft is judged when its minute comes and held on a standing lock; the alert is raised once per account per hour rather than once per message; an unlock is a fresh start, so the next message after a false positive is not the same shape again; a released webmail message gets its `\Sent` copy at the release; an API key is refused the compromise lock by the general unlock and refused every `compromise_*` setting. Left out, and said so in the doc: nothing reads the message's text (the DLP row); an IMAP or SMTP session already up is not dropped, what it goes on submitting is held on the standing lock; the exemption and sensitivity are not in the backup; no Control Panel page (the surfaces are REST, the Deck and hmctl). |
| ✅ | **Quarantine digests for users** | **Built 15 September 2026 on branch spam-lists, schema 6046; gated green with batch 10 on 16 September 2026.** One message a day to each account of a domain that has something newly held for it - off by default and per domain, at an hour the domain sets, spread across that hour by a task that wakes every five minutes and sends the accounts still waiting divided by the runs left, never an empty digest and never twice in twenty hours. Plain text with no remote anything, a link under each entry, one for everything the digest listed and one that stops it (also its `List-Unsubscribe`, with RFC 8058's one-click POST). Each link is 32 random bytes recorded against one account and one entry, good once, dead after `QuarantineDigestLinkHours` (72); the page it opens says what it will do and waits for a button, because mail scanners follow links; a released message is not held again by the list that held it. The address the links point at is `QuarantineDigestBaseUrl` - both settings in `hm_settings`, edited behind *Digest settings* on the Control Panel's Quarantine page - and the domain's switch and hour are on its dialog, in the Deck and over REST; the reader's own opt-out is in the webmail and at `/api/v1/me/quarantine-digest`. `AntiSpam.SendQuarantineDigests` sends now. Four regression tests (`QuarantineDigests`); see `hmailserver/docs/SenderListsAndDigests.md`. **Not done:** ~~a per-account hour~~ (supplied on 23 September 2026 by *One value, three levels* below: `quarantine_digest` and `quarantine_digest_hour` may be an account's own, and the pass sends each account at its own hour), an HTML digest. |
| 🔄 | **PST import** | **Built 24 September 2026 on branch `row100-pst`, bounded 25 September 2026 after its adversarial review; no schema step, one new setting (`PstImportDirectory`, empty). In motion, not done: the brief's child process - the reader run outside the service, killed on a stop - is not built, and until it is the reader runs in the service under the bounds below. Written without a compiler or a bench: every checker is green, and the fixture and the build run when the branch is gated.** Every migration from Exchange and Outlook has a PST somewhere, and the server now reads it itself: a migration of **kind `pst`** (Roadmap2's migration row's model, its routes, report, throttle and marks, and the Deck's Migrations view), a job per file, each into one account. **The reader is this project's own** (`Common/Pst`), written from Microsoft's published specifications and nothing else - [MS-PST]'s NDB layer (the header and its CRCs, the node and block B-trees, blocks decoded as `bCryptMethod` says - none, permute or cyclic - with their trailers and CRCs checked, XBLOCK and XXBLOCK data trees, SLBLOCK and SIBLOCK subnode trees), its LTP layer (heap-on-node over any number of blocks, BTH, property contexts, table contexts whose rows span blocks, the cell existence bitmap) and its messaging layer (the store and its password, the name-to-id map for the categories, the folders under the IPM subtree, messages with their recipients, attachments and embedded messages ten deep), [MS-OXRTFCP] (LZFu with its CRC, MELA with its RAWSIZE clamped) and [MS-OXRTFEX] (HTML and text carried in RTF). No third-party PST code, and nothing downloaded. **It is untrusted input, bounded twice over:** every size, offset and count is checked against the block it is in and the limit it is read with before anything is allocated from it, and every tree walk has a depth cap and refuses a page, block or node it has already been to in that walk (a data tree that names a block twice, or lists a block of nothing, is refused); and every page, block, heap allocation, table cell, subnode, attachment and embedded message read is charged to a **work budget** - 4,000,000 reads and 512 MB a message (or a folder of the list, or a folder's list of messages), 10,000,000 reads and one per 4 bytes of the file, and 1 GB and 64 bytes per byte of it, for the whole file in one run - with the job's stop asked at every read, so a pause, a cancel or a service stop waits for one read. A message keeps at most 128 MB, 10,000 attachments and embedded messages however deep, 5,000 recipients, 64 KB a table cell and 100 warnings; the list of folders at most 100,000 folders, 16 MB of paths and 1,000 sentences about what it could not read. A damaged block costs its message, a message past its limits is `too_large`, and a file past its budget fails the job with what it copied kept. **Each message is rebuilt as RFC 5322**, deterministically (boundaries from the store and the NID, none that a line of an embedded message begins with, no time of import): the transport headers where the file kept them, less MIME-Version and `Content-*`, else From, Sender, To, Cc, Bcc, Subject (the prefix marker removed), Date, Message-ID, In-Reply-To and References built from the properties (an identifier longer than a header line left out, with a warning), an Exchange address by the SMTP one beside it, RFC 2047 for what is not ASCII and no line break from a property; text/plain, text/html in its own charset or from `\fromhtml1` RTF, attachments with RFC 2231 names, message/rfc822 for embedded messages, clear-signed S/MIME byte for byte and opaque S/MIME as `application/pkcs7-mime` with no `smime-type`, and alternative, related and mixed only where needed. It is filed through `MailImporter::ImportMessage` as archived mail - the 2 GB ceiling and not the account's message size limit - the seam the migration row made, so `PlaceWorkingFile` and the container-magic rule hold (wrapped; HM7353 when it cannot be), with read as `\Seen`, unsent `\Draft`, flag status 2 `\Flagged`, replied `\Answered`, forwarded `$Forwarded`, the categories as keywords and the delivery time (else sent, else made) as the internal date, in the server's time as a delivered message's is. The special folders are found by the store's and the root folder's EntryIDs (or the Inbox's), never by name, and filed into the account's own folder of that use or one made with it; an account folder holding its use only by its name is given it for good first, and an ordinary folder whose path is a special folder's arrives beside it as `<name> (PST)`. **Where it runs: the server, not the Import Tool** - the job, its marks, report and REST and Deck surface are the migration row's, the tool reaches the server only over COM (none added: the prompt allowed no COM), and Linux has no tool. A job names a file, never a path, resolved only in `<pst_import_directory>/<the target's domain>/` with every link and junction followed, kept by the one name it has there however it was spelled, and checked again on the handle it opened (a handle whose final path cannot be told is refused; on Linux the open follows no last link and waits on no FIFO); a domain administrator reaches only files placed for their own domain, needs `manage_mailbox` there beside `manage_accounts`, and reads no path of the server's in a refusal. Refused with a sentence each: the 4K-page layout, an OST whose store names its folders by an Exchange server's EntryIDs, Windows Information Protection, any other version or encoding, a damaged or cut-short header, a password missing or wrong (CRC-checked as [MS-PST] describes), and a store a finished job already gave the account; a file Outlook holds open pauses the job. **A guard** stands in for the child process meanwhile: before the store is opened and its folders listed, before a folder's list of messages and before each message, the job writes where it is, so a reader that ended the service is let in once more there and the second time the thing is left out (`reader_stopped`) and named - no file can stop the service at every start through the reader, though a crash in it is still the service's. Marks after every message; a resumed job files nothing twice. The report adds `pst` (the file, the store, its record key, version and encoding, what was not read, what was counted and not imported by class - the folders' count afresh at each pass, the messages' once - the refusal) and per-folder `warnings`, every string from the file cut short, and the errors take their share of the report and no more. Error codes 7370-7371. Tests: `API/PstImport`, fourteen, against fixtures `build/generate-pst-fixtures.py` writes from the specification with the standard library alone (and checks with `--check`), which pypff also reads whole - the tree and its special folders under names IMAP's name table does not hold beside ordinary Sent Items and Junk E-mail, a chain 27 deep, every message once with flags, keywords, dates, headers, bodies and attachment hashes, and each thing a message comes in without; the three encodings giving the same bytes; each refusal by its sentence; a password; a damaged block and a looping B-tree; names outside the domain's directory, a junction out, one file under three spellings and the CSV heading; a file held open; a restart in the middle of a folder of ninety; the guard, with not_mail counted once across a pause; an account's own Sent Items and the file's beside it as Sent Items (PST); Drafts and Junk found on the Inbox; a full quota paused and resumed; the same file refused a second time; and the owner's sample (shape only, ignored where it is absent). `PstTester` in the ClassTester for the CRC and tables, [MS-OXRTFCP]'s own examples, RTF de-encapsulation, the header's refusals, and trees, heaps, tables and whole stores built byte by byte in memory with the damage the review found: an embedded message that holds itself three times over, an attachment listed five times, a recipient table of 5,100 entries naming one row with a work budget that runs out in its cells, a recipient's name of 70,000 bytes, a chain of 63 folders of one 3,000-character name with 300 more below, 1,200 folders named and missing, 70 levels, a data tree that names a block twice or lists a block of nothing, a stop seen inside a message and inside the store's opening, a compressed RTF body that claims 4 GB, a boundary a line of an embedded message begins with, and the budget's own limits; the rebuild's pieces and the naming rules. See [Migration.md](hmailserver/docs/Migration.md), *Outlook data files (PST)*. **Extended 25 September 2026 on branch `row100-pst-ext`, no schema step, errors 7410-7411:** **ANSI files** (Outlook 97-2002, [MS-PST]'s 32-bit NDB layer - its 512-byte header, 12-byte page and block trailers with the BID before the CRC, narrower B-tree, XBLOCK and SLBLOCK entries - in `PstFileAnsi.cpp`, each Unicode walk handing over to its twin, and a table's rows packed into the ANSI block's 8,180 bytes) are read; **an OST** of version 23 or ANSI is read where its store names its folders as a PST does, and refused with its own sentence where they are an Exchange server's EntryIDs, which [MS-PST] does not map; **contacts** go into the account's address books as vCard 4.0 cards (names, three e-mail addresses with an Exchange one by its SMTP address, telephones with vCard 4 types, home, work and other addresses, company, title, birthday, notes, categories and the photo; the root folder's contacts folder into the default book, every other into a book of its name); **calendar items** into its calendars as VEVENTs with their VTIMEZONE, the recurrence blob of [MS-OXOCAL] 2.2.1.44 as an RRULE (daily, weekly, monthly by day or nth weekday, yearly; count or UNTIL; Outlook's "day 30" as `BYMONTHDAY=28,29,30;BYSETPOS=-1`), deleted occurrences as EXDATE and changed ones as RECURRENCE-ID overrides, organiser, attendees and their answers, reminder, busy status and a UID from the global object id; **tasks** as VTODOs in the calendar the Tasks page lists; **notes and journal entries** as messages in folders of their own, a journal entry's type, times and people above its text - the contacts and calendars phases of the job, with the mail phase's marks and guard, and every object through the checks CardDAV and CalDAV make. Fixtures `ansi.pst` (which libpff reads whole), `offline.ost`, `offline-exchange.ost` and `items.pst`; three more `API/PstImport` tests (the ANSI file, the two OSTs, and the items read back over the contacts REST route, a CardDAV GET, the calendar route expanded across a clock change and the tasks route) on 10190; `PstItemsTester` for the ANSI NDB built byte by byte with its damage, the recurrence blob cut at every length and each count and field it must refuse, the time zones north and south of a clock change, the global object id, and the iCalendar and vCard through this server's own ICalendar, VCard and ContactCard. Rebased onto the row's bounds, the extension reads under the same work budget: the ANSI reader's pages, blocks and subnode entries are charged as the Unicode reader's are; each contact, calendar item and task is a unit of it, with the name-to-id map, each attachment looked at for a contact's picture (each once), the recurrence blob and each changed occurrence, and each time-zone rule; each phase opens the file with a whole-file budget of its own, asks the job's stop at every read and puts its marks back when it stops; an item keeps at most 5,000 attendees of 1,024 bytes a name and 100 warnings, and a series whose changed occurrences would repeat its description past 1 MB is `too_large` before they are built - `PstItemsTester` checks the ANSI reader's charges, the blob's and the zones', the stop reaching each, and the guard; how the contacts and calendars phases deal with a stop or a spent budget has no test of its own. Its adversarial review (nine should-fixes, no memory, bounds or injection defect) closed with: an item the account's store cannot take keeps its mark and retries the job (HM7411), as a message does; hidden folders and the contacts folders Exchange keeps for itself (the recipient cache, the GAL contacts, Skype's lists) are left out, and folders under Deleted Items are imported last, so that a deleted copy never wins a live contact's address; notes and descriptions longer than an item keeps are cut with a warning, never the item lost, and one kept only as RTF is read through the mail phase's RTF reader; an address the account only collected from mail it sent is adopted with the PST's card, as CardDAV adopts one; where a folder's items go is decided when its first item is stored and kept in its row, names cut at a character to 255, at most 50 calendars made a job, and the stop heard while the folders are planned; a zone's name is cut at a character, a blob that describes fewer changed occurrences than it changes is refused, a zone's fixed-date changes are read one way, the budget's limits are defined once, an OST is refused before its store is asked anything else, and the name-to-id map is tried again by the next item; an all-day appointment or a birthday placed without a zone says so. `items.pst` gained what shows the folder rules, the cut notes, the adopted address, the long name, the TZSTRUCT-only zone and the RTF-only description, which `API/PstImport` checks; `PstItemsTester` and `PstTester` check the cut names, the exception count, the fixed-date zone, the RTF reading and the OST's order; a stop in the planning loop, and the mail phase's retries of the name-to-id map, have no test, since nothing here can time a stop or damage the map for one entry and not the next. Its re-check found one must-fix in that round, now closed: an item the store refuses for good no longer retries the job for a day and then fails it - the folder's row counts an item's failures in a row, and the fourth is counted `not_saved`, named, logged once (HM7411) and passed, while a database that is away still pauses the job (its row cannot be written either); a folder whose own book or calendar is refused as often goes into the default, or is left out under Deleted Items. A folder under Deleted Items with no book or calendar of its own is left out, and a deleted event's UID is looked for across every calendar of the account; a folder's row keeps only the id of where it goes; a short text is read to at most 1 MB and a body to 4 MB, and a body's RTF is charged before it is decompressed; the 28th of February in a fixed-date zone stays the 28th; the phases read the name-to-id map once, under a unit of its own; adopting a collected address makes no book; the report's folders are found by an index. On MySQL, contacts, address books and a job's folders are in three-byte utf8 tables until a schema step of their own: a contact with a character outside the BMP is counted `not_saved` after its attempts, as Migration.md says. `clash.pst` - two UIDs that make one resource name - shows the refusal counted after three pauses and the next event arriving (`API/PstImport`), and `PstItemsTester` the 28th of February; untested are the retry of a database that is away, the ceiling of 50 calendars, a text past 1 MB, the zone and birthday warnings, and a collected address in another book. A third re-check (no must-fix) closed with: an item the store refuses is held while the next item shows whether the store works - the next one stored, the refusal is the item's own and it is counted `not_saved` at once, with no pause (`clash.pst` now finishes without one); the next one refused too, nothing is given up and the job pauses; the fall-back to the default book or calendar is kept only once its item is stored there; the rare report lines are saved at once; a deleted folder's left-out items are counted `folder_left_out` and a deleted event's UID is looked up in one query across the account; the report says once why a file's map could not be read; the RTF is charged after its header is checked, at most nine times its bytes; a row's folder text keeps U+FFFD for a character a MySQL utf8 column cannot hold; a database error while adopting a collected address is `not_saved`, not `already_there`; the clash test retries a 409. `items.pst` gained a deleted folder named as the default book (left out) and a deleted copy of an event (already there), and `PstItemsTester` the RTF charge; untested remain the fall-back pass and a failed map. A fourth re-check found that the look-ahead read the next item outside the reader's guard, so a crash in it could restart the service into the same crash for ever; the look-ahead is gone. A refused item is now held - kept in the folder's row with its count, two at most - and the job goes on past it: an item after it stored shows the store works and the held items, tried once more, are counted `not_saved` at once; at the folder's end they are tried once more, and with nothing stored their counts go up and the job pauses, a count of four deciding. Every read, of an item after the mark or of a held one, is written into the row first with its attempt, so the guard leaves out whatever stopped the service twice wherever it is read. Two adjacent own refusals no longer fail the job (`clash.pst` now holds three UID twins), a Deleted Items folder whose own book is refused for good is left out and counted, the fall-back leaves no false line, a deleted event's UID is looked up among the account's calendar ids so the per-calendar index serves it and a database error there is `not_saved`, and the map's line is written once a phase. A crash under the guard, and the fall-back pass, remain untested. Its fifth re-check found capped held items taken out of the row's mark before the first of them was read in the default, so a stop or crash in that read lost the others: a held item now leaves the mark only in the save that writes its outcome, the report saved with it. A held item's count starts at nought, so it is decided in its fourth run after three pauses, as stated (the `Tail` calendar of `clash.pst` pins exactly three); a deleted folder's own calendar made in the run is looked in for a second object of one UID; a second read of an item in a run keeps its guard attempt; the map's line is written once whenever a run finds it missing. Migration.md says the MySQL limit on characters outside the BMP holds before schema step 6102 only, and keeps the U+FFFD folder names for servers of 6101 or older. **Thinner than the row asks:** the reader runs in the service rather than in a child process (the row stays in motion until it does not); not in the Import Tool - no drag and drop; the file is copied into the import directory and the job started over REST, hmctl or the Deck; the 4K-page layout stays refused (Microsoft has published neither its pages nor its block compression, only third parties have reverse-engineered them, and no such file was at hand), and so does Windows Information Protection (the key never leaves the Windows account and device that protected the file; the sentence says to export from Outlook there with the protection removed); an OST caching an Exchange mailbox is refused at its store; a contact with no e-mail address is not kept, the address book holding each contact by one, and distribution lists are counted; a changed occurrence has the series' body and attendees (its own are in a message embedded in the series, not read), a series follows its zone's rule of the year it starts, and a lunar or Hijri series is refused; a recurring task comes in as its current occurrence; an appointment, contact or task in a mail folder is counted, not imported; attachments by reference, web links and OLE objects are named, not imported; an RTF-only body comes in as `body.rtf` beside a rough text; meeting requests and reports come in as their text; `PT_STRING8` outside the code-page table is not decoded; the password check could be tested only against files written here; the Deck shows a PST job with the migrations' words (its source is the file name) and its report's `pst` part only in the JSON; no fuzz target; and nothing here was compiled on Linux. |
| ✅ | **Event scripts on Linux** | **Built 24 September 2026 on branch `row98-linuxscripts`, with no schema change; the Linux suite on the lab VM is its gate, and it has not been run there yet.** The Linux builds run event scripts in JavaScript, in QuickJS 2025-04-26 - Fabrice Bellard's engine, MIT - vendored as source under `hmailserver/source/Server/quickjs` (the engine and the four libraries it needs, byte for byte as released, with its LICENSE and VERSION; `quickjs-libc.c`, where its `std` and `os` modules live, left out of the tree) and built by CMake as a C library of its own, with the definitions its Makefile passes. Not QuickJS-ng, which is published only on GitHub; and not on Windows, because the release has no MSVC support: Windows keeps VBScript and JScript, untouched. The `scriptlanguage` setting takes a third word, `JavaScript`, on both platforms - COM `Settings.Scripting.Language`, `PUT /api/v1/settings/scripting`, the Control Panel's field in all seventeen languages - so that a backup, an environment configuration or a configuration document (`hmctl config export`) carries across; each build refuses the language it cannot run when the script is loaded, whether the file for it exists or not (HM5016 or HM5710, once per load, the Linux line saying to set `JavaScript`), storing the language reloads nothing, every event is called in the language of the script in force, and `GET /api/v1/status` reports `build.script_languages`. The same events under the same function names, a rule's Run function action included, and the same objects under the same names (`Common/Scripting/ScriptBinding`): the message through `MessageData` as `InterfaceMessage` does it, with `Save()` writing the file; the client, `Result`, `EventLog`, the recipients; the fetch account and the account to read. `message.HeaderValue('X') = v` is `SetHeaderValue('X', v)` and `Flag(n) = on` is `SetFlag(n, on)`; `CreateObject` and `new ActiveXObject` throw saying COM is Windows. A handler is called with `JS_Call` and real values rather than source text, so no password or error description can end a string early. A fresh runtime for every event, the shape the Windows host has; `ScriptTimeout` through the engine's interrupt handler, which the interpreter polls at calls and branches and the regular expression matcher polls too, and which no `catch` can hold (HM7341) - it does not stop one call into a built-in that loops in C without polling, `includes` over an array-like of huge length, which EventScripts.md names; a 64 MB ceiling (HM7342); 512 KB of stack, a runaway recursion an `InternalError`; `Result.Value` and `.Parameter` take a whole Number and throw at anything else, so no handler bug turns into the 0 that lets a client in; no module loader, so `import()` is refused. [EventScripts.md](hmailserver/docs/EventScripts.md) has the three languages, every event with what `Result` does in it, the contract member by member against COM with each member that is not on JavaScript and why, a ported handler, and the threat model. Tested by `ScriptBindingTester`, which one thing runs: the bench program `script-binding-tests`, which the CMake build makes and `linux-build.yml` runs beside `spf-tests` - nothing in the server and neither regression suite runs it, so on the lab VM it is run by hand; and by `API/EventsJavaScript.cs`, 44 tests in both projects, skipped on Windows through `script_languages` and failed, not skipped, in the Linux project by a server that does not report JavaScript: one per event `API/Events.cs` tests, with a handler mirroring the one there, the two backup events started over `POST /api/v1/backup`; `build.script_languages`; a VBScript script refused at load with the sentence naming `JavaScript`; the timeout, the ceiling, a syntax error's line, `CreateObject`, a refusal through `Result`, an exception's stack, edits written, ES2023, a quoted password handed over as it is; and JavaScript counterparts of 15 of the 24 tests the Linux suite skipped for want of an engine. All 24 originals still skip there, with the language as the reason now, and nine have no counterpart: `ScriptObjectPolicy`'s five (COM classes) and `MessageHeaderObjects`' three (the COM header objects), and `WorkQueueLifecycle`'s one, which stops and starts the servers the shim cannot. Thinner than the row asked: one engine on Linux rather than one engine on both, since the only release this project could take has no Windows build; `Attachments`, `Headers`, the account's and the fetch account's writes and most of the account's other members are not on JavaScript, each named in the doc; `API/Events.cs` is not linked into the Linux project, since it needs `Infrastructure/BackupRestore.cs`, which is not - its tests would all have skipped there anyway, and their JavaScript mirrors run instead; and the row's "OnClientLogon (deny)" test cannot exist, because `OnClientLogon` has no `Result` on any platform - `OnClientValidatePassword`'s accept and deny stand for it. **Reviewed adversarially on the same branch (eight must-fix, eight should-fix, ten notes) and corrected the same day:** on Windows, storing `JavaScript` beside a loaded JScript or VBScript script made five callers build their call for the setting's language - an empty call, so a loaded `OnClientValidatePassword` stopped being asked and the stored password decided, without a word - and the callers now build for `ScriptServer::GetLoadedLanguage`, `FireEvent` refuses (and reports once per load) an empty or bare call and a call quoted for the other language during a reload, and a reload into a language the build does not run is refused even with no file for it; the Linux event log, one file never cleared, is read from a mark, where two ported tests could not pass and several passed on an earlier test's lines; a handler search that failed for any reason but the timeout refuses the load instead of installing the script with that handler off; the contract probe tells read from read/write; the AppImage installs QuickJS's licence as the other packages do; and the doc now says what the switch does not stop (the top level runs at every load), what the timeout does not bound, the `InternalError`, and which test runs `ScriptBindingTester`, where each had claimed more. The row as written: the Windows build hosts VBScript and JScript through Active Scripting; the Linux build has no engine, and the roadmap's Linux row skips every scripting test for it. One cross-platform engine - JavaScript through an embeddable interpreter - for the same events on both, with the Windows engines kept. |
| ✅ | **Passkeys and single sign-on** | **Done 18 September 2026** (`hmailserver/docs/PasskeysAndSingleSignOn.md`): two ways into the webmail and the Control Deck beside the password, both minted by the one route that has always minted a browser session - `POST /api/v1/session` - so each gets the same session table, the same idle and lifetime ceilings, the same one-per-device rule and the same entry in the account's device list, which says which credential it was. **Passkeys**: `hm_passkeys` (schema 6077) holds the public half of a WebAuthn credential - the credential id, the COSE key, the algorithm and the signature counter - for an account, or for the administrator under a null account id, since the administrator is a password in `hMailServer.ini` and has no row to point at. `GET/POST/DELETE /api/v1/passkeys` and `POST /api/v1/passkeys/options` answer *whoever signed in*, which is why they are classified with the session routes rather than as administration; enrolling one asks for the owner's own password again, as an application password does, and is refused outright on a request authenticated with one. Signing in is `POST /api/v1/passkeys/challenge` (no credential; the challenge is spent on first use, against a five-minute cookie) and then a `passkey` object where the password would be. `Common/Util/WebAuthn.{h,cpp}` is the reader - a small definite-length CBOR decoder, the COSE key, the authenticator data and the signature over `authData || SHA-256(clientDataJSON)` in OpenSSL, ES256 and RS256 only - with `WebAuthnTester` running fixed vectors from a software authenticator on every debug start. A passkey stands in for the one-time code, because registration insists the authenticator reported *user verified*. **Single sign-on**: `OidcIssuer`, `OidcClientId`, `OidcClientSecret` (through the secret store), `OidcScopes`, `OidcRedirectBaseUrl`, `OidcAutoProvision` and `OidcAdministrators` in `hm_settings`; discovery from `<issuer>/.well-known/openid-configuration`, cached; the authorization-code flow with PKCE at `/portal/oidc/start` and `/portal/oidc/callback`, the `id_token` verified against the issuer's JWK Set (signature, `iss`, `aud`, `exp`, `nonce`) and its *verified* `email` mapped to a mailbox - or, for the Deck, to the administrator when the address is in `OidcAdministrators`, which the issuer cannot overrule. The callback mints a one-use two-minute ticket in the URL fragment rather than a session, so the session is still minted in one place. **Off, or inert, in a stock installation**: passkeys are offered and nobody has one until they enrol it; single sign-on does nothing until an issuer is named, and creates no mailbox for a stranger unless `OidcAutoProvision` is on - and then only at a domain this server already has, with a random password nobody is told. **What is thinner than this row, said plainly**: the attestation statement is NOT verified and cannot be, because this project ships no manufacturer metadata to verify it against - so an installation cannot say “only hardware keys of this make”, and the options ask for attestation `none` rather than for a statement nobody checks; there is no OIDC logout in either direction, so an account disabled at the issuer keeps a session it already started until that session's own ceiling ends it; there is no claims mapping (no groups, no roles) and one issuer only; and nothing here reaches IMAP, POP3 or SMTP, which still take the account's password - this is the *pages'* front door. Surfaces: the webmail's Security page and sign-in card, the Deck's Passkeys view and sign-in card, eighteen texts in the twenty-three webmail catalogues and twenty in the Deck's twenty, six and nine new harness checks. `hm_passkeys` is audited like `hm_apppasswords` and is in `AccountStores`' `elsewhere_`: a credential bound to a relying party id restored on another host could never be used. **The review's six joins, fixed in the second commit**: both new credentials now meet what every other passwordless credential in this tree meets - `AccountLogon::RefusedByHold` and the domain's country policy, in one `AccountMayStartSession_` both call, so a passkey a takeover enrolled is refused with the password the compromised-account detection has just locked, and the last-logon stamp moves on for somebody who only ever signs in this way; `POST /api/v1/session` demands `X-Requested-With` when the credential is in the *body*, because a body is the one credential a cross-site form can carry and a login forgery would have put the reader in the attacker's mailbox; *user verified* is checked at every assertion and not only at enrolment, with `userVerification: "required"` asked of the browser on both pages, so a credential enrolled as two factors cannot sign in as one while the one-time code is skipped on the strength of it; the two unauthenticated challenge tables evict their oldest entry rather than refusing the newest, so a few hundred requests from a stranger cost the stranger their own slots instead of switching both buttons off for everybody (`PasskeyStoreTester` proves it on every debug start); `DELETE /api/v1/passkeys/{id}` is answered while passkeys are switched off, because the moment they are turned off is the moment a lost device's credential most wants removing; and the CBOR reader's byte-string guard is a subtraction rather than an addition, so a declared length near the top of `size_t` is refused rather than wrapping the cursor backwards and half-reading the document. |
| ✅ | **A password checked against known breaches** | **Done 15 September 2026, and off by default.** When a password is chosen, it is hashed with SHA-1 over its UTF-8 bytes and the first five hexadecimal characters are sent to a range endpoint with `Add-Padding: true`; the endpoint answers every suffix that follows them, padded so the answer's size says nothing, and the other thirty-five characters are compared on the server. Checked at every place a password is chosen - `Account.Password` over COM (the Control Panel, the Import Tool, scripts), `PUT /api/v1/accounts/{address}`, `POST /api/v1/domains/{domain}/accounts` (which had applied no password policy at all, and read the password through the ANSI code page), the webmail's `POST /api/v1/me/password`, a chosen app password over COM and REST, and the administrator password - after the policy and the reuse history and before the history is written, never at logon; `build/check-password-choice-sites.py` fails the build when a place stops asking or asks out of order. Four settings in `hm_settings`, not the ini: on, the endpoint (`https://api.pwnedpasswords.com/range/` by default; https, or plain http only to an address that parses as loopback, with `{prefix}` for a mirror), the minimum count, and what an endpoint that cannot be used does (lets the change through by default, or refuses it; `HM6600` to `HM6604` either way). A refusal is 400 with reason `breached` or 503 with `breach_check_unavailable`, and the Control Panel, the Deck and the webmail show it in the reader's language (17, 20 and 23 catalogues). An endpoint test on every surface - a Control Panel button, a Deck verb, `POST /api/v1/settings/breach-check/test`, `hmctl settings breach-test`, `Test-HmBreachCheckEndpoint`, `Settings.TestBreachCheckEndpoint` - asks for `00000` and never returns the endpoint's body. An empty answer is an endpoint that could not be used, not a verdict, so a mirror serving empty files cannot switch the check off in silence. On the way, the outbound HTTPS client gained one deadline for the whole request - name lookup, connection, proxy CONNECT, TLS handshake and reading the answer, on Windows and Linux alike, where before only the connection had one and on Linux nothing bounded a stalled read - so a hung mirror holds a REST worker for eight seconds at most; it reaches an address on this machine directly unless the proxy is on this machine too, writes an IPv6 literal's Host header as `[::1]:port`, and stopped treating any host beginning `127.` as loopback. `hmailserver/docs/PasswordBreachCheck.md`. Tested by an in-process tester (the SHA-1 vectors, including a UTF-8 one against its Latin-1 and UTF-16 digests, the endpoint rules, the range parser, the empty answer), an out-of-process harness against the compiled client (a trickled answer, a silent server and a stalled handshake each ended at the deadline, `[::1]`, the proxy rule; the previous client failed six of its checks), two regression fixtures against a fake range server on port 9551 (Security/PasswordBreachCheck and API/RestApiBreachCheck: what is on the wire, padding, the minimum as a boundary, both unreachable actions, an empty and a trickled answer, an IPv6 loopback endpoint, refusals with their negative controls, no history row after a refusal), and the Deck, webmail and command-line harnesses; the fixtures ran green in the batch-10 gate on 16 September 2026. The row as written: At every password change, the k-anonymity check against the breach corpus (five characters of the hash leave the server, nothing else), refused when found. |
| ✅ | **Spam traps** | **Built 16 September 2026 on branch spam-traps, schema step 6048 to 6049 (renumbered at fold); gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** An administrator declares addresses of a domain that were never real (`hm_spamtraps`, held as a local part and the domain's id so a rename carries them). An account, an alias or a distribution list cannot be given a trap's address and a trap cannot be given theirs - both directions, at every save, because either way round leaves a mailbox where the evidence should be. At `RCPT TO` a trap is refused with the words an address this server does not have gets, made by the same call (`SMTPConnection::CheckSpamTrap_`, which runs at `RCPT TO` before the delivery lookups, so a catch-all cannot accept a trap, greylisting cannot answer 451 for one, and a repeat of the same `RCPT TO` is refused again but counted, recorded and banned once), so nothing tells the sender which it was; then the connecting address is banned through the server's own auto-ban (an expiring `hm_securityranges` row, lifted by the same sweep, mirrored into the firewall by the same reconciliation, and matched by a third line in the packaged fail2ban filter), the envelope sender goes on the server's block list with an expiry, and the hit is recorded in `hm_spamtraphits` and counted into `hmailserver_spam_trap_hits_total` (also in the OTLP push and the metric history). Never banned: an authenticated session, an IP range that does not run the spam tests, the anti-spam white list, an incoming relay, an address on `AutoBanNeverBan`. Never blocked: the null sender, a sender claiming a domain this server hosts, and one the server's own allow list already names. **A hit trains nothing by itself, and the documents say so rather than implying otherwise**: the trap is refused at `RCPT TO`, so no content arrives and there is nothing to teach, and this server has no classifier of its own. What is done, off by default (`SpamTrapTeachSpamAssassin`), is that a message the same session goes on to send to a real mailbox in the same transaction is taught to spamd as spam through the same TELL a Junk move already uses. Four settings in `hm_settings` (ban minutes, block days, retention days, the lesson), the traps in the backup with their domain, `hm_spamtraps` on the audit trail and the hits deliberately not - they are traffic, written with nobody's authority. Every surface: COM (`Domain.SpamTraps`, `AddSpamTrap`, `RemoveSpamTrap`; `Utilities.GetSpamTrapHits`, `ClearSpamTrapHits`), REST with its OpenAPI text, the Control Deck (a card on the domain and a view of its own, in all twenty catalogues), `hmctl spamtrap` and `hmctl traphits`, five PowerShell cmdlets, and the configuration document. `hmailserver/docs/SpamTraps.md`. **Verified 18 September 2026:** `AntiSpam/SpamTraps` and `API/RestApiSpamTraps` (9, linked into the Linux suite) ran green in the batch-14 gates, and the SpamAssassin lesson is proven by `TheMessageASessionSentAfterAddressingATrapTeachesSpamAssassin` (the fixture's seventeenth test) against the suite's own spamd, which accepts TELL - a session that addresses a trap and then sends to a real mailbox produces one `TELL` with `Message-class: spam` into the local store. A real spamd has to be started with `--allow-tell` for the lesson to land, as `docs/SpamTraps.md` says; the benches' spamd is not, and the test does not need it to be. **Reviewed on the branch, and five things fixed:** the first cut had no `RCPT TO` hook at all - the monitor was written and never called, so a declared trap refused nothing and every surface announced a feature that did not run; the delete route did not percent-decode the address, which is what its own Deck and PowerShell clients send, so a trap could not be removed from either; the once-per-trap-per-session promise the metric and the documents make was kept nowhere; the trap question was asked before the address was known to belong to the domain being saved into, which refused an address in another domain with the wrong sentence; and both fixtures emptied the server's whole allow and block list instead of taking back their own entries. There is a test for the first two. **Reviewed a second time, and eight more things fixed:** the sender block asked for a row whose value was exactly the envelope sender, so an administrator's allow entry naming the DOMAIN was invisible to it and the automatic address block outranked it - it now asks `SenderListCache`, which is where precedence is decided, and an expired entry it reuses is dated now rather than keeping the date of the entry it replaces; the SpamAssassin lesson was given for every session, including the authenticated, white-listed and trusted ones the hit had just decided not to punish, so `Outcome` now carries whether the session may be taught from at all; clearing the record of hits was recorded nowhere over either COM or REST - the rows are traffic, so the one act whose whole effect is to remove evidence left no trace, and it is now a `RecordEvent` on both, pinned by `check-audit-choke-point.py`; the configuration document carried a trap's read-only `local_part`, which the create route refuses, so `config apply` failed with 400 on the first trap it had to make; the refusal test took its negative control from a connection made after the ban, which is refused at accept, so it compared a real 550 with an empty string; the two tables had no foreign key to `hm_domains` while every other domain-owned table does; the Deck's trap reload passed a variable to the fetch helper, which the parity census reads as every collection route the page knows, so `DeckParity.md` credited the domains view with twenty routes it never calls and neither trap GET; nine of the twenty catalogues translated "Banned" and "Blocked" to the same word in a cell that shows both badges side by side, so the badges now name what was acted on (IP, sender) in every language, and the French "hits" was "touches" - keystrokes. Three tests were added or corrected for those, and the timing difference between a trap refusal and an unknown address's is now written down as a sharp edge, with the firewall reconciliation moved off the `RCPT TO` thread so that what is left is three inserts. **Not done:** a trap does not tarpit or blackhole the session beyond the refusal, there is no per-network ban, and the desktop Control Panel has no page for it. The row as written: Addresses that were never real; a message to one auto-bans the sender and trains the score. |

### The organisation

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ⬜ | **Active-active nodes** | The warm-standby topology is documented and tested; what remains is two or more nodes serving the same domains at once - shared database, shared or replicated message store, queue ownership by node, sessions on any node - so a node can be taken down without a failover. |
| ⬜ | **Object storage for the message store** | Messages on S3-compatible or Azure Blob storage with the database as the index, which is how a store grows past one disk and how the active-active row shares a store. |
| ✅ | **Mailbox encryption at rest** | **Built 24 September 2026 on branch `row112-encryption`, with schema step 6098 to 6099. Not yet run against a bench: nothing here has been compiled, and the fixture runs when the branch is folded into a batch and gated.** A key per account - X25519, in `hm_accountkeys` - and every message stored in the account's folders from the switch on encrypted to its public half as the message store's stage 3, after compression: an ephemeral key per file, HKDF-SHA256 over the agreement, AES-256-GCM over the body with the header's size, its inner stages and both public keys bound in, the SHA-256 of the message left out (a digest in the clear would confirm a guess), so mail that arrives while nobody is signed in is encrypted all the same. The key's private half is wrapped by the account's password with Argon2id (PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 at 600,000 where OpenSSL has no Argon2) and sealed to the domain's recovery key (`hm_domainrecovery`), whose own private half is wrapped by a passphrase the administrator keeps and this server never stores; between the switch and the first sign-in it is held under the machine's DataProtector, since the switch has no password to wrap it by, and the doc says the server - and whoever has the machine's secret with the database - can open it then. A sign-in with the account's own password (`AccountLogon::Logon`: IMAP, POP3, SMTP AUTH, ManageSieve, DAV, the webmail, REST Basic) opens the key into memory; an IMAP or POP3 session holds it, and it closes `MailboxKeyIdleMinutes` (15) after its last use or at a restart. IMAP and POP3 refuse an app password, a token, a certificate, a ticket, the master user or SCRAM with the key closed with a sentence (`NO [UNAVAILABLE]`, `-ERR [SYS/PERM]`), and so does the webmail for a passkey, single sign-on or an app password. The blob question the compression row left: an encrypted mailbox holds no part once, since a blob shared between accounts cannot be under one account's key and a key that is not an account's is one the server can always use. The index: no term of an encrypted mailbox is stored and the sort's copy of its headers is not written - search reads each message while the key is open, slower, and only then - and both are dropped at the switch-on. Every writer into an encrypted mailbox encrypts - delivery, APPEND, the IMAP mirror, migrations and imports (read before they are encrypted, so an import with the key closed keeps its row), webmail drafts and Sent copies, messages put in over REST, a message COM or an event script adds, copies between mailboxes (a copy between two keys, or out to a public folder, re-encoded with the source's key open), a mailbox restore and the recover-deleted put-back, which encrypt what was kept before the switch on the way in - and the drafts (their attachments included), REST's and COM's are encrypted in memory and never written in the clear; a delivery's, an APPEND's and an import's plaintext is overwritten with zeros once it is stored encrypted or refused. A message that cannot be stored encrypted is refused rather than kept in the clear (5.6.0 when it is too large, 4.3.0 when this server's own write failed). Reading one decrypts it in memory: `MessageData` - SEARCH, PREVIEW, the webmail, eDiscovery, the rules, scripts and COM - parses a container from memory and writes no copy, a rewrite is encrypted in memory and renamed over the stored file, and SpamAssassin is not taught from an encrypted mailbox. Deleted items are kept encrypted with no subject or sender in the database. Passwords: the person's own change carries the key; an administrator's reset, COM's and SCIM's are refused while the key is closed, as is a reset link, naming `POST /api/v1/accounts/{address}/mailbox-encryption/recover`, which opens the key with the domain's passphrase and sets the new password through the policy - audited; a lost password and a lost passphrase are the mail lost, and `DELETE ...?confirm=forfeit` says so, refused under a legal hold. An app password reaches no `/api/v1/me` route of a keyed mailbox, the IMAP master user is refused it even while its key is open, only the owner's own uses keep the key open (an administrator's password set is not one), the switch-on ends the account's IMAP, POP3 and webmail sessions, and a webmail session is good only under the opening of the key it was made under, so a session whose key closed is ended and a stale one's request never ends a fresh one. A keyed mailbox is not moved to another domain; a second factor and a key are never on one account; replacing the recovery key keeps the old key beside the new one until every account is resealed, so a replacement cut short loses nothing, and one lock runs replacements and a switch-on's sealing one at a time. Which accounts are keyed is a set in memory - read at the start, changed by every write here, read again after a restore - so a server with no keyed account asks the database nothing for it, and a failed read changes nothing for any mailbox; only while the server has never read it is the answer unknown, and unknown is always temporary: a delivery waits in the queue and is tried again, IMAP answers `NO [UNAVAILABLE]`, POP3 `-ERR [SYS/TEMP]`, REST 503 - never a bounce while retries are left, a discard or an ended session. A key row that holds no key is damage, refused at once with a sentence naming the forfeit, which removes it (IMAP `[CONTACTADMIN]`, POP3 `[SYS/PERM]`, REST 409, a delivery bounced at once - to a sender outside the server with no more than that the mailbox cannot receive mail now). A crash between the renames that put an encrypted file in place is put right at the next start, by a sweep that reads file names only, and an import whose row save fails puts its file back as it was. `hmailserver --encrypt-store` and `--decrypt-store` (the passphrase from `HMAILSERVER_RECOVERY_PASSPHRASE`) rewrite what was there before, deleted items included, name by name. Zero access and this are never on one mailbox, each refusing the other. The Deck's account and domain cards, REST, the backup (the key with the account, the recovery key with the domain), `HM7360` to `HM7367`. Thinner than the row: no webmail card and no device key for a passkey sign-in; no Control Panel page and no COM property, for the switch or for `MailboxKeyIdleMinutes`; an account's key is never rotated; integrity is per file; a pending key opens with the machine's own secret until the first sign-in - on Linux a file inside the data directory; search over an encrypted mailbox reads every message; the calendar and the address book are not encrypted - an invitation delivered to a keyed mailbox is filed into its calendar as it arrived, and the doc's list of what stays in the clear names it (it did not until the second review). Reviewed adversarially twice on 25 September 2026 and corrected. See [EncryptionAtRest.md](hmailserver/docs/EncryptionAtRest.md). |
| ✅ | **Storage compression and attachment de-duplication** | **Built 24 September 2026 on branch `row110-compression`, in two commits - the message store's seam, then the format - with schema step 6097 to 6098. Not yet run against a bench: nothing here has been compiled, and the fixture runs when the branch is folded into a batch and gated. Measured on a real mailbox: to come - the integrator runs `--measure-store` on one before the saving is claimed, and the figures go here and in the doc.** The seam: every place that read, sized, copied, moved or deleted a stored message by its path - about 180 of them in eighty files - now goes through `MessageStore` (`Common/Persistence/MessageStore.h`): working files (the queue, the copy being delivered) stay plain RFC 822 and are read by path as before, stored files are read through the store's reader and doors, and the few sites that must have the stored file's name (`Message.Filename`, the indexers, the recover-deleted store) are listed with their reasons in `build/check-message-store-seam.py`, which CI runs and which refuses a call anywhere else. The store is a chain of stages over a backend, so the encryption-at-rest row is one more stage and object storage one more backend. The format: with `MessageStoreCompression` on (off by default, a row of `hm_settings`), a message delivered to a mailbox or put there by IMAP APPEND is stored as a container - an eight-byte magic no RFC 5322 message begins with, the original's size and SHA-256, the stages by id (1 deflate on the vendored zlib; 2 reserved for zstd; 3 reserved for account-key encryption) and the references - and each MIME part of at least `MessageStoreDedupMinKB` (256 KB) decoded is kept once, as its wire bytes, under `<data>\Blobs`, counted by name in `hm_storeblobs` so that the count is never fewer than the names that need it. The reader checks every length, every blob's hash and the whole message's hash, and refuses a damaged container (`HM7350`) rather than serve it. IMAP, POP3, the webmail, the sizes, the quota, search, SORT and a DKIM check see the original; `Message.Filename` names the container, which the doc says. `hmailserver --measure-store`, `--compress-store [--account] [--decompress]` and `--recount-store` walk the store (the rewrite reads each file back and compares it before renaming, keeps `DeliveryHardLinks` names one file, and is refused beside the running Windows service); `GET /api/v1/store`, COM's `Settings.MessageStoreStatus`, the Deck's dashboard and the Control Panel say what it saves. Backups carry the blobs with the data directory; a full restore rebuilds the counts, a mailbox restore brings in the blobs it needs. Thinner than it could be: only delivery and APPEND write the new form (everything else stores plain until `--compress-store`); zstd is not here; a container is decoded whole, so a message over 64 MB stays plain; the walk is not refused beside a running Linux server; and a blob shared between accounts is what the encryption-at-rest row must decide about, because it cannot be encrypted under one account's key. See [MessageStore.md](hmailserver/docs/MessageStore.md). |
| ✅ | **MySQL tables in utf8mb4** | **Built 25 September 2026 on branch `row-mysql-utf8mb4`, schema step 6101 to 6102, and proven the same day on real servers - MySQL 5.7.44 and 8.4.11, MariaDB 10.6.28, 11.4.13 and 11.8.9, in docker on the lab VM - but not yet run against a bench: nothing here has been compiled, and the server and the suite run when the branch is folded and gated.** Found by the integrator on 25 September 2026: 106 of the MySQL create script's 123 tables said `DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8`, which is MySQL's three-byte utf8mb3, fifteen named nothing and took the database's default, and two were utf8mb4 under whatever collation the server preferred, while the connection has said `SET NAMES utf8mb4` for years - so a character outside the Basic Multilingual Plane (an emoji, a CJK Extension B character) in a contact, an address book, a migration folder, a subject in the message index, a webmail setting, a signature, a role or a log line was refused with error 1366 under strict mode, and made a question mark without it. Every table in `CreateTablesMySQL.sql` now ends `ENGINE=InnoDB ROW_FORMAT=DYNAMIC DEFAULT CHARSET=utf8mb4 COLLATE=utf8mb4_general_ci`, and the step converts every table of an existing database (`ALTER TABLE ... ENGINE=InnoDB, ROW_FORMAT=DYNAMIC, CONVERT TO CHARACTER SET utf8mb4 COLLATE utf8mb4_general_ci`), the SQL log device's `hm_log` where an earlier build made it, and the database's own default. **The collation is `utf8mb4_general_ci`**, the twin of the `utf8_general_ci` the tables had, so every comparison and unique index over what they could hold compares as before (`utf8mb4_unicode_ci` takes `ß` for `ss`, `utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci` is MySQL 8's alone and counts trailing spaces); the three `utf8_bin` columns become `utf8mb4_bin`, converted column by column, because `CONVERT TO` would make them case-insensitive too and their unique indexes would refuse their own rows. **InnoDB and DYNAMIC** because a `varchar(255)` key is 1020 bytes at four a character, over MyISAM's 1000 and COMPACT's 767, and an old installation's tables can be either; the longest key in the schema is 1060 bytes, under DYNAMIC's 3072, so no index needed a prefix or a narrower column. **MySQL 5.7.9 or MariaDB 10.2.2 at the least**, now written in MigratingDatabaseBackend.md; on an older server the step's first statement fails and nothing is changed. SQL Server, Compact and PostgreSQL were never affected (nvarchar and UTF-8), and their steps only move the version. `build/check-mysql-charset.py` (with `--self-test`) holds every CREATE TABLE in the create script and in the upgrade scripts from 6102 on to the same options, the step to every table, the index lengths, and the table and the database the server creates itself (`SqlLogDevice.cpp`, `SQLStatement::GetCreateDatabase`, both utf8mb4 now). **The proof**, on each of the five servers: the create script as it was, set into an old installation's shape (five tables MyISAM, five InnoDB COMPACT, two latin1, `hm_log` in utf8); nine inserts with a 4-byte character refused with 1366; the step; the same nine taken and read back byte for byte; the rows from before intact and the unique indexes refusing what they refused before; all 124 tables InnoDB, DYNAMIC and utf8mb4_general_ci; the step a second time; and the new create script on a fresh database, every table utf8mb4. **Thinner than it could be:** `utf8mb4_general_ci` gives every character outside the Basic Multilingual Plane one weight, so two names that differ only in an emoji are one name to a unique index (a collation that tells them apart changes how everything else compares); where tables compared under another collation - every table on MariaDB 11.5 and later, the calendar tables on MySQL 8 and MariaDB 11, the tables of a database an administrator made that named no character set - two rows that collation kept apart stop the step with 1062, and it finishes when run again after one is renamed (both shown on the servers); the step rebuilds every table, so it takes as long as copying the database; an upgraded `text` column reads `mediumtext` afterwards; the tables of hMailServer 1 to 3 that an old database still carries and nothing reads (`hm_serverlog`, `hm_filters`, `hm_deliverylog`) are left as they are; and the sandbox's question mark for an emoji in a file name stays until it is lifted. See [MigratingDatabaseBackend.md](hmailserver/docs/MigratingDatabaseBackend.md#mysql-and-mariadb) and [Upgrading.md](hmailserver/docs/Upgrading.md). |
| ✅ | **A list manager** | **Built 24 September 2026, schema step 6091 to 6092, and reviewed adversarially the same day - ten must-fix findings, twenty should-fix and nine notes, every one answered in the commit after it. Not yet run against a bench: nothing here has been compiled but in the integrator's mirror, and the fixture runs when the branch is folded into a batch and gated.** A distribution list with the list manager switched on (`hm_distributionlists.distributionlistmanaged`, off on every list an upgrade finds, so an upgraded server distributes exactly as it did) answers its own addresses - `name-subscribe@`, `name-unsubscribe@`, `name-request@`, `name-owner@`, `name-bounces@` and `name-bounces+local=domain@` - read by `RecipientParser` before plus addressing and answered by `ListManager` in the delivery path, where moderation always was; an account, an alias or a list of exactly that name wins. A posting to it is one recipient of its own rather than its members, and the manager sends every member a copy of their own; a posting it cannot read stays in the queue and is tried again, and its sender is told `4.3.0` when the retries run out. **Joining**: closed (the administrator adds members; a request is answered with a refusal naming `name-owner@`), confirm (a token by mail, confirmed by the reply or by the page's POST) or moderated (the owner then approves on a page the notice links to, signed in to the webmail as the owner's own account, or with *Approve* on the Control Deck - never by a reply); the confirmation goes to the address being subscribed, at most once an hour per address, and a list answers at most twenty requests an hour from one requester and two hundred in all, so a request forged in somebody's name writes to them once and one stranger cannot close a list; an account the membership query answers is a member already, and a request in its name makes no row; a request lapses after a week. **Leaving and delivery**: by mail, answered with a link and a token and nothing more until the member follows it; on the member's own page (`/lists/unsubscribe?t=`, whose GET asks and whose POST acts) and by RFC 8058's one-click POST with no session; each message, a daily digest, or nothing for now, chosen there or on the webmail's Subscriptions page (*Lists on this server*, `GET/PUT/DELETE /api/v1/me/lists`). An account the membership query answers has a link of its own - its address and the list, with an HMAC under the list's secret (`distributionlistsecret`) - and leaves by it, by its one-click or by mail; a row then keeps it out, in the new state `left`. **Each copy**: the subject prefix once, Reply-To by the list's setting, the footer on the text and HTML parts as the disclaimer adder appends a rule's text (not on a signed or encrypted message), and `List-Id`, `List-Post` (NO for an announcement list), `List-Help`, `List-Subscribe`, `List-Unsubscribe` with the mailto and the member's own link, `List-Unsubscribe-Post` over an https base only, `List-Owner`, `List-Archive`, `Precedence`, an `X-Loop` that stops a posting going round two lists, and the member's tag; the envelope sender is `name-bounces@` or its VERP form; and, when the list's domain has DKIM on, a signature as that domain over the list's fields, `List-Unsubscribe-Post` included - a copy the list changed carries none of the posting's own. The links need `ListBaseUrl` (`hm_settings`) and the REST listener, and without a base URL the copies carry the mailto addresses alone. **The digest**: a table of contents and each message since the last one as a part of a `multipart/digest`, at the list's hour, never empty and never twice in twenty hours, and starting from the newest message when it is switched on (`ListDigestTask` every five minutes; `DistributionList.SendDigest` over COM sends it now). **The archive**: every distributed copy under the data directory's `Lists\<id>\` and a row in `hm_listarchive` with its month, listed by month on `/lists/{address}/archive` (public, or for a member's token or webmail session) and `GET /api/v1/lists/{address}/archive[/{id}]`, one message shown as text only - HTML reduced to its text in one pass and never rendered, at most 256 KB of it - kept for the list's `distributionlistarchivedays` whether or not the archive is still on, and deleted with the list. Surfaces: eleven new fields on the list routes and in the XML backup, each row's delivery, state and bounces on the members route, the Control Deck's editor and member table (*Approve*, *Reset bounces*), the Control Panel's list dialog, COM (`DistributionList.Managed` to `Owner` and `SendDigest`; `DistributionListRecipient.Delivery`, `HardBounces`, `SoftBounces`, `Confirmed`, `ResetBounces`), seven pairs of translatable server messages (`LIST_CONFIRM`, `LIST_REFUSED`, `LIST_HELP`, `LIST_APPROVE`, `LIST_BOUNCED`, `LIST_LEAVE` and `LIST_UNSUBSCRIBED`, each with its subject), 23 new texts in the Deck's twenty catalogues, 18 in the Control Panel's seventeen and 11 in the webmail's twenty-three. `hmailserver/docs/MailingLists.md`. Tests: `SMTP/MailingLists.cs` (REST on port 9966, thirty-four tests of the Windows suite, each with its negative control) and the manager's fields, a member's delivery and a row's state, counts and token carried through `Infrastructure/BackupRestore.cs`. **Thinner than the row, said plainly**: the list does not rewrite `From`, so a posting from outside from a domain publishing `p=reject` can fail DMARC at an outside member's provider when the list changed it - the author's signature cannot survive the prefix and the footer, and the list's own signature does not align with the author's `From`; there is no posting from the web, no configuration page for a list's owner outside the Control Deck, no approval by reply, no NNTP and no IMAP access to an archive; the digest is daily and its table of contents is in English, as are the list's pages; the archive is not searchable and its rows are not in the backup; the accounts a list's membership query finds choose no delivery of their own and their bounces are not counted; one that has left comes back through the administrator. The row as written: Distribution lists with moderation exist. A list a person subscribes to by mail and by page, with an archive, digests, per-member delivery settings and the List-* headers filled in, is what replaces Mailman for a small organisation. |
| ✅ | **Message recall within the server** | **Built 22 September 2026, schema 6085, and the adversarial review of the same day answered in a follow-up commit. Not yet run against a bench: the fixture compiles, the page harness passes, every checker is green and both builds say Build succeeded; the regression suite runs when the branch is gated.** What is built: a message this server accepts from one of its own accounts - an authenticated SMTP submission, credited to the account that signed in and never to the envelope, or the webmail's send - gets one row in `hm_recall` per recipient that resolved to a local account: the sender, the `Message-ID` without its brackets, the recipient, the queue row it was accepted as, the recipient's message row once delivered (`LocalDelivery` fills it in at the point where the copy is saved, and inserts a row for a recipient reached by expansion when rows for the same queue message exist and the delivered file carries their `Message-ID` - the sender tied by the queue row and the header together, never by a header alone). A copy the reader makes within their own mailbox - IMAP `MOVE` and `COPY`, the webmail's move, Archive, Junk and Trash, all copy-and-delete - is followed: `PersistentMessage::CopyToIMAPFolder` marks the copy with the id it came from and the save that assigns the new id writes a row beside the old one, so the recall finds the message wherever it was filed; a copy into another account's folder gets no row. Nothing for mail from outside, for mail the server writes, for an unauthenticated local-range submission, for a message with a `Message-ID` over 255 characters (a submission without one is given one at acceptance, and the webmail always writes one), for a confidential message (it has *Withdraw*), or past 200 recipient rows per sender per rolling day (the log says so once). `POST /api/v1/me/recall` `{message_id, sent_id}`, self-service, 403 on an app password, judged per recipient: `read` when any live copy of theirs carries `\Seen` (nothing done, every copy stays), else `recalled` when at least one unread copy was deleted through the page's own `DeleteOwnMessage_` (every unread copy goes; IMAP sessions see the expunge; the recover-deleted store gets its copy while the server-wide window is on), else `gone`; `failed` when the database did not answer or a delete could not be made, with nothing of that recipient's marked so a later call decides again, and a delete another recall took a moment earlier answered from the mark it left; a copy still in the queue answered `gone` and left unmarked; a copy whose file carries another `Message-ID` is not the recorded one. `elsewhere` counts the Sent copy's To/Cc addresses at domains this server does not host when `sent_id` is given (Bcc is not in the copy); the Sent copy gets `$Recalled` when something was recalled; 404 for no rows within `RecallDays`, which is also the answer for somebody else's message; idempotent; nothing sent to anybody. `RecallDays` (14; 1 to 365; `recall_days` on `PUT /api/v1/settings`), the rows indexed by sender, queue id, message id and creation time and swept by the minute task and the retention task; `recall_days` and `via_app_password` on `GET /api/v1/me` for the page. The webmail offers **Recall** in the Sent row's menu and the open message's More menu for a message from the account or one of its identities younger than `RecallDays`, not at all to a session on an app password, with the confirm sentence and a toast of the numbers, and a **Recalled** badge from the keyword; six strings in the 23 catalogues by hand. `docs/MessageRecall.md` says what is recorded and what is not, that a moved or copied message is followed, that read is `\Seen` and nothing more, that nothing reaches a recipient elsewhere and no notice is sent, the days ceiling and the per-day bound, and what is unproved. **The adversarial review of 22 September found and the follow-up fixed:** a moved copy answered `gone` (now followed), a database that did not answer recorded as `gone` for good (now `failed` and unmarked), the undelivered copy marked `gone` for good (now unmarked), a reissued queue id able to tie an outsider's message to a sender's rows (the `Message-ID` compared at delivery and at recall), a second copy of the page's delete path (the route's own now), no page harness (twelve checks), Recall offered to an app-password session, four sentences the code did not keep, no index on the sweep's column, and two concurrent recalls disagreeing.
