Tests: `API/RestApiMessageRecall.cs` (port 9806, fifteen tests, each with its negative control) - the webmail send to two local accounts and one outside address recalled from both with the `EXPUNGE` seen, `elsewhere` 1, `$Recalled` on the Sent copy and `sent_marked` on the second call; the `\Seen` copy staying as `read`; a copy deleted before the recall `gone`; a `MOVE`d copy recalled from where it was filed, a `COPY`'d one recalled twice over, a read recipient's two copies both kept; the authenticated SMTP submission recalled by its `Message-ID`; another account's 404 with nothing deleted; the unauthenticated local-range submission not recorded; the authenticated session whose `MAIL FROM` names another account credited to the login; a submission without a `Message-ID` given one and recalled by it while one over 255 is not recorded, with the log line and the 400; the 200-per-day ceiling with its single log line; the list's two members; the recalled copy in the recipient's recover-deleted list and put back; an address at a domain alias counted as here; the sweep proved distinct from the window; the app password's 403. Unproved: the confidential exclusion and the `failed` outcome. Thinner than the row asks: read is `\Seen` only (a POP3 fetch or an IMAP peek is invisible), a copy still in the queue at the moment of the recall is delivered afterwards and reached by the next call, and Bcc recipients elsewhere are not counted. |

### The person reading mail

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **Web Push** | **Built 16 September 2026 on branch `web-push` (schema 6054) and gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** A phone is told about new mail with the page closed: the server holds one subscription per device (`hm_pushsubscriptions`, at most `WebPushMaxPerAccount`, 10), signs each push with a VAPID key pair it keeps write-only (`Utilities.GenerateWebPushKeyPair`, the public half at `GET /api/v1/me/push`), and encrypts the payload to the device as RFC 8291 says (aes128gcm), so the push service carries nothing it can read. What a push carries is the device's choice - a count, the sender, or the sender and the subject - and nothing else; quiet hours are judged on the device's own clock; a sender list or a label list narrows what is announced; an endpoint that fails is retried with a backoff and dead-lettered after `WebPushMaxAttempts`, and one the push service says is gone (404, 410) is removed. `POST /api/v1/me/push/test` sends a push that says it is a test. Off by default (`WebPushEnabled`). `API/RestApiWebPush` (eleven tests, including a verification of the VAPID signature under the key the header names) ran green in that gate. Notifications while the page is open exist. Web Push through the browser's push service, with the server holding the subscriptions, means new mail is announced when the installed app is closed, which is the difference between a page and an app. |
| ✅ | **Saved searches as folders** | **Done 15 September 2026.** The search options panel takes a name and *Save search*: the search is kept in the account's preferences (`saved_searches`, twenty at most), listed in the folder navigation under *Saved searches*, and opening one runs it again with its name as the title - always current. The panel lists them with a remove. Three harness checks. |
| ✅ | **Manage subscriptions** | **Done 17 September 2026.** Settings → *Subscriptions* in the webmail lists every sender whose mail in the last 30, 90 or 365 days carries List-Unsubscribe, one row per From address, most first: the name, the list, how many messages and how many unread, and the newest. *Unsubscribe* asks the newest message of that sender, which names the list’s current way out, through the route a single message already had - RFC 8058’s one-click POST to a public address, or a message to its mailto; a list that offers only a page is a link opened apart with no referrer; *Block* puts the sender on the reader’s own blocked list and *Delete their mail* moves everything from that exact address to Trash after a question. The day a list was left is kept with the account (`unsubscribed`, the newest forty), so a list that keeps writing shows it was asked. Server: `GET /api/v1/me/subscriptions?days=` (1 to 365), reading at most 5,000 message headers newest first and saying so when that was not all of the window; Drafts, Sent, Junk and Trash are not read. `RestApiSelfService.TheSubscriptionsPageListsEachSenderOnce` on both benches, 18 harness checks, 20 texts in all twenty-three catalogues. |
| ✅ | **Brand logos** | **Done 17 September 2026.** The webmail shows the logo a sender’s domain publishes (BIMI) as the avatar of its mail - in the list, the conversation and the open message - and the initials whenever there is none. The brand is named only for mail this server judged `dmarc=pass` and recorded in its own Authentication-Results header, so it needs `AuthenticationResultsEnabled` and the DMARC check on. The logo is served by `GET /api/v1/me/brand-logo?domain=` only when `default._bimi` (at the domain or its organizational domain) is a `v=BIMI1` record with a location, the domain’s DMARC policy is quarantine or reject for all of its mail, the location is https at a public address, and the file is SVG Tiny PS (no script, foreignObject, image, link, animation, style element, event attribute, entity or external reference). One lookup a day per domain; the server fetches, so the brand never sees the reader. A Verified Mark Certificate is not checked, and the page says no more than DMARC proves. Switch: `BrandLogosEnabled` (on). `BrandLogoTester` pins the record, policy and SVG rules; `RestApiBrandLogos` walks five senders through the fake DNS zone (a passing brand refused at the private fetch, `p=none`, no record, a declining record, a spoof); five harness checks. [BrandLogos.md](hmailserver/docs/BrandLogos.md). |
| ✅ | **Contacts in and out** | **Done 15 September 2026.** The Contacts page exports the address book as vCard 3.0 (one card per contact: FN, N, EMAIL) or as CSV with the `Name,E-mail Address` header Outlook reads, and imports either. A vCard file can hold any number of cards: folded lines, escapes and several EMAILs on one card are read. A property marked quoted-printable is skipped rather than decoded, and a grouped one such as `item1.EMAIL` is not read. A CSV is read by its own header row: Google Contacts' `Name`, `Given Name`, `Family Name` and `E-mail 1 - Value`, Outlook's `First Name`, `Last Name` and `E-mail Address`, or a plain `name,email`. Import adds what is not there yet by address and says how many came in. The second number, "already there", is really everything not added, so repeats within the file and addresses the server refused are counted in it. The wiki's client page walks the Google and Outlook exports. Five harness checks, none of them on an Outlook CSV. |
| ✅ | **Snooze, scheduled send and reminders on the phone** | **Done 18 September 2026.** Walked at 390 by 844 pixels in Chromium against a bench with a seeded mailbox, screenshots read: snooze is on the reader's toolbar and its presets (three hours, tomorrow at nine, next week at nine, a date and time) wrap onto two lines and read; the Scheduled page lists what is snoozed and what waits to be sent, each with Cancel; compose is full-screen with Send at and Schedule reachable without scrolling and a scheduled send is confirmed in a sentence; a calendar event's Reminder is a select in a scrolling dialog. Two things the walk found and fixed in the phone stylesheet: the reader's thirteen toolbar controls sat on one 48-pixel line and ran past the right edge, giving the whole page a sideways scroll (they wrap now, and Open in a new window is not offered on a phone); and the message's date sat on top of the sender's address (it drops under the sender). Compose is two taps away, behind the menu, which is reachable but not Gmail's floating button; the search box's placeholder is cut to two letters at this width. Neither is broken; both are noted for the UX audit row. |

### The project

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ⬜ | **Published numbers** | Messages per second in and out, IMAP sessions per core, memory per session, on named hardware, against Postfix and Dovecot on the same machine, with the load generator in the repository so anyone can repeat it. The claim in this file's first line is not one until this row is done. |
| ⬜ | **Windows on ARM** | The Linux port builds for AArch64; the Windows build does not. Visual Studio's ARM64 tools and the same installer. |
| ✅ | **A dedicated low-privilege service account on Windows** | **Built 25 September 2026 on branch `row134-serviceaccount`, with no schema step. Not yet compiled or run against a bench: the fixture and the internal tester run when the branch is folded into a batch and gated, and the live registration is exercised by hand on a throwaway machine.** A new installation runs the service as the virtual account `NT SERVICE\hMailServer`; an upgrade keeps the account it has. The installer's *Service account* page says which applies and offers the other side (`/serviceaccount=virtual|localsystem|keep` silently). The design rests on one rule, sharpened by the review of the first round: nothing the service can write decides what an elevated program does for it. So `hMailServer.INI` is read-only to the service - it holds `[Database]`, `[Directories]` and `[Security]`, which elevated programs read - and the server tolerates that: the settings store's `[Settings]` copy is not refreshed (said once per start, the database the store as ever, a removal with the line still in the file kept stored as the default), and what can only live in the file is refused with a sentence that says what an administrator does instead: `[Directories]`, `[Database]` (the installer does its database step with the service still on LocalSystem and moves it afterwards, which also keeps DBSetup's dependency on a local database server working; DBSetup run later falls back to `sc.exe` as an administrator), `PasswordPepper`, the administrator password (a new elevated `hMailServer.exe /SetAdministratorPassword`, reading standard input) and the administrator's second factor (turned off by editing the file; enrolling needs the service on LocalSystem for the enrolment). `/Register` takes the account from its command line alone - `--service-account virtual|localsystem|keep|DOMAIN\name$` - and never from `ServiceAccountName`, which is retired, along with the password beside it. `hMailServer.exe /GrantServiceAccess` grants Modify on the program folder's own Data, Logs, Temp and Database folders - the program folder found from where the binary is - and on folders an administrator approved in `HKLM\SOFTWARE\hMailServer\ServiceAccessPaths`, a key it refuses to trust if anybody else may write it; Read on the program's own folders (not on the program folder itself, whose entry would walk the message store); the protected DKIM and update files and certificate files inside those folders on the file alone; and the REST API's key store in `Bin`. A folder `[Directories]` or the database names is reported, never granted, until approved after a question the elevated program asks itself (`--confirm`), naming each folder, each key file outside and each refusal; and no folder is granted, approved or not, that is or holds the program folder or is in it beside its four, is near the Windows folder, Program Files or the user profiles, is or holds ProgramData, is a drive's root, a share or a device path, or runs through a link - each folder held open while it is granted, each file granted through a handle with one name only. The installer moves the service only on a grant that ended in 0, asking in an attended installation and not moving in a silent one when something is not reached; the Control Panel's card moves it each way with buttons. The live update is refused under an account that cannot run an installer, and the administrator is sent to the release page and the Authenticode signature, never to the copy in `Data\Updates`; the auto-ban's firewall rules are refused once (HM7460), leftover rules named (HM7461); the SID type is set to unrestricted only for an account other than LocalSystem. The checklist's tenth item, `service_account`, is judged from the server's own token and shown on the Deck's dashboard. `docs/ServiceAccount.md` has the table of every right and what changed, the table of every write to `hMailServer.INI` and what became of it, and the way back. Tested by `ServiceAccountTester` (which folders a grant may reach - a `DataFolder` pointed at `Bin` among them - the plan, the logon for each `--service-account`, the grant itself, a hard link and a symbolic link refused), the installer script checks and the checklist fixture. The row as written: **Decided 22 September 2026: the shared-hosting request stays declined; the service account is to be built.** Discussion #267 asked, in another form, for a portable install under an ordinary account for shared hosting. That stays declined, and the reason is the machine: the secret store is machine-scope DPAPI (`DataProtector.cpp`, `CRYPTPROTECT_LOCAL_MACHINE`), so anyone with administrator rights on the box can read it; the COM registration is machine-wide; the message store is guarded by file ACLs an ordinary user cannot set against the other users of the same machine - and on shared hosting the other tenants are those users. What is right in the request is that the Windows service runs as LocalSystem, the most privileged account on the machine, while parsing untrusted input from the Internet in C++ (section 6's heap-corruption row is the reminder of what that costs); the Linux packages of this same fork already run as their own account with one capability (`hmailserver.service`: `User=hmailserver`, `CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE`, `ProtectSystem=strict`). The mechanism half exists: `hMailServer.exe /Register` honours `ServiceAccountName` in the file's `[Security]` section since the fix that made it apply at all (`hMailServer.cpp`, the `/Register` branch). What the row builds: the per-service virtual account `NT SERVICE\hMailServer` as the default for a NEW installation (no password, no account to create - Windows mints the SID; an upgrade keeps whatever the service runs as, and the installer says so on its page with a way back to LocalSystem); a `/GrantServiceAccess` verb, run elevated by the installer and by a Control Panel button, that grants the service's SID Modify on the data directory, the log folder, the temp and backup directories and the SQL Server Compact database, Read on the program folder, and the crash-dump folder the section-6 row wants; the DCOM access permission on the AppID unchanged (it is about the callers, who stay the administrators); a warning in the setup checklist and on the Deck's dashboard when the service still runs as LocalSystem; and the doc that says what changes for a SQL Server login under integrated authentication (a login for the virtual account on the same machine; a group-managed service account for a remote one), for a backup written to a network share (a virtual account is the computer account on the network), for a custom virus scanner or an event script (they run with less than LocalSystem), and how to go back. Tested by the ACL granter on a temporary directory, the installer script checks and the checklist; the live registration exercised by hand on the second bench, which registers the service from its own build. Thinner than the ask: no ports or firewall rules need granting on Windows - any account may bind any port and the firewall rule is per program - so that part of the original sentence was never needed. |
| ⬜ | **macOS** | The POSIX port should build on macOS with little work; a Homebrew formula makes it a developer's local server. |
| ✅ | **Every build input pinned and named** | **Built 25 September 2026 on branch `row-supplychain-pins`, no schema step: scripts, one checker, the manifest and documents, nothing compiled. Every checker is green, the new checker's self-test passes, the runtime pin's test passes under Windows PowerShell 5.1 and PowerShell 7, and nothing was built or run against a bench.** The gaps the evidence pack's review found on 25 September, each read again in the tree first. **The .NET Desktop Runtime the installer bundles is pinned.** `build/get-dotnet-runtime.ps1` took whatever `aka.ms/dotnet/10.0` pointed at and checked only that it was larger than 40 MB (the bench's copy turned out to be 10.0.11, downloaded 12 August). Now `hmailserver/docs/third-party-binaries.json` pins 10.0.12 (8 September 2026, a security release) under a new kind, `downloaded`: the address on builds.dotnet.microsoft.com and the SHA-512 Microsoft's release metadata publishes, the same from both of its hosts. The script refuses a pin that is not one (no single release, no SHA-512, an address that does not name the release), checks a download before it is put in place and a file already there as well, removes a file that is not the pinned one before it downloads - so a failed run leaves nothing for the installer to package rather than the wrong runtime - and `-Verify` checks without downloading. `build/show-dotnet-runtime-pin.ps1` prints the fields for a chosen release, with its CVEs, and changes nothing; moving the pin is the documented step in ThirdPartyBinaries.md, *Moving the .NET runtime pin*. `build/test-get-dotnet-runtime.ps1` holds the script to it with no network: a loopback listener serves the bytes each case chooses, and a wrong hash is refused, on disk or downloaded, with nothing placed (eleven cases; a copy of the script with the hash check removed fails four). **The SBOM names what it missed.** `build/native-dependencies.ps1` adds the vendored zlib (1.3.1, from `zlib.h`), what the installer carries - 7-Zip, the MariaDB client and SQL Server Compact with the SHA-256 of the archive each is fetched from, the MSVC runtime by version, the .NET runtime with its SHA-512 - and the Linux packages' system libraries, one per library `CMakeLists.txt` finds plus libstdc++, by name and with no version, because they are the distribution's: seventeen components beside Syft's, where there were four. It throws on a manifest component or a CMake library it has no entry for. The merge gives each its hashes (SPDX `checksums`, CycloneDX `hashes`) and an identifier to find it by, and the output is the same under PowerShell 5.1 and 7. **`build/check-sbom-inputs.py`**, pure Python so GitLab's image runs it, reads what the build pins by itself - third-party-binaries.json, the native-dependencies table, the kinds `get-installer-binaries.ps1` places and the build inputs they come from, the entry `get-dotnet-runtime.ps1` pins, what `CMakeLists.txt` finds, every `$(hMailServerLibs)` directory a native project reaches, every source directory with a LICENSE of its own - and fails when one is missing from the SBOM's component list or is there at another version or hash: native-dependencies.ps1's output where PowerShell is on the machine, its tables where it is not, and finished documents with `--sbom`, which the SBOM job now runs in place of its purl search. `--self-test` breaks each rule in a copy (twenty controls). **Still unpinned, each with its reason** (ThirdPartyBinaries.md, *Every build input, and its pin*): the AppImage tools, which `build-appimage.sh` takes from linuxdeploy's moving `continuous` release on GitHub with no hash, the input most in need of a pin and one this project cannot take a hash for while GitHub is closed to it; the Public Suffix List, whose committed generated header is the pin; the container's two packages; and the toolchains, Inno Setup's setup loader among them. The SLSA generator the review named was referenced by tag rather than by hash, in a GitHub workflow that has not run since 18 September. **Stale text:** ASSURANCE-CASE.md said the native server's coverage was unmeasured; it now gives the 73.48% of 15 September, what it measured and what it did not. README's Schema row said 6031 at schema 6100, and `build/check-schema-versions.ps1` now fails when the row and `REQUIRED_DB_VERSION` disagree (its self-test's healthy model, which had failed since the create-script rule was added, is repaired). |
| ✅ | **A Helm chart and a compose file** | **Done 15 September 2026.** `platform/packaging/docker/docker-compose.yml` (the server beside PostgreSQL, secrets from files, the REST listener over TLS from a mounted certificate, the image's own health check) and `platform/packaging/helm/hmailserver` (a StatefulSet of one with its three volumes, a LoadBalancer with the mail ports and the REST port, the two passwords as a Secret or an existing one, the REST certificate from a `kubernetes.io/tls` Secret, probes on port 25 or on the metrics listener's `/livez` and `/readyz`; the database pointed at, never bundled). Neither tool is on the bench, so `.github/workflows/packaging.yml` lints and renders the chart and resolves the Compose file on every change. |
| ⬜ | **The release chain off a single forge** | GitHub suspended the project's account on 18 September 2026, and three things a release depends on still name it. (1) Installed servers look for updates at `https://api.github.com/repos/Progressiverobot/hmailserver/releases` (`UpdateChecker.cpp`), which has answered 404 since; the Control Panel and the Deck say the check failed, and nothing else breaks. (2) An update is accepted only when its Sigstore bundle names the GitHub Actions workflow `sign-release.yml` of that repository and GitHub's token issuer (`SigstoreVerifier.cpp`), which nothing but GitHub can issue, so no release made anywhere else can be installed by an installed server's live update. (3) The release itself - Authenticode signing through Azure Artifact Signing, the Sigstore bundles, the provenance attestations, the Linux packages - is built by GitHub Actions workflows. The row: a feed on a hostname the project owns, serving the JSON shape existing servers already read, so that servers from 6.2.28 to 6.3.3 can be pointed at it with their per-server feed setting; a trust list rather than one pinned identity, keeping the GitHub identity for the assets already published and adding the new signer; the signing and packaging run from GitLab CI or on a machine the project controls; the package manifests (winget, Chocolatey, the PKGBUILD, the Helm chart, the container image on ghcr.io) and `build/get-installer-binaries.ps1`'s build inputs fetched from GitLab; the tests that pin the GitHub strings; and RELEASE.md's runbook. Until it is done, releases can be re-hosted but not cut. Every release up to 6.3.3 is re-published on GitLab, byte for byte, with its original signatures (24 September 2026). The Linux build and the Linux suite are no longer this row's: since 25 September 2026 they run in GitLab CI on the project's own runner, beside the whole Windows suite on the second bench (the row below, and `docs/ContinuousIntegration.md`), and so, from the same day, does the making and checking of what a release carries: the `.deb` and the `.rpm` (made by the Linux build job and installed in clean Ubuntu 26.04 and Fedora 44 containers, the `.deb` started and asked for the Control Deck), the container image (built from the `.deb` with kaniko and inspected, not pushed) and the SPDX and CycloneDX SBOMs (kept as a job's artifact). What stays here is signing; publishing - the packages and the SBOMs to the release, the image to a registry, the winget and Chocolatey manifests rendered from the signed installer; the AppImage, whose build tool is published only on GitHub; and the feed. A `v*` tag's pipeline ends in a manual `release` job that says so and fails. |
| ✅ | **Forge-neutral text in the product** | **Built 25 September 2026 on branch `row138-forge`, no schema. Not yet compiled or run against a bench: written in a worktree beside four other rows, it is compiled, built and gated when the branch is folded.** Nothing the server, the Control Panel or the help pages show sends a reader to GitHub any more; what still names it is the live update's default trust, which is the release-chain row's. The Control Panel's About link and the stalled-mail view's guide link point at the GitLab project, and the guide button's accessible name says it opens a web browser instead of naming a forge. The `Policy:` line of the security.txt the server serves names `SECURITY.md` at the repository root on GitLab, as the repository's own `.well-known/security.txt` does, and `Security/SecurityTxt.cs` asserts the new line. The update-feed field is "Update feed URL (empty = the built-in feed; set for a mirror)", with a placeholder on example.net, in all seventeen languages, and the settings search index is regenerated. The help pages: DiagnosingStalledMail.md links `SUPPORT.md` at the root; a GitHub discussion and a GitHub issue that were links to pages that answer 404 are named in words, the convention CONTRIBUTING.md sets; Fuzzing.md says the nightly job stopped with the account; ThirdPartyBinaries.md is rewritten to what the tree and `get-installer-binaries.ps1` say - no executable binary committed since 11 September 2026, thirty-five build inputs fetched, gathered, copied from Windows or generated, and how to take the `build-inputs-1` assets from their GitLab copy while the script still points at GitHub (CONTRIBUTING.md and SECURITY.md say the same); and RegulatoryScope.md records the installer as Authenticode-signed since 6.3.1 and says what the SBOMs, both signatures and the provenance check now depend on - GitHub Actions workflows that cannot run - pointing at the release-chain row above instead of solving it. The systemd units' `Documentation=` and the Linux packages' homepage point at GitLab too. Left as they are, each for a reason: the update feed and the Sigstore identity (`UpdateChecker.cpp`, `SigstoreVerifier.cpp`, the two signing-trust fields that show their GitHub defaults, the IDL's description of the check, and their tests), the package manifests (winget, Chocolatey, the PKGBUILD, the Helm chart) and the documents that describe the Linux build, its suite and the package-manager workflow as `.github/workflows/` jobs - all the release-chain row's; other projects that live on GitHub (LLVM, linuxdeploy, pyspf, upstream hMailServer, .NET); and history. |
| ✅ | **Checks on GitLab CI** | **Built 25 September 2026 on branch `row138-forge`, no schema. GitLab's lint API answers it valid, with its three jobs, and every job's script was run under Git Bash on the tree, with negative controls for the checkers' and the sign-off's failure paths; it has not run on a GitLab runner, because nothing was pushed. Extended 25 September 2026 on branch `ci-pipeline`, no schema: the Linux build and suite and the Windows suite on the project's own runners. The lint API answers the extended file valid; no job has run on either runner yet, and every duration is an estimate. Extended again 25 September 2026 on branch `ci-port-rest`, no schema: the rest of the old GitHub workflows, ported job by job, with nothing fetched from GitHub. The lint API answers the file valid with no warnings, and its dry runs give `master` and `v6.3.3` every new job and a pipeline with `HM_PIPELINE=fuzz` `linux-fuzz` alone; every new Linux job's script was run on linix in the job's own image, and passed.** A first `.gitlab-ci.yml`, one stage, a pipeline for a merge request or, without one, for the branch (master included), none for a tag, every job interruptible. *checkers* (python:3.13-slim-trixie, git installed) runs every `build/check-*.py` but `check-package-manifests.py`, the seven `--self-test` modes, `add-license-headers.py --check`, the six generators' `--check`, `generate-portal-languages.py` and `check-deck-parity.py` held to their files staying as committed, `check-linux-version-stamp.sh`, and a last "nothing rewrote anything" diff, and names every failure at the end instead of stopping at the first. *harnesses* (node:22-trixie-slim, with Python, OpenSSL and GnuPG) runs the Deck's and the webmail's script harnesses and the S/MIME and OpenPGP checks both ways. *sign-off*, on a merge request from a fork only, fails unless every commit from the base to the head carries a `Signed-off-by` trailer with its author's email, which is what CONTRIBUTING.md asks of outside contributors. Every script is started through `python3`, `node` or `bash`, never its shebang. Each checker was read for Windows assumptions and run under an audit of every path it opened (a case or separator that only NTFS forgives): none has one - `check-project-items.py`'s case-folding of MSBuild paths is deliberate - and `check-deck-script.py` and `check-portal-script.py` run under Python 3.10. CONTRIBUTING.md says what CI runs and what stays by hand, and the sentences that said no CI runs on GitLab are corrected (README.md, SECURITY.md, the merge-request template, and the help pages that named the GitHub style workflow). Estimated at about six compute minutes a pipeline from the scripts' run time here - not yet measured on a runner - against GitLab's free 400 a month. **The project's own runners**, for a protected ref only (`master`, `batch*`, `v*`) and never for a merge request: linix (the lab VM, docker executor) runs *linux-build* - the server with clang, every `*-tests` program, the OpenPGP writer against GnuPG - and *linux-suite*, the whole of `LinuxRegressionTests` against that binary on a pinned PostgreSQL 16 service, laid out as the VM's bench lays it out, published as a JUnit test report; bench150 (the second Windows bench, shell executor, LocalSystem) runs *windows-gate*, the whole Windows suite in one run in `C:\bench\hmailserver`, which its service is registered from, by `build/ci/windows-gate.ps1` - remote-gate.ps1's steps and exit codes versioned in the tree - under a gate lock (`C:\bench\gate.lock`, `build/ci/gate-lock.ps1`) that the maintainer's split gate takes too, with `HM_GATE_WHERE` for a subset. The Linux jobs' image is built on linix by hand from `build/ci/linux.Dockerfile` under a tag made from the file's hash, which `build/check-ci-image.py` holds the CI file to, because a job that builds images would need the host's Docker socket. The runners are `ref_protected`, the jobs' rules demand a protected ref that is not a merge request's, and the workflow rules keep one pipeline per change, so a fork's code never reaches either machine. `docs/ContinuousIntegration.md` has the runners, the image, the lock, LocalSystem's differences and the estimated durations (about three hours a protected pipeline, the Windows gate the longest). **The port of the rest**: on the shared runners, *editorconfig* (Alpine's editorconfig-checker, with a negative control) for every pipeline, and, when their files change, *chart-and-compose* (Alpine's Helm and Compose), *update-helper* (the update helper against fakes under dash, the units under `systemd-analyze`), *package-templates* (the winget and Chocolatey templates, offline) and *roadmap* (`check-roadmap.ps1`); on linix for a protected ref, *linux-build* makes the `.deb` and the `.rpm` after the version-stamp check, *linux-package-deb* and *-rpm* install them in clean Ubuntu 26.04 and Fedora 44 containers and start the packaged server as its unit would, *linux-image* builds the container image with kaniko and does not push it, *linux-db-upgrade-pgsql* and *-mysql* run the schema-upgrade gate on PostgreSQL 16 and MariaDB 11, *linux-cross* compiles every core file for AArch64, *linux-sbom* makes both SBOMs, and *linux-sanitize* runs `snmp-tests` under AddressSanitizer once the SNMP row's `HM_SANITIZE` exists and says it skipped until then; on bench150, *windows-dotnet* builds the Control Panel and the tools with warnings as errors, tests them, checks the settings search index and runs `dotnet format`, in its own checkout and under the gate lock; and a pipeline schedule the owner creates runs *linux-fuzz*, the MIME harnesses built with clang++ and libFuzzer on Linux, nightly, on a corpus the runner's cache carries. The CI image gained clang's sanitizer runtimes, llvm-symbolizer, the AArch64 cross GCC, rpm, the MariaDB client and library, PowerShell and Syft. Not ported, with the reason in the document: CodeQL, dependency review, Scorecard and the upstream watch (GitHub's own services), signing, provenance, publishing and the release-time manifests (the row above), the installer smoke test (ported separately), the coverage upload, the GCC and native AArch64 builds, and the AppImage. The runs by hand found four faults, fixed here: GnuPG 2.4.8 on Ubuntu 26.04 refusing the fixture passphrase in the agent that imported the keys (the Linux build's OpenPGP round trip failed); the upgrade gate's rewind leaving later steps' indexes behind; the MySQL 6085 -> 6086 step, which failed on every MySQL and MariaDB database because InnoDB will not drop an index the calendar foreign key uses; and `Charset::ToWideChar` reading an unterminated buffer on Linux when iconv refuses a sequence, which the first Linux fuzz replay found. Still by hand: everything the project's machines run, for an unprotected branch; `check-package-manifests.py` against a released installer, `check-schema-versions.ps1` and `check-db-scripts.ps1`; the GCC build and a build on AArch64 hardware. |

## 8. Declined in the deep dive, with reasons

| | Item | Why not |
|---|---|---|
| ⏸️ | **Apple push for iOS Mail** | Dovecot's XAPPLEPUSHSERVICE needs a certificate only Apple's own server program is issued; without it the extension is a promise the server cannot keep. IMAP IDLE is the honest answer. The webmail raises browser notifications only while its page is open; Web Push for the installed app (section 7) is not built yet. |
| ⏸️ | **Publishing BIMI for our domains** | It is DNS and a Verified Mark Certificate that costs a sender money each year; the server can document the record but there is nothing to ship. |
| ⏸️ | **A Bayesian classifier of our own** | Deferred in Roadmap.md and still right: rspamd and SpamAssassin are integrated and learn; a second learner in the server would be worse than both. |

## 9. End-to-end encryption

Transport is done: TLS everywhere, DANE, MTA-STS, a post-quantum key exchange, and S/MIME in the page since 11 September. What is not done is mail that stays unreadable to the server itself, and to anyone who takes the server. Proton and Tuta built businesses on that; theirs is proprietary in the sense that only their client reads their format. This server's version is built on OpenPGP, so that any client with a key reads the mail and no one is locked in - the standard is the feature. Roadmap.md deferred OpenPGP in the browser on 11 September as "the S/MIME page's design on another format"; it is un-deferred here, as the first row of the four.

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **OpenPGP in the page** | **Built 18 September 2026, schema 6080. Not yet run against a bench: the fixture compiles and the module's own check passes here and on the lab VM with GnuPG 2.4.8, and the regression suite runs when the branch is folded.** What is built: `PortalOpenPgp.js`, RFC 4880 version 4 in the browser on the Web Crypto API, `BigInt` and one AES of its own, **vendoring nothing** - served as `/portal-openpgp.js` and fetched only when a message, the Security page or a send needs it. Keys made (Curve25519 by default, RSA 3072 and 4096 offered) or imported, and kept as an ordinary OpenPGP secret key encrypted under a passphrase this server never sees, so the same bytes open in GnuPG; PGP/MIME (RFC 3156) signed, encrypted, sign-and-encrypted, verified and decrypted, with inline PGP read; a key page with fingerprints in four-character groups, expiry, publishing to this server's own Web Key Directory, and revocation - the certificate made with the key and saved at once, because it is the only thing that withdraws a key whose passphrase is lost; Autocrypt Level 1 written on every message from an address that has a key and read on every message opened, a first sighting kept at once and a key that would REPLACE one already held waiting behind a button with both fingerprints shown; and `GET /api/v1/me/openpgp/lookup`, which fetches a correspondent's key from their own domain's directory (advanced then direct, through the client that refuses every address inside this network) and then, only where an administrator turned it on, from the keyserver - and stores nothing, because the person decides. The ring is `hm_pgpring`, in the backup, with every field of a row read out of the KEY rather than the request, and a secret key whose private numbers are in the clear refused rather than stored. Five settings under `/api/v1/settings/openpgp`; `portal_enabled` on, `lookup_wkd` on, `lookup_keyserver` OFF. Proved by `build/check-portal-openpgp.js`, which runs the module against artefacts GnuPG 2.4.8 made (committed under `build/fixtures/openpgp/`, so CI needs no GnuPG) and, where it finds `gpg`, hands what the module made back to it: 91 checks, both directions, green on the lab VM. **What is NOT built: version 5 and version 6 keys and signatures, AEAD, DSA and ElGamal, the NIST and Brainpool curves, 3DES and the other legacy ciphers, BZip2, writing anything compressed, writing inline PGP, smartcards, and the web of trust** - each refused by name rather than mis-read - and a draft or a scheduled send cannot be sealed, because the draft route is given the plaintext. There is no Control Panel page and no COM member: this is the account's own key in the account's own browser. |
| ✅ | **The server publishes its users' keys** | **Built 16 September 2026, schema 6050; gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** What is built: a store for OpenPGP PUBLIC keys, one row per key and more than one key an account (`OpenPGPKeysPerAccount`, 8), so that a key being rotated and the key replacing it are both findable while correspondents catch up - carried by the backup, on the audit trail, and holding nothing secret. A reader of its own (`Common/Util/OpenPgpKey`, with `OpenPgpKeyTester` in `ClassTester`) that walks RFC 9580 packet framing, requires the shape of a transferable public key and the packets to add up, computes the fingerprint the standard way for version 4, 5 and 6 - a version 6 key is read, not refused - and refuses a SECRET key, a keyring, a trust packet, a version 3 key, a key with no user id or no self-signature, a truncated key and anything past 64 KiB, each with a sentence. The serving, `draft-koch-openpgp-webkey-service`, on the web services listener: the ADVANCED method under `/.well-known/openpgpkey/<domain>/hu/<hashed local part>`, the DIRECT method under `/.well-known/openpgpkey/hu/<hash>`, the policy file both require (empty, because every flag it can carry is about a submission service this server has none of), the binary key with the CORS header, over HTTPS only, and a 404 that is byte for byte identical for an address nobody has, an address whose owner published no key, a switch that is off and a domain this server does not host. Three switches, all OFF: the server's `OpenPGPWebKeyDirectoryEnabled`, the domain's `openpgp_publish` and the account's own - plus `openpgp_publish_new_accounts`, which decides only what an account created from now on starts with and never touches one that exists, and which a RESTORE is excluded from. An upgrade publishes nobody. The surfaces: COM (4 Settings, 2 Domain, 2 Account, 2 Utilities members), ten REST routes with the four self-service ones (`/api/v1/me/openpgp`, including the publish switch, because holding a key is not the same decision as telling the internet about it), a Control Deck page per domain with the two domain switches and the settings group, `hmctl openpgp` with five verbs, four PowerShell cmdlets, and `docs/OpenPGP.md`. Thirty regression fixtures, whose key vectors and fingerprints came from GnuPG 2.4.7 and whose hashes came from hashlib - the draft's own `Joe.Doe` → `iy9q119eutrkn8s1mk4r39qejnbu3n5q` among them. **What is NOT built: no encryption or decryption anywhere.** Nothing here signs, verifies, encrypts or decrypts an OpenPGP message; that is the row above this one, in the page. Nor is there key submission by mail, a keyserver in either direction, filtering of the served key's user ids, `OPENPGPKEY` DNS records, revocation handling, or a desktop Control Panel page. All thirty tests ran green in that gate. The row as written: Web Key Directory for every domain the server hosts: `/.well-known/openpgpkey/` on the web-services listener, advanced method, so that any client on the internet finds a user's key from the address alone. The keys are the ones made in the page or uploaded by an administrator for a client that keeps its own. |
| ✅ | **Zero-access mailboxes** | **Built 18 September 2026, schema 6083. Not yet run against a bench: the fixture compiles, the writer is proved against GnuPG 2.4.8 (the Linux build's `openpgp-writer`, run here and on every Linux CI run) and the page's checks pass; the regression suite runs when the branch is folded.** What is built: opt-in per account, the Proton model. `Common/Util/OpenPgpWriter` is the one OpenPGP message this server writes - a PKESK to an RSA key (RSAES-PKCS1-v1_5) or a Curve25519 key (X25519, the RFC 6637 KDF and AES key wrap) and a version 1 SEIPD with AES-256 in OpenPGP CFB and the MDC over a literal packet - and `ZeroAccess::SealFile` runs it in `LocalDelivery` after the account's spam overrides, the rules, the forward and the Sieve script and just before the row is saved: the account's copy is rewritten as a PGP/MIME message whose outer headers are the original's less the `Content-*` fields (the headers the server kept), the plaintext overwritten in place before the sealed file takes its name (and before it is removed when a seal fails), and a failure refuses the delivery rather than storing in the clear; the Sent copy of a message sent from the webmail is sealed the same way, and the doc and the page say in one sentence what is not - drafts, and what a mail client uploads. The key is one of the account's own in the ring; `hm_zeroaccess` holds two more copies of its secret half sealed in the browser - under 64 hex characters of **Argon2id** over the password (RFC 9106's t=3, m=64 MiB, p=4; BLAKE2b and the three Argon2 variants written in `PortalOpenPgp.js` on 32-bit halves, held to RFC 7693's and RFC 9106's vectors; 1.6 s in Node on the development machine, not measured on a phone) and under a **recovery key** of 30 random octets shown as eight groups of six base32 characters, which the page makes the person type back, with the sentence about a lost password ticked, before it stores anything. A key that expires is refused as the mailbox key at the switch-on, and the ring refuses to remove or revoke the key while the switch is on, because either would have every delivery refused from that day. Sign-in derives and opens the key with nothing typed; a password change through the page carries the key sealed again in the same request and the server refuses one without it; a password an administrator reset is met at the next sign-in by the dialogue that takes the previous password or the recovery key and seals the key under the current one. The server refuses by name: the administrator's message source and export answer 403, the ring's DELETE of the mailbox key 409, the opt-in 403 until `ZeroAccessAllowed` (off) is on; search indexes the headers and the armour, IMAP and POP hand out the ciphertext. Read-only to an administrator everywhere: COM `Account.ZeroAccess`, REST `zero_access`, a line in the Control Panel's account dialog, a card in the Deck's. `docs/ZeroAccess.md` says what it costs in the row's words, and what is true instead of "the plaintext never touches the disk". The adversarial review of 22 September found and the branch fixed: the expiring key, the Sent copy and the page's overclaim, the Argon2 reference index computed in double precision (exact now, and held to the reference implementation at 64 MiB - the effect had been one derivation in some millions, not one in fifty, because the second division hides most of the first's rounding), a revocation the ring's PUT let through, the public-folder and quarantine copies the doc had not named, the plaintext deleted unscrubbed on a failed seal, the switch on with no row shown as off, and the four claims with no test. Tests: `OpenPgpWriterTester` (with a manufactured expiring key), 15 checks in `check-portal-openpgp.js` (the RFC vectors, the 64 MiB reference, the server-sealed fixture opened by the page and by gpg), 27 in `portal-script-test.js`, a CI step that hands the writer's output to gpg, `SMTP/ZeroAccess.cs` (thirteen tests) and a backup test. Not built: a local bridge for IMAP and POP, search over the decrypted text in the browser, sealing mail already in the mailbox when the switch is thrown. |
| ✅ | **A password-protected message to anyone** | **Built 18 September 2026, schema 6082 (the step written 6080 to 6082 in its branch; the integrator renumbers it when the row holding 6081 lands first). Not yet run against a bench: the fixture compiles, the page harness and the module's GnuPG check pass here, and the regression suite runs when the branch is folded.** What is built: a **Protect with a password** control on the compose form - the password typed twice with a strength sentence, never sent anywhere, and a hint - under which the page builds the text and the attachments into one MIME message and encrypts it to the password as ONE OpenPGP message (a symmetric-key encrypted session key and an integrity-protected body: the shape `gpg -c` writes), then hands this server the armour in chunks; the server files it under `<DataDirectory>/Protected/` with a row in `hm_protectedmessages`, and what goes out by SMTP is a SHORT notice it writes itself - a sentence, the hint and the link `/portal/protected/<token>` - with a copy in Sent that the reader marks with whether the link was opened. The reading page takes no session: it fetches the ciphertext from `GET /api/v1/protected/<token>` (the 128-bit base64url token is the whole access control, rate-limited per address with loopback exempt, and a wrong token a plain 404 that says nothing and counts for nothing else, since a page fetches its link on load and an old link is such a token), asks for the password, decrypts in the browser, shows the text, offers each attachment to save, and takes a reply that is encrypted under the same password and filed under the sender with an internal notice. The server files only what has the SHAPE of an OpenPGP message to a passphrase - session keys, then one integrity-protected body, nothing else; a literal packet between them is discarded with the reason - and the page sends no file as a link while a message is protected, so the plaintext never rests here; the compose form, the reading page and the doc say in one sentence that it does not stop the recipient copying what they read, and the form says that the Sent folder keeps the notice alone. 25 MB a message, 250 MB and a thousand live an account, 1 MB and twenty replies, a second chunk for one offset refused under the lock, `protected_message_days` (30) under the OpenPGP settings, an app password refused on every route, a draft refused. Proved by `API/RestApiProtectedMessages`, 31 checks in `build/portal-script-test.js` (the reading page run with the real modules) and the symmetric half of `build/check-portal-openpgp.js` against `gpg -c` both ways. The row as written: For the correspondent with no key: the sender sets a password, the page encrypts the body and attachments (OpenPGP, symmetric) and sends a short message with a link to a reading page on this server; the recipient enters the password there and the browser decrypts; a reply written on that page goes back the same way. End to end, because the plaintext is never in the message that crosses the internet and never on this server; unlike "confidential mode", it promises only what it can keep. |
| ✅ | **Trust made visible** | **Built 18 September 2026, schema 6081.** What is built: the ring remembers a correspondent's key from the first time it is seen, and a DIFFERENT key arriving for an address already held - a lookup that answered another fingerprint, a key kept from a message's Autocrypt header, an import over the one held - is stored as a change: `changed_from` names the key it replaced, `changed_at` when, and until the person accepts it (`PUT /api/v1/me/openpgp/keys/{id}/accepted`) the page does not encrypt to it - a send stops and says why - and a signature it made is shown with the warning that names both fingerprints, "This correspondent's key changed on <date>. It was <old>; it is now <new>.", in the reader and on the keys page, with Accept beside it. Every fingerprint is shown in groups of four, the person's own included, and a correspondent's key can be marked verified in person (`PUT .../keys/{id}/verified`; the account's own session and never an app password's - and neither are the four writes to the ring itself, or the refusal would be decorative - 404 for another account's row, 400 for an own key), after which every good signature by it shows the green mark beside the sender; a good signature by an unverified key shows the plain verdict and the fingerprint; the mark is never inherited by a key that replaced a verified one, and verifying that key accepts the change in the same act. The five columns (`pgrverified`, `pgrverifiedat`, `pgrchangedfrom`, `pgrchangedat`, `pgraccepted`) are the ring's own and never the request's, travel in the backup, and every key's JSON answers `verified`, `verified_at` and - on a row that replaced another - `changed_from`, `changed_at` and `accepted`. A correspondent's key arriving for an address the account holds its OWN key for is a change of the own key - the page seals every message to the sender's own key, so a stranger's key under that address in silence would have every message sealed to the stranger - and the page never fills the account's address in for a correspondent's key; a key comes back to the address it was filed under, whatever the request names. Proved by six tests in `API/RestApiOpenPgpKeys` (the first key remembered and a change recorded, refused for a key that replaced none; the mark needing the account's own session, with the four ring writes refused on an app password; the mark not inherited; a key for the own address a change of the own key and a key with no address refused; a key coming back to its address; a pending key counting when no settled one is left) and twenty-three checks in `build/portal-script-test.js` (the three states on the rows and in the reader, the warning naming both fingerprints, the send that stops, Accept and the mark from the list and from the reader). Not run against a bench: the fixture compiles and the harness passes. What is NOT built: the web of trust, and any use of the mark by the server, which still signs, verifies, encrypts and decrypts nothing. Twelve strings in the twenty-three catalogues. |
| ✅ | **The webmail shell compressed on the wire** | **Built 16 September 2026 on branch portal-compression; gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** Both HTTP listeners answer `Accept-Encoding` with `gzip` or `deflate` (the zlib format, as RFC 9110 means it) on text, JSON, XML, a manifest and a message file, between a floor of 1 KB and a ceiling of 4 MB, never on a download or an image, with `Vary: Accept-Encoding` and a `Content-Length` that is the bytes sent; the page's script, its S/MIME module, its manifest and its service worker are joined and compressed once. Measured at zlib level 6: `Portal.js` 349,442 bytes to 90,595, `Portal.html` 102,792 to 23,196, `PortalSmime.js` 61,457 to 16,860 - the shell from 513,691 to 130,651, a quarter, before the OpenPGP bundle the rows above will add. `hmailserver/docs/ResponseCompression.md` says the rules and why BREACH does not reach a server with no CSRF token and a `SameSite=Strict` cookie. `API/RestApiCompression` decompresses the answers and compares them byte for byte with the plain ones; `HttpCompressionTester` proves the negotiation at the function. |

## 10. The second sweep: product by product, feature by feature

Gmail and Google Workspace, Outlook.com and Microsoft 365 with Exchange behind it, Proton Mail, Fastmail, Tuta, Zoho, Zimbra, Stalwart, mailcow and iRedMail, MDaemon, Kerio and Axigen, each walked through its own feature list and settings pages, and every item below checked against Roadmap.md and the page's own script before it was written down. Where a name is given in brackets it is the product that people know the feature from. Sorted by who feels the gap.

### Normal users: reading and writing mail

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **A message that expires** (Gmail's Confidential Mode) | **Done 17 September 2026.** The sender sets an expiry on the compose form - an hour, a day, a week, a month - and, if they want, a passcode; the message is not sent. The full message is kept under `<data directory>/Confidential/<account id>/` with a row in `hm_confidential` (schema 6069), each recipient gets a short notice with a link to `/confidential/<token>` under `ConfidentialBaseUrl` (falling back to the digest's and the import sign-in's base URL; with none set the option is not offered and the route refuses), and the sender's Sent folder keeps the full message. The page takes no credentials: a browser signed in to the webmail as a recipient reads at once (the account's own session, not a support session), any other proves itself with a six-digit code mailed to one of the recipient addresses (five tries a code, ten minutes, one a minute and five a day per address, six an hour per message, thirty wrong in all close the code path until the sender sends it again; a stranger's address gets the same page, status and limits, and no mail) or with the passcode (ten wrong pause it; stored as PBKDF2), and then holds a half-hour cookie scoped to the message's path; a wrong proof counts against the source address like a failed logon. Text only, HTML-escaped, images inline as data, a policy that runs nothing and frames nowhere, no-store, no referrer, a print stylesheet that hides everything, nothing to download; the first opening is recorded with who. `GET /api/v1/me/confidential` lists, `POST .../{id}/revoke` withdraws; expiry or withdrawal deletes the kept file (the minute task and the retention sweep; a failed delete is retried while the row names the file, nothing is touched while the database does not answer, and an orphan directory is decided by a query), the row stays thirty days. The kept copy counts against the mailbox's quota and an account has at most 1000 outstanding; an `expires_in` that is not a whole number of seconds is refused, never read as no expiry; a message that expires cannot be scheduled with Send at. *Messages that expire* in the webmail, on the Sent folder's menu and the palette; 9 harness checks; `RestApiConfidential`: the notice without the text, the Sent copy with it, the stranger refused, the code, the passcode, who opened it, revoke and expiry ending the page and the file, the refusals. **Said in the product as plainly as here:** beside the option, in the API's description and in [ConfidentialMessages.md](hmailserver/docs/ConfidentialMessages.md) - not end-to-end encryption, and no protection against a photograph; it stops a message being forwarded on by accident or found in a mailbox years later. The password-protected message in section 9 stays the end-to-end one. The row as written: The sender sets an expiry - an hour, a day, a week - and the message stops being readable when it passes. The body and the attachments are kept on this server rather than sent: what crosses the internet is a short notice with a link, the reader signs in (an account here, or a one-time code sent to the address, or a passcode the sender gives them another way), and the page shows the message with no download, no forward and no copy button, and no print stylesheet. The sender can revoke it before it expires, and sees when it was first opened. Where the recipient is on this server, the same expiry can be enforced in their mailbox rather than through a link - not done: the link is the one path, so that what the sender sees (opened, by whom) is true for every recipient alike. |
| ✅ | **Attachment reminder** (Gmail, Outlook) | **Landed 14 September (#259).** "attached", "see the attachment", "enclosed" in the body and nothing attached: one question before it sends. The compose form has the words in front of it already; it only has to read them. |
| ✅ | **@mentions in the body** (Outlook) | **Done 15 September 2026, in the plain text editor.** An @ followed by letters at the caret asks the address book as the To field does; the person chosen is written as @Name where the @ was and added to To if not there; a message whose subject or first line mentions the reader - @ and their first name, or the mailbox's name - carries an @ mark in the list. The name is text, not a link: a plain-text message has no links, and the formatted editor is not taught this yet. Two harness checks. |
| ✅ | **Shift-click and Ctrl-click in the list** (every desktop client) | **Landed 14 September (#259).** A shift-click on a row or on its box ticks every row from the last one clicked (or ticked with `x`) to this one, and a Ctrl-click (Cmd on a Mac) ticks or unticks the one row without opening it; a conversation row ticks every message of the conversation, and a plain click still opens the message. In the page's shortcut list and docs/WebmailShortcuts.md; six harness checks. The row as written: The list has one way to select several rows - the boxes, one at a time. Shift-click for a range, Ctrl-click to add one, as the folder page and every file manager does. |
| ✅ | **Nudges** (Gmail) | **Landed 14 September (#262):** "Received N days ago. Reply?" from the listing's own fields, and "Sent N days ago. Follow up?" on a sent message nothing answered, asked of the server once per Sent view through the new `in_reply_to:` operator; a `nudges` preference turns them off. The row as written: "Sent 3 days ago, no reply" on a message the reader sent that got none, and "received 3 days ago, not answered" on one that asked a question - a heuristic on dates and the answered flag, no learning. |
| ✅ | **Follow-up flags with a date, and a reminder** (Outlook) | **Landed 14 September (#262):** the star plus `$FollowUp` and a `$Due-YYYY-MM-DD` keyword on the message itself (the flags route takes any IMAP atom, so other clients still see a flag and a move keeps the date), today, tomorrow, next week or a date from the toolbar, a due badge on the row, the Starred view sorted soonest first, and a reminder once a day at sign-in and when the day turns. The row as written: A flag is on or off today. A flag with a due date, a Flagged view sorted by it, and a notification when it comes due; the date is the `$FollowUp` keyword plus a date the page keeps, so other clients still see a flag. |
| ✅ | **Quick steps** (Outlook) | **Landed 14 September (#262):** defined on a Settings card and kept in the preferences (`qs.<slug>`), as buttons over the list and on the digit keys 1 to 9; move, mark read, label, forward, in one press. The row as written: One button the reader defines: move to a folder, mark read, label, forward to an address, in one press, with a keyboard shortcut of its own. |
| ✅ | **Clean up conversation** (Outlook) | **Landed 14 September (#262):** from the row's menu and the message's, deleting the messages whose every line is quoted in a later message of the conversation - the newest, the unread, the starred and those with attachments kept - with Undo. The row as written: Delete the messages of a conversation whose whole text is quoted in a later one, so a thread of twenty is the three that said something. |
| ✅ | **Message pop-out** (Outlook, Gmail) | **Landed 14 September (#259).** Open a message, or the compose form, in its own window, so a person can read one while writing another. |
| ✅ | **Search operators the two have and this one lacks** | **All thirteen landed 14 September** - `cc:`, `bcc:`, `filename:`, `larger:`, `smaller:`, `older_than:`, `newer_than:`, `is:muted`, `is:pinned`, `category:`, `-word` and `OR` (#259), then `has:link` (#262: an `http` or `https` address in the text, or an `href` in the HTML, so a remote image alone is not a link) - each with a test; and `in_reply_to:<message-id>` besides, which the nudges ask. The row as written: `cc:`, `bcc:`, `filename:`, `larger:10M`, `smaller:`, `older_than:7d`, `newer_than:`, `is:muted`, `is:pinned`, `category:promotions`, `has:link`, `-word` to exclude, `OR`. The operators today: from, to, subject, has:attachment, before, after, in, is:unread/read/flagged/unflagged/answered, label. Each new one is a line in the server's search and a line in the options panel. |
| ✅ | **Search history and suggestions** (Gmail) | **Landed 14 September** - the history (#259), then a contact's name, or `from:`/`to:` and a name, completing to `from:<address>` under the box from the address book (#262). The row as written: The last searches offered under the box as it is focused, and a contact's name completing to `from:` as it is typed. |
| ✅ | **Saved searches** | Done 15 September 2026; section 7. |
| ✅ | **Recover deleted items** (Outlook) | **Done 17 September 2026.** The store the row asked for first: with `recover_deleted_days` set (0, off, by default; at most 365) a message a person deletes - IMAP EXPUNGE, UID EXPUNGE and CLOSE, a POP3 DELE at QUIT, the webmail's delete and emptying of a folder, the person's and the administrator's retention - is copied to `<data directory>/Recoverable/<account id>/` with a row in `hm_recoverable` (schema 6068: folder, subject, sender, size, flags, when) before `Messages::DeleteMessages` removes it. Only a delete made inside `RecoverableStore::Scope` is kept, and those scopes are the places a person deletes mail, so an IMAP MOVE, a sent scheduled draft, the delivery queue and an account deletion leave nothing behind. `/api/v1/me/recoverable` lists, restores into the original folder (or one of the same path, or the INBOX) with the flags and a notification to every session, and deletes for good; the six-hourly retention task ends what the window no longer covers and the directories of accounts that are gone. The webmail's *Recover deleted items* page, reached from the Trash folder's menu and the palette. Not in the backup (it carries the mailboxes). `RestApiRecoverDeleted`: an expunge restored with its flags, a MOVE not listed, a POP3 delete kept and deleted for good, nothing kept when off, the sweep ending a copy and its file; 4 harness checks. [RecoverDeleted.md](hmailserver/docs/RecoverDeleted.md). |
| ✅ | **Auto-delete and auto-archive the reader chooses** (Proton, Outlook) | **Built 16 September 2026 on branch `reader-retention` and gated green with batch 11 on the two Windows benches (1,703 + 1,090 tests, the three of this row among them).** Three choices on the webmail's preferences page - *Empty Trash after*, *Empty Junk after*, *Archive Inbox mail after*: never, 7, 30, 90 days or a year - kept with the account as `retention_trash_days`, `retention_junk_days` and `retention_archive_days` in `hm_accountprefs`, the store the page's other choices live in, so no schema step and nothing for an administrator to set. `MailboxRetentionTask`, the six-hourly sweep the administrator's policy runs on (`Utilities.RunMessageRetention` on demand), reads them for every account after the policy: Trash and Junk - the folders carrying the `\Trash` and `\Junk` designation, by attribute or by the names clients use - are emptied of what is older than the choice through the same path an EXPUNGE takes, and Inbox mail older than the choice is copied to the `\Archive` folder (made as a top-level `Archive` if there is none, as the page's own archive does) and then removed from the Inbox, so every open session is told. A value that is not a whole number of days from 1 to 3650 is off, and the page reads a stored value it does not offer as Never. Three tests in `Infrastructure/ReaderRetentionTests` (Trash emptied and Junk untouched, the Inbox archived into a folder that was made for it, a choice that is not days doing nothing) and three checks in the page's harness. The row as written: "Delete Trash and Junk after 30 days", "archive Inbox mail older than a year" as the reader's own settings, run by the server's retention task; today retention is the administrator's, per domain and account. |
| ✅ | **Masked addresses** (Fastmail's Masked Email, Proton's hide-my-email, Apple's Hide My Email) | **Done 17 September 2026.** *Settings, Masked addresses* in the webmail: the person says what an address is for and the server makes one in the account's own domain - a short form of the label, a dot and six random characters from an alphabet without the misread ones - checked against the accounts, aliases, lists and other masked addresses; the page copies it, lists each with how many messages it has brought, and copies, switches off and on, or removes it. RecipientParser finds a masked address after the accounts, aliases and lists (so an administrator's address always wins) and delivers to its owner while it is on, counting it; switched off or removed, mail to it is refused at RCPT as an unknown address, not given to the catch-all. While on it is in the From picker (`kind: masked`), so a reply to mail that came through it goes out from it, and `SMTPConnection::SenderPermittedFor` accepts it for its owner. `reader_masked_addresses_max` (20; 0 takes the page away). Schema 6067 (`hm_maskedaddresses`, carried by the backup); `/api/v1/me/masked-addresses`; `RestApiMaskedAddresses` delivers, sends as, switches off (550 at RCPT and on MAIL FROM) and removes; `BackupAccountStores` restores one and mail to it arrives; MaskedAddressesTester; 5 harness checks. [MaskedAddresses.md](hmailserver/docs/MaskedAddresses.md). |
| ✅ | **Other accounts, fetched** (Gmail’s "check mail from other accounts") | **Done 17 September 2026.** Settings → *Other accounts* in the webmail: a person adds a POP3 or IMAP mailbox to collect into their own - Gmail, Outlook.com, Yahoo Mail and iCloud Mail fill in the server and say what each asks for - chooses whether to leave the mail there and how often to look, ticks *Bring the folders over* for a move (the IMAP mirror), and then collects now, pauses, edits or removes it. Server: `/api/v1/me/fetch-accounts` with the six verbs of the administrator’s route, narrowed: the spam and virus filters are on and no recipients are read from the collected mail whatever is sent (a request asking otherwise is refused by name); the host must resolve to public addresses unless `reader_fetch_accounts_private_addresses` is on; at least 15 minutes between collections; at most `reader_fetch_accounts_max` (3; 0 takes the page away); an account an administrator set up with more than a person can set is shown and collected, not changed. `HttpsClient::HostIsPublic` is the address judgement `RequestPublic` already used. `RestApiReaderFetchAccounts` collects a real message from a POP3 simulator through the page’s route; 14 harness checks. The Microsoft and Google sign-ins are the next row’s. [Migration.md](hmailserver/docs/Migration.md). |
| ✅ | **Send-as an address that is verified** (Gmail) | **Done 17 September 2026.** *Send mail as* on the webmail's Other accounts page: the person adds an address they have at another provider, the server mails a six-digit code to it from the postmaster of the account's domain (null sender), and once the code is typed back the address is in the From picker (`kind: verified`) and accepted in MAIL FROM with `SmtpAuthenticatedSenderCheck` on - `SMTPConnection::SenderPermittedFor` asks `SenderAddresses::IsVerified` after the alias and the post right. The code lives in memory for an hour and is never stored; five wrong tries end it and a new one goes out no sooner than a minute on. An address of a domain on this server is refused (its owner shares it with an alias or the post right), as is one already writable or added, and `reader_sender_addresses_max` (5; 0 takes it away) bounds them. Each address shows the DMARC policy its domain publishes, and the page says plainly that a quarantine or reject policy means mail sent as it from here may not arrive. Schema 6066 (`hm_senderaddresses`, carried by the backup); `/api/v1/me/sender-addresses` with verify, resend and delete; `RestApiSenderAddresses` catches the code at an SMTP server the route points at and proves the rule on both the REST and SMTP paths; `BackupAccountStores` restores an address; 8 harness checks. [SendAs.md](hmailserver/docs/SendAs.md). |
| ✅ | **Signatures with images and layout** (Outlook, Gmail) | **Built 15 September 2026, for the account's signature.** Settings has a formatted signature beside the plain one - bold, italic, underline, a link, and an image from a file - saved as `signature.html` through `PUT /api/v1/me/settings`; the formatted signature goes on formatted messages, replacing the plain block when formatting is switched on, and the plain one stays on plain messages. **One limit for the account's signature as the webmail sets it:** 64 KB (64,000 bytes of UTF-8) for the formatted signature with its images, which fits MySQL's `text` column (65,535 bytes), the narrowest of the four backends, so no schema step. The page says so beside the editor before a file is chosen, draws an image that does not fit in what is left at most 600 by 200 pixels and then smaller until it does, refuses it with the KB still free when even that does not fit, and does not send a signature over the limit. The route refuses one with a sentence (the plain signature keeps its 20,000 characters), and it is a large request now, so the listener's 64 KB cap no longer refuses the request before the route sees it. **The image goes out as a cid: part, not a data: URL:** `InlineImageParts` moves each `data:` PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP that is the whole value of a `src` into an inline part of a `multipart/related` around the HTML, once per image and at most 64 different images a message - what the webmail's view of a message puts back in place, so a later one stays a `data:` URL - on a webmail send and a draft (so a scheduled send and another client show it too), and after `SignatureAdder` or `DisclaimerAdder` append HTML carrying one to an SMTP submission; the domain signature and the disclaimer had the same data: problem, since they are appended verbatim. The page's S/MIME module does the same before it signs or encrypts, since the server cannot change a sealed message. A file an event script adds afterwards (`Message.Attachments.Add`) no longer drops the HTML and its images: `MessageData::CreatePart` carries a related part whole, which also mends the same loss on a message Outlook or Thunderbird sent with an image and an attachment. The Sent copy and a draft show every image in the webmail, and a draft reopened does not attach again the images this webmail made parts (one another mail client embedded still comes back as a file). Tests: `RestApiSignatureImages` (seven: the limit at 64,000 bytes and one byte over beside a 20,000-character plain signature, the delivered message's related part, Content-ID and image bytes with the Sent copy's HTML, the draft, the domain signature over SMTP, a message with nothing to move, a file added through COM to a draft with and without a file, and sixty-five images of which sixty-four become parts; linked into the Linux suite too), `InlineImagePartsTester` in the class tests (the move, the 64, and a file or a text part added afterwards to each shape the move leaves), twelve portal harness checks and six in `check-portal-smime.js`, which pass; [Signatures.md](hmailserver/docs/Signatures.md) is the document. **Not held to the limit:** a signature set through the Control Panel, COM or the administrator's REST route, which on MySQL fails in the database over 65,535 bytes. Per-identity signatures are the identities' own row. **Gated:** `RegressionTests.API.RestApiSignatureImages` and `RegressionTests.Infrastructure.MainOperations.TestInternals` ran green in the batch-10 gate on 16 September 2026. |
| ✅ | **Trackers, counted** (Proton, Apple Mail) | **Done 15 September 2026.** The notice that remote images were not loaded says how many, from how many hosts, and how many look like tracking pixels (a pixel or less, hidden, or named as one - pixel, beacon, open, track); and the sender's habit, the number of their messages seen carrying pixels, kept in this browser and shown on the notice. Two harness checks. |
| ✅ | **Report phishing** (Gmail, Outlook) | **Done 15 September 2026, in the page.** *Report phishing* in the message's menu sends the message's source to the postmaster of the reader's domain as an attachment (with what the page knows of it in the text), files the message as junk, which teaches the filter, and blocks the sender as *Block this sender* does - on the reader's own blocked-sender list since the Safe senders row landed, where it was a Sieve rule - whose toast carries the Undo. A URL list for the SURBL-style check is the server's side and stays a row. Three harness checks. |
| ✅ | **Safe senders** (Outlook) | **Built 15 September 2026 on branch spam-lists, schema 6046; gated green with batch 10 on 16 September 2026.** Settings → *Safe senders* in the webmail: an address or a whole domain, safe or blocked, with a note, kept at `/api/v1/me/sender-list`. Read at delivery for that recipient alone, against the envelope sender and the From header's address: a safe sender's copy skips the spam verdict and the account's own thresholds, a blocked sender's is held in the quarantine where the server holds spam and marked where it does not - never deleted. *Block this sender* and *Report phishing* write to the list now instead of a Sieve rule, which could not cover a domain, could not expire, was invisible to an administrator, and discarded the message when the account had no Junk folder; rules written that way before are left where they are. One table with the domain's and the server's lists below it, the precedence in the next section's row. Eight regression tests (`SenderLists`) and twelve webmail harness checks; see `hmailserver/docs/SenderListsAndDigests.md`. **Not done:** an expiry in the webmail's editor. |
| ✅ | **Conditional formatting of the list** (Outlook) | **Done 15 September 2026.** *Colour rows* on the Filters page: a field (From, To or Cc, Subject, or any of them), a text and a colour, kept in the account's preferences as `row_colours` (twenty at most, fewer with long texts, since a preference value is at most 4,000 characters); the first rule that matches paints the row's leading edge in the list, and a collapsed conversation row takes the colour of its first message that matches. The Cc half of *To or Cc* matches nothing yet: the folder listing carries `to` but no `cc`. Four harness checks. |
| ✅ | **Multiple stars** (Gmail) | **Done 15 September 2026.** The star pressed again cycles: the star, a second in amber, a third in red, then none - the IMAP flag every client shows, with the keywords `$Star2` and `$Star3` on top of it that other clients ignore; the Starred view holds all three. In the list and on the open message. One harness check over the four presses. |
| ✅ | **The star key means two things** | **Built 15 September 2026 on branch star-key; gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** Found while the shortcuts document was being checked against the page: `s` on a row cycled the three stars, but `s` on an open message toggled the IMAP flag alone, so an open message could never be given the second or third star from the keyboard, and a third-starred message toggled off kept `$Star3` under a cleared flag. Now one function gives a message its next star and every way the page stars goes through it - the star on a row, the star over the open message, `s` on either, the row's menu (which now names the star it will give rather than Star or Unstar) and the palette - and it repaints every copy of the message the page holds, so the row's star follows the open message's. *Clear the flag* on a follow-up takes `$Star2` and `$Star3` off with the flag, and a follow-up set on a message that shows no star gives it the first star, not a leftover third. There is no bulk star and no swipe that stars. **The label menu was the one place left where a star could be changed behind the star's back**, and the first report of this row missed it: it listed `$Star2`, `$Star3`, `$FollowUp` and the rest as ordinary labels, and unticking one took the keyword off, left the flag and repainted nothing - a third-starred message dropping to the first star while the page went on painting the third. The page's own `$` keywords are no longer offered there and a label can no longer be named with one, so a star is only ever changed by a star. The presses for one message are sent in turn, each counting from the star the one before it asked for, so a key held down or pressed twice before the first answer comes back gives the second star rather than asking twice for the first and stopping there; and a press whose work throws gives its place in the queue up, where before it left the next press chained to a promise that would never run and that message's star dead for the rest of the session. A row's star is read out as the star the press will give, as the open message's already was - `aria-label` wins over `title`, and a row left at “Star” named the wrong press for every star but the first. The server needed no change: one `PUT /api/v1/me/messages/{id}/flags` already sets the flag and removes several keywords, whatever their case; `OneFlagsRequestTakesTheFlagAndEveryStarKeywordOff` in RestApiSelfService now pins that request. Verified: twenty new harness checks in `build/portal-script-test.js` - the open message cycled none, one, two, three, none with the keywords after every press and a label left alone, its row's star following, a leftover `$Star3` cleaned by the first press, a row cleared from the third star, the row menu's four steps, the star over the open message clicked twice, the palette naming the star it will give, a follow-up set on a message that shows no star, a third-starred follow-up cleared, two presses before the first answer comes back, the label menu offering no `$` keyword, a row's star read out at each of the four stars, and a press whose answer the page cannot use not stopping the star - of which thirteen, and the changed follow-up check with them, fail on the page as it was at cd5384010; all 354 pass now (334 before this row). The harness could not reach the label menu at all until now: its stub gave an element a read-only `id`, which is the one thing `renderLabelMenu` sets, so every attempt to open the menu threw. Each fix was put back in a scratch copy of the page and the check that covers it failed there - the queue one by killing the run with the rejection nobody handles. |
