The trace-based sections count deliveries and failures per domain and per day, the largest mailboxes and who is nearest their quota are read from the store as it is now, and the volume and storage sections come from the server-wide metric history. The two numbers the row was underway for are per domain since schema 6062: `hm_domainsamples` is one row per day and domain with spam and viruses counted by the domain of each local recipient as they happen (`DomainSamples`, fed by the SMTP session, the fetcher and the deliverer) and each domain's store size as sampled once an hour, and the volume and storage sections read it - per domain when a domain is named or the credential is restricted to domains, as a `by_domain` table beside the server-wide form otherwise - so a domain-restricted key is no longer refused them. What stays server-wide, and the index says so, is processed, delivered, deferred and bounced per domain; the traffic section is the per-domain count of deliveries. Tested in `API/RestApiReports.cs`; `docs/Reports.md`. |
| ✅ | **Follow one connection live** (Stalwart's tracing) | **Built 17 September 2026 on branch `row221`, with no schema change; every changed translation unit compiles on its own, the test project builds and every checker passes, and `RegressionTests.API.RestApiSessionFollow` ran green in the batch-14 gate of 18 September 2026 once its accounts were made at the suite's own domain rather than test.com (ee19d5228).** The Deck's Logs page opens with every connection the server has open now - the SMTP, POP3 and IMAP sessions it answers, and the ones it opens itself - and the last 200 that ended, each with id, protocol, peer, port, the account once it signed in, and when it started or ended; and **Follow** shows every line the log carries for one as the server writes it, with Copy for the whole pane. `GET /api/v1/logs/sessions` and `GET /api/v1/logs/sessions/{id}?after=<cursor>&wait=<seconds>` are the routes, under the log's own `view_logs` permission and server-wide. `Logger::Write_`, the chokepoint the syslog sink and the OTLP exporter hang off, copies every entry into a ring of the last 4,096 (`Common/Util/SessionFollow`), each filed under its session: the conversation lines by their session column, the `DEBUG` and `TCPIP` lines by the "session 41" or "Session Id: 41" in their text - the number having to follow a space, so that a token like `session41` quoted out of a request target or an address cannot put a stranger's sentence in front of a follower. A follow is a cursor and not a stream, because `HttpServer` writes a response whole and cannot hold one open for `text/event-stream`: the request for the lines after the cursor waits on the ring's condition variable - twenty seconds at most, and one waiting at a time, since a waiting follow holds one of the listener's four workers and those four are the webmail's as well - and answers the moment the next line lands, so the lines appear as they happen at the cost of one request per batch; a second follow is answered at once and the page pauses a second. The sessions are listed by `TCPConnection` itself - when it starts, after a PROXY header; at the three sign-ins; in its destructor, after its own "Ending session" line - so a conversation that lasted a quarter of a second can still be picked and read. `API/RestApiSessionFollow` opens two IMAP sessions at once, finds each in the list by its account, follows the first from the start and sees its LOGIN and not the password, follows it from the cursor and sees the NOOP it then sends and neither the LOGIN from before the cursor nor the NOOP the second session sent at the same moment, sees it listed as ended after the logout, proves that a waiting follow answers within a couple of seconds of the line rather than at the end of its wait and that a second follow made while it waits is answered at once, and makes two IP ranges whose names differ by one space to show that a log line quoting somebody else's text is filed under no session; the Deck's harness draws the list, follows, continues from the cursor, copies, stops, starts a second follow the stopped one must not pour its lines into, shows a refusal, and feeds the pane 5,200 lines to see the oldest fall out at 5,000. The fixture is linked into `LinuxRegressionTests` too. **Not built, and said in [FollowConnection.md](hmailserver/docs/FollowConnection.md):** nothing survives a restart or the ring; a session whose protocol is not logged shows nothing, and the page names the switch; the HTTP listeners' own requests (REST, the webmail, DAV), ManageSieve and the anti-virus scanner's own socket are not sessions here, since none goes through `TCPConnection` and none of their log lines carries a session id; the IMAP fetcher logs only through DEBUG; past a thousand open sessions the route lists the newest thousand, and the page says so but offers no way to reach the rest; no event-stream form until the listener can stream; no Control Panel page and no `hmctl` verb, the row naming the Deck; and the pane keeps the last 5,000 lines it was sent, the ones before them being in the log file listed under it. |
| ✅ | **syslog** (every Linux administrator's first question) | **Done 15 September 2026.** A copy of every log entry, as RFC 5424, beside the log files rather than instead of them - hooked to `Logger::Write_`, the same chokepoint the OTLP log exporter uses, so it exports exactly what the log mask already produces and an ERROR reaches a collector even when logging is off. `SyslogFormatter` renders the header (priority from facility and severity, version, an RFC 3339 timestamp with a real offset taken from the entry rather than from a second clock reading, hostname, `hMailServer`, the process id, and the log category as the message id - or the `ErrorManager` number, `HM5165`, on an error line that carries one), the structured data where it says something (`thread`, and `session` and `client` on a conversation line, under `hmailserver@32473`, which is the enterprise number IANA reserves for examples because this project has none), and the UTF-8 message behind the byte-order mark RFC 5424 asks for. `SyslogSink` is the transport: UDP truncated to 1024 bytes on a character boundary and never into the header, TCP and TCP over TLS (RFC 5425) octet-counted and truncating nothing, TLS verified by the server's own `CertificateVerifier` against the machine's root store with the host name checked. On Linux under systemd with no collector host configured it writes to the journal over `/run/systemd/journal/socket` in the native protocol, with the same fields plus `HMAILSERVER_SESSION`, `HMAILSERVER_CLIENT`, `HMAILSERVER_CATEGORY` and `HMAILSERVER_MSGID`, so `journalctl -u hmailserver HMAILSERVER_SESSION=41` follows one conversation. **Nothing here can delay or lose a message:** the entry is copied on the logging thread into a queue of 4096 that drops the oldest, every socket is on a worker thread of its own, a stream that will not connect backs off to a minute and is not retried while the back-off holds, and the outage is reported once at the start and once at the end rather than once per failure. Seven settings in `hm_settings` - not in `hMailServer.ini`, per the rule of the same day - with a Control Panel card, twelve COM properties on `IInterfaceLogging` and twelve REST keys under `/api/v1/settings/logging`, which is how it is turned on on Linux, where there is no Control Panel. `RegressionTests/Infrastructure/SyslogExport.cs` stands its own UDP listener up and asserts the header field by field on a real datagram, the facility and severity arithmetic for `local0` with a negative control, the structured data, the byte-order mark, that no datagram exceeds 1024 bytes and that the cut falls on a character boundary, that a category switched off is not sent while one switched on is, and that five deliveries to a collector refusing every connection still arrive and still arrive quickly. `hmailserver/docs/Syslog.md` is the walk. |
| ✅ | **Country blocking** (MDaemon's location screening, every firewall) | **Built 16 September 2026 on branch `country-blocking`, and gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip) - the server and the test project compile, every checker passes, and `RegressionTests.Security.CountryBlocking`, `RegressionTests.Security.CountryBlockingOverRest` and the two class tests inside `Infrastructure.MainOperations` ran green. The row is done when they pass.** The administrator supplies the database and this server ships none and downloads none: `GeoIpDatabaseFile` names a **MaxMind DB (`.mmdb`)** file, which `Common/Util/GeoIpDatabase` reads directly - the metadata found from the marker at the end, the 24-, 28- and 32-bit search-tree records, the pointer forms, the IPv4 space under `::/96` in an IP version 6 file and the `::ffff:0:0/96` alias, every offset bounds-checked and pointer chasing depth-limited, and nothing on that path throwing or logging. The binary form and not the CSV, because the CSV is two files that have to be joined, carries no build date, and would have the server hold a few million parsed ranges behind an index of its own; the `.mmdb` carries its own build epoch - which is what stale is measured against - and its index is the file. A policy is one string in one grammar - empty, `allow:GB,IE` or `block:CN,RU`, at most 255 characters, stored canonically however it was typed - per listener family (`CountryPolicySMTP`, `...IMAP`, `...POP3`, `...HTTP`, the last shared by the REST API and the web services) applied **at accept** in `TCPServer::HandleAccept` and in both HTTP accept filters, before the greeting, the handshake and the session slot; and per domain (`hm_domains.domaincountrypolicy`) applied **at sign-in** in `AccountLogon::Logon`, the one function IMAP, POP3, SMTP AUTH, ManageSieve, the DAV services and the REST API all pass through. **The exemption is a setting and not a hidden rule:** `CountryBlockingExemptRanges` ships as the loopback addresses and every private network and is the whole of it, so an administrator can read it and a test can take it away; an open session is never re-judged, so a policy change never drops one that has authenticated. **It fails open on purpose** - with no database that can be read everything is allowed and the reason is logged once, because a mail server that refused every connection over a renamed file is a worse failure than one that stops applying a geographic policy, and a reload that fails keeps the database that was working; with a database loaded, an address it has no country for is refused by an allow list and admitted by a block list, which is the only place the two differ and is written in both settings' own descriptions. **A refused client is told nothing:** the connection is closed as an IP range closes one, and a refused sign-in is byte-identical to a wrong password and is counted against neither the account's lockout nor the address's auto-ban, because the person refused is very often the owner, travelling. What is recorded instead: one application-log line per refusal in a fixed shape with the country in it, `hmailserver_country_blocked_total` and `hmailserver_country_blocked_by_country_total{country}` (always emitted - a country code comes from the database and not from the peer, so the cardinality is the ISO 3166-1 list), the same counter in the metric history, and a `countries` report section whose note says which half of it is windowed and which is since the server started. Schema 6050: one domain column and seven `hm_settings` rows, all empty or off. Every surface: COM (`Settings.GeoIpDatabaseFile`, `.GeoIpDatabaseMaxAgeDays`, `.CountryBlockingExemptRanges`, `.CountryPolicy(protocol)`, `Domain.CountryPolicy`, `Utilities.LookupCountry`, `Utilities.GeoIpDatabaseStatus`), eight REST keys in the settings group with a read-only `geoip_database_status`, the domain route's `country_policy`, the Control Deck's Settings and domain pages with the twenty catalogues, `hmctl` and the PowerShell module through their generic settings and domain verbs, and [CountryBlocking.md](hmailserver/docs/CountryBlocking.md). The MMDB reader is a unit with its own tests: `GeoIpDatabaseTester` builds whole databases byte by byte - three record widths, a shared record, a pointer, a registered-country fallback, an IPv6 network, a truncated file, a file with no marker, a record pointing past the data section, and a failed reload that keeps the working database - and `CountryBlockingTester` pins the grammar and what each policy means about an unknown country. **Not built, and deliberately:** the country is **not** put on every session and sign-in line, which the original wording of this row asked for - a lookup on every connection, paid on every server whether or not a policy is configured, is not a trade worth making for a log line, and where a country matters, in a refusal, it is in the line; there is no per-recipient or per-message country control; and the address judged is the one the TCP connection came from, so on a listener behind a PROXY protocol front end it is the proxy's and the policy belongs on the proxy. |
| 🔄 | **`winget install hMailServer`** (and Chocolatey) | **Built 15 September 2026, unreleased: no published release has run the workflow yet, and the package is not in the public repositories.** Three winget manifests and a Chocolatey package as templates under `hmailserver/installation`, rendered by `build/make-package-manifests.ps1` from the release's own signed installer - downloaded, its Authenticode signature required, and the SHA-256 computed from those bytes, because the signed installer is not the built one and a hash copied between two windows is a broken install for every user of it (6.3.3: `45A9D7F1…` as built, `3188BE2F…` as published). Every identity field is the installer's own - `MinVersion` as the minimum OS, `AppId` as the `hMailServer_is1` product code that lets `winget upgrade` recognise an installation winget did not make, `AppVersion` as the version it compares - and the silent switches are the smoke test's, less `/TYPE=full`, which on an upgrade would put the Control Panel back on a server that had left it out. `build/check-package-manifests.py` validates them against Microsoft's published schemas and holds the two packages to the same URL, hash and version. The `Package managers` workflow does two different jobs: on a pull request or push that touches a template or either script it renders with placeholder values and schema-checks offline, and packs nothing; on `release: published`, or a dispatch, it downloads the asset, requires the signature, renders, checks, runs `winget validate` when the runner has the client, packs the `.nupkg` and keeps everything as an artifact for ninety days, and submits only when a repository variable and the tokens are set. Checked by hand against 6.3.3: `winget validate` accepted the manifests, the schema check passed, and `choco pack` built the package. **What is left is not ours:** the first submission of a new identifier is reviewed by a person at microsoft/winget-pkgs and by Chocolatey's moderation, so the row stays underway until both have accepted it. Neither identifier is taken - `winget search hmailserver` finds nothing and the community feed has no `hmailserver`. `hmailserver/docs/PackageManagers.md` is the walk. |
| ✅ | **A PowerShell module and a Linux command-line client** | **Done 15 September 2026.** Two clients over the REST API, so both work against a Windows server, a Linux server or one on another machine, and neither needs COM: `hmailserver/source/Tools/Cli/hmctl`, one Python 3 file with nothing but the standard library behind it, which the Linux packages install as `/usr/bin/hmctl` (python3 recommended, not required), and `HmailServer.psm1` beside it - `Get-HmDomain`, `New-HmAccount`, `Set-HmSetting` and the rest, answering objects rather than text, every changing verb supporting `-WhatIf` and `-Confirm`, a domain or an address completing from the server itself. `hmctl` has verbs for domains, accounts, aliases, distribution lists, groups and their members, rules, routes, listeners, IP ranges, certificates, fetch accounts, API keys, the queue, held mail, the nine settings groups, the ini keys, the logs, the backup, an account's application passwords, the rule-criterion probe and the reports, and `hmctl api` for a route newer than the file. The module has verbs for domains, accounts, aliases, groups and their members, the settings groups, the ini keys, the queue, held mail, the logs, the backup and the rule-criterion probe; lists, rules, routes, listeners and IP ranges reach it only through the configuration document (`Export-HmConfiguration`, `Import-HmConfiguration`), and certificates, fetch accounts, API keys, application passwords and reports only through `Invoke-HmApi`. Exit codes a script can read: 3 when the credential was refused, 4 when what was named does not exist. **They are held together by a check rather than by intention:** `build/check-cli.py` stands a recorded server in front of both and asserts the method, the path, the Authorization header and the body of every request each one makes - 393 checks when PowerShell is on the machine (303 of them hmctl's), in CI - because what goes wrong in a client like this is the vocabulary pointing at a route that is not there, and a check needing a running server is a check nobody runs. It found four defects while it was being written, two of them in what a person would have called finished: `hmctl` refused `--json` typed after the verb, which is where it is typed; its bulk verbs read accounts from `/api/v1/accounts?domain=`, which is not a route (they are listed under their domain), and the 404 was being swallowed so every address looked new; the two clients disagreed about whether to send `active` on a create, and neither should invent a field the caller did not give; and the password prompt asked `isatty()`, which on Windows answers **true for the NUL device** - so an unattended run with stdin redirected would have sat at a prompt nobody could see until something killed it. `hmailserver/docs/CommandLine.md` is the walk. |
| ✅ | **Restore one mailbox, or one message, from a backup** (every hosted service) | **Built 22 September 2026 on branch `batch23`, with no schema change; the server, the test project and the Control Panel build, and every checker passes.** One operation, `MailboxRestore` (`Common/Application/MailboxRestore`), modelled on `BackupRestorer`'s staging and never on the executer's deleting: the archive - one inside the backup destination, named over REST, the Deck and `hmctl` only by the name the new listing answers (a path segment is not decoded for separators, so a path there is a name the destination lacks, 404), and over COM by that name or by its path inside the destination, anything else refused there with the reason - is opened and its index and version checked on the caller's thread, the account found in it (404) and on this server (404), and only then is the job queued on the maintenance work queue under the backup manager's one run slot, so that a mailbox restore, a whole restore and a backup never overlap (409). The job stages the account's own directory out of the archive (`BackupRestorer::StageAccountMessageStore`, one member of the 7z, not the store) before anything is touched, and writes the rows through `PersistentMessage::SaveObject` with a fresh UID, so the mod-sequences and the account's size follow, the folder's shared collection is refreshed and every session with it selected is told as delivery tells it; the full-text indexer is nudged. Two modes: *merge* creates each archived folder that is missing and adds each archived message whose file name is not in the live mailbox - the executer's GUID name is the identity, in whatever folder, so merging twice adds nothing - with flags, keywords and dates as archived; *replace* empties every live folder first (through the folders' own collections, under a `RecoverableStore` scope, so what it removes is kept only for `RecoverDeletedDays`, which is off by default - both confirm sentences say so) and then fills as merge would, the folders themselves kept; replace is refused with 400 when the archive holds no messages for the account, and a replace whose fill left anything failed ends failed with the counts, the `BACKUP ERROR` line and the alert. One message is merge of one row into the folder it was in or a named one, and `mode` given with `message` is refused. A row's file name is taken from the index only in the executer's own shape (`{` + 36 characters + `}.eml`, `MailboxRestore::IsArchivedFilenameSound`, held to examples by `MailboxRestoreTester` in `ClassTester::DoTests`); any other row is counted as unrestorable and never names a path. A refused start marks the registered job failed rather than leaving it queued; the live file names are read again after replace's emptying rather than assumed gone; the archive listing's size is 64-bit; and the staging names the files it needs to the archiver one by one through a list file, for the listing and the restore alike, never the account's directory. Surfaces: `GET /api/v1/backup/archives`, `GET /api/v1/backup/{archive}/mailboxes/{address}` (folders and messages as the archive holds them, subjects read from the staged files for at most 500 messages, `?folder=` to narrow, the passphrase in `X-hMailServer-Backup-Passphrase`), `POST .../restore` (`mode`, or `message` and `folder`) and `GET /api/v1/backup/restore-mailbox/{job}`, with OpenAPI blocks, classified `manage_server` for the restore, `view_mailbox` for the listing, `view_configuration` for the two reads, all four server-wide; COM `BackupManager.RestoreMailbox`, `RestoreMessage`, `DescribeMailbox` and `MailboxRestoreStatus` (ids 4-7); the Deck's Backup view gains **Restore a mailbox** (archive picker from the listing, address, merge/replace with the sentence that says what replace destroys, a confirm, the job's outcome under the button) and **Restore one message** (the listing as a table, a Restore per row, a target folder), twenty catalogues and fifteen harness checks; `hmctl backup archives`, `mailbox`, `restore-mailbox --merge|--replace [--message ID --folder F]` and `restore-status`, with six check-cli cases; the Control Panel's Backup page gains the same two cards over COM, seventeen resx catalogues translated. The request is recorded on the audit trail as the whole restore's is (`mailbox restore started`), and a job that cannot complete fails as a whole restore fails. `RegressionTests.Infrastructure.MailboxRestore` (REST port 9846): merge brings two deleted messages and a deleted folder back with their flags and leaves the other account and the untouched messages alone, twice merged adds nothing; replace after new mail empties and refills; one message into its own folder and into a named one; an unknown account 404, a percent-encoded path 404 over REST and the outside-path refusal proved over COM, a second restore during a backup 409, `mode` with `message` 400, replace of an account the archive holds nothing for 400; the search finds a restored message; a selected IMAP session sees the additions and a restored message's UID is above the folder's earlier UIDNEXT; the listing's shape; a job that fails after queuing from a hand-edited index (a missing file and an unsound name: failed 2, the log lines, the audit row) and a replace from it that ends failed with the BACKUP ERROR line; an encrypted archive's round trip with the passphrase in the body and the header, a wrong one refused; the BackupMessagesDBOnly refusal; subjects withheld past the 500 ceiling; the COM round trip; an auditor refused. Documented in the new section of `docs/Backup.md`. Thinner than the row asks: one archive at a time, with no point-in-time picker across several; no restore into a different account, and no recreation of a deleted account - the account must exist; an archive taken with `BackupMessagesDBOnly` holds no files and cannot restore a mailbox; a public folder is not an account's mailbox and is not restored here. |
| ✅ | **Mailbox rename and move** | **Built 16 September 2026 on branch `mailbox-move`, and gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** One server-side operation, `MailboxMove`, reached from `Account.MoveTo` and `Utilities.MoveMailbox` over COM, `GET` and `POST /api/v1/accounts/{address}/move` over REST, a Move or rename card in the Control Deck's account editor, a Move or rename button in the Control Panel's account dialog, `hmctl mailbox move` and `Move-HmMailbox`. The new address carries the domain, so a rename and a move to another domain on this server are one call. **What it is for is that the account keeps its identity:** the row is kept, so its rules, folders and their subscriptions, special-use designations and access-control entries, application passwords, second factor, contacts, calendars, preferences, known senders, password history, sender lists, group memberships and role assignment all follow without being touched - which making a second account and copying could never do. **What does not follow the id is what this had to do.** The message store (`hm_messages` holds only the GUID; the path is rebuilt from the ACCOUNT ADDRESS at read time, and `NameChanger::RenameAccount` took the domain component from the OLD address at both ends, so a move to another domain would have put the mailbox where nothing would look for it), the Sieve tree, the aliases whose value is the old address, the distribution list memberships, the recipients of mail still in the queue - which is how mail in flight reaches the moved mailbox even with the old address dropped - and the quarantined messages held for it. The old address is kept as an alias by default, with a switch to drop it. **Refused before anything is changed:** an address an account, an alias or a list already has (each said by name), a domain this server does not hold, a domain being moved INTO that is not active or is already at its maximum number of accounts or its maximum total size (the account's own save asks those two of new accounts only, by an id of zero, so an account arriving with an id would otherwise have walked past them), a store whose message file names are still full paths, and a mailbox in use - which is what the running server can actually be asked, the POP3 mailbox lock and an IMAP session with one of the account's folders selected. The documents say plainly what is NOT counted: an authenticated IMAP session that has selected nothing, a webmail request, a ManageSieve session, and an SMTP delivery in flight - the last of which can cost a message delivered while the store is being copied, so the caches are dropped the moment the account row carries the new address and the old directory is swept once for what landed in it after the copy passed. **Either it completes or the account is as it was:** the store first (copy then delete, so a failure leaves the original; a copy that raises part way through has its half tree removed before the refusal is answered, so the move can be asked for again), then the account row as one UPDATE, then the references, then the alias, each step undone by the next one failing. A rewrite that failed writes nothing and so counts nothing, which is what `references_incomplete` in the summary is for. What survives the process being killed is the journal, `hm_mailboxmoves` (schema step 6050), which the server reports once per start for any move left at a stage short of complete. A stage is written BEFORE the step it names wherever that step is not a single statement, so the copy has a stage of its own and `started` goes on meaning that nothing has been changed; `hmailserver/docs/MailboxMove.md` says what each stage leaves on disk and in the database and how to finish it by hand, which for a cross-domain move is to ask for the move again rather than to set the address. It is **one entry on the audit trail** naming both addresses: the rows a move writes are written with no actor on the thread so that one act is one entry rather than five. `manage_accounts` in BOTH domains, which for REST means the handler judges the domain in the body because the path's scoping cannot see it; the domain the mailbox is leaving is taken from the STORED row, so assigning `Account.Address` before the call cannot name a domain the credential does administer to move a mailbox out of one it does not. For the same reason the move itself is from the stored address and not from whatever the object in hand was last given. The listener now decodes a percent-encoded path segment before it routes - never a separator, so a segment cannot become two - because the Control Deck and the PowerShell module encode every address they put in a path, and `bob%40example.test` is not an address anything here can find. **Setting `Account.Address` and saving now calls the same implementation**, so the narrow rename gains all of the above; it keeps two differences for compatibility (no alias, and no refusal while in use, because the POP3 lock outlives the reply to QUIT), and the active-domain refusal is asked only of a move into another domain so that a rename inside a deactivated domain goes on working as it always has. A domain rename's cascade is untouched. **Deliberately not moved:** the archive and the message trace, which record what happened at an address at a time. **What is not verified:** nothing has been gated. `RegressionTests.Domains.MailboxMove` (ten tests over COM, every store assertion made by reading the mailbox back through a client) and `RegressionTests.API.RestApiMailboxMove` (seven over REST, linked into the Linux suite) are written and have not run, and the row is done when they pass. A review of the branch found twelve things and all twelve are fixed here, three of them with a test of their own: a move from a stale account object, a capitalisation-only rename that reported an error about mail it was delivering, and a cross-domain move past a domain's account limit. |

### Enterprise: policy, compliance and identity

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **External-sender tagging and first-contact tips** (Microsoft 365, Google) | **Done 15 September 2026.** Per domain, off by default, and while they are off nothing is read, queried or written. Three switches: `[EXTERNAL]` at the front of the subject (the text is the domain's own, at most 100 characters, so a domain whose readers do not read English can say it in the language they do); `X-hMailServer-External`, which the webmail turns into a line above the message and which - unlike the subject - costs the sender's DKIM signature nothing; and the first-contact note, stamped when an outside sender writes to an account that has had mail from other people but never from them. **The rule for "outside" is one function with a unit test** (`MessageOrigin::IsExternalSender`, run from `ClassTester::DoTests`): not authenticated, not through a trusted incoming relay, and neither the envelope sender nor the From address at a domain this server hosts - *either* of the last two being outside is enough, so a forged internal From on external mail is still tagged. The tag goes on **the recipient account's own copy** at local delivery, because the switch belongs to the recipient's domain and a message addressed both here and outside must not carry the tag out with it. The first-contact memory is `hm_knownsenders` (schema 6044), one row per account and correspondent. With the note on, a copy from an outside sender already remembered costs two database round trips, the first message from a new one three, and a copy from inside none. The recipients of mail the account sends over authenticated SMTP are recorded as well (`SMTPConnection::RememberOutgoingRecipients_`), so a reply to that mail is not announced. **Not yet recorded:** mail sent from this server's own webmail (`POST /api/v1/me/messages`) and by its send-later task, which queue the message directly and never reach that code. A reply to a message the reader sent from the webmail to someone new is therefore announced as a first contact, and no regression test covers the outgoing half. **What it deliberately does not do:** it is not anti-spoofing - a message forging a hosted address in both its envelope and its From is judged inside and not tagged, which is what DMARC is for - and an installation that lists its inbound filtering gateway as an incoming relay will see nothing tagged, because that list is a statement that the hop is its own. Both are said plainly in `hmailserver/docs/DomainTransforms.md`. |
| ✅ | **Impersonation protection** (Microsoft 365's anti-phishing policies) | **Built 22 September 2026, schema 6087. Not yet run against a bench: the fixture compiles, every checker is green and both builds say Build succeeded; the regression suite runs when the branch is gated.** What is built: per domain, off by default, and while it is off nothing is read - `Domain.ImpersonationAction` (0 off, 1 flag, 2 quarantine), `ProtectedPersons` and `ProtectedPartners` over COM at ids 177-179, `impersonation_action` (a digit or `off`/`flag`/`quarantine`), `protected_persons` and `protected_partners` on `GET`/`PUT /api/v1/domains/{domain}` with the two lists canonicalised on save and refused with a sentence (2,001 characters; an entry whose address is not one; a partner that is an address, a suffix, non-ASCII or not a domain name), an **Impersonation protection** tab of the Control Panel's domain dialog beside the tagging tab and a group of the Deck's domain editor, `hm_domains.domainimpersonationaction`, `domainprotectedpersons` and `domainprotectedpartners` in the domain's XML store, and the decision in one unit, `Common/Util/ImpersonationCheck`, run at local delivery on the recipient's own copy beside the external-sender tagger and on the tagger's own outside/inside decision, shared through `MessageOrigin::Verdict` rather than re-derived. **Two checks.** A borrowed name: the From display name, folded - NFKC on Windows, the invisible format characters (soft hyphen, zero-width spaces and joiners, bidi controls, the byte order mark) deleted, a capital I and Greek iota to l before the lower-casing, runs of white space and punctuation to one space, `rn`→`m`, `vv`→`w`, `1 l I |`→`l`, `0`→`o`, the Cyrillic and Greek look-alikes to the Latin letter, the Cyrillic capitals Т Н В М К and the palochka, Ԛ, Ԝ and Ѵ among them - equal to the folded person name of an active account of the domain or of a protected person, while the From address is not that account's, an alias of it (the domain's aliases are read beside its accounts), or the address given for the person; a display name that is an address is compared as one too, against the account's address and its aliases. A lookalike domain: the From address's domain and the envelope sender's when it differs, IDNA labels decoded by a Punycode decoder of the unit's own (RFC 3492's sample A among its vectors) and folded the same way, equal to a hosted domain (alias domains included) or a partner after the folding while not before it - both sides IDNA-decoded, so a hosted A-label written as its U-label is itself - or one Damerau-Levenshtein edit away inside one label with every other label the same - never an edit that adds, removes or moves a dot - and not itself hosted or a partner nor beneath one; the candidate and each parent name down to two labels are judged in turn, so `secure.examp1e.test` is caught by the `examp1e.test` inside it. Distance 1 is the rule and 2 is not, because on a short domain two edits reach an unrelated name. Flag writes `X-hMailServer-Impersonation: name "<shown>" matches <person> (<owner>); from <address>` or `domain <x> looks like <y>; from|envelope sender <address>` - one line, through the RFC 2047 encoder in UTF-8 so a Cyrillic shown name survives, the shown name and the person's cut at 120 characters - and leaves the subject alone; the webmail decodes it into a warning bar naming the person and the real address in twenty-three languages and marks the row; a stranger's forged header is removed where nothing is found, replaced where something is, and left alone with the action off; quarantine holds the copy through `QuarantineStore::Quarantine` with the reason and score 0, so the digest and the release work as for spam and a release does not re-check, and falls back to flag when the store is off. Exempt: a sender the recipient's own safe-sender list allows (the nearest scope that decides), and a From domain that is exactly hosted or a partner - the envelope is still judged. What a domain's judgement reads - its accounts' names folded with their aliases, its protected persons, the hosted and partner names decoded and folded - is held per domain id and forgotten when an account, an alias, a domain or an alias domain is saved or deleted and after sixty seconds as a safety net. One DEBUG log line with the addresses. A DMARC pass changes nothing. **What is thinner than the row asks:** NFKC runs on Windows only - the Linux build has no Unicode tables, so there the compatibility step is skipped and the confusable table does the rest; the confusable table is the roadmap's list plus the case pairs, the Cyrillic capitals above and Greek ο and ι, not Unicode's whole confusables file, and the doc says what slips past it (word order, decorations, a name only in the local part, a bidi override, composed diacritics, Greek lower case); and the name check compares the account's person name as the directory composes it, so an account with no name entered is protected only by its address. Tests: `SMTP/ImpersonationProtection.cs` on port 9836, `ImpersonationCheckTester` in `ClassTester`, and the portal harness for both header shapes and their absence. |
| ✅ | **Mail flow rules** (Exchange transport rules, Google's routing) | **Done 17 September 2026.** Global rules run in `SMTPDeliverer` for every message on every delivery attempt, after virus scanning and before DKIM signing and the split between local and external delivery; a domain's rules (`Domain.Rules`, `/api/v1/domains/{name}/rules`, the Rules pages with the domain chosen at the top; schema 6063) run after them for every message with a recipient in the domain or a sender it hosts, and a domain-restricted key reaches its domain's rules and no other's. Conditions: From, To, CC, Subject, body, size, the recipient list, delivery attempts, any named header, and the attachment name (`attachment_name`, every attachment's file name). Actions: delete, forward, reply, move, set a header, send using a route, copy, bind, script, stop, and now add a disclaimer (the text appended with the rule named in a header), reject with a text (the SMTP session answers 550 with it before accepting the message, asked at the end of DATA for the server's and the domains' rules), require TLS (a destination without STARTTLS is deferred naming the rule) and quarantine (held under the rule's name). Edited on the Control Panel's Rules page and in the Deck's Rules view, both with the scope picker, and through the REST routes; tested in `Rules/MailFlowRules.cs` on both benches and `API/RestApiRules.cs`; `docs/MailFlowRules.md`. |
| ✅ | **A domain disclaimer** (every business) | **Built 15 September 2026 on every way a person sends, and gated green with batch 10 on 16 September 2026.** Per domain, off by default, text and HTML - one box filled in is used for both, with its line breaks turned into line breaks in HTML. It is put on by `OutgoingFooters` (`Common/Util/OutgoingFooters.cpp`), one function that applies the domain signature and then the disclaimer to the same `MessageData` and commits both with one write, and all three ways a person sends call it: SMTP submission (`SMTPConnection::DoPreAcceptMessageModifications_`), the webmail's send (`POST /api/v1/me/messages`, which is also its reply, forward and phishing report) and the webmail's send-later (`ScheduledMailTask::SendDraft`). The rules are therefore the same code on all three, and the domain whose settings apply is the sending address's, resolved as `MAIL FROM` resolves it - not the signed-in account's: an account's own message gets its own domain's footers, a webmail message sent from an identity at another hosted domain (an alias there, or an address whose owner granted the post right) gets that domain's, as the same message over SMTP would, and send-later always sends as the account. Until that day only SMTP had it - the webmail wrote its messages into the queue itself, so a domain with the disclaimer on sent every browser-written message without it and logged nothing - and the domain signature, which had always had the same gap, is closed by the same change. Every path is **before** `DKIMSigner::Sign`, so this server's own signature covers the footer rather than being broken by it. It is appended in place to the `text/plain` and `text/html` parts wherever they sit in a multipart tree. **Once, not once per hop:** a reply that quotes a message already carrying the footer gets no second copy, compared after quote markers, tags and white space are removed from both sides. **Never** on a signed or encrypted message - `multipart/signed`, `multipart/encrypted`, the PKCS#7 and PGP types - which goes out exactly as written with a line in the application log naming it; since the same day the domain signature follows that rule too, where it used to be appended inside a `multipart/signed` and break it, on SMTP as well. Never on an auto-reply, a bounce or a list posting, never on a message with no recipient outside this server, never twice on one this server already stamped, and never on a message relayed in from outside that merely claims a hosted sender domain. On the webmail the account's own signature is the page's to write - it puts it in the compose box - so there the server adds only the domain's part, decided against the signed-in account's signature; under the overwrite method the domain's goes below the account's, because the server does not take text out of a message. It follows that a draft written in a desktop client and scheduled through the send-later route goes without the account's signature, and that the account's signature on a webmail reply or note to a colleague is the page's doing, not the domain's two switches'. `RegressionTests.API.WebmailFooters` drives the webmail's send and send-later through the footers, each exclusion on a message the footers visibly ran on, the account's signature not added twice (an identity's message included), a signed message over both the webmail and SMTP, and two domains' footers kept apart (an identity at the second domain included), beside the SMTP tests in `SMTP.DomainTransforms`. **Gated:** `RegressionTests.API.WebmailFooters` and the SMTP side of the change (`SMTP.DomainTransforms`, `SMTP.Signatures` and `AntiSpam.Basics`, which reach the restructured `SignatureAdder`) ran green in the batch-10 gate. It is a domain-wide footer, not a mail flow rule - no conditions, no per-account text, and a message addressed to both a partner and a colleague gets one footer that the colleague sees too. `hmailserver/docs/DomainTransforms.md` is the walk. |
| ✅ | **Journaling and dual delivery** (Exchange journaling, Google's dual delivery) | **Done 18 September 2026.** One row per rule (`hm_journalrules`, schema 6075): whose mail - a hosted domain, or one account in it - which way it is going (mail arriving for them, mail they send over an authenticated session, or both), where the copy goes (one address, local or anywhere else) and what shape it takes. Two shapes, which is what makes Exchange's journaling and Google's dual delivery one feature: `report` sends the Exchange envelope report - `Sender:`, `Subject:`, `Message-Id:` and one `Recipient:` line per **envelope** recipient, which is where the message actually went rather than what its headers say - with the original attached as `message/rfc822` and streamed through byte for byte; `copy` sends the message itself with one header line in front of it and nothing else changed, which is what a migration or a second system wants. Both are queued through the ordinary delivery queue, so a journal address elsewhere is delivered, retried and bounced like any other mail. Evaluated **once per accepted message**, in `SMTPConnection` after the row is saved and where the archive decision is made from the same recipient list, and once per message a **fetch account** collects, at the same point in that path - not at delivery, which runs again for every retry and every route - and one copy per matching **rule**, not per recipient. It never loops, and not by trusting anybody: a journal message carries `X-hMailServer-Journal`, whose value is the rule's id and a 128-bit mark the server process chose at random and stored nowhere, and the evaluation stops only for a message carrying that mark. A header a sender wrote decides nothing and is journalled as any other message would be - the header's name is published, and an archive that the people it is about can switch off with one line in their mail client is not an archive. There is no second guard and no rule is skipped for where its journal address points, so journalling a domain into a mailbox of that domain works; the one arrangement refused, and refused when the rule is written, is an account journalled into itself. The envelope sender of a journal message is the journalled domain's postmaster, so a journal address that stops answering bounces to the administrator rather than telling somebody outside that their mail is copied. One function decides every word - `Journaling::SaveRule` - and REST (`/api/v1/journal-rules`, administrator only: `view_configuration` to read, `manage_server` to write, refused to a domain-confined credential), COM (`Utilities.GetJournalRules`, `SaveJournalRule`, `DeleteJournalRule`), the Control Deck's Journaling view, the Control Panel's Journaling page and `hmctl journalrule` all go through it. Audited; carried in the backup under the domain, by the name rather than the ids a restore reissues. `RegressionTests.SMTP.Journaling` drives real deliveries through it - including one test entirely about the guard, where a sender writes the header themselves and is journalled anyway while the value taken out of a report the server really made is not - and `JournalingTester` pins the report's shape, the mark and the boundary's characters without a database. **What it deliberately is not:** not the on-disk archive, not an account's forward, not a mail flow rule (no conditions), not retention - once a copy leaves the queue this server keeps nothing and withdraws nothing - and not encryption: the copy carries whatever the message carried. **Not covered:** a message the webmail queues directly (`POST /api/v1/me/messages`), a send-later draft and a message an event script queues never reach either evaluation point, so the outbound half covers SMTP submission and not the browser - mail that arrives is covered whichever way it arrives; and there is no Linux port of the fixture. [Journaling.md](hmailserver/docs/Journaling.md). |
| ✅ | **Remote-domain settings** (Exchange) | **Built 15 September 2026, except the message format:** one record per remote domain or pattern, the most specific active record governing (an exact name over a pattern, a longer pattern over a shorter, an inactive record skipped so that switching off an exception never exempts a domain from the broader rule), the strictest combination when one connection carries several domains. It holds the TLS required (**Mandatory TLS per domain**, below), whether an automatic reply may go there (an account's out-of-office and the domain-wide one alike), whether mail may be forwarded there (an account's forward and a Sieve `redirect` alike), and what this server will attempt: the largest message, refused before any connection with 5.3.4 because it will not be smaller next time; simultaneous connections, a non-blocking slot per policy that defers with 4.4.5 rather than making a delivery thread wait; and messages a minute. The message format - Exchange's rich-text and character-set choices - is not built and is the part that stays open: this server sends a message as it was written. **Gated:** `RegressionTests.SMTP.RemoteDomainPolicyEnforcement` and `RegressionTests.API.RestApiRemoteDomains` ran green in the batch-10 gate on 16 September 2026. |
| ✅ | **Restricted delivery** (Google Workspace's restrict delivery) | **Built 22 September 2026, schema 6084. Not yet run against a bench: the fixture compiles, every checker is green and both builds say Build succeeded; the regression suite runs when the branch is gated.** What is built: the list is the domain's - `Domain.RestrictedDomains`, `restricted_domains` on `GET`/`PUT /api/v1/domains/{domain}`, a **Restricted delivery** tab of the Control Panel's domain dialog and a group of the Deck's domain editor, `hm_domains.domainrestricteddomains` in the domain's XML store - at most 2,000 characters of ASCII domain names separated by commas or spaces, lower-cased on save, each `partner.example` or `.example` for the domain and everything beneath it, which is the domain policy list's own spelling and whose matcher (`DomainPolicyEntry::Covers`) this reuses rather than writing a second; an address, a path, an address literal or a non-ASCII name is refused with a sentence over REST (400) and COM. An account follows its domain unless it says otherwise: `Account.RestrictedDelivery`, `restricted_delivery` (0/1/2 or `domain`/`restricted`/`exempt`), a three-way choice on both account editors, `hm_accounts.accountrestricteddelivery` - 1 is restricted even with an empty domain list (its own domain alone), 2 is exempt. Always allowed: the own domain and its alias domains, the domain's postmaster, the null sender (bounces must reach a restricted account); other hosted domains only when named. One decision, `Common/Util/RestrictedDelivery::Check`, enforced at `RCPT TO` in both directions - `550 5.7.1 <address>: this mailbox accepts mail from named domains only.` for an envelope sender a restricted recipient may not hear from, `550 5.7.1 <address>: this account may send to named domains only.` for a submission by a restricted account, per recipient, the `From` header never consulted - and at local delivery for a recipient reached by expansion (a list member, an alias target, a catch-all), dropped with a log line and a non-delivery report only when the sender is local; a restricted account's forward, rule forward or Sieve redirect to an unnamed domain is not made while the mailbox still gets the message, and its out-of-office (stored, domain-wide or Sieve) is not sent to a stranger. Every refusal is a DEBUG line; none is an error. `docs/RestrictedDelivery.md` says what is refused where and what it is not. **The adversarial review of 22 September found and the follow-up fixed:** the webmail's send, a read receipt, an unsubscribe by mail, an invitation's answer, the password-protected notice and a Sieve `notify` had skipped the outbound decision - each now runs it on the caller's account and refuses with the sentence (400 over REST, before the first chunk for a protected message); a `redirect`-only script whose redirects were all refused had dropped the message - it is kept now, with an application-log line; a plus-addressed direct recipient had been answered 250 and dropped in silence - the plus part is resolved before the RCPT lookup; a hosted domain named by its alias name never matched - both spellings are judged and the log says which; a list restored from a backup was taken unchecked - it goes through the canonicaliser and falls back to empty with a log line; two fixture counts had counted a message the helper had deleted; and the doc says what an alias or a list that reaches outside is (the administrator's exit), that a Unicode SMTPUTF8 envelope is compared as written, and that the delivery-time report goes to any hosted envelope sender, spoofed or not. Tests: `SMTP/RestrictedDelivery.cs` (port 9796, twelve tests) - each refusal with its negative control, the list with a restricted and an exempt member, the withheld account forward, rule forward and redirect (the redirect-only script keeping the message), the out-of-office withheld from a stranger who came through a list, the alias target dropped with the report reaching a local colleague and nobody outside, the webmail send refused and accepted, the REST refusals and round-trips, the two COM properties through `Save`; two Deck harness checks; seventeen Control Panel and twenty Deck catalogues by hand. Not built: a per-account list (the list is the domain's, by decision), a check of the `From` header, IDNA conversion of a Unicode envelope, and any effect on mail already in the mailbox. |
| ✅ | **Allow and block lists for the server** (Microsoft 365's tenant allow/block list) | **Done 17 September 2026.** A URL is an entry in every list - the reader's, the domain's, the server's - written as `https://host/anything` and stored as `host/`, covering a link in the message whose host is that host or a subdomain of it, with a leading `www.` dropped as the link extractor drops it. It is about what the message points at, not who sent it: consulted only when neither address decided anything, from the links the SURBL check extracts, with the same precedence and tie-break, and the body is read for links only for a recipient whose scopes hold a URL entry. Block holds or marks the message for that recipient like a blocked sender's; allow rescues a link the URL blacklists list. REST, COM, the Deck and the Control Panel take a URL where they took an address, and the Control Panel's two list editors gained the expiry - the switch and the date, with the COM setter accepting the date as text. Two tests in `AntiSpam/SenderLists`: the link blocked for the account that listed it and nobody else, with the same sender unmarked without the link; and the blacklisted link rescued for the account that allowed it while the other recipient's copy stays marked; plus the URL's canonical form and three refusals. `hmailserver/docs/SenderListsAndDigests.md` says what a URL entry covers. The row as written: The server's list and each domain's, in the same table and shape as a reader's safe senders: an address or a domain, allow or block, the reason kept, an expiry that the reads honour before the hourly sweep removes it. Precedence written down and tested: the account's list, then its domain's, then the server's, the nearest with an opinion the only one consulted; inside one list the more specific entry wins and a block wins an exact tie; the same value twice rewrites the entry. The account comes first, unlike Microsoft 365, because Blocked senders already refuses a sender for everyone during the SMTP conversation and nothing overrules it. Edited in the Control Panel (a page beside Blocked senders, a tab of the domain dialog), in the Deck (an eighth list, a card in the domain editor; ten harness checks), at `/api/v1/sender-list` and `/api/v1/domains/{domain}/sender-list`, and over COM (`Settings.AntiSpam.SenderList`, `Domain.SenderList`). Beside it, and unchanged: Blocked senders (`hm_blocked_senders`) still refuse a listed envelope sender with 550 5.7.1 at MAIL FROM when its score (100 by default) crosses the delete threshold, and the anti-spam white list (`hm_whitelist`, a sender wildcard plus an IP range) still lets matching mail past the spam tests and greylisting; both are tested in `RegressionTests/AntiSpam/SenderBlacklist.cs` and `Whitelisting.cs`. **Not done:** URLs as entries, for which the SURBL-style check is the place; an expiry in the Control Panel's editor. |
| ✅ | **eDiscovery** (Google Vault, Microsoft Purview) | **Built 24 September 2026, schema 6096 to 6097. Not yet compiled or run against a bench: written in a worktree beside two other rows, it is compiled, built and gated when the branch is folded.** Cases, searches, holds and exports, at `/api/v1/discovery/cases` on the REST listener (`Common/Util/RestApiDiscovery.cpp`, members of `RestApiServer` as SCIM's are), kept in four new tables, `hm_discoverycases`, `hm_discoverysearches`, `hm_discoveryholds` and `hm_discoveryexports`, every one of them on the audit trail. **A case** has a name and a description, is opened and closed, and a closed case runs no search, places no hold and makes no export; closing it releases every hold it placed. **A search** names its custodians - addresses, domains, or `*` for every mailbox - and any of a date range, words that must all be found, an attachment's name, type or least size, and whether it reads the mailboxes, the archive or both; there is no sender or recipient criterion, the words being looked for in the whole decoded header. It is queued and run one at a time on the maintenance queue (`DiscoverySearch`), a search or an export the server stopped in the middle of marked failed when the runner next starts, and its hits are written to a results file beside the case and read back a page at a time, what it could not read paged on its own. The words mean what IMAP `SEARCH TEXT` means, a case-insensitive substring over the decoded headers and body parts, the row's own matcher rather than the full-text index, which a search uses only to leave out what cannot match; it reads the mailboxes, their deleted items in the recoverable store, and the on-disk archive; a zero-access mailbox is matched on its headers alone, listed, and withheld from an export with a line in the manifest giving the reason, and a message encrypted end to end is matched on its headers and listed as not searched when they lack a word; nothing past 80 MB a message is read, and such a message is listed as not searched. **A hold** is on an account, on a domain - every account in it - or on the archived copies a finished search found; while one is live the account cannot be deleted, nor its domain, nor its messages deleted in bulk, nor replaced by a restore, nor moved to another domain, nor purged from its deleted items, a full restore with the domains option is refused before it deletes anything, and the retention sweep, the recoverable store's sweep and the archive's sweep and address erasure pass it by, over REST, COM and SCIM alike, each refusal naming the legal hold (SCIM's removal also suspends the account and ends its sessions); a message a person deletes over IMAP, POP3, the webmail or REST, or moves out of the account over IMAP, is kept in the recoverable store whatever `RecoverDeletedDays` says, and stays there until the hold is released. **A rule's Delete action on a held account keeps the copy:** the account rule's Delete, a forward that keeps no copy, a Sieve reject or discard and the account's spam delete threshold put the message into the recoverable store as a person's delete does (`LocalDelivery::KeepHeldCopy_`) - sealed to its key first for a zero-access mailbox, never kept in the clear - and the log line says the hold kept it; a copy that cannot be kept is delivered instead. It does not reach the server's or a domain's rules, spam and virus deletion before a message is the account's, the queue, or the confidential and password-protected stores. **An export** of a finished search is a zip beside the case - the messages as EML byte for byte under the custodian and the folder, an mbox per folder in parts of at most 256 MB, `manifest.json` with every file's SHA-256, what was withheld and why and what the search could not read, and `SHA256SUMS` - whose own SHA-256 the case answers; an export that would pass 4 GB, or leave the data directory's volume less than a gigabyte free, is refused with a sentence that says so, and one past 500 MB is not downloaded but named with its path on the server's disk. A backup leaves the cases' files out and a restore leaves them where they are. **Who:** two permissions appended after `provision`, `view_discovery` to read and `manage_discovery` to act; the server administrator holds both, the auditor reads, and the download needs `view_mailbox` beside `view_discovery`, which `build/check-role-choke-point.py` pins - together they hand over the custodians' deleted items, which no other administrative route does - and a search of the archive needs `view_archive` as well, to run, read and download. A credential confined to named domains names no custodian outside them and never `*`, holds nothing outside them, and does not see a case that reaches outside them, nor an empty case somebody else opened. **The audit trail** records, while it is on, a case opened and closed, a search with its whole query and who ran it, a hold placed and released, an export asked for, and a download with its SHA-256; the case's own rows keep every search, hold and export whatever the switch says. **Surfaces:** the Control Deck's Discovery view (the cases, a case's searches with their progress and hit counts, the search form, the holds, the exports with Download, in the twenty catalogues), and a read-only Discovery page in the Control Panel that reads the cases and their audit lines over the REST listener with the credential it signed in with, finds the listener as the server starts it - its fallback to the ACME certificate included - and says so when the listener is off, bound to another machine's loopback address, or behind an administrator's second factor; no COM change and no `hmctl` verb, since neither has an archive group to add this beside. [Discovery.md](hmailserver/docs/Discovery.md), a cross-reference in Journaling.md, the rows in Roles.md, Backup.md's table, the README. Fixture `Infrastructure/Discovery.cs` (twelve tests, REST port 10060): a search by custodian, date and words finds exactly the messages made to match, a word only in the body of a message over 1 MB among them, and IMAP `SEARCH TEXT` finds the same two of three messages by the same word; an archive hold keeps its copy from an address's erasure that takes the unheld one; an attachment criterion; a hold that keeps what an `EXPUNGE`, the retention sweeps, an account rule's Delete, a restore's replace and the account's deletion over REST and COM would have removed, found by a search and released; an IMAP `MOVE` out of a held account; a domain hold with its refused deletion and its refused move; a Sieve discard; a held zero-access mailbox's sealed copy, header-only hits and withheld export; a full restore refused under a hold; an export whose EML files, manifest, `SHA256SUMS` and mbox verify byte for byte and whose making and download are on the audit trail; the roles; a query refused with its reason; the 6097 schema probes. `API/Roles.cs` counts twenty-six permissions. **Thinner than the row asks:** no PST - EML and mbox, which a third-party EML-to-PST converter turns into PST, the manifest's digests showing what was converted; no sender or recipient criterion; no text from attachments but `text/*` parts, at most 1 MB of text a message; no export past 4 GB (no ZIP64 and no split parts) and no download past 500 MB, and nothing removes an export; one search or export at a time, on one server; a zero-access mailbox's ciphertext is not exported; 100,000 hits a search, 10,000 unsearched messages listed and 10,000 archived copies held by one request; no hold on the server's or a domain's rules, on spam and virus deletion before a message is the account's, on the delivery queue, on the confidential and password-protected stores or on archived copies with no index row; a hold the custodian can see; no saved query, no review tags, no redaction and no de-duplication across custodians; the backups are not searched and do not carry the cases; and the Control Panel reads and does not act. |
| ✅ | **Roles** (every admin console) | Beyond the one administrator and the domain-scoped API key: a help-desk role that resets passwords and nothing else, a read-only auditor, a domain administrator who signs in to the Deck for their domain. **Built 15 September 2026 on branch `roles` and merged with batch9 the same day - the audit trail records the role a change was made under, and the backup carries each account's role - then, on the owner's decisions after the integration review: the reports need `view_configuration` and `view_message_trace` both, since their senders and recipients are who mails whom (the route's second permission, held by `check-role-choke-point.py` to the auditor rule); the backup carries the custom roles and restores them before the accounts, and an assignment naming a role neither the archive nor the server has is dropped with the account's admin level lowered to an ordinary account's and named in the backup log; a role and a role assignment are on the audit trail in words, before and after; an unlock is on it too; and an event script's COM credential no longer outlives the script on the server thread it ran on. The review of those six closed what they left open: the archived admin level is withheld until the assignment is back, and the assignment goes back first thing after the account's row, so a restore that stops part-way leaves no account wider than its role; a role the restoring server already has is kept, never widened, with the difference named; the role checker pins the reports' second permission and the AND that asks for it; a level not given back reaches the error log (`HM6129`); the readable role rows carry no noise and lose no name; and every way a lockout is lifted is on the audit trail - `Account.UnlockMailbox`, which now asks `unlock_account` for anybody's mailbox but the caller's own, and the logon-failure list cleared over REST and over COM. And a custom role somebody holds is refused deletion - `409` over REST, saying how many accounts hold it, and that sentence shown by a new *Delete* on a custom role's row in the Deck - where deleting it had taken its assignments away and silently handed an account narrowed from server administrator the whole server back, and an account whose assigned role has come to hold nothing is an ordinary account rather than its admin level. Gated green with batch 10 on 16 September 2026: the create script builds at schema 6047 on SQL Server Compact, the Deck's script harness passes at 495 checks, and the thirty-four regression tests (twenty-nine in API/Roles.cs, five in Infrastructure/BackupAccountStores.cs) pass.** Four built-in roles - `serveradmin`, `domainadmin`, `helpdesk`, `auditor` - over twenty-two permissions, with custom roles made over REST, stored in `hm_roles` and `hm_roleassignments` (schema 6047), the built-in ones' permissions in the code so that a change to the table cannot hand anybody authority. One decision, `RoleManager::Allows`: `Authorize_` asks it for every REST route, the ~300 COM gates that ask `GetIsServerAdmin`/`GetIsDomainAdmin` now ask it for two permissions the narrow roles do not hold, and `build/check-role-choke-point.py` fails a route nobody classified and any route that would let an auditor write or stop it reading. Additive: an account with no assignment holds what its admin level always gave it, the upgrade writes no row, a read-only key is the auditor and a full one the server administrator. An account holding a role signs in to the Control Deck with its own password; the Deck and the Control Panel leave out what a role may not use; a help desk resets and unlocks from the Deck's account list and traces one whole address in its domain; the refusal for another domain's account reads the same whether it exists; no credential may reset the password of, or change, an account whose authority it does not take in, so a help desk cannot sign in as its domain administrator; and `*` lets a confined role reach every domain. Found on the way and closed: any account signed in over COM could reach another account's full object through a permission on a folder of its own, and set its password. Not in it: a Deck view of the message trace (there is none for anybody), the audit trail's own reading routes (the `alerts-audit` row's), and a Linux port of the fixture. [docs/Roles.md](hmailserver/docs/Roles.md). |
| ✅ | **SCIM provisioning** (Entra ID, Okta, Google) | **Built 24 September 2026, schema 6096. Not yet compiled or run against a bench: written in a worktree beside two other rows, it is compiled, built and gated when the branch is folded; every checker is green but the schema chain, which waits for 6095, the step before this one.** Accounts created, renamed, suspended and removed by the organisation's identity provider over SCIM 2.0, with the aliases and the lists: the LDAP backend authenticates; SCIM provisions. `/scim/v2` on the REST listener (`Common/Util/RestApiScim.cpp`, members of `RestApiServer` as the send API's are): `ServiceProviderConfig` (patch, filter with maxResults 200, changePassword and etag; not bulk and not sort; one scheme, oauthbearertoken), `Schemas` and `ResourceTypes` listing only what is kept, and `Users` and `Groups` with GET, POST, PUT, PATCH and DELETE - PATCH a method no route of the listener had taken before. Every answer the listener gives under `/scim/` is `application/scim+json` and every refusal RFC 7644's error document with `status` and `scimType`, including the 401, 403, 409, 429 and 500 `ProcessRequest_` gives before or around a handler, which a `/scim/` path now gets in SCIM's shape - all but the HTTP layer's own 400, 408 and 413, which come before the path is read; everything under `/scim/` is a SCIM route kind, a path SCIM does not serve included, so that its 404 is SCIM's too, `/Bulk`, `/Me` and `POST .search` are 501, and `sortBy` and `sortOrder` are ignored, as RFC 7644 allows a server that does not sort. **Who:** an API key and nothing else - `Authorize_` answers the administrator password, an account's password and a session 401 before the password's allow-everything - whose role holds the new permission **`provision`**, appended after `send_through_api` (the bit is the position, the token what `hm_roles` stores), held by serveradmin, settable on a custom role, and appended to serveradmin's `rolepermissions` row by the 6096 scripts and the create scripts; SCIM's reads need it as its writes do, the documented exception to `build/check-role-choke-point.py`'s auditor rule beside the key routes, since an auditor reads the same accounts at the account routes. A key confined to named domains provisions those alone: the SCIM kinds name no domain, so the handlers confine every listing, answer an account elsewhere with the same 404 as none, and refuse an address elsewhere with the very 400 a domain this server does not host gets; a key may not change or remove an account whose authority it does not take in (`RoleManager::Encompasses`). **That rule was fixed at its root on the way:** `Encompasses` took in every account for any grant holding `administer_server`, so a full key confined to one domain could set the password of an account there that administered another domain, audited every domain or was the server administrator by role, and sign in as it - over `PUT /api/v1/accounts`, the password reset and the role routes as well as here; it now judges such a grant by its list like any confined grant, and takes in nothing that reaches every domain. **The mapping:** a User is an account - id and userName the address, compared without regard to case; the first and last names, sixty characters each; displayName composed, and split at its last space when written alone to an account with no name yet - to one with names it is accepted and not kept; active the switch, whose clearing suspends and ends the browser sessions and drops the live connections of the sign-in list, as a new password does; emails the address (work, primary) and the aliases whose value it is (other), an alias added or removed through emails made or deleted; password write-only through the policy, the reuse history and the breach check, judged before anything is written and the old hash recorded only once the whole change is (`check-password-choice-sites.py` now takes the breach check through a call to a named site of the same file); externalId in the new `hm_accounts.accountexternalid` (nvarchar(255), indexed for the filter, carried by `Account`, `PersistentAccount`, the backup's `ExternalId` attribute and COM's `Account.ExternalId` at dispid 87). A Group is a distribution list - id and displayName the address, members its confirmed members; a member added is confirmed as an administrator's add is (a waiting or departed row confirmed, not doubled), a member removed has its row deleted, a member is never the list's own address or another list's, a confined key is judged only on the members it names - a member elsewhere is neither added nor removed by it - and a membership query is neither shown nor changed. Filters `userName eq`, `co` and `sw`, `externalId eq` (exact), `emails.value eq` (the address or an alias of it), `active eq` and, for groups, `displayName eq`; anything else 400 invalidFilter; startIndex, count, attributes and excludedAttributes. PATCH takes `add` and `replace` on active, userName, externalId, displayName, name and its two parts, password and emails, `remove` on externalId, displayName and the name (a remove of active, userName or password is 400), Entra's `emails[type eq "work"].value` (the account's own address, never another) and path-less replace with dotted keys, capitalised ops and `"False"` as a boolean, applied in order. **A change is all or nothing:** everything - the aliases against their saves and each domain's limit of aliases, the rename's target and the mailbox in use, the account's own save, the password - is judged before anything is written; a write that fails after the first puts back what the change wrote, and a rename that happened stays and is answered 500 naming the new id (HM7221), with HM7223 for anything that could not be put back; an attribute of the core schema this server does not keep, or an extension's, is accepted and not kept, and one that names nothing is refused by name. meta carries resourceType, location and a weak ETag hashed from the representation answered, and If-Match is honoured on PUT, PATCH and DELETE (412); **no created and no lastModified**, since neither table keeps a time. A rename - a PUT of a new userName, or Entra's PATCH of it - is `MailboxMove::Move`, refused 409 while the mailbox is in use, the new id answered and the old address kept as an alias of type other until a later PUT or PATCH leaves it out; a rename onto one of the account's own aliases takes that alias's place. DELETE removes the account first, then the aliases emails answered and the address's row in every distribution list, which `PersistentAccount`'s delete leaves alone (HM7224 for what could not be). Every write reaches the audit trail under `key:<id>` with nothing written for it here, a rename as the move's entry and the account's update. [Scim.md](hmailserver/docs/Scim.md), with the Entra and Okta set-up and the mappings to remove; a paragraph in PasskeysAndSingleSignOn.md, the row in Roles.md. Fixture `API/Scim.cs` (twenty tests, REST port 9936), each with its negative control - among them a confined full key refused a domain administrator of another domain, an auditor of every domain and the server administrator, over SCIM and over the account routes; a change refused whole at a full domain's alias limit; a live IMAP session dropped by a suspension and by a new password - and `BackupAccountStores.TheExternalIdComesBack`; `API/Roles.cs` counts twenty-four permissions. **Thinner than the row asks:** no bulk, no sorting, no `/Me`, no POST `.search`, no filter with `and` or `or`; no enterprise extension and no attribute beyond the mapping (a title, a telephone number, a manager are accepted and not kept); no groups but distribution lists - the sharing groups are folder-permission principals, not identity groups; not a SCIM client; a group's externalId is not kept; a list's address changes only inside its domain; a rename changes the id, and an identity provider that keeps the old id is answered 404; an identity provider that replaces emails removes the aliases it does not list, one an administrator made included; nothing but SCIM and COM shows externalId - not the Control Panel, the Deck, the REST account routes, hmctl or PowerShell; a group's rename keeps nothing at the old address; untested are the 405, `/Me` and `POST .search` 501s, the SCIM-shaped 429, 409 and 500 from `ProcessRequest_` and the session CSRF 403, If-Match on the group routes, a 409 of a rename while in use, `emails[type eq "other"].value` and a replace of emails, attributes and excludedAttributes, password reuse and the breach check's 503, a group PUT and a group rename, COM's refusal of a long or multi-line ExternalId and the 255-character limit on a save, and the part-way failures that the database alone can cause; and Google Workspace, which provisions only its own catalogue's applications, has no set-up here. |
| ✅ | **SAML, beside OIDC** | **Built 24 September 2026, no schema step. every checker is green.** The identity providers that still speak only SAML 2.0 - ADFS, and Entra ID or Okta set up the SAML way - sign people in to the webmail and the Control Deck, ending in OIDC's one-use ticket, OIDC's `POST /api/v1/session` and OIDC's mapping rules, which moved into `OidcSignIn::MapAddress` so that both protocols call one function (OIDC's behaviour unchanged, its log line included). **A cryptographic-parser row first and a sign-in row second**, because a SAML Response's signature cannot be verified without canonicalising the XML it covers and the tree could not: `Common/Util/XmlDocument` is a strict, namespace-aware UTF-8 reader that keeps the prefixes, the declarations where they were written, attributes by namespace and text node by node, and refuses a DOCTYPE, any entity but the five predefined, a processing instruction, an attribute written twice or under two prefixes, an undeclared prefix, the `xml` prefix bound elsewhere or a prefix bound to the `xmlns` namespace, and a malformed, overlong or surrogate UTF-8 sequence, with ceilings of 64 levels, 20,000 elements, 256 attributes on one element, 128 namespace declarations in the document, 512 characters in a namespace name and 1 MB, comments dropped and the text either side joined;
