`Common/Util/XmlSignature` verifies exactly one enveloped signature - exc-c14n, one Reference naming the element's own ID (written once in the document), the transforms exactly enveloped-signature then exc-c14n, SHA-256, rsa-sha256 or ecdsa-sha256, strict base64, KeyInfo ignored and the key the stored certificate's, the signature over SignedInfo checked before the signed element is canonicalised and its digest compared - and hands back the signed bytes re-read, which is all the caller uses (`XmlSignatureTester`: vectors .NET's `SignedXml` signed, RSA and ECDSA P-256, verifying, every single-byte change to them refused, and SHA-1, HMAC, XSLT, XPath, base64, an empty Reference, a second signature, an ID written twice, canonicalisation with comments, a PrefixList of 33, an InclusiveNamespaces that is not empty, an ECDSA value of odd length that is not the r and s halves, and a 1024-bit RSA certificate refused by name). `Common/Util/SamlSignIn` is a Web Browser SSO service provider: `GET /portal/saml/start[?deck=1]` sends the browser to the identity provider with an unsigned AuthnRequest, raw DEFLATE (the vendored zlib, window bits -15) and base64 in the redirect, and sets the `hmsaml` cookie (HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax, `Path=/portal/saml`, ten minutes; Secure over TLS, or behind a proxy on the loopback that says `X-Forwarded-Proto: https`); `POST /portal/saml/acs` takes the HTTP-POST binding's form, at most 256 KB (`IsLargeRequest_` grants the route and the new `LargeRequestCeiling_` holds it to its own ceiling and the ordinary timer, through a per-route ceiling `HttpServer` gained), and verifies one Assertion directly under the Response, every signature the Response's or the Assertion's own, the Assertion signed itself unless `saml_require_signed_assertion` is off (then the Response's signature is enough - both ways tested), Destination and Recipient the ACS, InResponseTo an issued, unused request coming back with its RelayState (ten minutes, one use, server-side) and repeated by the signed SubjectConfirmationData, both Issuers, a bearer confirmation, the Conditions' NotBefore/NotOnOrAfter with a minute of skew, every AudienceRestriction naming this server, an AuthnStatement and the assertion's ID not seen before; what it verified is kept under a one-use token for two minutes and the browser sent on (303) to `GET /portal/saml/finish`, which **goes on only when the browser holds the cookie the start gave for that request** - so a Response carried to another person's browser, the login forgery the request binding alone cannot see, is refused there - and only then maps the address (the emailAddress NameID or the one value of `saml_email_attribute`), so no mailbox is made for a Response that ends in the wrong browser, and mints the ticket; the page a refusal goes back to is chosen by the server's record of the sign-in and never by the Response. `GET /saml/metadata` answers this service provider's metadata. Nine hm_settings rows, REST and the Deck only, as OIDC's are: `saml_idp_entity_id`, `saml_idp_sso_url`, `saml_idp_certificate` (write-only, one or two PEM certificates through `GatewayEncryption::AcceptableKeyText`, stored normalised and within the setting's 4,000 characters; `saml_idp_certificate_fingerprints` answers SHA-256, SHA-1 and expiry, and the name is withheld by `AuditTrail::IsSecretName` - the fragment `idpcertificate` - so that the list of what is changed from stock answers `(set)` and the audit trail `(changed)`, and the certificate is answered back nowhere), `saml_sp_entity_id` (default `<base>/saml`), `saml_email_attribute`, `saml_require_signed_assertion` (on), `saml_auto_provision` (off), `saml_administrators` and `saml_button_label` (default Sign in with SAML, said in each page's language; its own words so that the SAML button never reads as the OIDC one when one identity provider serves both); `signin-methods` gains `saml` and `saml_label`, and the webmail and the Deck a second button. a Response started in one browser and finished in another, with that browser's cookie and with none, refused, the token spent and no mailbox made, beside each finished in its own; the email claim, two values and markup, and an Okta-shaped double signature with a PrefixList; the Deck by the list and provisioning on and off; an unsigned Response, another key carrying its own certificate in KeyInfo, both signatures with either bad, SHA-1, XSLT, an empty Reference, a Response-only signature both ways and a rolled-over certificate; the wrapping attack with the same ID and with another and two assertions (each refused by the count of assertions), an Assertion not directly under the Response, a stray signature and each ID written twice; a status other than Success, InResponseTo not issued and issued for another sign-in, a SubjectConfirmationData answering another sign-in, Destination, Recipient, each Issuer under the other right, a confirmation that is not bearer, an expired confirmation, Conditions not yet valid and expired and each side of the minute of skew, another audience and a second AudienceRestriction, no AuthnStatement, IdP-initiated and EncryptedAssertion; a DOCTYPE, a signed Response of about 170 KB taken, bodies of 300 KB and 1.1 MB answered 413, and a NameID of `user@domain<!---->.evil` read whole; the assertion-ID replay cache reached by a second sign-in, and the two-hundred-entry table evicting its oldest; the settings' refusals (an elliptic curve of 160 bits among them) and round trips, the certificate absent from the settings, the list of what is changed from stock and the audit trail, and the metadata without a KeyDescriptor. The Deck and webmail harnesses check the button, its words, and that with single sign-on switched off neither button stays. **Thinner than the row, by decision or by fact:** no COM property and no Control Panel page (OIDC has neither); the finish's cookie can be planted by whoever can set cookies for this server's host - a sibling subdomain serving pages they control - so the doc says not to serve anybody else's pages under the webmail's parent domain; a second sign-in started in the same browser replaces the cookie, and the first is refused at the finish; the ten-minute and two-minute expiries are read in the code and not tested, since a test would wait them out; no single logout, no encrypted assertions (refused with a sentence), no signed requests, no metadata fetch, no artifact binding, no attribute queries, no IdP-initiated sign-in, one identity provider; SAML has no email_verified, so the attribute the address comes from must be one the directory controls, which the doc says and this server cannot check; ECDSA is verified in the internal tester and not in the fixture (.NET Framework's SignedXml signs RSA only); what fits in 4,000 characters was measured - two 2048-bit certificates with room to spare, two ordinary 4096-bit ones (about 1,800 to 1,900 characters each) too, and two with long subjects not, the refusal naming the stored length; the wrapping tests are refused by the count of assertions before the verifier runs, and that the element used is the signed bytes read back is held by the internal tester alone; and the canonicalisation examples that are not encoded are Canonical XML 3.1, which needs processing instructions and comments outside the root, and 3.5, which needs a DTD - both of which the reader refuses - and 3.7, an XPath node-set, which this server never canonicalises. | ✅ | **Delegated mailboxes announced to Outlook** (Exchange automapping) | **Built 22 September 2026, no schema change, no new setting. Not yet run against a bench: the fixtures compile, every checker is green and both builds say Build succeeded; the regression suite runs when the branch is gated.** Built as two halves, because what the row asks for cannot be had as written. Exchange's automapping is an `AlternativeMailbox` element in the Autodiscover POX answer, which Outlook reads for an Exchange (MAPI) account and ignores for an IMAP one - and this server speaks IMAP. So, half one: `WebServicesServer::HandleAutodiscover_` names, after its three `Protocol` blocks, one `AlternativeMailbox` (Type Delegate, DisplayName the owner's person name or else the address, SmtpAddress and OwnerSmtpAddress the owner's, XML-escaped) per account that has opened a folder to the address asked about with a right it can look the folder up and read it with - the owners `ACLManager::GetOwnersDelegatingTo` names, one walk shared with the `shared` list of GET `/api/v1/me/folders`, which now draws its owners from it; the public folders are not among them. The elements are answered only to a request that authenticated over HTTPS, HTTP Basic through the DAV logon (`Dav::Authenticate`: the same password schemes, app passwords, per-name lockout and auto-ban accounting as IMAP), as the very address it asks about; no credentials, or another account's, get exactly the old answer, a wrong password is 401 with the Basic challenge and the connection closed, a credential over plain HTTP is 403 before it is looked at. Half two, the mechanism that works: the IMAP "Other Users" namespace, `#Users` in NAMESPACE and the owner's folders under `#Users.owner@domain` in LIST, which Thunderbird and Outlook's IMAP code walk and show under the account by themselves, in the tree since 21 August 2026 and proven in `RegressionTests/IMAP/SharedMailboxes.cs` - one test added there for the piece that was missing, a grant revoked by the owner vanishing from LIST and SELECT. **Thinner than the row asks:** nothing here was measured in Outlook, because there is no Outlook on either bench; Outlook sends credentials to Autodiscover only after a 401, which this server never returns for an unauthenticated Autodiscover request, so an Outlook IMAP profile never sees the element - and would ignore it if it did; what puts the delegated mailbox into its folder tree is the Other Users namespace, not this element. Tests: seven in `RegressionTests/API/ClientDiscovery.cs` (the POX answer's shape and its agreement with the autoconfig ports; the elements to the delegate alone, escaped, in id order, none to the unauthenticated, to a bystander, to the delegate asking about somebody else or for a lookup-only grant, none for a public folder, gone when revoked; a wrong password refused as the DAV logon refuses it and locking the name after three as the POP3 lockout test proves it, the neighbour untouched; a credential over plain HTTP refused before it is checked). README and IMPLEMENTATION-NOTES say the two halves and what was not measured. **Reviewed adversarially on the same branch (no must-fix, eight should-fix) and corrected:** the DAV logon the element adopts had never asked the compromise hold, so an account on hold was refused by IMAP, POP3, SMTP and REST and honoured by Autodiscover, CalDAV and CardDAV - `Dav::Authenticate` asks it now, for all three; only a Basic header is a credential, a Negotiate, NTLM or Bearer header getting the settings as before rather than a 401 Outlook would answer with a prompt; a sign-in is recorded as its own `autodiscover` protocol, not as `dav`; the README says that behind the TLS-terminating proxy it recommends the lockout and the auto-ban see the proxy's address, that app passwords, auto-ban accounting and a TLS listener are as `Dav::Authenticate` does and not tested here, and no longer claims a client behaviour nobody measured (a subscription-driven folder pane shows what the owner subscribed to); the dead close line went. Three tests added: the held account refused 401 by Autodiscover and by CardDAV and let back in by the compromise unlock; the schemes that are not credentials; the 401 closing an HTTP/1.1 connection while a 200 keeps it - and the delegations test asserts the `shared` owners of GET `/api/v1/me/folders` equal the elements before and after the revocation (the route had no test of `shared` before), and the IMAP revocation test asserts the NO. The wiki's client page is the maintainer's. |
| ✅ | **An organisation CA for S/MIME** (Exchange with AD CS) | **Built 24 September 2026, schema 6094 to 6095, and its adversarial review applied the same day (five must-fix, nineteen should-fix, ten notes). Not yet run against a bench: written in a worktree beside two other rows, it is built and gated when the branch is folded; every checker is green, the page's certificate request is held to OpenSSL by `build/check-portal-smime.js`, and the first commit's non-COM units compiled under MSVC /WX in the integrator's mirror.** A domain's own **S/MIME authority**, created in the Control Deck's domain editor (`POST /api/v1/domains/{domain}/smime-ca`): a self-signed P-256 root (RSA-3072 on request), CN "{domain} S/MIME Authority", CA:TRUE pathlen:0, keyCertSign and cRLSign, emailProtection and a critical name constraint to the domain's own addresses, ten years, its private key in `hm_smimeca` under the platform's secret store itself - DPAPI with the machine's key on Windows, the stored-secret key file on Linux - and never the legacy Blowfish scheme, whatever `ProtectStoredSecretsWithDPAPI` says: when the store is unavailable no authority is created. **The person's key never leaves the browser**: `PortalSmime.js` makes an RSA-2048 (default) or P-256 key pair and a PKCS#10 request, wraps the private key under the account password as an import does, and `POST /api/v1/me/smime/enroll` - the account's own sign-in, not an app password or a support session - signs the request (its key RSA 2048-8192 or P-256/P-384, its self-signature verified, exactly one ASCII address of at most 128 characters, the account's own or an active alias of its own domain pointing at it) for one year with the account's name, emailAddress and rfc822Name, emailProtection, digitalSignature with keyEncipherment (RSA) or keyAgreement (EC), SKI and AKI, and a CRL distribution point and caIssuers URL naming the root by its SHA-256 fingerprint under the new `SmimeAuthorityBaseUrl` setting when it is set (neither while it is empty, and the doc says what that costs). **Two phases**: the certificate is pending - not answered to colleagues, superseding nothing - until its key arrives in the account's own store (or `POST .../issued/{serial}/confirm` for a client that keeps it elsewhere); then, unless `keep`, the address's earlier certificates are revoked as superseded, and a store that is refused leaves them valid. Five enrolments per account a day, ten valid at once. Every certificate is recorded in `hm_smimeissued`; a colleague's confirmed, valid ones are answered by address (`GET /api/v1/me/directory/{address}/smime`: the reader's own domain, an active account that still owns the address, the opt-out not asked, 404 while the directory is off), and the compose page, when a message to a colleague is to be encrypted and no usable certificate is stored, fetches and keeps every one and encrypts to all. The server marks such a certificate (`hm_smimekeys.smimeauthority`) from the SHA-256 of its own bytes and answers where the authority's record has it now (`authority_status`), so the page neither signs with nor encrypts to a revoked or expired one, looks a colleague up again, and after a second enrolment signs with the new certificate. The root (PEM and DER) and a signed X.509 v2 CRL (nextUpdate a week, made again after a revocation and at most daily) are served over plain HTTP by the web services listener at `/smime-ca/{domain}.pem` and `.crl` and `/smime-ca/{fingerprint}.*`, never by REST; the webmail's chain check trusts the domain's current root and a retired one while its certificates live, refuses a revoked or unrecorded leaf from the authority's own record, and says "certified by your organisation" on the badge. Revocation by an administrator, by the person, by a confirmed enrolment, by the account's deletion (cessationOfOperation, the account id cleared, each row named on the audit trail, nothing written for an account issued none) and by the address's erasure (revoked, the address and the certificate taken off the row, the serial kept for the CRL); the first revocation's date and reason stand. Rotation keeps the old root published and trusted until its last certificate expires, carries the serial counter on and wipes the retired key afterwards; a renamed domain's enrolments are refused until it is rotated; the authority and what it issued are in the configuration backup, every restored value checked and re-encoded; deleting a domain deletes both tables' rows by cascade; both tables are on the audit trail with the private key withheld. Surfaces: the webmail's Security page gains **From your organisation** (Get a certificate from {domain}, the key choice with the P-256 sentence, keep, the issued list with Revoke, and the sentence when the domain has none; 23 catalogues, `portal-script-test.js`); the Deck's domain editor gains **S/MIME authority** (create, download the root, rotate, the issued list with revoke and its reason, the key-does-not-open warning; 20 catalogues, `deck-script-test.js`); **no COM and no Control Panel**, by decision - the Control Panel reads over COM alone. Fixture `API/RestApiSmimeAuthority.cs` (REST port 9946, web services 9947). **Thinner than the row:** certificates are issued on the person's enrolment in the webmail, not pushed to every account; no AD CS integration, no OCSP, no key escrow and so no recovery of mail encrypted to a lost key; clients other than this webmail learn of a revocation only from the CRL, and only with a base URL set; a colleague's page stops encrypting to a revoked certificate at its next reading of the store, not the moment it is revoked; a root rotated because its key was exposed stays trusted for the certificates it signed until they expire, unless each is revoked and the root taken off the clients; the webmail cannot encrypt to a P-256 certificate; one address per certificate, not a domain alias's form of it nor another domain's alias; a renamed account or domain needs its people to enrol again, and a renamed domain a rotation; no automatic renewal; no Control Panel view; the CRL is made per process and cached in memory; the correspondents' certificates other accounts keep (`hm_smimekeys`) are not reached by the address erasure (the gap row on erasure names them). [SmimeAuthority.md](hmailserver/docs/SmimeAuthority.md). |
| ✅ | **Gateway encryption to partner domains** | **Built 24 September 2026, schema 6094, and corrected the same day by its adversarial review (four must-fix, twelve should-fix, nine notes, every one applied). Not yet compiled or run against a bench: written in a worktree beside four other rows, it is compiled, built and gated when the branch is folded; every checker is green but the schema chain, which waits for the steps before this one.** Mail to a named partner domain is encrypted at the server with the partner's S/MIME or OpenPGP key, whatever the sender's client did. The remote domain policy gains three fields - `policyencrypt` (0 none, 1 OpenPGP, 2 S/MIME), `policyencryptkey` (the partner's armoured OpenPGP public key or PEM certificates) and `policyencryptfallback` (0 refuse, 1 send unencrypted) - and `ExternalDelivery` groups a server's recipients by the policy that encrypts them, so that a batch is one partner's or nobody's, and writes the partner's copy for that batch alone, from the queued message, which is never rewritten: a recipient elsewhere in the same submission, behind the same smart host included, gets the message as it was. `Common/Util/GatewayEncryption` does the sealing - PGP/MIME through the zero-access mailbox's `OpenPgpWriter`, to a subkey whose binding signature verifies against the primary key (`ChooseEncryptionKey` verifies bindings and subkey revocations since this row: RSA, Ed25519 and ECDSA on the NIST curves), and S/MIME enveloped-data through OpenSSL's CMS with AES-256-CBC and the content key by RSAES-OAEP, or by ECDH with the X9.63 KDF over SHA-256 - with the whole original under the key and a header that is the original's less the `Content-*` fields, `MIME-Version` and the DKIM and ARC signatures, **the Subject readable**; the copy is DKIM-signed and ARC-sealed afresh by `DKIMSigner` (a copy over 50 MB goes unsigned, and the log says so). A message already encrypted end to end goes as it is - judged from the CMS content type or the OpenPGP packets, never from the label; a signed one, or one only labelled encrypted, is encrypted, signature inside. One key per partner domain. Whatever the method, a paste carrying private-key material anywhere in it, of either kind, or over 60,000 characters is refused and nothing is stored, and the audit trail withholds the column's text; a save that changes the key or the method refuses, by name, a key that cannot be read, an expired or not-yet-valid certificate, RSA under 2048 bits, a curve under 256 and an OpenPGP key with no bound encryption subkey, and takes one that names no address in the domain with a warning; a save that changes neither does not judge the key again, so the fallback can still be switched once a key has expired; and the key is judged again at every delivery. What cannot be encrypted (a key expired since, a message over 50 MB) is refused permanently with `5.7.10` and a delivery status notification naming the policy, or, when the fallback says so, sent unencrypted and logged; a failure of the server's own (a locked file, a full temporary directory, no memory) is deferred with `4.3.0` whatever the fallback. The copy is made after every shaping deferral, the MX lookup and MTA-STS, and measured against the size ceiling itself; one application log line per encrypted batch and an `encrypted` (or `unencrypted`) message-trace event per recipient, naming the key - for OpenPGP the primary's fingerprint and the subkey's. Surfaces: REST `encrypt` (a PUT that leaves it out keeps it), a write-only `encrypt_key` (a PUT that leaves it out keeps the key held; white space removes it) and `encrypt_fallback` on the policy routes, the listing answering `encrypt_key_fingerprint`, `encrypt_key_subkey_fingerprint`, `encrypt_key_expires`, `encrypt_key_subject`, `encrypt_key_problem` and `encrypt_key_warning` and never the key, an update saved as a copy and the collection read again; COM `RemoteDomainPolicy.Encrypt`, `EncryptKey` (answers empty), `EncryptFallback`, `EncryptKeyFingerprint`, `EncryptKeyExpires` and `EncryptKeySummary` at ids 20-25, with the `eRemoteEncryption` enum, a refused `Save` leaving the running record untouched - the setters write the object's own copy of it - and reading the object back as the database holds it; the Deck's policy editor (the key box write-only and never filled, the facts read-only, an **Encryption** column with a **cannot encrypt** mark) and the Control Panel's policy dialog (the paste box and **The key held**); the XML backup carries the key, one expired since included. Fixture `SMTP/GatewayEncryption.cs` (fifteen tests; REST port 9976, simulators on 9977 and 9978), and `GatewayEncryptionTester` and `OpenPgpWriterTester` in the internal tests. **Thinner than the row, by decision:** one gateway key per partner domain and none per recipient address; no key discovery (WKD, LDAP) - the administrator pastes the key; no signing at the gateway; no decryption of replies, since this server holds no gateway key of its own; the Subject and the rest of the header are not hidden; RSAES-OAEP with its default SHA-1 parameters, which every S/MIME client reads, rather than SHA-256 ones; a message relayed for somebody else loses its author's DKIM signature and any ARC chain on the outside, keeping both inside; and the partner's key is not on the Control Panel's External setup page. [RemoteDomains.md](hmailserver/docs/RemoteDomains.md), "Gateway encryption". |
| ✅ | **A user's data, exported** (GDPR article 20) | **Done 17 September 2026.** Everything the server holds on a person as one .zip - every folder with mail as an mbox, the address book as vCard, the calendars, the webmail's preferences, the active Sieve script, the log lines naming the address and an account.xml in the backup's form with the rules, app passwords, fetch accounts, S/MIME keys, files and the rest - written to the server's disk by `Account.ExportArchive`, `POST /api/v1/accounts/{address}/export` with `archive`, `hmctl privacy export` and `Export-HmAccountArchive`, and downloaded by the operator (`GET /api/v1/accounts/{address}/export.zip`, the Deck's account row, the Control Panel's account dialog) or by the person from their own Settings page (`GET /api/v1/me/export.zip`), up to 500 MB as a download. The erasure now reaches the rows naming the address in other people's address books and first-contact memory, sweeps the archive tree file by file so a copy under legal hold keeps its file and its row, and - asked by name (`Utilities.EraseAddressLogLines`, `include_logs`, `hmctl privacy erase --logs`, `Remove-HmAddressTraces -IncludeLogs`) - rewrites the log files so every occurrence reads [erased]. | ✅ | **A new installation is secure before anyone configures it** | **Built 25 September 2026 on branch `row-secure-defaults`, with no schema step and no new setting. Not yet compiled or run against a bench: written in a worktree beside four other rows, it is compiled, built and gated when the branch is folded into a batch.** The evidence pack's review of 25 September found it the biggest default gap: a new installation listened on 25, 587, 110 and 143 with connection security *none*, and neither shipped IP range required TLS for authentication, so SMTP `AUTH LOGIN`, IMAP `LOGIN` and POP3 `USER`/`PASS` from another machine crossed the network in the clear until an administrator changed it. `hMailServer.exe --apply-new-install-defaults` (`hmailserver` the same on Linux; `Common/Application/NewInstallDefaults`) makes a self-signed certificate for the machine - RSA 2048, 825 days, the host as subject and subjectAltName, the key written readable by the service's account and the administrators only (`ProtectedFile`) under `<data>/Certificates` - records it as *Self-signed (made at installation)*, gives it to the four listeners with STARTTLS **optional** (required on 25 it would refuse mail from servers that cannot start TLS; required on the others it would refuse this computer's own clients), and sets the *Internet* range to require TLS for authentication while *My computer* is left alone - so a password from another machine is refused before STARTTLS (`530`, `BAD`, `-ERR`, and AUTH not advertised) and taken after it. It changes **only a database this installation created**, never one that existed before, whatever its size: COM's `CreateInternalDatabase` and `CreateExternalDatabase` and `hmailserver --create-database` mark the database they make with the `hm_settings` row `NewInstallDefaultsPending` (not a setting; kept out of the backup), `SetDefaultDatabase`'s attach and every upgrade do not, and the verb requires the mark - at the Linux unit's start, one made within a day of the machine's clock either way, since it carries its time; a run by hand has no deadline - clears it once it has decided, and still asks the create script's shape - the four listeners without TLS, the two ranges with their options, no certificate, no account, no domain, no route - exiting 5, changing nothing, on anything else, and 2, judging nothing and keeping the mark, when the database cannot be read well enough to tell; a listener that could not be given the certificate leaves the range as it was, so no listener is left that nobody outside can sign in to. **Where it runs:** the Windows installer, after the database step of a new installation and before the profile and the service's start, with the service stopped (exit 4 while it runs) - on the DBSetup branch the mark tells a created database from an attached one; on Linux `--create-database` leaves `.new-installation` in the configured `DataFolder` beside the database it made, and says whether the unit will find it, and the unit's `ExecStartPre=- ... --if-marked` - with no file, it looks in the same configuration's `DataFolder` - answers it at the first start (one database attempt, `TimeoutStartSec=300`) and removes it, opening nothing at any other; the verb refuses root, whose key the service could not read. An upgrade is never marked, so an upgraded server keeps what it has. **The create scripts are unchanged**, so the benches are too: they are made by `upgrade-test-database.ps1`, `bench-setup.ps1` and `--create-database`, none of which runs the installer or the unit, `PerformBasicSetup` writes the classic ports and ranges back through `SetDefault` before every test, and the suite signs in from 127.0.0.1. **The checklist** has a tenth item, *Passwords only over TLS* (`sign_in_tls`), for every server: `sign_in_plain` for each range outside the loopback that takes a password before TLS on listeners that all offer STARTTLS with a certificate, naming them, and the first finding to carry a **fix** - the `PUT /api/v1/ipranges/{id}` with `require_tls_for_auth` - which the Deck offers as a button and the Control Panel as its IP ranges page; `sign_in_no_tls`, with no fix and a button to the Deck's Ports view, while one of those listeners has no TLS to start, since requiring it then would lock every client out; *TLS on the submission port* says `submission_self_signed` while the port holds the installation's certificate, and the ACME client now gives its certificate to the listeners holding that one as well as to those with none. Error codes HM7510 and HM7511. [SecureDefaults.md](hmailserver/docs/SecureDefaults.md); fixtures `Security/NewInstallDefaults` (the verb against the bench with the service stopped, a password from the bench's own private address refused on SMTP, IMAP and POP3 before STARTTLS and taken after it, 127.0.0.1 unchanged, exit 5 on a second run) and `API/RestApiSetupChecklist` (the item, its fix, the self-signed finding); the Deck's button in `deck-script-test.js`; the Linux packaging job checks the first start. **Not covered:** the AppImage and the container, which run no unit - each runs the verb by hand, as the document says (the Arch package's unit is the same, and its first start after `--create-database` does the same) - and a certificate a person trusts, which is the operator's: ACME, or their own. The checks the installer smoke test is to add are listed in the document. **The review of 25 September** found one must-fix - a small server in service passed the young-server test, so the DBSetup attach branch and a Linux reinstall onto an existing database would have locked out its plaintext sign-ins - answered by the create-path mark and the no-account rule above; and should-fixes answered in the same follow-up: the fix withheld while a listener has no TLS, root refused on Linux, the document's checks put in the future tense, the fixture's teardown guarded step by step with its restart in a finally, preflight naming a leftover `Certificates` rather than reserving it, the installer logging every result and saying an exit 5 on the built-in branch, a local service account written `.\name` resolved, and the profiles' now-redundant step reworded. **Its re-check** found one must-fix - `--create-database` wrote the marker under the configured `DataFolder` while the unit looked for it at a fixed path, so a new installation with its data moved was never secured though the command said it would be - answered by `--if-marked` without a file, `--create-database` saying "secures it" only when systemd, the unit and the packaged configuration are there (and naming the drop-in a `DataFolder` outside `ReadWritePaths` needs), and notes answered in the same commit: a read that fails is not a verdict (exit 2, the mark and the file kept), no domain and no route, a mark COM could not write reported as HM7512, the installer offering the verb by hand only while the mark is untouched, the port deletes in preflight and the fixture bounded, and a listener bound to a loopback address only no longer counted for a range outside it. **The next re-check** found two must-fixes, both a passing database failure spending the one decision: a domain table that could not be read counted the sample domain's mailboxes as the administrator's (exit 5), now asked on its own before the account count; and a listener list read short while the defaults were applied would have required TLS on the Internet range with no listener offering it, now read before anything is made (exit 2 when it cannot be) with the range set only once all four listeners offer STARTTLS. And should-fixes: a mark more than a day from the clock, either way, not acted on at the unit's start and kept for a run by hand, which has no such limit (an image first booted days after its `--create-database` is secured that way), the installer saying that an exit 1 which changed nothing can be run again, the unit's step before every start reading only `DataFolder` and `ProgramFolder` (`IniFileSettings::ReadDataDirectory`, one resolution with `LoadSettings`) so that the loader's reports are not logged twice at every start, and preflight writing the stock ports and ranges back whatever became of its deletes. |
| ✅ | **Session policies** | **Built 16 September 2026 on branch `sessions-and-devices` (schema 6050); gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** Four policies, each a row of `hm_settings` (`SessionIdleSeconds`, `SessionLifetimeSeconds`, `SessionOnePerDevice`, `SessionBindToAddress`) and a column of `hm_domains`, resolved into one answer by `SessionPolicies` and applied to every signed-in browser - the webmail, the portal and the Deck - at every request rather than at sign-in, so a shortened timeout reaches the sessions that exist. Every one of the eight values is 0 after the upgrade, and 0 means *not set*: the thirty minutes idle and twelve hours in all this server has always enforced apply where nothing is, so nobody is locked out and nobody gets a longer session than they had. A domain's value wins where set; its two switches take three values (0 inherit, 1 on, 2 off) so a domain can say *not here*; the administrator's own session belongs to no domain. Settable over COM (`Settings.Session*`, `Utilities.SetDomainSessionPolicy`), REST (the settings group's four keys, a domain's four fields, and `GET /api/v1/session-policy` and `/domains/{domain}/session-policy` for the resolved document), the Deck (the settings page, a fifth group of the domain dialog), `hmctl settings set` / `domain update` and `Set-HmSetting` / `Set-HmDomain`. **Not built:** a new sign-in from a new device announced to the recovery address - there is no recovery address yet (section 12's recovery row). **Gated:** `RegressionTests.API.RestApiDevices` (nine tests: a session past its idle timeout refused, past its lifetime refused however busy, refused from another address when bound, one session per device, a domain's policy winning, and an upgrade leaving every policy unset among them) ran green in that gate. [docs/DevicesAndSessions.md](hmailserver/docs/DevicesAndSessions.md). The row as written: Idle timeout, absolute lifetime, one session per device, and a session bound to the network it began on, as settings; a new sign-in from a new device announced to the recovery address. |

### Deliverability and the mail path

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **IP warm-up** (every sending service) | **Built 24 September 2026, schema 6093, with the answer to its adversarial review the same day. Not yet run against a bench: nothing was compiled on the branch - the integrator compiles, builds and gates it - and every checker that runs without a build is green.** A schedule is a row of `hm_warmup` for one outbound address - the local address a delivery binds, from a global rule's bind-to-address action or `SMTPDeliveryBindToIP`, the first of the remote's family, as `GetLocalAddress_` chooses it - with its start (a UTC date, never later than today), its plan and a switch; `hm_warmupcounts` holds per schedule, UTC day and provider the recipients `sent` and `deferred` with the ceiling and the warm-up day in force. The provider is the one the batch's first mail host belongs to - the host its address was chosen for - never the recipient's domain (`SMTP/OutboundProviders`: Google, Microsoft, Yahoo, Apple, Proton by fixed host-name suffixes, and `other`). The plans are fixed tables (`WarmupCeiling` is `IpWarmup::Ceiling`): standard 50, 100, 200, 500, 1,000, 2,000, 5,000, then 10,000 to day 10, 20,000 to 14, 50,000 to 21, 100,000 to 30 and none after; gentle half of each over twice the days (sixty); fast twice each over half the days (fifteen); `other` at five times. Enforced in `DeliverToSingleDomain_` after DNS and MTA-STS: an address with an enabled schedule and a provider with a ceiling today reserves the batch's recipients of the day's room - the ceiling less what was sent less what deliveries in flight hold, those held in memory only - and holds the rest through `DeferBatchWithoutCounting_` (`4.4.5`, counted as deferred): to just after the next midnight UTC when the day's ceiling is reached, one to five minutes when deliveries in flight hold the room; when the attempts are over the reservation is settled, what was delivered added to sent, so a refused connection moves nothing. A later host that would be reached from another warming address is skipped. `RescheduleDelivery_` writes the log line and the report's text from the path the message takes, and a hold longer than the retry interval that is counted past the retry span keeps its own length, so each counted try lands on a new day - the shaping row's one-to-five-minute deferrals are never longer than an interval and are unchanged. The counts are written through under one lock and read again after `Application::Reinitialize`. `GET`/`POST /api/v1/warmup`, `PUT`/`DELETE /api/v1/warmup/{id}` (a PUT cannot move a schedule to another address), `GET /api/v1/warmup/{id}/history` (the stored day and ceiling, days with nothing delivered or held left out) under `view_queue` and `manage_queue`, refused to domain-confined credentials, the schedules on the audit trail and in the configuration backup beside the remote domain policies; `in_bind_setting` says whether the setting binds the address as a delivery reads it. The Deck's queue page gains a Warm-up box - per schedule the day, per provider sent against the ceiling and deferred, Edit and Add opening a small editor - in twenty catalogues, with the harness, and the page stands when the listing fails. `docs/IpWarmup.md` with the providers and the three plans as tables, how long a backlog is carried, and what is not tested; a step and a paragraph in `OutboundShaping.md`; rows in `Backup.md` and `AlertsAndAuditTrail.md`. Tests: `SMTP/IpWarmup.cs` on port 9926 - fifty to Google on day 1 and the fifty-first, the batch's last, held until after midnight UTC with its tries unchanged while another provider goes, and the history newest first with its stored day and ceiling; a hold past its retry span counted and still after midnight; no schedule, a disabled one, no bind address and a completed plan holding nothing, and a schedule switched on in force at the next delivery; a count changed behind the server read again after a reinitialise; a refused connection moving nothing against a partial refusal counting what was accepted; day 9 on the three plans; the routes' round-trips and refusals; the permissions with their controls - and `WarmupPlanTester` in the internal suite. **Thinner than the row asks:** the providers and the plans are fixed, not editable; a schedule is per address, not per pool, and what does not fit waits rather than moving to another address; the ceilings rise by the calendar, not by the providers' answers; a recipient the provider refused is given back, so only accepted mail is bounded; a backlog is carried for as many days as the message's retries allow, then returned; no COM member and nothing in the Control Panel, because the row names the Deck. |
| ✅ | **Mandatory TLS per domain** (mailcow's TLS policy map, Exchange connectors) | **Built 15 September 2026, as one field of a remote domain policy** (schema 6045, `hm_remotedomainpolicies`; Control Panel, Deck, REST `/api/v1/remote-domains`, COM `Settings.RemoteDomainPolicies`). Per named domain or pattern, outbound mail requires `encrypted` (STARTTLS must succeed), `verified` (and the certificate must chain and name the host) or `dane` (and match a DNSSEC-validated TLSA record, looked up even with `DaneEnabled` off and for a routed delivery); a delivery that cannot meet it is **deferred with 4.7.0 and a sentence naming the policy**, never sent in the clear, where a required STARTTLS that is not offered is otherwise (a route set to STARTTLS required, MTA-STS enforce) a permanent 5.7.0 naming no rule. It composes with MTA-STS and DANE as the strongest of the three, by OR at the one place the requirement is set, so nothing on the page can weaken what a domain publishes; and it applies to routed deliveries too, because it is a statement about the domain rather than about how its server was found. Inbound, mail FROM a named domain on a cleartext session is answered 530 5.7.0 at MAIL FROM, except from an authenticated submission and from a configured incoming relay, where the hop the policy is about happened before the gateway. `hmailserver/docs/RemoteDomains.md` is the walk. **Gated:** `RegressionTests.SMTP.RemoteDomainPolicyEnforcement` and `RegressionTests.API.RestApiRemoteDomains` ran green in the batch-10 gate on 16 September 2026. |
| ✅ | **Recipient callout for backup MX** | **Built 15 September 2026, as a field of the remote domain policy.** At RCPT TO, last of the refusals - after relaying, authentication, the DNS blacklists and greylisting, so a client this server was going to refuse never makes it open a session to anybody - an unauthenticated recipient at a domain whose policy asks is checked with the primary: the policy's host or the domain's MX hosts, skipping any address and port this server listens on, at most two; EHLO, MAIL FROM:<>, RCPT TO, QUIT, and no body. A 2xx accepts, a 5xx is 550 5.1.1 without repeating the primary's words, and **everything else accepts** - a 4xx, a refused null sender, no greeting, no host, and a timeout, which is one deadline over the whole conversation on a private io_context rather than SO_RCVTIMEO, whose Windows-shaped argument Linux refuses because it takes a struct timeval there. Verdicts are cached (60 minutes by default, one minute for no verdict), one remote is asked at most ten times a minute and beyond that recipients are accepted unasked, and no callout is made for a domain this server hosts. The documentation says in so many words that a callout can enumerate a third party's addresses and belongs only on a domain this server backs up. **Gated:** `RegressionTests.SMTP.RemoteDomainPolicyEnforcement` and `RegressionTests.API.RestApiRemoteDomains` ran green in the batch-10 gate on 16 September 2026. |
| ✅ | **Bounce handling for lists** | **Built 24 September 2026 with the list manager ("A list manager" above), in the same schema step, and reviewed with it; not yet run against a bench.** A delivery report to a managed list's `name-bounces+local=domain@` is about that member whatever it says, and one to `name-bounces@` about the final recipients of its RFC 3464 report, read with `DeliveryReportParser`: a `5.x.x` status is a hard bounce, a `4.x.x` or an unreadable one a soft one, only a failure counts - a delay warning, or a report of delivery, is not a bounce - and each member is counted once per report; a report larger than 2 MB is read as far as that. `ListHardBouncesToUnsubscribe` (1) hard bounces, or `ListSoftBouncesToUnsubscribe` (5) soft ones within `ListBounceWindowDays` (30), set the member's delivery to none with the reason recorded, and the owner is sent `LIST_BOUNCED` once, at that moment; the counts are added where they are stored. The owner's reset (`POST /api/v1/lists/{address}/members/{member}/reset-bounces`, the Control Deck, `DistributionListRecipient.ResetBounces`) or the member turning delivery back on clears the counts, and the reset sends each message again only to a member the bounces stopped, never to one who chose to pause; a delivery that succeeds clears nothing. Anybody can write to a bounce address, so a report counts only when it carries the member's tag - a SHA-256 of the member's token and the list's address, which every copy carries in `X-hMailServer-List-Member` and a report brings back in the header it returns - and a report without it, or about somebody who is not a member, is logged and dropped. The bounce goes to no mailbox. The server's own suppression list still applies to the list's domain; `BounceSuppression.md` says how the two meet under each `BounceSuppressionAction` (with refuse, the default, the domain's refusal of a member comes back to the list as a hard bounce; with drop the list is told nothing), and the counts stay separate. Tests in `SMTP/MailingLists.cs`: a hard report pausing the member and telling the owner once while a forged one counts for nothing, a plain report counted only with the member's tag, a delay warning not counted, five soft ones within the window, a report about a stranger logged and dropped, the reset restoring each message, and the reset leaving a member's own pause alone. **Thinner than the row, said plainly**: "hard bounces unsubscribing" is built as pausing - the member's delivery is set to none and they stay a member until they or the owner act, so a bounce can never unsubscribe anybody; there is no probe before the pause, as Mailman sends one; a report that returns no header - some old servers send only text - cannot carry the tag and is dropped, so a member behind such a server is never paused; and the accounts a list's membership query answers have no row to count bounces on. The row as written: Section 7's list manager: VERP addresses, hard bounces unsubscribing, soft bounces counted. |
| ✅ | **Greylisting, hardened** (discussions #281, #282, #284) | **Built 18 September 2026 on branch `disc-greylist`, schema 6072.** The triplet is keyed by a fixed-width digest of each address (`glsenderhash`, `glrecipienthash`: trimmed, domain lower-cased, UTF-8, MD5 as 32 hex characters - text rather than `binary(16)` because the parameter layer speaks text and every backend compares it alike), the address columns are widened to 320 and the index moved from the addresses to the digests; nothing is rewritten at upgrade - a row from before the step has a NULL key, is matched once by its first 200 characters as it was written and rewritten with its key, and the second lookup stops for good once the sweep finds no such row. Two settings and a third that qualifies them, all `hm_settings` rows seeded off (`GreyListingSlideOnEarlyRetry` 0, `GreyListingAbuseThreshold` 0, `GreyListingAbuseLockMinutes` 60): a retry before the window opens can move the window, and the Nth such retry answers `550 5.7.1` for the lock's minutes, after which the triplet starts over as a first attempt rather than walking through; a confirmed triplet is never refused, whatever the threshold. `GreyListing::GetAllowSend` became a three-way verdict (Allow, Defer, Reject) and `SpamProtection::PerformGreyListing` a four-way one, the fourth being Pending. The bypass list (`hm_greylisting_bypass`; `AntiSpam.GreyListingBypassEntries` over COM, `/api/v1/greylisting-bypass` over REST, a Control Panel page beside the white list in 17 languages, the Deck's Lists view in 20, the backup, the audit trail): a domain matched exactly or a dotted suffix at a label boundary, matched against the envelope domain, the HELO host or either, with the results it must carry - at least one of SPF, DKIM, DMARC; an entry requiring nothing is refused. SPF decides at RCPT TO; an entry requiring DKIM or DMARC lets the recipient through and the greylisting it was spared runs after DATA with the results in hand, and only while that test is switched on, since otherwise the entry can never be satisfied. `docs/Greylisting.md` is the walk. A result an entry requires must belong to a name that entry covers, which is the one rule the three requirements share: a DKIM pass counts only for a `d=` the pattern covers (anybody may sign a message with a key they publish themselves), a DMARC pass only for a `From` domain it covers, and an SPF pass only for the identity SPF was checked for - and an entry matched on the HELO earns one from a check of the HELO name itself, RFC 7208 section 2.3, made only when such an entry asks for it. **Off by default, with one exception that is not:** the key case-folds the domain, so `A@Example.com` and `a@example.com`, which were two triplets on PostgreSQL (and on MySQL with a binary collation) and already one on SQL Server, are one everywhere - it errs towards challenging a sender once rather than once per spelling, it applies to new triplets only, and `docs/Greylisting.md` says so where it says the rest is unchanged. **Tests:** `AntiSpam/GreyListingHardened` (the long-address collision proved through the threshold, the threshold and its lock with a classic control, the slide against a classic triplet over sixty-six seconds, an SPF-only entry at RCPT TO, a DKIM entry after DATA and with DKIM off, a HELO suffix at a label boundary, the identity rule's three negative controls each paired with the positive case that proves the result was a pass, and a row from before the schema step found by its addresses and rewritten with its key) and `API/RestApiGreylistBypass` (the resource, eleven refusals, a domain-scoped key and an account refused on all four routes, the three settings and their bounds, the OpenAPI entry); the CP's 778 unit tests and the Deck's 797 checks pass. **Thinner than the row asks, and said so in the document:** the key is an index and not a unique constraint on any backend (deliberately - SQL Server's old constraint turned a race into an accept); the delay's unit is still the minute; old rows are converted as they are matched rather than backfilled; the Deck's editor and hmctl's `settings set` carry the three settings, but there is no hmctl command for the bypass list, which no small collection has. The row as written: Three things one operator asked for on 17 September 2026, each off by default so a stock installation changes nothing. The triplet's identity is a fixed-width key that does not truncate: today the sender and recipient are stored as `nvarchar(200)` and written with `.Left(200)`, so two addresses that share their first 200 characters are one triplet (RFC 5321 allows 320). A sliding wait on early retries and a reject after N of them: a retry before `BlockEnd` moves the window instead of merely counting, and a triplet hammered N times becomes a 550 with a short lock, which is what stops a bot that retries on a timer. A per-domain greylist bypass that is a privilege granted rather than a property of SPF: a list of envelope domains or HELO suffixes with the authentication results each must carry (SPF, DKIM, DMARC, any combination) before greylisting is skipped for it, beside the global `BypassGreylistingOnSPFSuccess`, which lets a hijacked mailbox at a well-configured provider through. |
| ✅ | **SMTP policy the score cannot express** (discussions #283, #285, #286) | **Built 18 September 2026 on branch `disc-smtp-policy` (schema 6073, `hm_domainpolicylist`; seven `hm_settings` rows seeded at start), every one of the three off by default so a stock installation changes nothing.** SPF: `UseSPFSoftFailScore`, `UseSPFTempErrorScore` and `UseSPFPermErrorScore` (0, and 0 is unscored as before) added in `SpamTestSPF::RunTest` by Fail's own path; `SPFTempErrorDefer` answers a TempError `451 4.4.3` for the transaction before the score is read, through a `Defer` result the connection turns into the reply; Neutral and None unscored, SoftFail never a Pass for the greylisting bypass, PermError never deferred. The null sender: `RejectMultiRcptNullSender` answers the second and every later `RCPT TO` of `MAIL FROM:<>` with `550 5.7.1` and refuses the transaction's `DATA` and `BDAT` too, at the greylisting layer (unauthenticated, a range that runs the spam tests, never the anti-spam white list); `BanMultiRcptNullSender` and `BanMultiRcptNullSenderHours` (24) ban the connecting address once per session through the auto-ban range, named `Auto-ban: multi-recipient null sender <address>`, mirrored into the firewall and matched by the packaged fail2ban filter; a single-recipient null sender is untouched. The domain list: `hm_domainpolicylist` (pattern, `envelope`/`helo`/`both`, `white`/`black`, note), cached whole in `DomainPolicyList` and reloaded on every save and delete, consulted at `MAIL FROM` for an unauthenticated session of a range that runs the spam tests - black a `550 5.7.1` before greylisting, scoring or any lookup, white skipping greylisting, the SPF deferral and the score's `550`/`554`/quarantine while every test still runs and logs and the mark threshold still marks; `.example.com` covers the domain and everything beneath it, the most specific pattern wins and black wins a tie; REST `/api/v1/domain-policy-list` (server-wide, `manage_settings` to write), COM `AntiSpam.DomainPolicyList`/`AddDomainPolicyListEntry`/`RemoveDomainPolicyListEntry`, the Deck's Lists view, a Control Panel page in 17 languages, the backup archive and the audit trail. Docs/SmtpPolicy.md, and `AntiSpam/SmtpPolicy.cs` (fifteen tests, each with its negative control, and a `WithServFail` zone added to the fake DNS server for the TempError) - **written and compiled, not yet run against a server; this row is done when they run green in a gate.** Left out, and why: the row's "hard PTR/HELO refusals" are in this server the score-threshold refusal, since PTR and HELO are scored tests and not separate rejects, so white skips that refusal and nothing else; no `hmctl` verb (the row names none); no expiry or per-domain scope on a list row, because the list decides before there is a recipient; the null-sender check counts the distinct envelope addresses a transaction accepted, so a repeated `RCPT TO` is still one recipient and a list or an alias that expands to many is still one; the domain list is ASCII and does not cover an SMTPUTF8 sender's Unicode domain, because the server has no IDNA converter; and a session from an incoming relay is judged by the relay's HELO, so rows for mail behind a relay should key on the envelope domain. The row as written: Three more from the same operator, all off by default. `MAIL FROM:<>` with more than one `RCPT TO` is not a bounce - a DSN has one recipient - and can be refused with a 550 and, optionally, a short ban of the connecting address; a single-recipient null sender is untouched. A domain white and black list keyed by envelope domain or HELO suffix rather than by the IP of the week: black is a 550 before greylisting and scoring, white skips greylisting and the hard PTR/HELO refusals while the tests still run and log; inbound and unauthenticated only, and distinct from the per-recipient allow and block lists, which act at delivery. SPF's other results scored separately: SoftFail, TempError and PermError each get a score of their own (0 today, and 0 stays the default), Neutral and None stay unscored, and TempError can instead answer 451 - the RFC's own answer to a check that did not finish - so a resolver blip defers rather than refuses. |

### Recovery and account safety, for the person

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **Recovery address and codes** (every provider) | **Built 18 September 2026, schema 6076** (`hm_accountrecovery`, `hm_recoverycodes`, `hm_recoverytokens`; `AccountRecovery.h`, [AccountRecovery.md](hmailserver/docs/AccountRecovery.md)). On the webmail's **Security** page a person sets a recovery address - another mailbox of theirs, proved by following a link the server really posts to it, and never answered in full again to anybody, the account holder and the administrator alike: what every surface shows is the masked form `o....@e.........example` - and makes ten one-time codes, shown once, hashed with the scheme the account password uses, each good once, a new set ending the last. Setting either asks for the account's own password again and is refused to a request authenticated with an app password. A code is presented in `X-hMailServer-Recovery-Code` wherever a one-time code is asked for - `POST /api/v1/session`, `POST /api/v1/me/password`, minting an app password, setting the address, making a new set - and the webmail offers a field for one beside the authenticator's at every one of them. It is matched **only after the account's own password has been verified**, and inside `PasswordValidator::ValidatePassword` rather than in front of it, so that the per-name lockout and the domain country policy `AccountLogon::Logon` puts first apply to it and a right password costs no more work than a wrong one; and it is **spent only once the whole attempt has been accepted**, at every route and not just the sign-in, so neither a guess at the password nor a password the policy refuses can burn the set; the sign-in answers `recovery_codes_left` and the page says how many are left. *Forgotten your password?* on the sign-in card posts to the unauthenticated `POST /api/v1/password-reset`, which **always answers 202 and the same sentence** - for an address that is no mailbox, for one with no recovery address, for one never confirmed, for a directory-linked account and for one just rate limited - with the limits charged before anything is looked up (one a minute and six an hour per address; twenty an hour per client and no minute rule, since a client address is very often an office NAT - and skipped altogether for a loopback peer, because nothing here reads a forwarded-for header and a reverse proxy on the mail server would otherwise be a ceiling on the whole installation). The **same per-address budget** covers the confirmation message a recovery address is proved with, counted for the account that asked as well as for the address it would go to, so setting an address and asking for its confirmation again answer 429 with `retry_after` rather than letting an account post mail at a stranger as often as it likes, or at a fresh stranger each time; both are also refused to a request authenticated with an app password, as removing an address is. The link goes to the confirmed recovery address and nowhere else, is 32 random bytes stored only as a digest, lives `recovery_reset_link_minutes` (30), and opens `/portal/reset?token=` - a script-free page - whose POST applies the same password policy, reuse history and breach check as the webmail's own form, ends **every browser session of the account**, tells the mailbox and the recovery address, and raises the new `account.password-reset` alert (seeded on, no cool-down). An administrator sees `recovery_address_set`, `recovery_address_verified`, the masked address and `recovery_codes_unused` on `GET /api/v1/accounts/{address}`, on the Control Deck's **Recovery** card and on the Control Panel's **Recovery** tab (COM `Account.RecoveryAddress`, `.RecoveryAddressVerified`, `.RecoveryCodesUnused`, `.ClearRecoveryAddress()`, `.ClearRecoveryCodes()`), and can clear **either the address or the code set** on any of them - two acts, because they answer two reports, "that address is not mine any more" and "somebody else has seen my code card", and because a set of codes stands in for the second factor so somebody has to be able to end one. They can read none of it. Setting, clearing, ending a set and making codes are on the audit trail. **What it is not:** it does not enrol a second factor - a lost authenticator gets back in with a code, and re-enrolment is still an administrator's over COM, because that is where enrolment lives; confirming an address and completing a reset write no audit row, because both happen with no actor on the thread, and the doc says so. **Gated:** `RegressionTests.API.RestApiAccountRecovery` and `RegressionTests.Infrastructure.BackupAccountStores.TheRecoveryAddressAndCodesComeBack` compile here and are for the integrator's bench run; twelve checks in `build/portal-script-test.js` cover the page, two of them the lost-authenticator journey through the Security page. |
| ✅ | **A breach check on the password** | **Done 15 September 2026**, as section 7's *A password checked against known breaches*: the webmail's password form, the account editors and - over COM on Windows - the administrator password are all refused a password found in the breach corpus, in the reader's language, with the check off by default (on Linux the administrator password is set only by `hmailserver --set-admin-password`, which runs with no database open and is not checked). The row as written: Section 7. |
| ✅ | **Passkeys** | **Done 18 September 2026**, as section 7's *Passkeys and single sign-on*: WebAuthn credentials for the webmail and the Control Deck, with OIDC sign-in beside them. |

## 11. Learning it: tutorials, tours and in-product help

Everything below is about the first hour, which is the hour this product currently spends worst. A new
administrator meets a Control Panel of forty-seven pages, a Deck of ten views and a webmail, and the help is
a wiki in another window. Nothing here is a mail feature; all of it is why a good mail server gets returned.
The Welcome page's twelve intents and the setup verdict on the dashboard are the ground this builds on.

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **Tutorial helper functions** | **Done 15 September 2026.** One mechanism, the same shape in all three surfaces, and the contract for it is a section of `hmailserver/docs/ControlPanelDesign.md`. **A step** is an element - by automation id in the Control Panel, by element id or a stable selector in the two pages - one sentence, an optional condition that ends the step when the thing has happened, and what to do when the element is not there: skipped, or the tour ended *saying which step could not be shown*. A step never acts; the only thing it does besides pointing is open the page its element is on, because an element on a page nobody has opened is in no tree to be found in. **A tour** is an ordered list of those with an id, a name and a version - the id is what the finished record is kept under, the version is what puts a tour that has grown a stop back in front of somebody who finished the old one. **Where it is kept:** one line, `firstrun=1|firstrun@3v1`, the same form in all three - `HKCU\Software\hMailServer\ControlPanel` under `TourProgress` beside the window bounds and the palette's history, `localStorage` under `hmsTours` for the Deck, and the account's preferences under `tours` for the webmail, so a reader who starts the walk on a laptop finishes it on a phone. **Refusable, resumable, and never in the way:** the ring takes no pointer events and the card is the only thing on its layer that does, so every control underneath - the very one being pointed at - stays usable; nothing is focused when a step opens and the Control Panel puts the focus back where it found it; `F6` reaches the card and `Escape` ends the tour only while the keyboard is already inside it, so Escape still belongs to the page everywhere else; the card's four captions are the only ones in the Control Panel with no Alt key, deliberately, because a card that floats over any of forty-seven pages cannot own a key without taking one that page already has. **Accessibility is in it rather than after it:** the card is a polite live region, the Control Panel raises a UI Automation notification with each step's sentence so the walk is usable without sight and without focus being taken, and the highlight is a solid ring in the accent *and* a dashed one inside it, so it survives greyscale, colour blindness and High Contrast. **It is the change that ends a step, not the state** - somebody who already has a domain reads the sentence about domains and presses Next rather than watching the step vanish. **Every sentence goes through the catalogues**: 23 new texts in the Control Panel's seventeen languages, 17 in the Deck's twenty, 14 in the webmail's twenty-three. Where the code is: `Services/Tour.cs`, `Services/TourSession.cs` and `Services/TourProgress.cs` are WPF-free and compiled into `ControlPanel.Core`, as `ConnectionStatus` and `SettingsPageStatus` are, with 39 tests on the model, the stepper and the record (750 from 711); `MainWindow.Tour.cs` and `Views/Scaffold/TourOverlay.cs` are the half that needs a window. The two pages' harnesses execute theirs: `build/deck-script-test.js` 370 checks from 336 and `build/portal-script-test.js` 306 from 274, covering a tour that runs, a step whose element is missing skipped rather than hung on, a condition that moves the tour on by itself and one that does not because it was already true, the finished state remembered, and the whole thing drawn in German. **What is not in it:** a tour cannot branch, and it cannot wait for something the surface has no cheap way to ask about - the conditions are counts and visible state, nothing more. |
| ✅ | **The first-run walk, in each surface** | **Done 15 September 2026**, one per surface, each ending where the person wanted to be rather than where it started. **The Control Panel's** is seven stops: a domain (waiting for one to exist), the domain list, a mailbox (waiting for one), the listeners, a listener that offers TLS (waiting for one), the certificates (waiting for one), and the dashboard's External setup card, the server's own list of what still needs doing. The listeners stop ends the tour if it is not there, because the rest is meaningless without it. **The Deck's** is the same seven stops in a browser, over its domains, ports, certificates and dashboard views, opening each view as it reaches it. It waits only for a domain and a TLS listener, and its last stop is the dashboard's Server state tile, because the Deck has no counterpart to the External setup card. **The webmail's** is four: the reading pane (which ends when a message is opened), the compose window (which ends when one is opened), labels, and search. **The ways in:** on the Welcome page, a button in the header and, until the walk has been finished, a notice above the twelve intents. In the Ctrl+K palette, a row of its own above the page list, ranked ahead of a page of the same name, because somebody who types "show me around" has said what they want in so many words. And a help button in the header of any page a tour visits, which starts the walk *at its first stop on that page*. The shell sets that button from the catalogue, so a page gains it by being named in a tour rather than by being edited. The Deck's way in is the ◎ *Show me around* button in its header (the `?` beside it is the keyboard shortcuts), which starts at the first stop on the view showing. The webmail's is *Show me around* in the account menu. Seven automation ids were added for the steps to point at (`domains-add`, `domains-grid`, `domains-accounts`, `ports-grid`, `ports-add`, `certs-grid`, `dashboard-verdict`), and four for the walk's own controls (`page-help`, `tour-overlay`, `welcome-show-me`, `welcome-start-walk`). Nothing was moved or renamed, which is what keeps the search index, the palette and `SettingsSearchIndexTests` passing. **What is not in it:** the walk points at the certificate list but does not walk somebody through ACME, and there is one walk per surface rather than one per page. A second tour is now a list in `TourCatalog` and a few sentences in the catalogues, which is the point of the row above. |
| ✅ | **Help where the question is** | **Done 22 September 2026.** A help button on every page of the three surfaces, opening what the documentation says about that page beside it, never instead of it, and the same text in the binary. **The text** is `hmailserver/docs/*.md` - what the wiki's reference pages are written from, in the tree so CI holds it - embedded by `build/generate-help-pages.py` as `Common/Util/HelpPagesData.cpp` (71 documents, 1.1 MB, in pieces of four thousand characters, with each document's name, byte length and level-one and level-two headings with their lines, and a build tag; `--check` in the style workflow) and served unauthenticated, as `/deck-lang/` is, at `GET /api/v1/help` (every page with its title and headings, the wiki base URL and the tag) and `GET /api/v1/help/{page}` (`text/markdown`, a day's `Cache-Control`, an ETag from the tag and `304` to a request carrying it; a path, a dot-dot, an extension and an unknown name one and the same 404). **The map:** one table per surface - `Services/HelpMap.cs` for the Control Panel's 63 navigation keys, `HELP_MAP` in `WebAdmin/index.html` for the Deck's 37 views, `HELP_MAP` in `Portal.js` for the webmail's 26 route heads - each entry a document and an optional heading; `build/check-help-map.py` holds all three to the documents (every page mapped, every document real, every heading in it by the generator's own parser, no document unreachable unless listed as reference-only, 27 of them) with `--self-test` as its negative control. **The panes,** the same shape in all three, in the register of the tours: a right-hand pane that follows the page, the document rendered by a small converter of each surface's own (headings of both kinds, paragraphs, emphasis, inline code, code blocks, nested lists, links, quotes, rules and tables; nothing else, and anything else as its text; DOM nodes and never markup in the two pages, a `FlowDocument` in the Control Panel), scrolled to the mapped heading, the other headings listed at the top, a search box over the open document, *Open on the wiki* and *All documentation* at the foot, a link to another document of the set opened in the pane. The Control Panel's `?` in the command bar and F1, from its own embedded copy of the same files (`EmbeddedResource` straight from `hmailserver/docs`, so it cannot be behind them), the pane a splitter-sized third column whose width and state live in the registry beside the window bounds; the Deck's `?` and F1 with Escape to close (the shortcut list keeps the `?` key and gets a keyboard glyph), the state in `localStorage`; the webmail's `?`, F1 and *Help for this page* in the palette, the renderer served as `/portal-markdown.js` beside the S/MIME module and carried by the service worker, and copied inline into the Deck, whose policy allows no other script. **The wiki link:** `HelpWikiBaseUrl`, an `hm_settings` row seeded at start and shipped as the project's GitLab wiki, `help_wiki_base_url` over REST (and so on the Deck's Settings page), on the Control Panel's Advanced page and `Settings.HelpWikiBaseUrl` over COM (dispatch id 175); empty hides the link, a value that is not an absolute http or https URL is refused in the same words on every door. Catalogue strings in seventeen, twenty and twenty-three languages, the Control Panel's two captions with a mnemonic in each. `hmailserver/docs/Help.md` is it written down. **Thinner than the row asks:** the text is the repository's documentation, not the wiki's own pages (the wiki cannot be built in; *Open on the wiki* is the bridge); no search across all documents in the pane, only within the open one; a page whose subject has no document of its own - the ports, the certificates, the groups - opens the nearest one; no tour stop for the button. **Tests:** `API/RestApiHelp.cs` on port 9826 (the list against the files' own headings, every page byte for byte with the headers and the 304, the four 404s, no credential asked, the setting over REST and COM with empty allowed and a non-URL refused on both doors with nothing changed); `ControlPanel.Tests` `HelpMapTests` and `MarkdownBlocksTests` (the parser over a fixed document, three lookups, the map's coverage of every navigation page and its headings against the files, the pane's scroll target); the Deck's and the webmail's harnesses (the button, the fetch path, a heading, a list and a table rendered as nodes, the map for three views, F1 and Escape, the palette entry, the list of every document). Also on this branch: the journaling page's related link to `archive`, a page the Control Panel does not have, which `NavigationMapTests` failed on. |
| ✅ | **A sample domain that can be thrown away** | **Done 18 September 2026.** One call makes `example.test` and one takes it away and says what went: `POST` and `DELETE /api/v1/setup/sample-domain`, `hmailserver --sample-domain create` or `remove` on Linux, `hmctl sample-domain status`, `create` and `remove` anywhere, a button on the Control Panel's Welcome page and another under the Deck's first-hour checklist. **What it makes:** the domain with every default one made in the Control Panel gets; `alice`, `bob` and `carol`, all on one password the server mints per creation - twenty random characters from the generator an app password comes out of - and shows once, in the answer that made the sample and in the sentence every surface prints; the public list `everyone@example.test` of the three; one rule on Alice that files a subject carrying *Invoice* into a folder called *Invoices*; and eight messages with real subjects, a thread, an attachment and an invoice. **The mail is not written into the mailboxes.** Each message is composed as RFC 5322 text, written to the delivery queue and handed to the server's own deliverer, so the list expands into fourteen deliveries, the rule fires and creates the folder it names - which is how you can tell it ran - quota is counted and every session is told. **The marker:** the domain's id goes into the setting `sample_domain_id` and the removal removes the domain carrying exactly that id and still named `example.test`, so an `example.test` this server did not make - the regression suite's own, or one somebody added after reading RFC 2606 - is never created over and never removed, and both answer 409 with the state `foreign`. A marker whose domain has already gone is cleared and reported as cleared - but a domain table that could not be *read* is a fourth state, `unknown`, answered with 500 and nothing changed, because clearing the marker on a failed read would orphan a sample that is still there. The removal also drops the sample's own mail still in the delivery queue, counted as `queued`, and leaves alone any queued message with a recipient outside the domain. The first-hour checklist leaves the sample out of the domain count, the account count and the three record lookups, with a not-needed finding naming it so the count says why it is what it is. **Who:** reading needs `view_configuration`, making and removing need `manage_server` and nothing less, and a credential confined to named domains is refused all three. `hmailserver/docs/SampleDomain.md` says what it is; the row's own honesty is there too. **Proved by** `API/RestApiSampleDomain` on port 9696 - the sample made whole with the three mailboxes signing in and holding 4, 5 and 4 messages and the invoice in Alice's *Invoices* folder, the suite's own `example.test` refused in both directions and left untouched, the removal counting 3 mailboxes, 1 list, 1 rule and 14 messages and a second one refused, the checklist not counting it, and the same documents over COM with all three routes 403 to a mailbox's own credentials - and by twelve checks in the Deck harness (804 from 792) and four hmctl cases, one of them the 409 that must exit 2. **What is thinner than the row asks:** the sample fills the mail stores and nothing else, so the webmail's calendar, address book and task list still open on nothing; there are no choices at all, since a sample with options is the configuration screen this exists to skip; and the one password still bypasses the password policy and the breach check - the only place in this server that sets a password without asking either, which twenty random characters make safe but does not make ordinary. The three mailboxes are real mailboxes that can sign in and relay wherever the address ranges allow, so the sample must be removed before the server takes real mail. The documentation says all of it plainly. **Reviewed and corrected the same day:** the published fixed password became the minted one (the default Internet range permits authenticated relay from anywhere, so a documented password was three known credentials on every installation that had tried the sample); `Examine` now tells a failed read of `hm_domains` from an absent domain; the removal drops the sample's queued mail instead of leaving it addressed to deleted mailboxes; `hmctl` exits 2 on a refusal as its own help promised; the checklist's `sample_domain` sentence was added to both clients' text maps in all twenty and seventeen languages; and the fixture, which did not compile against the Linux shims, uses `get_ItemByName` - the shims' `Rule.From` now answers a rule's criteria and actions, which it never had. |
| ✅ | **A checklist that knows what is done** | **Done 17 September 2026.** The server judges its own first hour: `GET /api/v1/setup/checklist` and `Utilities.GetSetupChecklist` answer eight items - a domain, an account, TLS on the submission port, SPF, DKIM, DMARC, a backup destination and a second factor on the administrator password - each done, action needed, cannot tell or not needed, with findings that carry a code, its arguments and an English sentence, so that a client renders them in its own language and a script switches on the code. The judgement is made from what the server can see: the port table with a certificate that exists (port 25 is never the submission port, and a plain port beside a secured one still needs action), the backup folder on its own disk, the administrator credential, and SPF, DKIM and DMARC looked up in DNS for every active domain the way a receiving server looks them up, a failed lookup being cannot-tell rather than missing; `?dns=0` skips the lookups and the record items say which host to look up. The Deck gains a Checklist view, first after the dashboard, with a card per item and Check again, its texts in all twenty catalogues; the Control Panel's External setup page and dashboard card carry the first-hour items ahead of the twelve they had, asking the server without lookups and resolving SPF and DMARC off the window thread as they already did DKIM, in all seventeen languages. `hmailserver/docs/SetupChecklist.md` has the document and the codes. Six regression tests in `API/RestApiSetupChecklist`: the eight items in order with the four counts, two domains told apart by their records in the suite's own zone, the skipped lookup saying so over REST and over COM, the stock port 587 needing action and a STARTTLS port with a certificate done, the backup destination judged on disk, and the second factor reported as it is. The Deck harness draws all four states and a code the page does not know in the server's own words. The row as written: The Control Panel already has a checklist checked against the server. `ExternalSetupChecks` builds twelve items: DKIM, ARC filtering, ARC sealing, MTA-STS, TLS-RPT, DANE, encrypted keys, ACME, client certificates, OAuth2, autoconfig and the service account. Each is Done, Not needed, Action needed or Cannot tell, read from COM, the ini file, the disk and DNS lookups. The External setup page shows every item, and the dashboard's card shows the items that need action, each one click from the page that owns it. **Missing:** the first-hour items this row names. Of a domain, an account, TLS on the submission port, SPF, DKIM, DMARC, a backup destination and an administrator second factor, only DKIM is on the list.
