SPF and DMARC are checked per domain on the DNS records page, not on the list. Also missing: any such list in the Deck or over REST, and a test, since nothing tests `ExternalSetupChecks`. |

## 12. The enterprise questions nothing here answers yet

Section 10 covers most of what a buyer asks. These are the ones it does not, found by walking the same comparison again with a procurement checklist rather than a feature list. Each is genuinely absent, checked against the code rather than remembered.

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **The backup does not carry half of what an account now has** | **Found 15 September 2026 by checking the wiki against the code, fixed the same day.** The defect: `BackupExecuter` wrote what the business objects' `XMLStore` methods knew how to write, and nothing read `hm_contacts`, `hm_accountprefs`, `hm_scheduled`, `hm_files`, `hm_smimekeys`, `hm_calendars` or `hm_calendarobjects`. `Message::XMLStore` wrote a message's flags and not its keywords. Every one of those tables cascades from `hm_accounts`, and a restore calls `Domains::DeleteAll()` first. So restoring a backup taken by 6.3.3 or earlier **deleted** an account's address book, its webmail settings, its scheduled messages, its files, its S/MIME keys, its calendar and its labels, and the archive it restored from had never held them. **What landed:** `Common/Application/AccountStores.{h,cpp}`, one declaration of each table, every column and the role each plays, which the backup reads through and the restore writes back through. It carries those seven tables and `hm_passwordhistory` besides, with `hm_calendarobjects` as a child store under its calendar. It also carries the keywords on a message, one line each way, which is what brings back the labels, the second and third stars, follow-up flags, mute and pin. A value crosses as an attribute where every character of it is safe in one, and otherwise as base64 of its UTF-8 under `<column>.base64`, so a vCard, an iCalendar object or a PEM block cannot make an index unparseable. Nothing identity-shaped crosses. `hm_scheduled` names a message and a folder, and those are carried as the folder's path and the message's UID and resolved against what the restore has just put back. A reference that does not resolve drops its row, counts it and names it in the log, because a scheduled send pointing at whatever was given that id next is worse than a lost row. An older archive restores unchanged, and a newer one is still refused by version. When an archive names a table this build has no column for, those rows are counted in the log and the rest of the restore goes ahead. The verified restore now reconciles the per-account rows it wrote against the archive it reads back, per table, and discards an archive that does not hold them; without that, the verification would have kept passing while a store was missing. **And the part that stops it recurring:** `build/check-account-stores.py`, in CI, reads the three `CREATE TABLE` scripts against that declaration. It fails by name when a table that reaches `hm_accounts` through `ON DELETE CASCADE`, or any table with an account-id column, is in neither `stores_` nor `elsewhere_`; when a column of a declared store is undeclared; when the three dialects disagree about one; or when a store has no test. On the wave's own tree that was 78 checks with 22 per-account tables accounted for. On the folded tree it fails, as it was written to, naming `hm_knownsenders`: the first-contact memory the domain-transforms wave added the same morning, which is in neither list yet. Nine tests: one per store (the calendar test carrying its objects), one for the labels, and one for the verified restore's reconciliation of the per-account rows. The store tests share one shape: make the data through the surface that really writes it, back up, delete the whole domain, restore, and find it exactly as it was. `hmailserver/docs/Backup.md` is what the archive holds and what it does not. The wiki's *Backup and Restore* §15.1a (the "what it does not carry" table and its advice to use the database server's own tool instead) is obsolete from this release on. |
| ✅ | **Rooms and equipment** (Exchange resource mailboxes) | **Built 25 September 2026 on branch `row299-rooms`, schema 6100 to 6101, and corrected the same day by its adversarial review and three re-checks, every finding answered. Not yet compiled or run against a bench: written in a worktree beside four other rows, it is compiled, built and gated when the branch is folded; every checker is green but the schema chain, which waits for 6099 and 6100, the steps of two rows folded before this one.** A room or a piece of equipment is an **account** with a kind - `hm_accounts.accountkind`, person (0, every account there was), room (1) or equipment (2); `kind` on the account's REST routes, a select on the Deck's account editor, `Account.Kind` over COM at id 88 - so its aliases, quota, calendar, delegation, directory entry, backup and mailbox move are an account's. It **cannot be signed into**, as Exchange's resource mailbox is a disabled user: every password logon refuses it in `PasswordValidator` before the script hook, SASL's authorisation name as well, SCRAM where IMAP, POP3 and SMTP look the account up, `XCLIENT LOGIN=` from a trusted upstream, every logon that proves identity without a password at `AccountLogon::RefusedByHold`, and a webmail session opened before is ended at its next request. Its **booking policy** is `hm_bookingpolicies` (one row per room, the defaults without one), `GET`/`PUT /api/v1/accounts/{address}/booking-policy` and a card on the Deck: answer automatically (on), the booking window (180 days from now, at most 1080), the longest booking (1440 minutes, 0 no limit), working hours only with the days, start, end and zone, conflicts allowed (no), recurring meetings allowed (yes; a series is checked occurrence by occurrence to its end, which must be inside the window), who may book (the server's own domains, or addresses, @domains, distribution lists and groups, alias domains resolved on both sides) and capacity (published in the directory); and the **delegates**, the calendar's delegate shares made and taken away by the same route - confined to the credential's domains - who answer for the room from its calendar, `From:` the room with `Sender:` themselves. **Who is asking** is decided from what the server recorded when the message arrived, never from a header: schema 6101 puts on the queued message the account an authenticated session (SMTP AUTH, the webmail's sends) submitted it as, and the domain the inbound DMARC check passed for (`hm_messages.messagesubmitterid`, `messageauthdomains`, on the queue row only and never longer than the column). A local organiser is believed only from their own session or one allowed to send as them; one from outside only when named among the bookers and vouched for by DMARC (SPF alone vouches for nobody); anything else is neither filed nor answered, changes and cancels nothing, and the delegates are told once an hour per organiser. **The automatic answer** is decided just after the delivery from a queue kept in the database (`hm_bookingqueue`), so a stop or a crash puts a decision off and a minute's sweep takes it up - each delivery tried at most three times, counted before each try, then given up to the delegates - on a work queue of the rooms' own, one delivery at a time per room (`Common/Util/ResourceBooking.cpp`, handed on by `Imip::FileOnDelivery`): who may book, RFC 3834 (no answer to an empty reverse path or an `Auto-Submitted`), an answer memory of 20 an hour per organiser (`hm_bookinganswers`), recurrence, the window, the longest booking, working hours, and a conflict check of the room's own - every booking not cancelled is busy, whatever its `TRANSP` or any `PARTSTAT`, and a booking that cannot be expanded in full leaves the request for the delegates. An accepted booking is filed `ACCEPTED`; a declined update takes the old booking out; a change to one occurrence (`RECURRENCE-ID` only) is decided alone and merged into the series, or that occurrence alone taken out with an `EXDATE`; the organiser gets `METHOD:REPLY` from `Imip::ReplyObject` - the UID, the SEQUENCE, the room as `ATTENDEE` with `CUTYPE=ROOM` and `PARTSTAT` - from the room's address through the server's own queue, `Auto-Submitted: auto-replied`, a decline saying why and quoting nothing of the request. A `CANCEL` from the believed organiser frees the slot. Left for the delegates - never put into the room's calendar, which holds only what the room decided, and told within the organiser's hourly budget: automatic answers off, a request sent automatically, the hour's answers spent, and a decision that failed, which also writes one error-log line (HM7430-7434). A delegate's REST or CalDAV write to the room's calendar takes the same in-process lock, and a delegate's decline, over REST or from a CalDAV client, takes the booking out; two servers on one database are not serialised (the Active-active nodes row). An answer about a booking goes only to the organiser the room recorded when it accepted it (`hm_bookingdecisions`), while the booking still names them, and only when a delegate gave it: a plain write share's CalDAV decline takes the booking out and tells nobody, and nothing a calendar object says addresses mail `From:` the room. Rooms are in the directory as `room` with their capacity (and `KIND:location` on the CardDAV card), their free-busy follows the booking rule, and over CalDAV every principal says its `calendar-user-type` (RFC 6638) and a room's principal and the `principal-property-search` REPORT (RFC 3744) are a client's room finder, within the domain and while the directory is on. The policy and the record of whom each booking was accepted from are in the backup; the policy is on the audit trail; and the address erasure reaches both, with the answer memory. docs/RoomsAndEquipment.md says what Exchange's Calendar Attendant does that this does not, and what no test proves. Tests: `API/RoomsAndEquipment.cs` (ports 10210-10212), twenty-one, a DMARC pass, occurrences held without their series, a series CANCEL naming the held occurrence, an emoji and the CalDAV declines of a plain writer and of a delegate among them; `ResourceBookingTester` in the server's own tests; `BackupAccountStores.TheBookingPolicyComesBack` and `WhomARoomAcceptedABookingFromComesBack`; four Deck harness checks. |
| ✅ | **Distribution lists that define themselves** | **Built 18 September 2026 on branch `row292` (schema 6074); not yet gated - the fixture compiles here and runs on the bench when the branch is folded.** A distribution list carries one more text, `hm_distributionlists.distributionlistmembership` (200 characters, empty by default): a **membership query**, answered against the server's accounts at the moment a message is sent to the list rather than kept in a table somebody maintains. Five forms, and no others: empty (a static list - the recipients and nothing else, which is every list made before this existed and what a new one still starts as), `domain` (every active account of the list's own domain), `domain:other.example` (every active account of a named domain this server hosts), `pattern:sales-*@example.com` (active accounts whose address matches, `*` for any run and `?` for one, case ignored) and `everyone` (every active account of every active domain). The query is a **union** with the list's own recipients and never a replacement: the one contractor outside the domain stays a row beside "everybody in the domain", and an address that is both is delivered to once, because `RecipientParser` deduplicates as it always has. Everything else about the list is untouched - the four send modes, require-authentication, require-sender-address, the bounce address and moderation all hold, and under *membership* mode a sender the query would answer is a member and may post, asked of the account cache rather than by resolving the whole query at RCPT TO. A query is judged at **save** time in `PreSaveLimitationsCheck`, so COM, REST, the Control Panel and the Deck are refused a bad one in the same sentence; a query that was valid and stopped being so - the domain deleted or disabled since - delivers to the rows, logs error 6980 and says why on the members route, because a list that answers nobody is smaller than it was rather than a refused message. A query that could not be ANSWERED is the opposite case and is never confused with it: if the accounts cannot be read, `RCPT TO` is refused `451` and the sender retries (error 6982), and the members route answers `503` - the union with the rows would otherwise hide the failure perfectly, one row expanding, `250` returned, and a message for a thousand people reaching one of them. Renaming a domain rewrites every query that names it, and leaves one the rename would push past the column alone rather than letting the database decide half way through (error 6981). A credential confined to named domains may set empty, `domain`, or a pattern in a domain it holds - its whole grant, over COM exactly as over REST; the forms that reach another tenant's accounts are the server administrator's, and a form such a credential may not set is one it may not read the answer to either, so the members route refuses it `403` rather than handing a tenant every address on the server. **Surfaces:** `DistributionList.Membership` over COM (a property, so no re-registration), `membership` on the list document and on its POST and PUT, `GET /api/v1/lists/{address}/members` (each member marked `row` or `query`, with `problem` when the query could not be answered), the Deck's lists table and editor with a *Delivers to now* section, the Control Panel's *Membership query* field in seventeen languages, and `hmctl list` plus `hmctl listmember`. **What is thinner than the row asked:** there is no directory-attribute form, and that is a decision rather than an absence. `hm_accounts` has no department and no title - the first and last name are the whole of what an account says about the person - so `title:` and `department:` would need a column, an editor and a migration first. The server does group accounts, though: `hm_groups` and `hm_group_members` exist and are administered over COM, the Control Panel, the Deck and REST, and a `group:<name>` form would be one more branch in the parser and one more join. It is deliberately left out because a group is an ACL principal for shared folders rather than a statement about the person, and it is server-wide and belongs to no domain, so like `everyone` only the server administrator could set it; making a group a mail concept is a decision worth taking on its own row. [DistributionLists.md](hmailserver/docs/DistributionLists.md) says the same. Nothing is cached either: the query runs against the database for every message to the list, which is the price of never being out of date. Nothing is stored, and nothing outlives the message: the query is resolved once per SMTP transaction rather than once per `RCPT TO`, its pattern compiled once for the whole scan, so repeating `RCPT TO` at a public list buys no scan per command. **Proved by** `RegressionTests.SMTP.DistributionListQueries` (a domain query reaching every active account and not a disabled one; a pattern query; rows and query deduplicated; moderation still applying to a query list; a query naming a domain this server does not host refused at save; a query whose accounts cannot be read refused `451` with nothing delivered; a domain administrator held to the domains it holds; a rename that would overflow the column leaving the query alone), `RegressionTests.API.RestApiCoverage` (the field, the members route, the unreadable kind, and a domain-confined key refused both the write and the read), the Deck harness's ten new checks and `build/check-cli.py`'s two. |
| ✅ | **Devices, listed and revocable** | **Built 16 September 2026 on branch `sessions-and-devices` (schema 6050); gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** `hm_signins` holds one row per account and per client, where a client is the protocol, the address and the user agent - the only identity a mail protocol honestly offers - with when it was first and last seen, how many times, and which credential it used: the account's own password, an app password *by name*, an OAuth2 token, a client certificate or single sign-on. IMAP, POP3, SMTP submission, CalDAV/CardDAV, ManageSieve and the browser sign-in all record it; fifty per account, the least recently seen going; a write throttled to once a minute per client. Shown to the person on the webmail's Security page (the client the page is on marked *This browser*) and to an administrator over `GET /api/v1/accounts/{address}/devices`, `Account.GetDevices` over COM (a JSON array rather than a collection, so nothing needs an elevated registration), the Deck's Devices view, `hmctl device` and `Get-HmDevice`. Revoking one ends what it holds now - its browser session's token invalidated, its live IMAP, POP3 and SMTP connections dropped where they stand through a weak-pointer registry of authenticated connections - and with `credential=revoke` deactivates the app password it signed in with, which is the one press for a stolen phone; `forget=true` removes the entry. Not a ban: the entry stays marked and a sign-in with a credential that still works clears it. Refused to a caller on an app password; on the audit trail when an administrator does it. Not in the backup, on purpose: the sessions it describes died with the installation that wrote it. **Gated:** `RegressionTests.API.RestApiDevices` (two sign-ins listed separately, a revoked session refused on its next request, an IMAP client listed with its app password's name and its connection dropped on revoke) ran green in that gate; the portal harness (345 checks) and the Deck harness (563) have. [docs/DevicesAndSessions.md](hmailserver/docs/DevicesAndSessions.md). The row as written: Every client that has signed in to an account, by protocol, address and last use, shown to the account holder and to the administrator, with a button that ends the session and revokes the application password behind it. The sessions and application passwords are both there and both listed; what is missing is the device as the thing a person recognises, and the one press that answers "my phone was stolen". |
| ✅ | **Throttling per destination, and per tenant** | **Built 23 September 2026 on branch `batch24`, reviewed adversarially and corrected the same day; not yet run against a bench - the fixture compiles, every checker is green and the three builds succeed.** The row's text was partly stale when it was picked up: per-destination concurrency and a per-destination rate DID exist, per remote domain policy (`max_connections`, `max_messages_per_minute`, rows 257 and 258, [RemoteDomains.md](hmailserver/docs/RemoteDomains.md)), but neither had a test, and `MaxOutboundPerDestinationPerMinute` had none anywhere. What was missing, and is built ([OutboundShaping.md](hmailserver/docs/OutboundShaping.md)): **a back-off that reads the remote's reply** - `SMTP/DestinationBackoff`, keyed by the delivery target (the recipient domain, or a route's host); a 421, a 4xx with enhanced status 4.7.0, 4.7.28, 4.7.500, 4.7.650, 4.7.652 or 4.3.2, or a 4xx whose English text carries one of fifteen fixed phrases opens a window of `OutboundBackoffMinutes` (2; 0 off) in which nothing is sent to that destination and to nobody else; the first delivery after it probes, and the same answer doubles the window - once per probe - up to `OutboundBackoffMaxMinutes` (60); a 5xx, 4.7.1 and any reply that says greylist, 4.2.x (one mailbox), 4.5.3 (too many recipients for one message), 4.4.x (a 421 4.4.2 is this session's timeout), a refused connection and this server's own deferrals are deliberately not a signal, and the exclusions win over the text. **Concurrency for every destination**: `MaxOutboundConnectionsPerDestination` (0, off), the policy's `max_connections` for targets no policy names, a policy's value winning where present. **A share per tenant**: `MaxOutboundConnectionsPerTenant` (0, off), the tenant being the hosted domain of the envelope sender through the domain aliases, and `(external)` for everything relayed - a share of connections bounded by the delivery threads, not a volume quota. **And the rule that binds them**: every deferral this server chooses - those four and the three that existed - goes through `DeferBatchWithoutCounting_`, and when every failure of a pass was one the next try is the deferral's own length (the rest of the window; one to five minutes by the message's id for a rate or a ceiling) with the tries counter untouched and `FlagFirstPassDone` set in the same statement, so that DKIM/ARC signing, the mirror copy and a rule's policy notice happen once and not once per held pass; a remote failure takes the counted path as before, so a message with one try can no longer be bounced having never been offered to anybody - but a message queued longer than its retry schedule spans, (retries + 1) times the minutes between tries, takes the counted path whatever deferred it, so a destination that says slow down for good ends in the bounce it always was rather than a hold for as long as the backlog lasts. `GET /api/v1/queue/shaping` (the windows with since, until, window, reply, host, deferred, probing; the connections open by destination and by tenant) and `DELETE /api/v1/queue/shaping/{destination}` (404 when none; audited; logged), under the queue's two permissions; the Deck's queue page gains *Slowed destinations* with a Resume button, in twenty catalogues; the four settings on the Control Panel's Delivery tab beside `MaxOutboundPerDestinationPerMinute` (seventeen catalogues), COM `Settings.OutboundBackoffMinutes`/`OutboundBackoffMaxMinutes`/`MaxOutboundConnectionsPerDestination`/`MaxOutboundConnectionsPerTenant` at ids 176-179, and REST on `/api/v1/settings` with range refusals; four `hm_settings` rows seeded at start, no schema step. `RegressionTests/SMTP/OutboundShaping.cs` (port 9856): the window opened at MAIL FROM and at the banner with the second message held without a connection and another destination untouched, the clear-and-retry, the four non-signals, the probe (one wait of about a minute) doubling on the same answer and clearing on a delivery, the uncounted deferral at one try waiting with zero tries and arriving once the rate's window has drained with no bounce, a held message mirrored once and not once per pass, three messages held behind a destination answering 421 to every session all bounced within the schedule's span, the policy's two limits, the server-wide ceiling and the policy winning over it, the tenant share for a hosted domain and for `(external)`, and the settings round trips and refusals; `DestinationBackoffTester` inside the service. Thinner than the row asks: no per-tenant volume quota beyond the per-account one; the back-off state is per process, not shared between nodes; the signal table is fixed, not editable, and its phrases are English; a hold is bounded by the retry schedule's span and not by a delayed delivery report, which this server does not send; a deferral by a rate or a ceiling costs one delivery pass per message every one to five minutes for as long as the backlog lasts; a slow-down reply from a later MX host after an earlier host's plain 4xx to the same recipient is not seen, because the delivery client keeps the first non-fatal reply. |
| ✅ | **Attachments opened somewhere safe** | **Built 24 September 2026 on branch `row297-sandbox` (schema 6091), and its adversarial review applied the same day; not yet run against a bench - the fixture is written and the integrator compiles and runs it.** ClamAV says whether a file is known to be bad; what an unknown file does when it is opened takes a sandbox and minutes, and this is the route to one ([Sandbox.md](hmailserver/docs/Sandbox.md)). **A sandbox is an HTTP service the administrator names**, not a vendor integration: `SandboxSubmitUrl` takes each file as `multipart/form-data` (`file`, `filename`, `sha256`, `message_id` - the queue id) with a bearer `SandboxToken` (visible ASCII, at most 2048 characters, stored protected as the single sign-on client secret is, and left out of the backup archive), and answers `{"id":...}` (at most 1000 characters); `SandboxVerdictUrl` is asked with `id=` (or `sha256=`, or `{id}`/`{sha256}` in the address, `{id}` becoming the hash when there is no id) and answers `{"state":"pending|done","verdict":"clean|malicious|suspicious|error","score":0-100,"detail":...}`, a suspicious verdict without a numeric score being an error; both https, or plain http to this machine, stored trimmed. The switch is refused on while either address is empty, and a configuration that has it so anyway holds nothing and says so in the application log at each start. **The hold** (`Common/AntiVirus/Sandbox`): after the anti-virus scan and before the rules, a message that no signed-in user of this server submitted, with an attachment whose extension - of either of its names, trailing dots and spaces aside - or media type is in `SandboxTypes` (the executable and document set; an archive submitted whole, a forwarded message opened five levels deep), becomes `hm_messages.messagetype` 5, `Message::HeldForSandbox` - a new state rather than the anti-virus fail-closed hold, which is not a state but a next-try time - in no mailbox, off the queue's delivery attempts, out of the quarantine and its digest, its files rows of the new `hm_sandboxholds`. The delivery thread only records the hold; a thread of its own submits the files within a second and asks the verdicts every `SandboxPollSeconds` (30), each request with its own ceiling and each pass with a minute's budget, the timeouts decided before any request: a pending answer waits, a failed request waits and is counted, a failed submit is tried again from the queued message, and `SandboxTimeoutMinutes` (15) counts from the hold's start. More files to submit than `SandboxMaxFiles` (10), a message that cannot be read, and a hold that cannot be recorded after five tries are decided by `SandboxTimeoutAction` with a header saying why - nothing is delivered unexamined. A malicious answer decides at once; anything else waits for every file. The decision is recorded on the rows and the message goes back into the queue, where the deliverer carries it out: every file **clean** delivers it with `X-hMailServer-Sandbox: clean (<n> file(s), <seconds>s)`; any **malicious**, or suspicious at or above `SandboxScoreThreshold` (70), quarantines it through `QuarantineStore::Quarantine` with the verdict's detail as the reason, so the recipient's digest lists it and `message.quarantined` is raised as today - or, while `QuarantineEnabled` is off, returns it to its sender with 5.7.1 - and the release of what the sandbox quarantined delivers it without a second submission (`FlagReleasedFromSandboxQuarantine`, set only for an entry whose reason is the sandbox's; any other release, the spam quarantine's above all, is examined like new mail); an **error** on a file once every file has answered, or the **timeout**, follows `SandboxTimeoutAction` (0 delivered with a header saying so, 1 returned to the sender with 5.7.1, or discarded with a log line when there is no sender to return it to). A sender's own `X-hMailServer-Sandbox` is removed on the first pass. Verdicts are remembered by SHA-256 in the new `hm_sandboxverdicts` for `SandboxCacheHours` (24), clean and malicious alike, server-wide, so a known file is not submitted again and a known-malicious one is quarantined at once; an error is never remembered. A file over `SandboxMaxSizeKB` (20480, at most the 81920 the parser takes) is not submitted and the header says so. A file name outside the Basic Multilingual Plane is stored with a question mark, which MySQL's utf8 needs. **Exempt**, and nothing else: what this server's signed-in users submit - an authenticated SMTP session, the webmail's send and what goes through its code, marked `FlagSubmittedBySignedInUser` when the server creates the message - to anyone, while `SandboxInternalMail` is off, so that their outgoing documents are never uploaded to the third party unasked (a stolen account's mail to colleagues is then not examined either, and the doc says so); a message with no attachment of a submitted type; a message the anti-virus deleted; the release of what the sandbox quarantined. The compromised-account hold takes precedence: a locked account's held message is moved to review, where no verdict reaches it. One application-log line per submission and per verdict, and `sandbox-submitted` and `sandbox-verdict` in the message trace. **What the administrator sees:** `GET /api/v1/sandbox/held`, a page at a time with the total (the held messages with their files, submission ids, states and failed-request counts), `GET /api/v1/queue` marking every held message `held: sandbox` with `held_since` and the submission ids, its Retry and Delete answering 409 naming the sandbox's routes (and a `held` column in `hmctl queue list` and on `Get-HmQueue`), `POST /api/v1/sandbox/held/{queue_id}/release` (delivered without the verdict, the header saying `released by administrator`; 409 when a verdict decided first) and `DELETE .../held/{queue_id}` (discarded), both under `manage_queue`, refused to a read-only key and to a credential confined to named domains, and both on the audit trail, as COM `DeliveryQueue.Remove` of a held message is; the thirteen settings on `PUT /api/v1/settings` with the same refusals as COM (https unless loopback, the switch and the addresses judged together, the types parsed and written out lower case and each once, a type with a slash that is no media type refused, the token's characters and length, the ranges), COM `Settings.Sandbox*` and `SetSandboxToken` at ids 190 to 202, the Control Panel's Anti-virus page on a Sandbox tab - the addresses before the switch, and a button that removes the token - and its External setup page, which says whether a sandbox is named and that whether it answers cannot be told from there (seventeen catalogues), and the Deck's queue page with a *Held for a sandbox* box whose release and discard ask first and whose states are in the reader's language (twenty catalogues; the settings themselves are drawn in the Deck's Server group from the OpenAPI document). Turning the sandbox off, or emptying an address, puts what it holds back in the queue, delivered with a header saying it was not examined. The person sees nothing: the webmail shows no held message. The legacy Blowfish scheme that a protected setting falls back to refuses a value longer than it holds (HM7277) rather than ending the process. Tests: `RegressionTests/AntiVirus/Sandbox.cs` on ports 9986 to 9988, twenty-six tests with a `FakeHttpEndpoint` playing the sandbox, which gained a per-path answer sequence (pending, pending, done) and a stall, and one in `SMTP/BlockedAttachmentTests.cs`. **Thinner than the row asks:** no vendor adapter is shipped, and the doc says which products speak something close; files only, no link is detonated; archives are not opened here; there is no `OnSandboxVerdict` event script, because the scripting surface has no `OnVirusFound` or any other anti-virus event to extend - the trace, the log and the quarantine's webhook are the surfaces. |
| ✅ | **SNMP** | **Built 25 September 2026 on branch `row304-snmp`, no schema step. Written without a compiler or a bench, as the row was briefed: every checker is green. The first commit compiled on Windows and on Linux, where `snmp-tests` passed; the review's fixes were written without a compiler too, and the fixture runs when the branch is folded.** The row's premise stood - the metrics were Prometheus and OpenTelemetry, which is right for anything built this decade and wrong for the monitoring system a bank already owns - and there is now a read-only **SNMP agent** beside them, off by default ([Snmp.md](hmailserver/docs/Snmp.md)). **What it speaks:** SNMPv3 with the User-based Security Model for one user at authPriv only - HMAC-SHA-256 (`usmHMAC192SHA256AuthProtocol`, RFC 7860) and AES-128-CFB (RFC 3826), keys made from the passwords by RFC 3414 A.2 with SHA-256 and localised to the engine - with RFC 3414 section 4's discovery (a Report of `usmStatsUnknownEngineIDs` carrying the engine ID, boots and time; an authenticated `usmStatsNotInTimeWindows` outside the 150-second window) and a Report for every other refusal the USM names; SNMPv2c only while `SnmpV2cEnabled` is on as well, the doc saying what its community in the clear costs; SNMPv1 not at all. Get, GetNext and GetBulk; Set answered `noAccess`; no traps and no informs. **Who it answers:** nobody until `SnmpAllowedAddresses` names a manager - a datagram from anywhere else is dropped before a byte is decoded, moving no counter and costing no cryptography. **What it serves:** the system, snmp, snmpEngine, snmpMPDStats and usmStats groups, and the mail MIB from the same counters `/metrics` serves - `mtaStoredMessages` the delivery queue's depth, `mtaTransmittedMessages` the delivered count, and one `mtaGroupTable` row per protocol the server runs with its sessions, its protocol OID and name, and for SMTP the queue and the age of its oldest message - with every object that no counter feeds answered `noSuchObject` and the doc naming each and why; sixteen more `/metrics` series sit under a placeholder enterprise arc, 1.3.6.1.4.1.32473 (RFC 5612's documentation number), **not registered with IANA**, which the doc says. **The bounds** on untrusted input, in `Common/Util/SnmpBer` and `SnmpEngine`: 8192 octets in and out, every length checked against what remains before it is read, no indefinite or five-octet length, no multi-octet tag, INTEGERs of one to eight octets, OIDs of at most 128 sub-identifiers below 2^32, nesting at most eight deep and no recursion, 64 bindings a request, GetBulk's repetitions held to 64 and an answer to 512 bindings and to the request's `msgMaxSize`, and an SNMPv2c answer to eight times its request (at least 484 octets), so that a forged source address cannot make the agent an amplifier - the doc saying what a recorded SNMPv3 request replayed inside its window still reflects, and recommending single manager addresses and a management interface; a packet that does not decode counted in `snmpInASNParseErrs` and never logged. **The engine:** its ID the enterprise form over a seed made once per installation and the machine's name, so two nodes sharing a database are two engines; nothing written for the agent while it is off; the seed written by its first start, only where there is none yet, so that nodes starting together agree on one; `snmpEngineBoots` kept per engine in a `SnmpBoots` row named by the engine ID, read from the database and written one higher before the socket opens at every start, and the agent refusing to start when it cannot be or finds the row damaged (7421); seed and counts kept as the agent's own records in `hm_settings`, left out of the backup and the changed-from-stock list. **Settings:** eleven rows of `hm_settings` (`SnmpEnabled`, `SnmpPort` 161, `SnmpBindAddress`, `SnmpAllowedAddresses`, `SnmpV3User`, `SnmpV3AuthPassword`, `SnmpV3PrivPassword`, `SnmpV2cEnabled`, `SnmpV2cCommunity`, `SnmpContact`, `SnmpLocation`), keys of `/api/v1/settings` and so of the Deck's Settings page and `hmctl`, the two passwords and the community stored protected, write-only, withheld from the audit trail and the changed-from-stock list, and left out of the backup; no COM property, as the metrics listener has none. The listener follows the metrics listener's lifecycle (`Application::StartServers` and `StopServers`); on Linux the packaged unit's `CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE` already lets it bind 161. Errors 7420 to 7422, none reachable while the agent is off. **Tested** by `SnmpTester` - the BER, the reader's bounds, the cryptography (AES-128-CFB against NIST SP 800-38A F.3.13, HMAC-SHA-256-192 and the SHA-256 key localisation against values computed independently), SNMPv2c and SNMPv3 exchanges against a clock it sets, two answers under two salts, a request truncated at every length, every element of a request and of a scoped PDU cut short inside true outer lengths (the scoped PDU signed after the cut, so the decoder behind the digest is reached), every cut that leaves an element unfinished refused and counted once at every level of each message, a Get past the SNMPv2c cap answered `tooBig`, thirteen malformed shapes, four thousand requests turned over at random and two thousand signed plaintexts - inside the service on Windows and as `snmp-tests` on Linux, which `-DHM_SANITIZE=ON` builds under AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer; and by `RegressionTests/Infrastructure/SnmpAgent.cs`, a manager with its own BER and USM over a real socket on test port 10200: discovery, a Get of `sysUpTime`, `sysObjectID` and an MTA-MIB counter, two answers under two salts, the counter moving on a delivery, a GetNext walk, a bounded GetBulk, a wrong password answered with a Report and no data, an address outside the allow list not answered until a range names it, SNMPv2c refused while off and answered when on, and the REST refusals and the secrets never read back. Not tested: a real Net-SNMP manager against it, which is the bench step that matters most. |

A job logs in (LOGIN, or XOAUTH2 with a token refreshed from its refresh token), maps each folder to the account's own - the INBOX, the special uses by attribute or by name, the rest by path - EXAMINEs it and fetches above its mark with BODY.PEEK, and files each message through **one import entry**, `MailImporter::ImportMessage` (the account, the folder as a list of names, its special use, the message file, the flags, the keywords, the internal date; APPEND's size ceiling, the volume's room and the quota; a duplicate by Message-ID, else Date, Subject and size, against what the same job wrote), which the PST import names as its seam. Marks every 50 messages and at each folder's end; a job that ran before reads what it wrote since it first started and skips it, so an interruption anywhere copies nothing twice; a changed UIDVALIDITY takes the folder again against the same keys; a service stop leaves its jobs running and the next start queues them; a network failure retries after 1, 5, 15 and then 60 minutes, 24 attempts, and a refused password fails at once. Gmail's labels arrive once, into the first folder a message is met in, All Mail last, Starred and Important not copied. Contacts and calendars follow the mail - RFC 6764 CardDAV and CalDAV for an IMAP source, Google's CardDAV and CalDAV, Microsoft Graph - and a phase that cannot run is skipped with its reason, never a failure. A job without a secret gets a sign-in link for its owner, once, kept as a hash, good only for the mailbox it copies. REST: `/api/v1/migrations` (list, create, get, delete refused while a job runs), `.../start`, `pause`, `resume`, `summary.csv`, `.../jobs` (a JSON job, or CSV `sourceuser,secret,target`, refused whole naming the line), `.../jobs/{job}`, `report`, `retry` (with a mended secret), `cancel` and `signin`; `view_accounts` to read, `manage_accounts` in the target's domain to write, no new permission; the migrations and jobs in the audit trail. The Deck's **Migrations** view (progress bars, the create dialog from the route's schema, the CSV pasted or chosen, each job's state, retry, cancel and report) in the twenty languages, 33 harness checks. recorded on the audit trail as `server`/`drain`/`entered` and `left`, and a stop or a reinitialise while draining records the `left` itself under the actor `server`, with the checker guarding all three calls; ## 13. The settings themselves

Where a setting lives, who may change it, when it takes effect and what it was before. The server has two
tables: `hm_settings`, which the COM `Settings` object and the Control Panel's classic pages have always
used, and `hm_inisettings`, which since schema 6042 holds the 241 keys the `[Settings]` section of
`hMailServer.ini` used to be the store for. **That flip has landed** (the first row below): the database
decides, the file's section is a cache the server rewrites at every start, a value edited into it is named
in the error log and put back, and a `[SettingsOverride]` section is the deliberate door for a database that
is unreachable or wrong. The rows below it are what that store makes possible and was blocking.

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **The database is the settings store; the file is the bootstrap** | **Landed at schema 6042.** The rule since 15 September 2026, that a new setting goes in the database and no new key is added to `[Settings]`, is in `CONTRIBUTING.md` at the repository root (`.github/CONTRIBUTING.md` until the move to GitLab), where a contributor meets it. `build/check-ini-coverage.py` now holds it against a committed baseline (`build/ini-settings-baseline.txt`, 266 keys across five sections, each with the default the server reads it with): a new `[Settings]` key fails CI with the rule quoted and `hm_settings` named, and a new bootstrap key is allowed with the baseline updated in the same commit. The precedence `hm_inisettings` was built for is flipped. `IniSettingStore::Synchronize` reads the table and adopts any `[Settings]` key the table has never seen; that is the migration, done once on the first start at 6042 and idempotent afterwards. It then makes the file match. A key edited in the file is named in the error log (HM5804) and NOT applied, and its line is put back. A row whose line has been deleted is written back rather than dropped, because deleting a line is no longer how a setting is returned to its default (`DeleteIniSetting`, `DELETE /api/v1/settings/ini/{name}` and the Control Panel are). Writes invert with it: `IniSettingStore::WriteSetting` stores the row FIRST and brings the file into line afterwards, because file-first would let a failed row write report success and then be reverted at the next start. `[SettingsOverride]` is the door. It is applied over everything, read even when the table cannot be, and announced by name at every start (HM5807); storing a key an override shadows says so too (HM5808). The whole `[Settings]` section stays mirrored into the file, and not for tidiness: `hMailServer.exe /Register` reads `ServiceAccountName` and `ServiceAccountPassword` with no database open at all, because registering the service happens before there is one. The Control Panel's `IniFeatureStore` inverts to match. It reads and writes over COM even when the file is on the same machine, and a Control Panel with no session says settings need a connection rather than writing a file that will be undone. The schema step carries no DDL on purpose, since the table has been right since 6011. The version bump IS the change: `Application::OnDatabaseConnected` refuses a mismatch in both directions, and that is what keeps a 6.3.3 server, which would let the file win, away from a database that is now the store. **Left for the rows below:** `[SendingLimits]`/`[SendingLimitsOverrides]` and `[LDAP]` are still read from the file, outside the `IniFileSettings` seam. Moving them needs section-qualified rows rather than the bare key names `hm_inisettings` holds today. `[GUILanguages]` stays by the rule itself, since `Languages::Load` runs before the database is open. The container entrypoint was moved the same day (d174b2939): `docker-entrypoint.sh` stores `HM_REST_PORT`, `HM_REST_BIND_ADDRESS`, `HM_REST_CERTIFICATE_FILE` and `HM_REST_PRIVATE_KEY_FILE` with `--set-setting` on every start rather than writing them into a file the store overrules, so a changed variable takes effect on the next start against the same database. The wider seeding of the database from the environment is *Configuration from the environment*, below. |
| ✅ | **A setting that takes effect when it is saved** | **Built 22 September 2026 on branch `batch26`, reviewed adversarially and corrected on 23 September, and landed after its first bench, whose three failures that were the tree's - two list assertions the row had broken by growing each entry, and a per-minute rate limit whose window survived a save - are fixed with it, and the server compiles and links on Linux (clang, Ubuntu 26.04).** The row's premise held when it was picked up: every `[Settings]` key was latched into one of ~240 members of `IniFileSettings` when the service started, a COM, REST or `hmctl` save persisted the value and applied it at the next start, and the Control Panel said "applies after a service restart" in a page footer and under thirty-odd fields. What is built ([SettingsApply.md](hmailserver/docs/SettingsApply.md)): **one immutable block, swapped** - the members are `IniFileSettings::Values`, a load builds a new block and publishes it with one atomic store and a generation counter, every getter reads through `Current()` - a copy per thread of the last block it loaded, checked against the counter, because on the C++14 Windows build `std::atomic_load` of a `shared_ptr` takes one process-wide spin lock - and every String getter returns a copy (a reference into a block that may be replaced is a use-after-free, and the header says so); the bootstrap sections are not in it, and are read at a start, a reinitialise and COM's `Settings.Directories`, never by a save. **A mark per key, in the baseline**: `build/ini-settings-baseline.txt` gained a fifth field, `now`, `reinitialize` or `restart`, decided key by key from the code that reads it (211 now; 28 reinitialize - the REST, metrics, web-services and ManageSieve listeners' addresses, ports, credentials and certificates, the six TLS context settings, `MaxNumberOfExternalFetchThreads`, `DatabaseStatementTimeout`, `AcmeEnabled` and `AcmeCertificateDirectory`; 28 restart - `ServiceAccountName`, `ServiceAccountPassword` and the 26 bootstrap keys), required by `check-ini-coverage.py` (an unmarked line fails with a sentence saying what the three mean; the check proves that negative control on every run) and carried into `IniSettingsBaseline.h` as `Applies`. **A save publishes**: `WriteSettingsValue` and `RemoveSettingsValue` - every door to the store, the audit trail's revert included - take the reload lock, store the value and, in the same hold, read the store back READ-ONLY (`IniSettingStore::ReadStoredValues`: no file or table write, no adoption, no report), re-apply the `[SettingsOverride]` values the last start or reinitialise loaded without re-reading the section, build a block in which each `now` key takes the stored value and each other keeps its running one, and publish it; then, with the lock released, the subscribers of every key that moved are called, one at a time. The review found the first version reconciling the file against the table on every save, outside any lock shared with the store writes, so two saves at once could bring a DELETE back or put an older value over a PUT that had answered 200; the reconcile (HM5804, HM5803, adoption) is a start's and a reinitialise's again. A reinitialise takes the `now` and `reinitialize` keys and re-reads `[SettingsOverride]`, a start everything. **Subscribers** (`IniFileSettings::Subscribe`) re-latch what was copied at start, each reading the value it takes when it runs: the greylist cleaner's period (`Scheduler::SetTaskInterval`, the scheduler now writing a next run time only under its lock, and not moving on a period restarted during a run), the backup schedule's registration, the three OpenTelemetry exporters (restarted under a lifecycle lock of their own), the web services' DAV-redirect cache, `HM6119` when the fault injector is set, and the one-minute windows of `MaxSubmissionsPerIPPerMinute` and `MaxOutboundPerDestinationPerMinute`, started again when either is saved so that a limit counts from when it was saved; the two that reach the scheduler copy it and their task under a lock a service stop's reset takes too. **Pending, applies, Apply now**: `GET`, `PUT` and `DELETE /api/v1/settings/ini/{name}` answer `applies` and `pending` - `applies` null, with the sentence COM refuses with as `applies_refused`, for a name the loader does not read - and the list `GET /api/v1/settings/ini` keeps its entries as `{name, value, secret}`, every stored name among them whether the loader reads it or not, with an `applies` map of every key the build reads and a `pending` array of names beside them; `GET /api/v1/server/settings/pending` answers `[{name, stored, running, applies, secret}]`, recomputed from the store against the running block (a value put back is not pending); COM `Settings.IniSettingApplies(name)` (refusing a name the loader does not read, taken as spelled), `Settings.PendingIniSettings` and `Settings.IniSettingAppliesAll` (ids 180 to 182); the OpenAPI text gives the ini-backed group rows the baseline's sentence and `x-applies`, and its PUT and DELETE say what a save and a removal publish; `POST /api/v1/server/reinitialize` answers `drained: true` when it ends a drain. The Control Panel shows **Applies on save / After reinitialize / After a restart** under every field kept in the store (every mark in one COM call per page open, a failed call not remembered), writes only the fields whose value changed, reads the pending list once per save, says how many of a page's keys wait, drops the blanket footers and the per-field restart sentences (seventeen catalogues), and offers **Apply now** in a pending bar at the foot of the window (a reinitialise, asked first and asked differently while draining, then waiting for Running, then saying how many keys still wait for a restart); against a server from before this it marks every field **After a restart** and says a restart applies a save, as it did. The Deck shows the same marks beside its ini-backed fields, a note naming the three, and the same banner above the Settings view, in twenty catalogues. Docs: SettingsApply.md (the marks, the inventory with the reason for every key that is not `now`, the swap, the subscribers, what a save does and does not re-read, the pending list, the older server), a paragraph in Upgrading.md, a README clause, ControlPanelDesign.md's page lifecycle. Tests: `RegressionTests/Infrastructure/SettingsApplyOnSave.cs` (REST 9876, web services 9877, ManageSieve 9878-9879), linked into the Linux suite with the three COM members answered over REST: `MaxSubmissionsPerIPPerMinute` applying with no reinitialise and returning to its default with a DELETE, a saved limit counting from when it was saved, `CalDavRedirectUrl` re-latched after the cache was primed, `ManageSieveServerPort` stored and not applied until the reinitialise, `ServiceAccountName` still pending after one, `MetricsServerAuthPassword` pending as `(set)`, `HM6119` from the save's subscriber, twenty PUT/DELETE pairs on each of two keys at once ending as each client's last acknowledged request left them, a name the loader does not read refused over COM and null over REST in the same words, the reinitialise's `drained`, the list's entries kept as `{name, value, secret}` with its `pending` array and `applies` map against the pending route and the baseline file, and COM's three members; `IniFileSettingsTester` in the internal suite (the swap under load; a `now` key in effect and told to its subscriber once; a removal publishing its default with no reload of the tester's own; a `reinitialize` key held and pending; eight threads alternating two hundred writes and deletes of a key each beside a ninth reloading, every key's row and running value what its thread did last; the timings logged); `Roles.cs` holding the pending route to the administrator password; `IniSettingsMirror` holding a save to keeping an override without re-reading the section and a reinitialise to re-reading it; the Deck harness the badge, the note, the banner and Apply now's questions and yes path; `ControlPanel.Tests` the words, the one read of the marks and of the pending list, a failed read not remembered, and an older server told apart. **Thinner than the row asks**: 28 keys still need a reinitialise and two `[Settings]` keys a restart (and the service account a re-registration), because a listener, a TLS context, the pool's connections and a work queue are not rebuilt in place - Apply now makes that one click; a change to `[SettingsOverride]` waits for a reinitialise or a start, and an override of a `restart` key for a start; a value another node or `hmailserver --set-setting` stores reaches this server at its next save, reinitialise or restart, since nothing watches the table; the pending list covers the `[Settings]` keys only; the row's own examples `TCPIPThreads` and `MaxNoOfDeliveryThreads` are `hm_settings` properties, not `[Settings]` keys, so `IniSettingApplies` refuses them and the tests use `ManageSieveServerPort`; the mark lives in the baseline the server reads, not in the palette's `SettingsSearchIndex`; the Deck has no page for the ini keys beyond its three group rows, and its session ends with the reinitialise; the branding and file-link routes reload once per field they write; the bootstrap strings are still rewritten in place under a lock their readers do not take, a race older than the block; `IniFileSettingsTester` does not run in the Linux suite; `hmctl` gained no pending verb. |
| ✅ | **One value, three levels** | **Built 23 September 2026 on branch `batch27`, schema 6088; reviewed adversarially the same day - five must-fix findings, eighteen should-fix and twelve notes, every one applied - and not yet run against a bench.** The row's premise held when it was picked up: a handful of settings had a domain column of their own - the maximum message size, the digest's switch and hour, the external tag, the session policies, the quota shares - and one an account column (the retention), each read at its site with its own rule for which level won, every other setting was the server's, and nothing showed what was in effect for an account or where it came from. What is built ([SettingLevels.md](hmailserver/docs/SettingLevels.md)): **one resolver** - `Common/Application/EffectiveSetting`, a table of 27 settings, every one to the account level, each exactly one of three kinds: a **limit** (15: the message size, the recall and recover-deleted windows, the two sending limits, the four session policies, the quota grace and send-stop shares, the four reader maximums), which a level below may tighten and never loosen - a looser value refused when written with the sentence naming the level above, and capped when read if the level above has since tightened past it; a **gate** (4: zero-access, the OpenPGP portal, a self-edited directory, plus addressing), the server administrator's at every level; a **preference** (8: the digest's switch and hour, the two tags, impersonation, the warning share, the country policy, the retention), where the deepest level wins. the four upgrade scripts and the three create scripts, Compact built from the MSSQL one), holds the levels no column held - no row is "take the level above" - cached per owner and per name for a minute and dropped by every write and by emptying the caches, each value checked when written and again when read (`HM7311` once per name until the caches are emptied); `Domain.PlusAddressingEnabled` now refuses a domain administrator a change. **hmctl** `domain set` and `account set`, sending a field's own inherit value and refusing inherit where a field has none. Only the four REST ones are `[Settings]` keys, and since schema 6042 those take effect only on the first start against a database; after that the stored value wins and HM5804 names the edit. A Kubernetes user expects every setting to come from the environment, and a Secret to be able to supply any of them as a file. With the database as the store, the entrypoint seeds the database instead of rewriting a file. That also settles what happens when the environment and the stored value disagree: the environment is the deployment's intent and wins on start, and the log says which values it overrode. |
