each with a title, a paragraph that names what the profile takes away, the steps it cannot take ("Require SSL/TLS when authenticating" on an IP range, which is what refuses a password in the clear, Incoming relays, a port's TLS, the database pool for the delivery threads, a domain's DKIM, the addresses only the administrator knows) and one sentence of why beside every entry. **A diff first**: `GET /api/v1/settings/profiles` and `GET .../profiles/{name}` (every entry beside the value the server holds, whether it changes, the route and key it is written through, when it takes effect, its level, the environment's variable, the row's refusal, the caution and the confirmation it asks, and a `diff_token` of what was shown), COM `GetProfiles` and `GetProfile`, the Deck's checklist, the Control Panel's **Setting profiles** page and `hmctl`. **Then a snapshot, then the writes**: the apply (REST, COM `ApplyProfile(name, confirmed, diffToken)`, `hmailserver --apply-profile`, `hmctl`) is refused as stale when the server no longer answers the diff whose token it brings, checks every entry and applies all or nothing, saves the way back in `ProfileSnapshot` and applies nothing when the database does not take it (HM7301), writes each entry through the setter its REST settings row runs or a `[Settings]` key through the locked store write - never straight into the property set - skips a setting the environment manages, reads each back and says which wait for a reinitialise or a restart, records `applied` or `failed` after the writes, and is one audit event. **A listener, a trust or data is never taken silently**: an entry that closes a listener, trusts an address range, turns a filter or a check off or weakens it (a throttle or limit set to 0, AUTH PLAIN, a spam threshold past the shipped value), or shortens a window of days in force - the next sweep deleting what the shorter window no longer keeps, which no revert brings back - asks for an explicit confirmation of the diff (`{"confirm": true, "diff_token": ...}`, a confirmation without its token refused 400), `--confirm` on the command line, a box in the Deck and the Control Panel, `HM_PROFILE_CONFIRM`. **The way back**: the revert (REST, COM, `--revert-profile`) puts back only what still holds the profile's value, a `[Settings]` key byte for byte, and says what waits; discard keeps the settings; an unreadable way back can only be discarded (HM7300), and the pages offer that; `decline` records that the shipped settings are kept (the installer's None, `HM_PROFILE=none`, a button in both pages). The records are read from the database whenever they decide anything, so a record the command line wrote is the running server's too; the command-line verbs refuse while the Windows service runs (exit 4), and `--if-new` leaves a server of ten accounts alone (exit 5). **At setup**: the checklist's first item is a profile on a young server - `profile_failed` and `profile_declined` beside the other four codes; the installer's last page, after the database step for the built-in database and for an external one, stops the service the database tools started and runs `--apply-profile NAME --once --if-new --confirm`, saying on every failure that the profile was not applied; the container applies `HM_PROFILE` once before the server starts. The changed-from-stock document names a profile only as the source of a value its apply wrote and that still holds it. The generator refuses a port, a path, a bind address, a command or an address the server calls, a database value, a secret, a record, a value outside its row's range for a property or a `[Settings]` key, a reason of more than one sentence, a caution missing or unearned, a window whose 0 it does not know, and an entry that opens a listener, trusts an address range or weakens a check that the profile's summary does not name. Tested in `API/RestApiProfiles.cs` (REST on 10030, twenty-two tests: the relay and the sender applied with every entry written and an effect of each read from its reader - the directory, the message trace - as the office password policy and the gateway's quarantine are; the stale token; the window; the unreadable way back; the verbs with the service stopped; the young server at ten accounts), the Deck harness (952 checks), `check-cli.py` (1608) and `ControlPanel.Tests` (838). **Thinner than the row asks:** four profiles and not user-defined ones, and no parameter on one - a value only the administrator knows is a step, not an entry; one snapshot, the last apply's; the server level only, which for a limit is the ceiling below it; the gateway profile has seventeen entries, not thirty, because the rest of that shape is an IP range, Incoming relays and a domain's settings, which are no setting; a caution is decided by the value an entry sets, and for a window by the one it replaces; switching a control on asks no confirmation; the way back longer than 4000 characters, a write failing part way and the records' absence from the XML backup are untested; the installer page, the entrypoint's `HM_PROFILE` and the POSIX verbs have been written and read, not run - on Linux nothing tells the verbs whether a server is running. The row as written: The defaults suit one kind of deployment. A named profile - a small office server, a server behind a filtering gateway, an outbound relay, a high-volume sender - applied at setup or from the Deck, that sets thirty settings at once and says exactly which thirty and what they were, with a diff before it is applied and a way back. The setup wizard asks none of this today, and a new administrator is left to find each one. |

## 14. What a mail platform is asked to do, and this one does not

A third sweep, this time from the question an integrator asks rather than the one an administrator or a
reader asks: what does an application, a deliverability team or a compliance officer need from the server
that it cannot get today? Each row was checked against the code before it was written, and each says what
exists so that the gap is the only claim in it.

| | Item | Detail |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ | **Mail in, to a webhook** (SendGrid and Mailgun call it inbound parse) | **Built 25 September 2026 on branch `row339-inbound`, schema 6099 to 6100, beside four other rows, and put through an adversarial review the same day whose findings the second commit answers; not compiled and not run - the integrator builds it and gates it.** An address or a whole domain of a hosted domain is routed to a URL (`/api/v1/inbound-webhooks`, the Control Deck's Inbound webhooks page; no COM and no Control Panel page). The recipient path takes a routed address before the list manager's addresses and the accounts - RCPT TO is accepted for it whether or not a mailbox has it - and the delivery path takes it off the message in the same order, after the rules, the scan and the sandbox and before the list manager, keeps a copy through the message store and queues it; the pass posts it, first from a drain posted at once and then every minute, one pass at a time and nothing ever waiting for one, and an endpoint that is down or a route switched off holds up no other route. A route that delivers as well puts back what its address reaches as RCPT TO decides it, a list's posting rules and moderation included. The body is the parsed message - sender, recipients, subject, text, HTML, attachments and the verdicts (the spam classification, and SPF, DKIM and DMARC from this server's own Authentication-Results) - as SendGrid's multipart/form-data fields or, per route, one JSON document (the row's "as JSON"), with the raw message on request; signed exactly as an outbound webhook is and retried on its schedule (`WebhookMaxAttempts`, the 0/1/5/15/60-minute ladder). Per route: whether the mailbox gets the message too, what a message never accepted becomes - kept for the administrator (the dead letter, readable, retried and removed over REST and on the Deck) or returned to its sender with a delivery-status report - a size ceiling and an attachment policy (carried, named only, left out). The endpoint may be on the administrator's own network but never a link-local or cloud metadata address, judged at every connection; a route made by any other credential, an API key among them, reaches public addresses only; no redirect is followed; the URL is read back whole only by a credential that may change the route, and the audit trail records it as changed, never by its value. A new permission, `manage_inbound_webhooks`, is what an application's key is given, judged in the route's domain. Thinner than a hosted inbound parse: SendGrid's field names and not Mailgun's; one post per route per message; no proxy; at most 80 MB a message; not in the configuration backup, though a domain deleted takes its routes and an erasure sweeps the queue ([InboundWebhooks.md](hmailserver/docs/InboundWebhooks.md)). |
| ✅ | **A send API for applications** | **Built 24 September 2026 on branch `batch31`, schema 6089, and its adversarial review applied the same day. The fixture's twenty-two tests run alone and green against the bench's service, every checker green, the server and the regression tests building; the rest of the suite was not run and the Linux server not compiled.** The row's premise held when it was picked up: `POST /api/v1/me/messages` was the webmail's and answered only an account's own credentials, an API key could be scoped to a role and to domains but never to a sender, nothing counted what a key sent, and the one thing that knew what became of a message - the message trace - answered the server's administrators and recorded neither a deferral nor the remote's reply. What is built ([SendApi.md](hmailserver/docs/SendApi.md)): **the key becomes a sender** - three fields beside the others in the key store, `SendFrom` (an address of a hosted domain, or `*@domain`; empty, as on every older key, sends nothing), `SendPerMinute` and `SendPerDay` (0 sets none of the key's own; a key whose sender is `*@domain` or an address that is no account's must have a `SendPerDay`), set on `POST /api/v1/apikeys` and in the Deck's key form (twenty catalogues) and shown in its list; the route refuses with a sentence a sender carrying a line break, a bracket, `=` or `;` (the store's own syntax), a sender at a domain this server does not host or has switched off, a sender on a read-only key or on a role without the permission, a sender outside the key's own domains, a `*@domain` or non-account sender without a day ceiling, and a ceiling without a sender; read back from the store, each fails closed. A new permission, **`send_through_api`**, appended to the vocabulary (the built-in `serveradmin` holds it, a custom role may be given it, `docs/Roles.md` lists it), classified for `POST /api/v1/send` in `PermissionFor_` and mutating; a key sends only when its role holds it AND it names a sender, both decided in `Authorize_`, which refuses the route to the administrator password and to accounts first. **The route**, `Common/Util/RestApiSend.cpp`: bearer key only; `{from?, to[], cc[], bcc[], reply_to?, subject, text?, html?, headers?{}, attachments?[{filename, content_type, data}]}`, a member it does not take refused by name; `from` defaults to the key's address and must match its sender (the display name free); headers limited to `X-` fields other than `X-hMailServer-`, `List-Unsubscribe` with its RFC 8058 `List-Unsubscribe-Post` (one more than the row's list, since the first is half of one-click unsubscribe without the second), `In-Reply-To` and `References`, anything else 400. The message is built and checked by **the webmail send's own code**, refactored out of `HandleMeMessageSend_` into `ResolveSendRecipients_` (the recipient ceiling with the server's sentence, what `RCPT TO` asks, restricted delivery for the account the send is judged on, the suppression list), `WriteComposedMessage_` (the MIME, the attachments, the inline images) and `CheckComposedMessage_` (the domain's signature and disclaimer, the blocking rules, the maximum size, 413), which the webmail's send now calls in the same order; the envelope sender is `from`, a `Received` field goes first naming this server, the send API and the key's label - not its id, not the peer - no Sent copy is made, DKIM signs it at delivery as it signs the domain's mail, and the sender account's own sending quota is consumed when `from` is an account's address. Per-key ceilings through `RateLimiter`: `api-send:<key id>` for the minute, `api-send-day:<key id>` on the account quota's persisted period machinery for the day; 429 with `Retry-After` and a sentence, counted only once every other check has passed - the ceilings in turn, each given back when a later one refuses or the message is not queued (`CountedUsage`). 202 `{queue_id, message_id, recipients, recorded}`. Every send is recorded in **`hm_apisends`** (`apsid`, `apskeyid`, `apsqueueid`, `apsmessageid`, `apssender`, `apsrecipients` nvarchar 4000, `apscreated`; the four `Upgrade6088to6089*`, the three create scripts, DBUpdater's step and name, a schema probe, the `Constants.h` paragraph), kept `MessageTraceRetentionDays` and then removed by the hourly pass of `RemoveExpiredRecords`. **Asking afterwards**: `GET /api/v1/send/{queue_id}` answers the key that sent it and nobody else (the same 404 as an unknown id), `{queue_id, message_id, sender, recipients, created, state, trace, recipients_state: [{address, state, when, status, code, reason}]}`, the states `queued`, `deferred`, `delivered`, `bounced`, `refused` - and `unknown`, said below - derived from the trace's events for the queue id and, while the message waits, from the queue; `GET /api/v1/send?page=&size=` lists the key's own sends newest first, 200 at most a page; an administrator's key that names no sender reads an empty list, and the trace routes stay the server-wide view. For that the trace now holds what the row asks of it: a **`deferred`** event for every attempt made to a remote that ends without a verdict (none for a hold of the server's own shaping), and the detail of a failed or deferred delivery written as `[<enhanced status>] <the remote's reply>` (`MessageTrace::DeliveryDetail`; a permanent failure's code is the remote's own 5xx, a give-up's the last reply's own 4xx, 550 where no remote replied); and a delivery to several local accounts is journalled under the queue id for every recipient, where only the one that reused the queue row was before. The reports count `delivered` and `failed` as they did, and read no other event. Docs: `SendApi.md` (new, in the index, reference-only in the help map), the root README's REST paragraph, `Webhooks.md` (a subscription confined to the sender hears the same deliveries pushed - for recipients outside this server), `Roles.md`, `Reports.md`, `SettingLevels.md`. Tests: `RegressionTests/API/RestApiSend.cs` (REST 9896), nine at first and twenty-two since the review (below), each with its negative control - a noreply@ key sending text, HTML and an attachment that arrives from noreply@ with that Return-Path, its bytes intact by SHA-256 and a Received line naming the API and the label, nothing in any folder of the sender, and the same body refused from a key with no sender, with a from outside it, from a read-only key, from the administrator password and from an account; a `*@domain` key sending as two local parts and refused another hosted domain's and an unnamed from, and keys refused at a domain not hosted; a remote holding its reply read as queued, then one recipient delivered with the time and one bounced with the remote's own 554 5.7.1 and its reply; a 451 4.3.0 read as deferred with its status and reply, then delivered after the retry; a second key's 404 identical to an unknown id's, the list only the caller's, newest first and paged; `send_per_minute = 2` refusing the third with 429 and `Retry-After` beside a key without it sending three, 413 over the size beside a message under it, four refused headers beside two that arrive, the recipient ceiling's own sentence beside a list at it; a role that lost `send_through_api` refused where it had sent, and a key refused at making with such a role; the trace off reading unknown and on reading delivered; the 6089 probe. The Deck harness checks the three fields, a ceiling that is no number refused in the page, and the list. **Thinner than the row asks**: no templates and no substitutions; a forward or a list's re-send is a new queue id and is not followed, as the trace's header says; the states need the trace, which is off by default - with it off a message that has left the queue reads `unknown` - and an address that resolved to others is answered under its own name only - the state its addresses hold together while the queue holds them, `unknown` once it has let them go; a key's sender cannot be changed, only a new key issued, as with every other field of a key; the key's ceilings count messages, not recipients; the minute is in memory and a restart forgives it; the desktop Control Panel's key page does not show the three fields; the compromised-account detection is not asked, there being no session behind a key. **The adversarial review of 24 September found and the follow-up fixed** (4 must-fix, 15 should-fix, 14 notes): the answer named every address a recipient resolved to - a list's members, a masked address's owner, an alias's target - so a send-only key could read any list's membership and unmask any masked address; it now answers the addresses the body named and no other, folding a queue row into its original address and dropping a trace event nothing maps to a named one, and the 202 counts the named addresses; an account-quota refusal had already spent the key's minute and day - `RateLimiter::Refund` and `RefundAccountUsage` give them back, through `CountedUsage`, when a later ceiling refuses or the message is not queued, and the webmail's send, which now consumes the account's sending quota too (429 with SMTP's sentence), uses the same; the `unknown` reasons were false for a partly delivered send with the trace off and for a local refusal with it on - four sentences now, by where the message stands and whether the trace is on; the README and `Webhooks.md` promised states the default install does not give - both say the trace is off by default, and the answer and the listing carry a `trace_note` when it is; address erasure reaches `hm_apisends`; `send_from` refuses the characters that would be lines or sections of the key store; the 6089 step indexes `hm_apisends` by key and time and by queue id and `hm_messagetrace` by queue id, and one send's read of the trace is bounded to its newest 500 events; no `deferred` row for a hold of the server's own shaping; an array recipient is one mailbox, so a quoted display name may hold a comma; a domain or an account switched off sends nothing (403), and a key is not made at a switched-off domain; a `*@domain` or non-account sender needs `send_per_day`; the key list's column has a catalogue key of its own (`The key sends as`, twenty catalogues) and the DMARC view's `Sends as` has its seven translations back; any GET under `/send/` is the send route's 404; the Received field names the label only; a give-up's code is the last reply's own; a refusal is logged once a minute per key; the reports' query reads `delivered` and `failed` only; revoking a key deletes its records, and the hourly pass removes those of a key no longer in the store; the trace's own `DeleteExpired` now runs in the same hourly pass - nothing had swept the trace but a COM call; and the Control Panel's store preamble names the three fields. Thirteen tests added (the named-only answer against the trace's own view, the refund's order, the day ceiling, the webmail's quota, a DKIM signature verified against its record, a give-up's code, local journalling and a rule's removal, the trace off part way, header injection, the store read back, key creation's refusals, a sender switched off, erasure, revocation). Left as gaps, and said so: erasure does not reach `hm_heldmessages`, `hm_confidential` or `hm_accountrecipients` (the row below); a draft the webmail scheduled is not counted against the sending quota when its minute comes; the hourly orphan sweep, the 4,000-character cut of `apsrecipients`, the listing's ten-thousand reach, `recorded:false`, restricted delivery for an account sender and the `policy_rejected` and suppression refusals through `/send` have no test. |
| ✅ | **Address erasure reaches the held, the expiring and the remembered-recipient stores** | **Built 24 September 2026 on branch `row341-erasure`, no schema. Not yet compiled or run against a bench: written in a worktree beside four other rows, it is compiled, built and gated when the branch is folded.** Found by the review of the send API row above. `AddressTraceEraser::Erase` now reaches the four stores this row named and three more of the same kind that a sweep of the create scripts found, each with its own `failedStores` entry. **Messages held for review** (`hm_heldmessages`) that the address sent or is a recipient of are discarded whole, as the quarantine's entries are and as the review page's Discard does (`CompromiseDetector::EraseAddress`, HM7380 when one cannot be): the message names the address, and a release afterwards would carry it on. **Messages that expire** (`hm_confidential`) have the address taken out of the recipients of another sender's message, which the others go on reading, and out of `opened_by`, which then reads `[erased]`; one the address sent, or one left with no recipient, is ended as a withdrawal ends it and its row removed, and the address's one-time codes and reading grants leave memory (`ConfidentialMessages::EraseAddress`). **The recipients an account remembers writing to** (`hm_accountrecipients`) and **the out-of-office memory** (`hm_autoreplies`) lose the address's row; **the correspondents' S/MIME certificates** (`hm_smimekeys`, kind 2) and **OpenPGP keys** (`hm_pgpring`, kind 2) other accounts keep are removed; and **password-protected messages** (`hm_protectedmessages`) to the address, and the replies it wrote, are ended (`ProtectedMessages::EraseAddress`). Every comma-joined list is matched whole. The archive index's own sweep (`PersistentArchiveIndex::RemoveByAddress`) matched with `LIKE` alone - erasing `carol@` took `mcarol@`'s rows, and behind the `[_]` escape an address with an underscore was never found on MySQL or PostgreSQL - and now matches whole too, and says when it fails. **A legal hold outranks the erasure.** It never deletes a message from a mailbox; in the stores it does delete from, a held archived copy keeps its file and its row as before, and a held account's messages held for review, that expire or are password-protected are left as they are. The REST answer gains `held`, the count of what a hold kept; the application log says it, the Deck's toast says it and `hmctl privacy erase` prints it. Holds that could not be read (every question then answered "held") fail the erasure instead, naming them (HM6250, `500`), and nothing is said of a legal hold. eDiscovery's own tables and files are the case's record and are not erased; the compression row's tables hold no address. **Left, each with its reason in PersonalData.md:** mail in mailboxes, the deleted items, their indexed headers and a list's archive; other people's calendar events; configuration that names an address (a forward, a recovery address, send-as identities, fetch and route logins, a push sender filter, a rule, a list's moderator, the sender lists, a domain's postmaster and protected persons, a journal rule, an alert recipient, a webhook filter); the DMARC and TLS reporting organisations' contacts; the hash-chained audit trail and the alert events nothing prunes; the spam-trap record and the block it made; the delivery and webhook queues; a mailbox move's journal and a migration job's source login; the archive's Inbound folder and backups. [PersonalData.md](hmailserver/docs/PersonalData.md), Discovery.md's hold table and what a hold does not reach, and a paragraph each in CompromisedAccounts.md, ConfidentialMessages.md, OpenPGP.md and OutOfOffice.md. Fixture `API/RestApiErasureStores.cs` (REST port 10160, seven tests): each store beside an erasure of an address that is only part of the erased one; a held message discarded with its queue row and file when the address is a recipient, its sender, or reached through an alias; a message that expires losing the address from who opened it, and one the address sent ended; a protected message's ciphertext and its reply ended; kind-1 certificates and keys kept; and a hold on the sending account keeping its held, expiring and protected messages, counted in `held`, until the case is closed. `Infrastructure/ArchiveIndexTests.cs` gains the longer address's rows kept and an underscored address in capitals found; `API/RestApiArchive.cs` asserts the held copy counted once. **Answered after its adversarial review** (one must-fix, seventeen should-fix and notes): unread holds fail the erasure; held messages are matched through the queue's recipient rows (the original addresses and alias targets, not only the 2,000-character review copy) and the envelope sender; a discard answers success only when the queue row, the recipient rows, the queue file and the review row are all gone, which the review page's Discard now shares; a recipient rewrite of a message that expires is conditional on the row as it was read and read back; an address's `[` no longer hides it from SQL Server's `LIKE` (the send API's record included); a protected upload chunk can no longer recreate a file an erasure or a withdrawal deleted; the log lines name no erased address; the Deck's and the Control Panel's confirmations name the new stores in every catalogue. **Thinner than it could be:** COM's `EraseAddressTraces`, and so the Control Panel, answers the count alone - the log line says what a hold kept - and the IDL helpstring still lists the older stores, left for the next IDL change since this row has no COM; a kept copy or ciphertext that cannot be deleted leaves its row withdrawn and the erasure failed, for the next run to finish; an erasure removes the index rows of archived copies in other people's trees and Inbound, whose files stay (PersonalData.md says so); and the archive's own search still escapes `_` and `%` in SQL Server's way, so on MySQL and PostgreSQL a search for a term with either finds nothing - the erasure no longer depends on it, and the search is left for a row of its own. |
| ✅ | **Bounces read, and a suppression list** | **In the tree since 15 September 2026 (branch bounce-suppression), unreleased; schema 6049.** A refusal met while delivering and an RFC 3464 delivery report arriving for a hosted sender are classified against RFC 3463 by `BounceClassifier` - hard (X.1.1, X.1.2, X.1.3, X.1.6, X.1.10, X.2.1), mailbox full (X.2.2, counted as hard only with `BounceMailboxFullIsHard`), soft (class 4), policy (X.7, which never lists) and other - under a trust rule: the remote's own enhanced code is re-read from its verbatim reply and only that is believed, so the 5.1.1 this server's own report derives from a bare `550` is counted as other, never hard - in a delivery report too, where an smtp Diagnostic-Code is re-read the same way rather than its Status believed - and a failure no remote server answered is never hard either; an identical permanent refusal of several recipients by one host is taken to be the session, not the mailboxes. Counted per sending domain and address in `hm_bouncecounts`; a run of hard bounces reaching `BounceHardBounceThreshold` with no delivery between puts the address on the domain's list in `hm_bouncesuppressions`; a remote's acceptance sets the run aside in the register rather than ending it, and the message's report gives it back, so a threshold above one is reachable behind a smart host (added on review, 15 September 2026, with the run carried forward onto a later acceptance and every undecidable case adding nothing). A report is believed only with a null sender, addressed to one local address, not spam, arriving unauthenticated over SMTP here, not failing this server's own SPF/DMARC result where it writes one, to a verifying prvs address when BATV is on, and matching - once - a SHA-256 of Message-ID, recipient and sender written to `hm_bouncereportkeys` when a remote accepted the message, with at most 50 recipients of one report looked up; it is delivered unchanged either way. The list is applied at SMTP RCPT TO for a submission and on the webmail's send route (550 with the entry's status and a sentence; 400 `recipient_suppressed` with the entry's source, translated in all 23 portal languages as a bounce or as an entry an administrator made), and at delivery for everything else - refuse with an RFC 3464 report, drop, or observe (`BounceSuppressionAction`) - fail-open with HM6622 when the list cannot be read. Eight settings in `hm_settings`, all off or inert by default; six REST routes and `/api/v1/settings/bounces` in the OpenAPI document, scoped per domain for restricted keys and audited when changed by hand; the Deck (a Suppressions view per domain, the settings group, 40 texts in 20 catalogues); the Control Panel (a Suppression list page and a card on Delivery of e-mail, 74 texts in 17 languages, over six new members of `Utilities` and eight of `Settings`, so no new COM interface); `hmctl` and `HmailServer.psm1`; an hourly sweep for expiry and retention; `Utilities.EraseAddressTraces` and domain deletion take the rows. Documented in [docs/BounceSuppression.md](hmailserver/docs/BounceSuppression.md). Every changed C++ unit compiled in a mirror with the release flags, and every regression source compiled with Roslyn against an interop from the branch's IDL; the four ClassTester testers and 36 regression tests (SMTP/BounceSuppression, SMTP/BounceReports, API/RestApiBounces) are for the gate. Limits, stated in the page: a co-recipient who saw the Message-ID can forge a report for another visible recipient (turn `read_delivery_reports` off if that matters), without the stage a reply was given at, the session guard both demotes identical per-recipient refusals (two dead recipients at one Microsoft 365 tenant in one message count as other) and cannot see a single-recipient message refused at EHLO, AUTH, MAIL FROM, DATA or BDAT with a remote 5.1.x, which is counted as the code says (both need a stage flag in SMTPClientConnection), the run through reports can come out short when reports arrive out of order, and addresses are compared lower-cased. The complaints row below can list an address with the reserved source 3. |
| ✅ | **Complaints, read** (the feedback loops) | **Built 16 September 2026 on the branch `feedback-loops`, reviewed and corrected on the same branch, and gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** Everything the row asks for is written. A report a provider posts to the configured complaint mailbox (`ComplaintMailbox`, empty by default) is parsed as RFC 5965 - Feedback-Type, User-Agent, Original-Mail-From, Original-Rcpt-To, Arrival-Date, Source-IP, Reported-Domain, and the Message-ID, From, To and Subject of the message it wraps, the Subject decoded from its RFC 2047 encoded word - and recorded per sending domain and per sending account in `hm_complaints` (schema step 6049 to 6050, which also adds `hm_complaintvolume`, `hm_bouncecounts.bccomplaints`, `hm_bouncecounts.bclastcomplaint` and the `complaints.rate.high` alert rule). **Only the configured mailbox is an intake**, and the reason is the code’s rather than taste’s: a feedback report has none of a delivery report’s own evidence - its envelope sender is the provider’s, and the Message-ID and recipient it names are known to every other recipient of that message - so the one thing that tells it from a forgery is that it arrived where the provider was told to send it; reading any message anywhere would let a stranger who can mail one user stop this server sending to any address of that user’s domain. Three further gates need no setting: the report must be addressed to the mailbox and to nothing else of this server’s, the domain it is about must be one this server hosts - resolved from Original-Mail-From, then the returned message’s From, then Reported-Domain, each step taken when the one before does not *resolve*, which is what keeps a BATV `prvs=` or SRS return path from dropping the report - and an Original-Rcpt-To this server hosts lists nobody. A complaint is counted in `bccomplaints` with its own date in `bclastcomplaint`, never in a bounce counter, never in `bclastdelivered` and so never in the run of hard bounces, and past `ComplaintSuppressionThreshold` (**0 by default - record, count, list nobody**, which is the observe-only setting the page tells an operator to start with, since one forged report to a discoverable address would otherwise refuse whatever address it named) the reader’s address goes on **the same suppression list the bounces feed**, with `bssource` 3 and a 5.7.1 refusal that says a provider reported the mail as spam rather than claiming the mailbox is dead - so the refusal at RCPT TO, the removal at delivery, the page, removal by hand and the expiry sweep all apply unchanged. That list is applied whenever bounce processing **or** complaint processing is on, which is a change to shipped bounce behaviour and is now said in `BounceSuppression.md`, in the bounces settings description and on the Deck (`list_applies` beside `processing_enabled`). `not-spam` is recorded and counts towards nothing; the same report twice is one complaint, by a key over the Message-ID, the reader, the sender and the arrival time. The **complaint rate** is complaints over recipients a remote accepted, per domain, over a fixed seven-day window, in parts per million, with a domain that sent fewer than 100 messages in the window not judged at all; it is three gauges on `/metrics` under `MetricsPerDomainEnabled` - built by the metrics refresher thread and copied by the request thread, because that endpoint’s rule 5 is that a scrape touches no database - `GET /api/v1/complaints/rates`, `Utilities.GetComplaintRates` and `hmctl complaints rates`, and it raises `complaints.rate.high` at 1000 parts per million - the figure the providers call too high - seeded on by the schema step as the DKIM rotation rule was. Four settings in `hm_settings` over COM (ids 145 to 148), `/api/v1/settings/complaints`, the Deck’s Settings page and both command-line clients; the listing over `GET /api/v1/domains/{domain}/complaints` (scoped to its domain, view_configuration **and** view_message_trace as the suppression list’s rows are, and carrying `mailbox_configured` rather than the mailbox address, which a domain-confined caller has no business learning), `Utilities.GetComplaints`, the Deck’s per-domain Complaints view with the rate above the table, `hmctl complaint list` and `Get-HmComplaint`; the hourly sweep as a task and as `POST /api/v1/complaints/expire`, which prunes the reports on `ComplaintRetentionDays` and the daily tally on that or the seven-day window, whichever is longer, so a short retention cannot empty the window the rate is measured over. An address erasure forgets the complaints naming it; a backup does not carry one, and `AccountStores.cpp` says why. `hmailserver/docs/Complaints.md` is the administrator’s page, and says what the configured mailbox does **not** stop. **What stands between this and done is the gate:** the in-process `FeedbackReportParserTester` and `ComplaintTrackerTester` (from `MainOperations.TestInternals`), and the fixtures `SMTP/FeedbackReports` (13 tests) and `API/RestApiComplaints` (7), none of which has been run. What was checked without the bench: `build.ps1` Release at 0 errors and 0 warnings with MIDL rebuilding the type library, `build-tests.ps1` compiling the fixtures, the OpenAPI fragments of both REST files parsed as JSON the way the compiler concatenates them, and check-schema-versions, check-db-scripts (which builds the database and runs all 288 probes), check-sql-separators, check-upgrade-inserts, check-account-stores, check-audit-choke-point, check-role-choke-point, check-authz-choke-point, check-deck-script, check-deck-languages, check-catalogues, check-localisation, check-mnemonics, check-portal-script, check-portal-languages, check-ini-coverage, check-cli, check-test-settings-writes, check-password-choice-sites, check-deck-parity, check-roadmap, add-license-headers and the generate --check pairs. What it does not do, on purpose: group complaints by campaign (nothing here knows what a campaign is - the subject and Message-ID are recorded and can be searched), act on a `not-spam` report by removing a listing, or read a report from anywhere but the mailbox. The row as written: The large mailbox providers send an abuse report (RFC 5965, `message/feedback-report`) when a recipient presses "this is spam". This server has nowhere for one to arrive: a mailbox per domain that parses them, counts them by sending account and campaign, adds the complainant to the suppression list above, and warns when the rate crosses the threshold the provider publishes. Deliverability is mostly this. |
| ✅ | **DKIM keys that rotate** | **Built 15 September 2026 on the branch `dkim-rotation`, and gated green with batch 10 on 16 September 2026.** Everything the row asks for is written. A domain has a rotation policy - on or off (off everywhere until switched on), days between rotations (180), a propagation period in hours (72), a retention period in days (7) and a key size (2048, 3072 or 4096) - as five columns of `hm_domains` (schema step 6047 to 6048, which also adds `hm_dkimrotations` and seeds the `dkim.rotation.stalled` alert), through COM, `PUT /api/v1/domains/{domain}`, the Deck's domain editor, the Control Panel's DKIM tab and the XML backup. The server generates the next key itself (it had never written a DKIM key; only the desktop Control Panel had, on its own machine) into `<DataDirectory>/DKIM` with a protected DACL or 0600, stages it as the domain's secondary selector, shows the TXT record for the domain and, with alias signing on, every domain alias, and checks it from `DNSResolver` - the resolver DKIM verification uses - at every pass of a task that runs every fifteen minutes. The propagation clock starts at the first pass that sees the record carrying exactly that key and goes back to zero at any pass that does not; signing moves only after the whole period, in a pass that has just checked again, with the retiring slot free; the old selector is kept for the retention period and then retired, its key file deleted only if the rotation generated it, it is inside the DKIM directory, it holds a key and nothing else names it. Rotate now, check, switch (with a force that skips the period and never the check), retire and cancel over COM, seven REST routes in the OpenAPI document (scoped to a key's domains, the run refused to a domain-restricted key), the Deck's key-rotation card, the Control Panel's rotation section and DNS records page, `hmctl dkim` and seven PowerShell functions; a save or PUT that would put back a selector the rotation moved is refused (409), and both command-line clients leave the rotation-owned keys out of a configuration document (and out of an apply while a rotation started by hand holds them). `hmailserver/docs/DkimKeyRotation.md` is the administrator's page. An independent review the same day, reading the engine against its page, found six things wrong and all six are fixed on the branch, as ungated as the rest: a retire that never deleted its key (the guard counted the retiring domain's own row), a backup restore that deleted every generated key before putting back domains that name them, a deleted domain's keys removed before the delete had succeeded, the schedule still rotating inactive domains, a `dkim.rotation.stalled` alert that a failed *Rotate now* on an unscheduled domain left raised for good, and the clock-moving test hook documented as leaving nothing behind when the times it records persist (now documented as a test's tool). **What stands between this and done is the gate:** the in-process `DkimRotationTester`, and the fixtures `AntiSpam/DKIM/DkimKeyRotation` (18 tests), `API/RestApiDkimRotation` (8, also in the Linux suite), `Infrastructure/BackupRestore.ARestoreKeepsTheKeyFilesTheRotationGenerated` and the existing `AntiSpam/DKIM/DualSelectorRotation`, `API/RestApiDomains` and `API/RestApiAlerts`, on the Windows bench and the Linux run; and `ControlPanel.Tests`' `DkimRotationStatusTests`. What was checked without the bench: every changed C++ translation unit compiles with the build's flags and MIDL compiles the IDL; the regression suite's and the Linux suite's sources type-check with csc against an interop made from this branch's typelib; `check-db-scripts.ps1`, `check-sql-separators.py`, `check-deck-script.py`, `check-deck-languages.py`, `check-cli.py`, `check-localisation.py`, `check-mnemonics.py` and `check-catalogues.py` pass. What it does not do, on purpose: edit DNS, generate Ed25519 keys, or sign with both keys during a switch. The row as written: A key is generated once, written to a file and used until somebody remembers. What is needed is the ordinary practice: two selectors live at once, a new key published and given time to propagate, signing moved to it, the old one kept for the reports that arrive late and then removed - on a schedule, per domain, with the DNS the administrator must publish shown at each step and checked from the resolver before the switch. The DNS-record view already knows how to show and verify a record. |
| ✅ | **A backup that is encrypted** | **Built 16 September 2026 on branch `backup-encryption`; gated green on 16 September 2026 with batch 11, run across the two Windows benches (2,791 tests between them, none failing at the tip).** A passphrase (schema 6049 on the branch, the `backupencryptionpassphrase` row of `hm_settings`, kept under the machine's secret store as the route and relay passwords are) turns it on, and every archive is then encrypted as it is written: 7-Zip AES-256 with `-mhe=on`, so an archive without its passphrase cannot even be listed - measured, not assumed, by running `7za l` against one. The passphrase reaches 7-Zip on its **standard input**, never on a command line where the process list would show it: `ProcessLauncher` now writes a process's whole standard input before it starts, which also means an archive that asks for a passphrase nobody gave fails instead of waiting for ever at a console prompt nobody can see. The child's own output is left alone on Linux, where it is the journal's and is the only account of a failure anybody gets beyond an exit code, and goes to the null device on Windows, where a service has no console to write to. It is never logged, never in a COM or REST read (write-only, reported as set or not set), and never in the archive - the settings section the archive carries skips it as the stored `[Settings]` skip `PasswordPepper`, so a restore cannot overwrite the passphrase this server is using either. Every copy the server makes of it is overwritten rather than released - the bytes given to 7-Zip the moment they are in the pipe - so that what is left in a minidump is the settings store's copy alone, which `Backup.md` says plainly rather than claiming more. **The plaintext never goes to the destination.** Both halves of a backup are written to a file before they are archived - the index, which carries every account and the server's own stored secrets, and a whole copy of the message files, because 7-Zip is handed a directory - and an unencrypted run stages both in the destination. An encrypted one stages them under `TempDirectory` instead and removes the directory on every way out, because the destination is the share or the cloud mount the encryption exists because of, and a plaintext copy of every mailbox sitting there for the length of the compression is the exposure being prevented. The archive is byte-for-byte the same shape: 7-Zip names members from the last component of the path it is given. It costs temp-directory room the size of the message store, which is checked before the copy starts and refused with both numbers if it is not there. The verification extracts with it, and an archive that was meant to be encrypted and reads back unencrypted is discarded rather than called a backup. Four configurations refuse rather than write an archive nobody asked for: a stored passphrase this machine cannot open, a passphrase with compression off (which copies the message files beside the archive, where nothing encrypts them), the two halves of the setting disagreeing the other way - no passphrase recorded as set with one still stored, which is what an interrupted clear leaves and which would otherwise write an archive encrypted with a passphrase the log said was not being used - and a temp directory too small to stage in. The restore asks for it everywhere - `BackupManager.LoadEncryptedBackup` over COM, the Control Panel's restore card, `hmctl backup restore`, and **`POST /api/v1/backup/restore`, which is the first restore the REST API and so the Control Deck have ever had** (COM is Windows-only, so a Linux server previously had no way to restore at all) - and refuses before anything is touched, telling "this is encrypted and you gave no passphrase" apart from "that passphrase is wrong, or the archive is damaged" by reading the archive's own header (which answers the narrow question of whether the archive's HEADERS are encrypted, as this server's always are; an archive from elsewhere with only its contents encrypted is named as a possibility rather than called corrupt). The REST route's check is bounded at two minutes and answers 504 rather than hold one of four HTTP worker threads on an archive whose drive has stopped answering; no other way in has that bound, or needs it. A restore is now **recorded**: one audit row for the request - who asked, which archive, which parts, encrypted or not - written by the caller on the thread that knows who they are, over COM and over REST. What the restore then replaces is still not recorded, because it runs on the maintenance work queue where no actor exists, and `docs/AlertsAndAuditTrail.md` now says which half is which for both ways in rather than for COM alone. An archive taken without a passphrase still restores without one. `hmailserver/docs/Backup.md` has the walk, including what is lost if the passphrase is lost: everything in every archive written with it. **Gated:** `RegressionTests.Infrastructure.BackupEncryption` (eight tests: the file names hidden, the restore with the passphrase, the plaintext never appearing in the destination, the refusals with the server untouched, the passphrase in no read or log or archive, the unencrypted archive, the refused passphrases, and the REST route's refusals and restore) ran green in that gate. **And a gap worth naming rather than leaving to be discovered:** the one restore a Linux server has is the one with no Linux test behind it. The fixture is Windows-only - it drives COM to set up - so the Linux suite covers none of this, and the Linux-specific code it depends on has never been executed: the POSIX half of `ProcessLauncher::SetStandardInput`, `Compression`'s search for `7zz`/`7z`/`7za`/`7zr`, and the secret store's failure path that makes the backup refuse rather than write in the clear. The stdin behaviour was measured against the bundled 7za 19.00 on Windows only; p7zip and `7zz` are reasoned about, which is why the passphrase is written three times rather than once. `7zr` cannot encrypt at all and the run says so. |
| ✅ | **A calendar shared with somebody** | **Built 22 September 2026, schema 6086, no new setting (the one choice is an account preference). Not yet run against a bench: the fixture compiles, every checker is green and both builds say Build succeeded; the regression suite runs when the branch is gated.** The named-account share (read-only or read-write) had landed with the calendar page; this row adds the three things the organisation asks for next. **Free-busy for everyone in the domain:** `GET /api/v1/me/freebusy?address=&from=&to=` answers, for an active account of the caller's own domain (an alias domain's spelling resolved; another hosted domain, an unknown address and an account that chose not to be shown are one 404, so nothing is enumerated), the busy periods of that account's own calendars over a window of at most 62 days - every VEVENT instance expanded through the bounded recurrence module the calendar-query uses, an event that is TRANSPARENT, CANCELLED or declined left out, tentative when the account's PARTSTAT is TENTATIVE or NEEDS-ACTION - merged and sorted, with no title, location or description; the account's `freebusy` preference (`domain` or `nobody`, offered in Settings in one sentence) hides it; the CalDAV `free-busy-query` REPORT (RFC 4791 section 7.10) on any calendar the caller may read answers the same computation as a bare VFREEBUSY, no ORGANIZER or ATTENDEE (`Util/FreeBusy.h`, one unit under both); and the page's event editor draws an availability strip for the event's day once guests are named and a start is set, one row per person with the busy blocks and the event's own span outlined, a guest elsewhere marked as not on this server and one who chose nobody as not shown. **A calendar shared with a group:** `PUT /api/v1/me/calendars/{id}/shares/{name}` takes the name of one of the server's groups beside an account's address (a name that is both is the account); `hm_calendarshares` gains `sharegroupid` and the unique index widens to (calendar, account, group); a member sees the calendar exactly as a named sharee does, resolved at read time from the group's members, so a member added later sees it and one removed no longer does, an account reached by several shares has the widest rights of them and the owner is never listed their own calendar a second time through a group; the share is the group's and not a member's to leave, and leaving while a group still reaches the calendar gives the own share back and says so with the group's name; the DELETE by name is one 404 whether the name is a group or not; shared with a group as a delegate, every member may answer for the owner; a group deleted takes its share rows with it in its own removal, and the owner's listing drops a row whose group is gone, there being no foreign key because 0 is not a group; `GET /api/v1/me/calendars/{id}` lists shares as `{address}` or `{group, members}`. **A delegate who answers invitations on the owner's behalf:** a share gains `delegate` (`sharedelegate`, implies write); a METHOD:REQUEST delivered to a local account that names it as a guest (its address, an alias domain's spelling or an account alias) and not as the organiser is filed into its default calendar as it arrived (NEEDS-ACTION), replacing an older copy of the UID and never a newer, never a copy another organiser holds, keeping the answer given at the same SEQUENCE, and nothing is filed from a message flagged as spam, from a REQUEST that names somebody else, or for an account with no calendar row; the answer from the message updates that one event rather than doubling it, the two paths being one (`Imip::KeepInvitation`); `POST /api/v1/me/calendar/events/{id}/reply` with `partstat` is allowed to the owner and to a delegate, 403 to a plain writer and a reader, 400 for an event the owner is not a guest of, sets the OWNER's PARTSTAT with `SENT-BY="mailto:<delegate>"` on the ATTENDEE when a delegate answered (kept in the stored object, so the owner's page says who answered) and mails the organiser the REPLY with that ATTENDEE, From: the owner, Sender: the delegate, from the delegate's own address - what a mail client shows as "on behalf of"; the delegate's own calendar is not touched, and the owner answering in person clears the mark; the page offers Accept, Maybe and Decline on behalf of the owner in a calendar shared as a delegate, and to the owner in their own. `API/RestApiCalendarSharing.cs` (ports 9818-9819; 9816 was ClientDiscovery's) holds eleven cases with their negative controls - the adversarial review of the same day added the forgery, the spam and the uninvited REQUEST, the resend, the owner in the group, the widest rights, the leave that is not one, the one 404, the group delegate, the inactive account, the clock change, the daily rule, the shared report's scope, the stepped token and the owner-not-a-guest refusal - `build/portal-script-test.js` the strip and the on-behalf buttons, `docs/Calendar.md` the three sections. **Thinner than the row asks:** this is not RFC 6638 scheduling (no schedule-outbox or inbox, no free-busy of another server), and the delegation is this server's own - over REST and the page, its result shown through CalDAV's `owner` and `current-user-privilege-set` - not a grant over CalDAV's ACL properties; a room booking (section 12) is not here. |
| ✅ | **An out-of-office policy, not just a message** | **Done 17 September 2026.** The rest of the policy: a window for the classic reply - `Account.VacationMessageDays` and `Domain.VacationDays`, 0 to 3650, `vacation_days` over REST - after which the same sender is answered again while the reply stays on, 0 being once per absence as before; the once-per-sender memory moved from process memory to a table, `hm_autoreplies` (schema 6060), so a restart repeats nothing and days mean days, forgotten when the reply is switched off; an internal text on the account (`VacationInternalSubject`, `VacationInternalMessage`) that colleagues get and strangers never do, and an internal-only switch (`VacationMessageInternalOnly`) that answers colleagues and nobody else, the domain's external override still answering strangers with the domain's text in its place; and the domain form in the Deck gains the internal text, the override and the window, the account form the four new fields, the Control Panel's two dialogs the same, all in every language. `hmailserver/docs/OutOfOffice.md` is the policy written down. Five more tests in `SMTP/OutOfOfficeScope`: the memory across a restart and forgotten on the toggle, the window crossed and not crossed with 0 kept to once, the account's internal text to colleagues and the general one to a stranger, the internal-only reply silent to a stranger until the domain's override speaks, and the domain reply's own window. The row as written: A domain has its own out-of-office reply, which answers for an account that has none of its own (neither a stored message nor a Sieve vacation), and at most one reply answers any message. The domain reply can carry a separate subject and text for senders inside the installation, and leaving its external text empty makes it internal-only. With the external override on, an outside sender gets the domain's text in place of an account's personal one, while colleagues still get the account's. Every reply goes through one creator. It refuses a null return path, `Auto-Submitted`, `Precedence: bulk/list/junk`, any `List-*` header, `X-Auto-Response-Suppress` and `postmaster`/`mailer-daemon`; it sends with a null return path; and it answers each sender once. An administrator sets any account's reply, dates included, without signing in as them, in the Control Panel's account and domain dialogs, over REST, and in the Deck's account Auto-reply section. Six tests in `SMTP/OutOfOfficeScope.cs`. **Missing:** a period for the classic reply. Its once-per-sender memory is held in memory until the reply is switched off or the service restarts, rather than for N days; only Sieve's `vacation :days` has a window. Also missing: an internal-only reply or an internal text on the account itself, and the internal text and external override in the Deck's domain form, which REST already accepts. |
